L   # bad - repeated alternate patterns with the same conditions don't depend on the order
L   case x
L   in 0 | 1
L     first_method
L   in 1 | 0
L     second_method
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   case x
L   in 0 | 1
L     first_method
L   in 2 | 3
L     second_method
L   end
L 
L   # bad - repeated hash patterns with the same conditions don't depend on the order
L   case x
L   in foo: a, bar: b
L     first_method
L   in bar: b, foo: a
L     second_method
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   case x
L   in foo: a, bar: b
L     first_method
L   in bar: b, baz: c
L     second_method
L   end
L 
L   # bad - repeated array patterns with elements in the same order
L   case x
L   in [foo, bar]
L     first_method
L   in [foo, bar]
L     second_method
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   case x
L   in [foo, bar]
L     first_method
L   in [bar, foo]
L     second_method
L   end
L 
L   # bad - repeated the same patterns and guard conditions
L   case x
L   in foo if bar
L     first_method
L   in foo if bar
L     second_method
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   case x
L   in foo if bar
L     first_method
L   in foo if baz
L     second_method
L   end
C Lint/DuplicateMethods
S Details
L Checks for duplicated instance (or singleton) method
L definitions.
L 
L NOTE: Aliasing a method to itself is allowed, as it indicates that
L the developer intends to suppress Ruby's method redefinition warnings.
L See https://bugs.ruby-lang.org/issues/13574.
L 
L By default the cop can only detect duplicates within a single file.
L When `AllCops/UseProjectIndex` is enabled and the `rubydex` gem is installed,
L the cop additionally consults the project-wide index and reports methods
L whose duplicate definition lives in another file.
L 
L NOTE: The project index does not record whether a definition in another
L file is wrapped in a conditional, so a platform-specific redefinition in
L another file may still be reported. Aliasing the method to itself (see above)
L before redefining marks the redefinition as intentional and is respected
L across files. With `AllCops/ActiveSupportExtensionsEnabled: true`, Active
L Support's redefinition markers (`silence_redefinition_of_method` and
L `redefine_method`) are honored the same way.
L 
L NOTE: Methods defined with `define_method` are not recorded in the project
L index, so a duplicate whose other definition uses `define_method` cannot
L be detected across files.
L 
L Cross-file duplicates whose other definition lives in a file matching one
L of the `AllowedCrossFilePaths` patterns are not reported. This suits files
L that redefine application methods but are never loaded together with them,
L such as standalone scripts. Patterns are matched with the same glob (or
L regexp) semantics as `Exclude`, against the other file's path relative to
L the directory of the `.rubocop.yml` that configures the cop; absolute
L patterns are matched against the absolute path. Offenses inside such files
L themselves are best silenced with an ordinary per-cop `Exclude`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   def foo
L     1
L   end
L 
L   def foo
L     2
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   def foo
L     1
L   end
L 
L   alias foo bar
L 
L   # good
L   def foo
L     1
L   end
L 
L   def bar
L     2
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo
L     1
L   end
L 
L   alias bar foo
L 
L   # good
L   alias foo foo
L   def foo
L     1
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   alias_method :foo, :foo
L   def foo
L     1
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   class MyClass
L     extend Forwardable
L 
L     # or with: `def_instance_delegator`, `def_delegators`, `def_instance_delegators`
L     def_delegator :delegation_target, :delegated_method_name
L 
L     def delegated_method_name
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class MyClass
L     extend Forwardable
L 
L     def_delegator :delegation_target, :delegated_method_name
L 
L     def non_duplicated_delegated_method_name
L     end
L   end
S Example: AllCops:ActiveSupportExtensionsEnabled: false (default)
L   # good
L   def foo
L     1
L   end
L 
L   delegate :foo, to: :bar
S Example: AllCops:ActiveSupportExtensionsEnabled: true
L   # bad
L   def foo
L     1
L   end
L 
L   delegate :foo, to: :bar
L 
L   # good
L   def foo
L     1
L   end
L 
L   delegate :baz, to: :bar
L 
L   # good - delegate with splat arguments is ignored
L   def foo
L     1
L   end
L 
L   delegate :foo, **options
L 
L   # good - delegate inside a condition is ignored
L   def foo
L     1
L   end
L 
L   if cond
L     delegate :foo, to: :bar
L   end
L 
L   # good - Active Support's redefinition markers signal an intentional
L   # redefinition of a method defined in another file
L   silence_redefinition_of_method :foo
L   def foo
L     1
L   end
S Example: AllowedCrossFilePaths: ['script/**/*'] (default: [])
L   # Cross-file duplicates whose other definition lives in a file
L   # matching one of the patterns are not reported.
L 
L   # good - assuming `AppHelper#format` is also defined in
L   # `script/backfill.rb`, which is never loaded with this file
L   class AppHelper
L     def format
L     end
L   end
S Example: DelegatingMethods: ['delegate', 'expose'] (default: ['delegate'])
L   # A project's own `delegate`-shaped macros can be registered so the
L   # methods they define are recognized (with
L   # `AllCops/ActiveSupportExtensionsEnabled: true`).
L 
L   # bad
L   def foo
L     1
L   end
L 
L   expose :foo, to: :bar
C Lint/DuplicateRegexpCharacterClassElement
S Details
L Checks for duplicate elements in `Regexp` character classes.
S Example
L   # bad
L   r = /[xyx]/
L 
L   # bad
L   r = /[0-9x0-9]/
L 
L   # good
L   r = /[xy]/
L 
L   # good
L   r = /[0-9x]/
C Lint/DuplicateRequire
S Details
L Checks for duplicate ``require``s and ``require_relative``s.
S Safety
L   This cop's autocorrection is unsafe because it may break the dependency order
L   of `require`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   require 'foo'
L   require 'bar'
L   require 'foo'
L 
L   # good
L   require 'foo'
L   require 'bar'
L 
L   # good
L   require 'foo'
L   require_relative 'foo'
C Lint/DuplicateRescueException
S Details
L Checks that there are no repeated exceptions
L used in 'rescue' expressions.
S Example
L   # bad
L   begin
L     something
L   rescue FirstException
L     handle_exception
L   rescue FirstException
L     handle_other_exception
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   begin
L     something
L   rescue FirstException
L     handle_exception
L   rescue SecondException
L     handle_other_exception
L   end
C Lint/DuplicateSetElement
S Details
L Checks for duplicate literal, constant, or variable elements in `Set` and `SortedSet`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   Set[:foo, :bar, :foo]
L 
L   # good
L   Set[:foo, :bar]
L 
L   # bad
L   Set.new([:foo, :bar, :foo])
L 
L   # good
L   Set.new([:foo, :bar])
L 
L   # bad
L   [:foo, :bar, :foo].to_set
L 
L   # good
L   [:foo, :bar].to_set
L 
L   # bad
L   SortedSet[:foo, :bar, :foo]
L 
L   # good
L   SortedSet[:foo, :bar]
L 
L   # bad
L   SortedSet.new([:foo, :bar, :foo])
L 
L   # good
L   SortedSet.new([:foo, :bar])
C Lint/EachWithObjectArgument
S Details
L Checks if each_with_object is called with an immutable
L argument. Since the argument is the object that the given block shall
L make calls on to build something based on the enumerable that
L each_with_object iterates over, an immutable argument makes no sense.
L It's definitely a bug.
S Example
L   # bad
L   sum = numbers.each_with_object(0) { |e, a| a += e }
L 
L   # good
L   num = 0
L   sum = numbers.each_with_object(num) { |e, a| a += e }
C Lint/ElseLayout
S Details
L Checks for odd `else` block layout - like
L having an expression on the same line as the `else` keyword,
L which is usually a mistake.
L 
L Its autocorrection tweaks layout to keep the syntax. So, this autocorrection
L is compatible correction for bad case syntax, but if your code makes a mistake
L with `elsif` and `else`, you will have to correct it manually.
S Example
L   # bad
L 
L   if something
L     # ...
L   else do_this
L     do_that
L   end
L 
L   # good
L 
L   # This code is compatible with the bad case. It will be autocorrected like this.
L   if something
L     # ...
L   else
L     do_this
L     do_that
L   end
L 
L   # This code is incompatible with the bad case.
L   # If `do_this` is a condition, `elsif` should be used instead of `else`.
L   if something
L     # ...
L   elsif do_this
L     do_that
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L 
L   # For single-line conditionals using `then` the layout is disallowed
L   # when the `else` body is multiline because it is treated as a lint offense.
L   if something then on_the_same_line_as_then
L   else first_line
L     second_line
L   end
L 
L   # good
L 
L   # For single-line conditional using `then` the layout is allowed
L   # when `else` body is a single-line because it is treated as intentional.
L 
L   if something then on_the_same_line_as_then
L   else single_line
L   end
C Lint/EmptyBlock
S Details
L Checks for blocks without a body.
L Such empty blocks are typically an oversight or we should provide a comment
L to clarify what we're aiming for.
L 
L Empty lambdas and procs are ignored by default.
L 
L NOTE: For backwards compatibility, the configuration that allows/disallows
L empty lambdas and procs is called `AllowEmptyLambdas`, even though it also
L applies to procs.
S Example
L   # bad
L   items.each { |item| }
L 
L   # good
L   items.each { |item| puts item }
S Example: AllowComments: true (default)
L   # good
L   items.each do |item|
L     # TODO: implement later (inner comment)
L   end
L 
L   items.each { |item| } # TODO: implement later (inline comment)
S Example: AllowComments: false
L   # bad
L   items.each do |item|
L     # TODO: implement later (inner comment)
L   end
L 
L   items.each { |item| } # TODO: implement later (inline comment)
S Example: AllowEmptyLambdas: true (default)
L   # good
L   allow(subject).to receive(:callable).and_return(-> {})
L 
L   placeholder = lambda do
L   end
L   (callable || placeholder).call
L 
L   proc { }
L 
L   Proc.new { }
S Example: AllowEmptyLambdas: false
L   # bad
L   allow(subject).to receive(:callable).and_return(-> {})
L 
L   placeholder = lambda do
L   end
L   (callable || placeholder).call
L 
L   proc { }
L 
L   Proc.new { }
C Lint/EmptyClass
S Details
L Checks for classes and metaclasses without a body.
L Such empty classes and metaclasses are typically an oversight or we should provide a comment
L to be clearer what we're aiming for.
S Example
L   # bad
L   class Foo
L   end
L 
L   class Bar
L     class << self
L     end
L   end
L 
L   class << obj
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class Foo
L     def do_something
L       # ... code
L     end
L   end
L 
L   class Bar
L     class << self
L       attr_reader :bar
L     end
L   end
L 
L   class << obj
L     attr_reader :bar
L   end
S Example: AllowComments: false (default)
L   # bad
L   class Foo
L     # TODO: implement later
L   end
L 
L   class Bar
L     class << self
L       # TODO: implement later
L     end
L   end
L 
L   class << obj
L     # TODO: implement later
L   end
S Example: AllowComments: true
L   # good
L   class Foo
L     # TODO: implement later
L   end
L 
L   class Bar
L     class << self
L       # TODO: implement later
L     end
L   end
L 
L   class << obj
L     # TODO: implement later
L   end
C Lint/EmptyConditionalBody
S Details
L Checks for the presence of `if`, `elsif` and `unless` branches without a body.
L 
L NOTE: empty `else` branches are handled by `Style/EmptyElse`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   if condition
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   unless condition
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   if condition
L     do_something
L   elsif other_condition
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   if condition
L     do_something
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   unless condition
L     do_something
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   if condition
L     do_something
L   elsif other_condition
L     nil
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   if condition
L     do_something
L   elsif other_condition
L     do_something_else
L   end
S Example: AllowComments: true (default)
L   # good
L   if condition
L     do_something
L   elsif other_condition
L     # noop
L   end
S Example: AllowComments: false
L   # bad
L   if condition
L     do_something
L   elsif other_condition
L     # noop
L   end
C Lint/EmptyEnsure
S Details
L Checks for empty `ensure` blocks.
S Example
L   # bad
L   def some_method
L     do_something
L   ensure
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   begin
L     do_something
L   ensure
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def some_method
L     do_something
L   ensure
L     do_something_else
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   begin
L     do_something
L   ensure
L     do_something_else
L   end
C Lint/EmptyExpression
S Details
L Checks for the presence of empty expressions.
S Example
L   # bad
L 
L   foo = ()
L   if ()
L     bar
L   end
L 
L   # good
L 
L   foo = (some_expression)
L   if (some_expression)
L     bar
L   end
C Lint/EmptyFile
S Details
L Enforces that Ruby source files are not empty.
S Example
L   # bad
L   # Empty file
L 
L   # good
L   # File containing non commented source lines
S Example: AllowComments: true (default)
L   # good
L   # File consisting only of comments
S Example: AllowComments: false
L   # bad
L   # File consisting only of comments
C Lint/EmptyInPattern
S Details
L Checks for the presence of `in` pattern branches without a body.
S Example
L   # bad
L   case condition
L   in [a]
L     do_something
L   in [a, b]
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   case condition
L   in [a]
L     do_something
L   in [a, b]
L     nil
L   end
S Example: AllowComments: true (default)
L   # good
L   case condition
L   in [a]
L     do_something
L   in [a, b]
L     # noop
L   end
S Example: AllowComments: false
L   # bad
L   case condition
L   in [a]
L     do_something
L   in [a, b]
L     # noop
L   end
C Lint/EmptyInterpolation
S Details
L Checks for empty interpolation.
S Example
L   # bad
L   "result is #{}"
L 
L   # good
L   "result is #{some_result}"
C Lint/EmptyWhen
S Details
L Checks for the presence of `when` branches without a body.
S Example
L   # bad
L   case foo
L   when bar
L     do_something
L   when baz
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   case condition
L   when foo
L     do_something
L   when bar
L     nil
L   end
S Example: AllowComments: true (default)
L   # good
L   case condition
L   when foo
L     do_something
L   when bar
L     # noop
L   end
S Example: AllowComments: false
L   # bad
L   case condition
L   when foo
L     do_something
L   when bar
L     # do nothing
L   end
C Lint/EnsureReturn
S Details
L Checks for `return` from an `ensure` block.
L `return` from an ensure block is a dangerous code smell as it
L will take precedence over any exception being raised,
L and the exception will be silently thrown away as if it were rescued.
L 
L If you want to rescue some (or all) exceptions, best to do it explicitly
S Example
L   # bad
L   def foo
L     do_something
L   ensure
L     cleanup
L     return self
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo
L     do_something
L     self
L   ensure
L     cleanup
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo
L     begin
L       do_something
L     rescue SomeException
L       # Let's ignore this exception
L     end
L     self
L   ensure
L     cleanup
L   end
C Lint/ErbNewArguments
S Details
L Emulates the following Ruby warnings in Ruby 2.6.
L 
L [source,console]
L ----
L $ cat example.rb
L ERB.new('hi', nil, '-', '@output_buffer')
L $ ruby -rerb example.rb
L example.rb:1: warning: Passing safe_level with the 2nd argument of ERB.new is
L deprecated. Do not use it, and specify other arguments as keyword arguments.
L example.rb:1: warning: Passing trim_mode with the 3rd argument of ERB.new is
L deprecated. Use keyword argument like ERB.new(str, trim_mode:...) instead.
L example.rb:1: warning: Passing eoutvar with the 4th argument of ERB.new is
L deprecated. Use keyword argument like ERB.new(str, eoutvar: ...) instead.
L ----
L 
L Now non-keyword arguments other than first one are softly deprecated
L and will be removed when Ruby 2.5 becomes EOL.
L `ERB.new` with non-keyword arguments is deprecated since ERB 2.2.0.
L Use `:trim_mode` and `:eoutvar` keyword arguments to `ERB.new`.
L This cop identifies places where `ERB.new(str, trim_mode, eoutvar)` can
L be replaced by `ERB.new(str, trim_mode: trim_mode, eoutvar: eoutvar)`.
S Example
L   # Target codes supports Ruby 2.6 and higher only
L   # bad
L   ERB.new(str, nil, '-', '@output_buffer')
L 
L   # good
L   ERB.new(str, trim_mode: '-', eoutvar: '@output_buffer')
L 
L   # Target codes supports Ruby 2.5 and lower only
L   # good
L   ERB.new(str, nil, '-', '@output_buffer')
L 
L   # Target codes supports Ruby 2.6, 2.5 and lower
L   # bad
L   ERB.new(str, nil, '-', '@output_buffer')
L 
L   # good
L   # Ruby standard library style
L   # https://github.com/ruby/ruby/commit/3406c5d
L   if ERB.instance_method(:initialize).parameters.assoc(:key) # Ruby 2.6+
L     ERB.new(str, trim_mode: '-', eoutvar: '@output_buffer')
L   else
L     ERB.new(str, nil, '-', '@output_buffer')
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   # Use `RUBY_VERSION` style
L   if RUBY_VERSION >= '2.6'
L     ERB.new(str, trim_mode: '-', eoutvar: '@output_buffer')
L   else
L     ERB.new(str, nil, '-', '@output_buffer')
L   end
C Lint/FlipFlop
S Details
L Looks for uses of flip-flop operator
L based on the Ruby Style Guide.
L 
L Here is the history of flip-flops in Ruby.
L flip-flop operator is deprecated in Ruby 2.6.0 and
L the deprecation has been reverted by Ruby 2.7.0 and
L backported to Ruby 2.6.
L See: https://bugs.ruby-lang.org/issues/5400
S Example
L   # bad
L   (1..20).each do |x|
L     puts x if (x == 5) .. (x == 10)
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   (1..20).each do |x|
L     puts x if (x >= 5) && (x <= 10)
L   end
C Lint/FloatComparison
S Details
L Checks for the presence of precise comparison of floating point numbers.
L 
L Floating point values are inherently inaccurate, and comparing them for exact equality
L is almost never the desired semantics. Comparison via the `==/!=` operators checks
L floating-point value representation to be exactly the same, which is very unlikely
L if you perform any arithmetic operations involving precision loss.
S Example
L   # bad
L   x == 0.1
L   x != 0.1
L 
L   # bad
L   case value
L   when 1.0
L     foo
L   when 2.0
L     bar
L   end
L 
L   # good - using BigDecimal
L   x.to_d == 0.1.to_d
L 
L   # good - comparing against zero
L   x == 0.0
L   x != 0.0
L 
L   # good
L   (x - 0.1).abs < Float::EPSILON
L 
L   # good
L   tolerance = 0.0001
L   (x - 0.1).abs < tolerance
L 
L   # good - comparing against nil
L   Float(x, exception: false) == nil
L 
L   # good - using epsilon comparison in case expression
L   case
L   when (value - 1.0).abs < Float::EPSILON
L     foo
L   when (value - 2.0).abs < Float::EPSILON
L     bar
L   end
L 
L   # Or some other epsilon based type of comparison:
L   # https://www.embeddeduse.com/2019/08/26/qt-compare-two-floats/
C Lint/FloatOutOfRange
S Details
L Identifies `Float` literals which are, like, really really really
L really really really really really big. Too big. No-one needs Floats
L that big. If you need a float that big, something is wrong with you.
S Example
L   # bad
L   float = 3.0e400
L 
L   # good
L   float = 42.9
C Lint/FormatParameterMismatch
S Details
L This lint sees if there is a mismatch between the number of
L expected fields for format/sprintf/#% and what is actually
L passed as arguments.
L 
L In addition, it checks whether different formats are used in the same
L format string. Do not mix numbered, unnumbered, and named formats in
L the same format string.
S Example
L   # bad
L   format('A value: %s and another: %i', a_value)
L 
L   # good
L   format('A value: %s and another: %i', a_value, another)
L 
L   # bad
L   format('Unnumbered format: %s and numbered: %2$s', a_value, another)
L 
L   # good
L   format('Numbered format: %1$s and numbered %2$s', a_value, another)
C Lint/HashCompareByIdentity
S Details
L Prefer using `Hash#compare_by_identity` rather than using `object_id`
L for hash keys.
L 
L This cop looks for hashes being keyed by objects' `object_id`, using
L one of these methods: `key?`, `has_key?`, `fetch`, `[]` and `[]=`.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe. Although unlikely, the hash could store both object
L   ids and other values that need be compared by value, and thus
L   could be a false positive.
L 
L   Furthermore, this cop cannot guarantee that the receiver of one of the
L   methods (`key?`, etc.) is actually a hash.
S Example
L   # bad
L   hash = {}
L   hash[foo.object_id] = :bar
L   hash.key?(baz.object_id)
L 
L   # good
L   hash = {}.compare_by_identity
L   hash[foo] = :bar
L   hash.key?(baz)
C Lint/HashNewWithKeywordArgumentsAsDefault
S Details
L Checks for the deprecated use of keyword arguments as a default in `Hash.new`.
L 
L This usage raises a warning in Ruby 3.3 and results in an error in Ruby 3.4.
L In Ruby 3.4, keyword arguments will instead be used to change the behavior of a hash.
L For example, the capacity option can be passed to create a hash with a certain size
L if you know it in advance, for better performance.
L 
L NOTE: The following corner case may result in a false negative when upgrading from Ruby 3.3
L or earlier, but it is intentionally not detected to respect the expected usage in Ruby 3.4.
L 
L [source,ruby]
L ----
L Hash.new(capacity: 42)
L ----
S Example
L   # bad
L   Hash.new(key: :value)
L 
L   # good
L   Hash.new({key: :value})
C Lint/HeredocMethodCallPosition
S Details
L Checks for the ordering of a method call where
L the receiver of the call is a HEREDOC.
S Example
L   # bad
L   <<-SQL
L     bar
L   SQL
L   .strip_indent
L 
L   <<-SQL
L     bar
L   SQL
L   .strip_indent
L   .trim
L 
L   # good
L   <<~SQL
L     bar
L   SQL
L 
L   <<~SQL.trim
L     bar
L   SQL
C Lint/IdentityComparison
S Details
L Prefer `equal?` over `==` when comparing `object_id`.
L 
L `Object#equal?` is provided to compare objects for identity, and in contrast
L `Object#==` is provided for the purpose of doing value comparison.
S Example
L   # bad
L   foo.object_id == bar.object_id
L   foo.object_id != baz.object_id
L 
L   # good
L   foo.equal?(bar)
L   !foo.equal?(baz)
C Lint/ImplicitStringConcatenation
S Details
L Checks for implicit string concatenation of string literals
L which are on the same line.
S Example
L   # bad
L   array = ['Item 1' 'Item 2']
L 
L   # good
L   array = ['Item 1Item 2']
L   array = ['Item 1' + 'Item 2']
L   array = [
L     'Item 1' \
L     'Item 2'
L   ]
C Lint/IncompatibleIoSelectWithFiberScheduler
S Details
L Checks for `IO.select` that is incompatible with Fiber Scheduler since Ruby 3.0.
L 
L When an array of IO objects waiting for an exception (the third argument of `IO.select`)
L is used as an argument, there is no alternative API, so offenses are not registered.
L 
L NOTE: When the method is successful the return value of `IO.select` is `[[IO]]`,
L and the return value of `io.wait_readable` and `io.wait_writable` are `self`.
L They are not autocorrected when assigning a return value because these types are different.
L It's up to user how to handle the return value.
S Safety
L   This cop's autocorrection is unsafe because `NoMethodError` occurs
L   if `require 'io/wait'` is not called.
S Example
L   # bad
L   IO.select([io], [], [], timeout)
L 
L   # good
L   io.wait_readable(timeout)
L 
L   # bad
L   IO.select([], [io], [], timeout)
L 
L   # good
L   io.wait_writable(timeout)
C Lint/IneffectiveAccessModifier
S Details
L Checks for `private` or `protected` access modifiers which are
L applied to a singleton method. These access modifiers do not make
L singleton methods private/protected. `private_class_method` can be
L used for that.
S Example
L   # bad
L   class C
L     private
L 
L     def self.method
L       puts 'hi'
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class C
L     def self.method
L       puts 'hi'
L     end
L 
L     private_class_method :method
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class C
L     class << self
L       private
L 
L       def method
L         puts 'hi'
L       end
L     end
L   end
C Lint/InheritException
S Details
L Looks for error classes inheriting from `Exception`.
L It is configurable to suggest using either `StandardError` (default) or
L `RuntimeError` instead.
S Safety
L   This cop's autocorrection is unsafe because `rescue` that omit
L   exception class handle `StandardError` and its subclasses,
L   but not `Exception` and its subclasses.
L 
L When `AllCops/UseProjectIndex` is enabled and the `rubydex` gem is
L installed, indirect inheritance is also detected: a class whose parent
L (defined anywhere in the project) ultimately inherits from `Exception`
L is reported, without autocorrection.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: standard_error (default)
L   # bad
L 
L   class C < Exception; end
L 
L   C = Class.new(Exception)
L 
L   # good
L 
L   class C < StandardError; end
L 
L   C = Class.new(StandardError)
S Example: EnforcedStyle: runtime_error
L   # bad
L 
L   class C < Exception; end
L 
L   C = Class.new(Exception)
L 
L   # good
L 
L   class C < RuntimeError; end
L 
L   C = Class.new(RuntimeError)
C Lint/InterpolationCheck
S Details
L Checks for interpolation in a single quoted string.
S Safety
L   This cop's autocorrection is unsafe because although it always replaces single quotes as
L   if it were miswritten double quotes, it is not always the case. For example,
L   `'#{foo} bar'` would be replaced by `"#{foo} bar"`, so the replaced code would evaluate
L   the expression `foo`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   foo = 'something with #{interpolation} inside'
L 
L   # good
L   foo = "something with #{interpolation} inside"
C Lint/ItWithoutArgumentsInBlock
S Details
L Emulates the following Ruby warning in Ruby 3.3.
L 
L [source,ruby]
L ----
L $ ruby -e '0.times { it }'
L -e:1: warning: `it` calls without arguments will refer to the first block param in Ruby 3.4;
L use it() or self.it
L ----
L 
L `it` calls without arguments will refer to the first block param in Ruby 3.4.
L So use `it()` or `self.it` to ensure compatibility.
S Example
L   # bad
L   do_something { it }
L 
L   # good
L   do_something { it() }
L   do_something { self.it }
C Lint/LambdaWithoutLiteralBlock
S Details
L Checks uses of lambda without a literal block.
L It emulates the following warning in Ruby 3.0:
L 
L   $ ruby -vwe 'lambda(&proc {})'
L   ruby 3.0.0p0 (2020-12-25 revision 95aff21468) [x86_64-darwin19]
L   -e:1: warning: lambda without a literal block is deprecated; use the proc without
L   lambda instead
L 
L This way, proc object is never converted to lambda.
L Autocorrection replaces with compatible proc argument.
S Example
L   # bad
L   lambda(&proc { do_something })
L   lambda(&Proc.new { do_something })
L 
L   # good
L   proc { do_something }
L   Proc.new { do_something }
L   lambda { do_something } # If you use lambda.
C Lint/LiteralAsCondition
S Details
L Checks for literals used as the conditions or as
L operands in and/or expressions serving as the conditions of
L if/while/until/case-when/case-in.
L 
L NOTE: Literals in `case-in` condition where the match variable is used in
L `in` are accepted as a pattern matching.
S Example
L   # bad
L   if 20
L     do_something
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   # We're only interested in the left hand side being a truthy literal,
L   # because it affects the evaluation of the &&, whereas the right hand
L   # side will be conditionally executed/called and can be a literal.
L   if true && some_var
L     do_something
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   if some_var
L     do_something
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   # When using a boolean value for an infinite loop.
L   while true
L     break if condition
L   end
C Lint/LiteralAssignmentInCondition
S Details
L Checks for literal assignments in the conditions of `if`, `while`, and `until`.
L It emulates the following Ruby warning:
L 
L [source,console]
L ----
L $ ruby -we 'if x = true; end'
L -e:1: warning: found `= literal' in conditional, should be ==
L ----
L 
L As a lint cop, it cannot be determined if `==` is appropriate as intended,
L therefore this cop does not provide autocorrection.
S Example
L   # bad
L   if x = 42
L     do_something
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   if x == 42
L     do_something
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   if x = y
L     do_something
L   end
C Lint/LiteralInInterpolation
S Details
L Checks for interpolated literals.
L 
L NOTE: Array literals interpolated in regexps are not handled by this cop, but
L by `Lint/ArrayLiteralInRegexp` instead.
S Example
L   # bad
L   "result is #{10}"
L 
L   # good
L   "result is 10"
C Lint/Loop
S Details
L Checks for uses of `begin...end while/until something`.
S Safety
L   The cop is unsafe because behavior can change in some cases, including
L   if a local variable inside the loop body is accessed outside of it, or if the
L   loop body raises a `StopIteration` exception (which `Kernel#loop` rescues).
S Example
L   # bad
L 
L   # using while
L   begin
L     do_something
L   end while some_condition
L 
L   # good
L 
L   # while replacement
L   loop do
L     do_something
L     break unless some_condition
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L 
L   # using until
L   begin
L     do_something
L   end until some_condition
L 
L   # good
L 
L   # until replacement
L   loop do
L     do_something
L     break if some_condition
L   end
C Lint/MisplacedMagicComment
S Details
L Checks for magic comments placed where Ruby silently ignores them.
L 
L The `encoding` magic comment is only honored on the first line of a file,
L or on the second line when the first line is a shebang. Anywhere else it
L is silently ignored - even directly below another comment or a blank line.
L 
L Other magic comments (such as `frozen_string_literal`) are honored
L anywhere before the first token of code, but are ignored after any code,
L with a warning emitted only when running Ruby with `-w`.
L 
L A magic comment misplaced ahead of a shebang also renders the shebang
L ineffective, since a shebang is only recognized on the first line.
L 
L NOTE: An `encoding` comment that is only preceded by other magic
L comments is not flagged by this cop; that case is handled by
L `Lint/OrderedMagicComments`. `shareable_constant_value` is never
L flagged, as Ruby intentionally allows it mid-file with block scoping.
S Safety
L   This cop's autocorrection is unsafe because moving the magic comment
L   to its effective position activates it, which changes runtime
L   behavior (e.g. the source encoding or string mutability).
S Example
L   # bad
L   # Documentation comment
L   # encoding: ascii-8bit
L   puts 'hello'
L 
L   # good
L   # encoding: ascii-8bit
L   # Documentation comment
L   puts 'hello'
L 
L   # bad
L   require 'foo'
L   # frozen_string_literal: true
L 
L   # good
L   # frozen_string_literal: true
L   require 'foo'
L 
L   # bad
L   # frozen_string_literal: true
L   #!/usr/bin/env ruby
L   puts 'hello'
L 
L   # good
L   #!/usr/bin/env ruby
L   # frozen_string_literal: true
L   puts 'hello'
C Lint/MissingCopEnableDirective
S Details
L Checks that there is an `# rubocop:enable ...` statement
L after a `# rubocop:disable ...` statement. This will prevent leaving
L cop disables on wide ranges of code, that later contributors to
L a file wouldn't be aware of.
L 
L You can set `MaxRangeSize` to define the maximum number of
L consecutive lines a cop can be disabled for.
L 
L - `.inf` any size (default)
L - `0` allows only single-line disables
L - `1` means the maximum allowed is as follows:
L 
L [source,ruby]
L ----
L # rubocop:disable SomeCop
L a = 1
L # rubocop:enable SomeCop
L ----
S Example: MaxRangeSize: .inf (default)
L   # good
L   # rubocop:disable Layout/SpaceAroundOperators
L   x= 0
L   # rubocop:enable Layout/SpaceAroundOperators
L   # y = 1
L   # EOF
L 
L   # bad
L   # rubocop:disable Layout/SpaceAroundOperators
L   x= 0
L   # EOF
S Example: MaxRangeSize: 2
L   # good
L   # rubocop:disable Layout/SpaceAroundOperators
L   x= 0
L   # With the previous, there are 2 lines on which cop is disabled.
L   # rubocop:enable Layout/SpaceAroundOperators
L 
L   # bad
L   # rubocop:disable Layout/SpaceAroundOperators
L   x= 0
L   x += 1
L   # Including this, that's 3 lines on which the cop is disabled.
L   # rubocop:enable Layout/SpaceAroundOperators
C Lint/MissingSuper
S Details
L Checks for the presence of constructors and lifecycle callbacks
L without calls to `super`.
L 
L This cop does not consider `method_missing` (and `respond_to_missing?`)
L because in some cases it makes sense to overtake what is considered a
L missing method. In other cases, the theoretical ideal handling could be
L challenging or verbose for no actual gain.
L 
L Autocorrection is not supported because the position of `super` cannot be
L determined automatically.
L 
L `Object` and `BasicObject` are allowed by this cop because of their
L stateless nature. However, sometimes you might want to allow other parent
L classes from this cop, for example in the case of an abstract class that is
L not meant to be called with `super`. In those cases, you can use the
L `AllowedParentClasses` option to specify which classes should be allowed
L *in addition to* `Object` and `BasicObject`.
L 
L When `AllCops/UseProjectIndex` is enabled and the `rubydex` gem is installed,
L the constructor check additionally consults the project-wide index: if the
L class' entire ancestry is resolvable and no ancestor defines `initialize`,
L no offense is registered, since `super` would only reach the no-op
L `Object#initialize`. Classes whose ancestry contains an unresolvable
L superclass or mixin (e.g. one defined in a gem) are still reported.
S Example
L   # bad
L   class Employee < Person
L     def initialize(name, salary)
L       @salary = salary
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class Employee < Person
L     def initialize(name, salary)
L       super(name)
L       @salary = salary
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   Employee = Class.new(Person) do
L     def initialize(name, salary)
L       @salary = salary
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   Employee = Class.new(Person) do
L     def initialize(name, salary)
L       super(name)
L       @salary = salary
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   class Parent
L     def self.inherited(base)
L       do_something
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class Parent
L     def self.inherited(base)
L       super
L       do_something
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class ClassWithNoParent
L     def initialize
L       do_something
L     end
L   end
S Example: AllowedParentClasses: [MyAbstractClass]
L   # good
L   class MyConcreteClass < MyAbstractClass
L     def initialize
L       do_something
L     end
L   end
C Lint/MixedCaseRange
S Details
L Checks for mixed-case character ranges since they include likely unintended characters.
L 
L Offenses are registered for regexp character classes like `/[A-z]/`
L as well as range objects like `('A'..'z')`.
L 
L NOTE: `Range` objects cannot be autocorrected.
S Safety
L   The cop autocorrects regexp character classes
L   by replacing one character range with two: `A-z` becomes `A-Za-z`.
L   In most cases this is probably what was originally intended
L   but it changes the regexp to no longer match symbols it used to include.
L   For this reason, this cop's autocorrect is unsafe (it will
L   change the behavior of the code).
S Example
L   # bad
L   r = /[A-z]/
L 
L   # good
L   r = /[A-Za-z]/
C Lint/MixedRegexpCaptureTypes
S Details
L Do not mix named captures and numbered captures in a `Regexp` literal
L because numbered capture is ignored if they're mixed.
L Replace numbered captures with non-capturing groupings or
L named captures.
S Example
L   # bad
L   /(?<foo>FOO)(BAR)/
L 
L   # good
L   /(?<foo>FOO)(?<bar>BAR)/
L 
L   # good
L   /(?<foo>FOO)(?:BAR)/
L 
L   # good
L   /(FOO)(BAR)/
C Lint/MultipleComparison
S Details
L In math and Python, we can use `x < y < z` style comparison to compare
L multiple values. However, we can't use the comparison in Ruby. However,
L the comparison is not a syntax error. This cop checks the bad usage of
L comparison operators.
S Example
L   # bad
L   x < y < z
L   10 <= x <= 20
L 
L   # good
L   x < y && y < z
L   10 <= x && x <= 20
C Lint/NameTypo
S Details
L Checks for probable typos in constant and method names: a name that
L does not resolve anywhere in the project, used in a namespace the
L project does define, with a close-named sibling to suggest instead.
L 
L The check is powered by the project-wide index, so it only runs when
L `AllCops/UseProjectIndex` is enabled and the `rubydex` gem is installed.
L Without the index the cop does nothing.
L 
L Constants are checked only in qualified references (`Foo::Bar`) whose
L namespace resolves in the index; bare names cannot be distinguished
L from constants provided by gems or the standard library. Methods are
L checked only in calls on constant receivers (`Foo.bar`) whose entire
L indexed ancestry is resolved, so methods gained through gem classes or
L dynamic definitions never produce offenses. In both cases an offense
L requires a similarly named alternative to exist — an unknown name
L alone is not reported, since the index does not see gems or the
L standard library.
L 
L For the same reason, a namespace whose root segment names a gem in the
L bundle (`Flipper` for the `flipper` gem) is left alone: a project that
L reopens it (`module Flipper; module Adapters; ...`) makes it resolve in
L the index, while the members the gem itself defines stay invisible and
L would look like typos. Enabling `AllCops/ProjectIndexIncludesGems`
L indexes those sources and restores the check.
L 
L Names that appear as symbols or inside string literals in the same
L file are never reported, since they usually belong to runtime
L definitions the index cannot see (`stub_const`, `const_set`,
L `define_method`, and the like).
S Example
L   # bad - Services::UserCraetor is not defined, Services::UserCreator is
L   Services::UserCraetor.new
L 
L   # good
L   Services::UserCreator.new
L 
L   # bad - Report.generate_sumary is not defined, Report.generate_summary is
L   Report.generate_sumary
L 
L   # good
L   Report.generate_summary
S Example: CheckConstants: false
L   # good - constant references are not checked
L   Services::UserCraetor.new
S Example: CheckMethods: false
L   # good - method calls are not checked
L   Report.generate_sumary
S Example: AllowedNames: ['generate_sumary']
L   # good - the name is explicitly allowed
L   Report.generate_sumary
C Lint/NestedMethodDefinition
S Details
L Checks for nested method definitions.
S Example
L   # bad
L 
L   # `bar` definition actually produces methods in the same scope
L   # as the outer `foo` method. Furthermore, the `bar` method
L   # will be redefined every time `foo` is invoked.
L   def foo
L     def bar
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L 
L   def foo
L     bar = -> { puts 'hello' }
L     bar.call
L   end
L 
L   # good
L 
L   # `class_eval`, `instance_eval`, `module_eval`, `class_exec`, `instance_exec`, and
L   # `module_exec` blocks are allowed by default.
L 
L   def foo
L     self.class.class_eval do
L       def bar
L       end
L     end
L   end
L 
L   def foo
L     self.class.module_exec do
L       def bar
L       end
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L 
L   def foo
L     class << self
L       def bar
L       end
L     end
L   end
S Example: AllowedMethods: [] (default)
L   # bad
L   def do_something
L     has_many :articles do
L       def find_or_create_by_name(name)
L       end
L     end
L   end
S Example: AllowedMethods: ['has_many']
L   # bad
L   def do_something
L     has_many :articles do
L       def find_or_create_by_name(name)
L       end
L     end
L   end
S Example: AllowedPatterns: [] (default)
L   # bad
L   def foo(obj)
L     obj.do_baz do
L       def bar
L       end
L     end
L   end
S Example: AllowedPatterns: ['baz']
L   # good
L   def foo(obj)
L     obj.do_baz do
L       def bar
L       end
L     end
L   end
C Lint/NestedPercentLiteral
S Details
L Checks for nested percent literals.
S Example
L   # bad
L 
L   # The percent literal for nested_attributes is parsed as four tokens,
L   # yielding the array [:name, :content, :"%i[incorrectly", :"nested]"].
L   attributes = {
L     valid_attributes: %i[name content],
L     nested_attributes: %i[name content %i[incorrectly nested]]
L   }
L 
L   # good
L 
L   # Neither is incompatible with the bad case, but probably the intended code.
L   attributes = {
L     valid_attributes: %i[name content],
L     nested_attributes: [:name, :content, %i[incorrectly nested]]
L   }
L 
L   attributes = {
L     valid_attributes: %i[name content],
L     nested_attributes: [:name, :content, [:incorrectly, :nested]]
L   }
C Lint/NextWithoutAccumulator
S Details
L Don't omit the accumulator when calling `next` in a `reduce` block.
S Example
L   # bad
L   result = (1..4).reduce(0) do |acc, i|
L     next if i.odd?
L     acc + i
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   result = (1..4).reduce(0) do |acc, i|
L     next acc if i.odd?
L     acc + i
L   end
C Lint/NoReturnInBeginEndBlocks
S Details
L Checks for the presence of a `return` inside a `begin..end` block
L in assignment contexts.
L In this situation, the `return` will result in an exit from the current
L method, possibly leading to unexpected behavior.
S Example
L   # bad
L   @some_variable ||= begin
L     return some_value if some_condition_is_met
L 
L     do_something
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   @some_variable ||= begin
L     if some_condition_is_met
L       some_value
L     else
L       do_something
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   some_variable = if some_condition_is_met
L                     return if another_condition_is_met
L 
L                     some_value
L                   else
L                     do_something
L                   end
C Lint/NonAtomicFileOperation
S Details
L Checks for non-atomic file operation.
L And then replace it with a nearly equivalent and atomic method.
L 
L These can cause problems that are difficult to reproduce,
L especially in cases of frequent file operations in parallel,
L such as test runs with parallel_rspec.
L 
L For examples: creating a directory if there is none, has the following problems
L 
L An exception occurs when the directory didn't exist at the time of `exist?`,
L but someone else created it before `mkdir` was executed.
L 
L Subsequent processes are executed without the directory that should be there
L when the directory existed at the time of `exist?`,
L but someone else deleted it shortly afterwards.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe, because autocorrection change to atomic processing.
L   The atomic processing of the replacement destination is not guaranteed
L   to be strictly equivalent to that before the replacement.
S Example
L   # bad - race condition with another process may result in an error in `mkdir`
L   unless Dir.exist?(path)
L     FileUtils.mkdir(path)
L   end
L 
L   # good - atomic and idempotent creation
L   FileUtils.mkdir_p(path)
L 
L   # bad - race condition with another process may result in an error in `remove`
L   if File.exist?(path)
L     FileUtils.remove(path)
L   end
L 
L   # good - atomic and idempotent removal
L   FileUtils.rm_f(path)
C Lint/NonDeterministicRequireOrder
S Details
L `Dir[...]` and `Dir.glob(...)` do not make any guarantees about
L the order in which files are returned. The final order is
L determined by the operating system and file system.
L This means that using them in cases where the order matters,
L such as requiring files, can lead to intermittent failures
L that are hard to debug. To ensure this doesn't happen,
L always sort the list.
L 
L `Dir.glob` and `Dir[]` sort globbed results by default in Ruby 3.0.
L So all bad cases are acceptable when Ruby 3.0 or higher is used.
L 
L NOTE: This cop will be deprecated and removed when supporting only Ruby 3.0 and higher.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe in the case where sorting files changes existing
L   expected behavior.
S Example
L   # bad
L   Dir["./lib/**/*.rb"].each do |file|
L     require file
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   Dir["./lib/**/*.rb"].sort.each do |file|
L     require file
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   Dir.glob(Rails.root.join(__dir__, 'test', '*.rb')) do |file|
L     require file
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   Dir.glob(Rails.root.join(__dir__, 'test', '*.rb')).sort.each do |file|
L     require file
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   Dir['./lib/**/*.rb'].each(&method(:require))
L 
L   # good
L   Dir['./lib/**/*.rb'].sort.each(&method(:require))
L 
L   # bad
L   Dir.glob(Rails.root.join('test', '*.rb'), &method(:require))
L 
L   # good
L   Dir.glob(Rails.root.join('test', '*.rb')).sort.each(&method(:require))
L 
L   # good - Respect intent if `sort` keyword option is specified in Ruby 3.0 or higher.
L   Dir.glob(Rails.root.join(__dir__, 'test', '*.rb'), sort: false).each(&method(:require))
C Lint/NonLocalExitFromIterator
S Details
L Checks for non-local exits from iterators without a return
L value. It registers an offense under these conditions:
L 
L * No value is returned,
L * the block is preceded by a method chain,
L * the block has arguments,
L * the method which receives the block is not `define_method`
L or `define_singleton_method`,
L * the return is not contained in an inner scope, e.g. a lambda or a
L method definition.
S Example
L   class ItemApi
L     rescue_from ValidationError do |e| # non-iteration block with arg
L       return { message: 'validation error' } unless e.errors # allowed
L       error_array = e.errors.map do |error| # block with method chain
L         return if error.suppress? # warned
L         return "#{error.param}: invalid" unless error.message # allowed
L         "#{error.param}: #{error.message}"
L       end
L       { message: 'validation error', errors: error_array }
L     end
L 
L     def update_items
L       transaction do # block without arguments
L         return unless update_necessary? # allowed
L         find_each do |item| # block without method chain
L           return if item.stock == 0 # false-negative...
L           item.update!(foobar: true)
L         end
L       end
L     end
L   end
C Lint/NumberConversion
S Details
L Warns against the usage of unsafe number conversions. Unsafe
L number conversion can cause an unexpected error if auto type conversion
L fails. The cop prefers parsing with a number class instead.
L 
L Conversion with `Integer`, `Float`, etc. will raise an `ArgumentError`
L if given input that is not numeric (eg. an empty string), whereas
L `to_i`, etc. will try to convert regardless of input (``''.to_i => 0``).
L As such, this cop is disabled by default because it's not necessarily
L always correct to raise if a value is not numeric.
L 
L NOTE: Some values cannot be converted properly using one of the `Kernel`
L methods (for instance, `Time` and `DateTime` values are allowed by this
L cop by default). Similarly, Rails' duration methods do not work well
L with `Integer()` and can be allowed with `AllowedMethods`. By default,
L there are no allowed methods.
S Safety
L   Autocorrection is unsafe because it is not guaranteed that the
L   replacement `Kernel` methods are able to properly handle the
L   input if it is not a standard class.
S Example
L   # bad
L 
L   '10'.to_i
L   '10.2'.to_f
L   '10'.to_c
L   '1/3'.to_r
L   ['1', '2', '3'].map(&:to_i)
L   foo.try(:to_f)
L   bar.send(:to_c)
L 
L   # good
L 
L   Integer('10', 10)
L   Float('10.2')
L   Complex('10')
L   Rational('1/3')
L   ['1', '2', '3'].map { |i| Integer(i, 10) }
L   foo.try { |i| Float(i) }
L   bar.send { |i| Complex(i) }
S Example: AllowedMethods: [] (default)
L   # bad
L   10.minutes.to_i
S Example: AllowedMethods: [minutes]
L   # good
L   10.minutes.to_i
S Example: AllowedPatterns: [] (default)
L   # bad
L   10.minutes.to_i
S Example: AllowedPatterns: ['min*']
L   # good
L   10.minutes.to_i
S Example: AllowedClasses: [Time, DateTime] (default)
L   # good
L   Time.now.to_datetime.to_i
C Lint/NumberedParameterAssignment
S Details
L Checks for uses of numbered parameter assignment.
L It emulates the following warning in Ruby 2.7:
L 
L   $ ruby -ve '_1 = :value'
L   ruby 2.7.2p137 (2020-10-01 revision 5445e04352) [x86_64-darwin19]
L   -e:1: warning: `_1' is reserved for numbered parameter; consider another name
L 
L Assigning to a numbered parameter (from `_1` to `_9`) causes an error in Ruby 3.0.
L 
L   $ ruby -ve '_1 = :value'
L   ruby 3.0.0p0 (2020-12-25 revision 95aff21468) [x86_64-darwin19]
L   -e:1: _1 is reserved for numbered parameter
L 
L NOTE: The numbered parameters are from `_1` to `_9`. This cop checks `_0`, and over `_10`
L as well to prevent confusion.
S Example
L   # bad
L   _1 = :value
L 
L   # good
L   non_numbered_parameter_name = :value
C Lint/NumericOperationWithConstantResult
S Details
L Certain numeric operations have a constant result, usually 0 or 1.
L Multiplying a number by 0 will always return 0.
L Dividing a number by itself or raising it to the power of 0 will always return 1.
L As such, they can be replaced with that result.
L These are probably leftover from debugging, or are mistakes.
L Other numeric operations that are similarly leftover from debugging or mistakes
L are handled by `Lint/UselessNumericOperation`.
L 
L NOTE: This cop doesn't detect offenses for the `-` and `%` operator because it
L can't determine the type of `x`. If `x` is an `Array` or `String`, it doesn't perform
L a numeric operation.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because the autocorrection drops the operands, which
L   discards any side effects of evaluating them and can change behavior when
L   the result is not actually constant. For example, `x / x` raises
L   `ZeroDivisionError` when `x` is `0`, and returns `Float::NAN` (not `1`)
L   when `x` is `0.0`; replacing it with `1` silences that.
S Example
L   # bad
L   x * 0
L 
L   # good
L   0
L 
L   # bad
L   x *= 0
L 
L   # good
L   x = 0
L 
L   # bad
L   x / x
L   x ** 0
L 
L   # good
L   1
L 
L   # bad
L   x /= x
L   x **= 0
L 
L   # good
L   x = 1
C Lint/OrAssignmentToConstant
S Details
L Checks for unintended or-assignment to a constant.
L 
L Constants should always be assigned in the same location. And its value
L should always be the same. If constants are assigned in multiple
L locations, the result may vary depending on the order of `require`.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because code that is already conditionally
L   assigning a constant may have its behavior changed by autocorrection.
S Example
L   # bad
L   CONST ||= 1
L 
L   # good
L   CONST = 1
C Lint/OrderedMagicComments
S Details
L Checks the proper ordering of magic comments and whether
L a magic comment is not placed before a shebang.
S Safety
L   This cop's autocorrection is unsafe because file encoding may change.
S Example
L   # bad
L 
L   # frozen_string_literal: true
L   # encoding: ascii
L   p [''.frozen?, ''.encoding] #=> [true, #<Encoding:UTF-8>]
L 
L   # good
L 
L   # encoding: ascii
L   # frozen_string_literal: true
L   p [''.frozen?, ''.encoding] #=> [true, #<Encoding:US-ASCII>]
L 
L   # good
L 
L   #!/usr/bin/env ruby
L   # encoding: ascii
L   # frozen_string_literal: true
L   p [''.frozen?, ''.encoding] #=> [true, #<Encoding:US-ASCII>]
C Lint/OutOfRangeRegexpRef
S Details
L Looks for references of `Regexp` captures that are out of range
L and thus always returns nil.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because it is naive in how it determines what
L   references are available based on the last encountered regexp, but
L   it cannot handle some cases, such as conditional regexp matches, which
L   leads to false positives, such as:
L 
L   [source,ruby]
L   ----
L   foo ? /(c)(b)/ =~ str : /(b)/ =~ str
L   do_something if $2
L   # $2 is defined for the first condition but not the second, however
L   # the cop will mark this as an offense.
L   ----
L 
L   This might be a good indication of code that should be refactored,
L   however.
S Example
L   /(foo)bar/ =~ 'foobar'
L 
L   # bad - always returns nil
L 
L   puts $2 # => nil
L 
L   # good
L 
L   puts $1 # => foo
C Lint/ParenthesesAsGroupedExpression
S Details
L Checks for space between the name of a called method and a left
L parenthesis.
S Example
L   # bad
L   do_something (foo)
L 
L   # good
L   do_something(foo)
L   do_something (2 + 3) * 4
L   do_something (foo * bar).baz
C Lint/PercentStringArray
S Details
L Checks for quotes and commas in %w, e.g. `%w('foo', "bar")`
L 
L It is more likely that the additional characters are unintended (for
L example, mistranslating an array of literals to percent string notation)
L rather than meant to be part of the resulting strings.
S Safety
L   The cop is unsafe because the correction changes the values in the array
L   and that might have been done purposely.
L 
L   [source,ruby]
L   ----
L   %w('foo', "bar") #=> ["'foo',", '"bar"']
L   %w(foo bar)      #=> ['foo', 'bar']
L   ----
S Example
L   # bad
L   %w('foo', "bar")
L 
L   # good
L   %w(foo bar)
C Lint/PercentSymbolArray
S Details
L Checks for colons and commas in %i, e.g. `%i(:foo, :bar)`
L 
L It is more likely that the additional characters are unintended (for
L example, mistranslating an array of literals to percent string notation)
L rather than meant to be part of the resulting symbols.
S Example
L   # bad
L   %i(:foo, :bar)
L 
L   # good
L   %i(foo bar)
C Lint/RaiseException
S Details
L Checks for `raise` or `fail` statements which raise `Exception` or
L `Exception.new`. Use `StandardError` or a specific exception class instead.
L 
L If you have defined your own namespaced `Exception` class, it is possible
L to configure the cop to allow it by setting `AllowedImplicitNamespaces` to
L an array with the names of the namespaces to allow. By default, this is set to
L `['Gem']`, which allows `Gem::Exception` to be raised without an explicit namespace.
L If not allowed, a false positive may be registered if `raise Exception` is called
L within the namespace.
L 
L Alternatively, use a fully qualified name with `raise`/`fail`
L (eg. `raise Namespace::Exception`).
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because it will change the exception class being
L   raised, which is a change in behavior.
S Example
L   # bad
L   raise Exception, 'Error message here'
L   raise Exception.new('Error message here')
L 
L   # good
L   raise StandardError, 'Error message here'
L   raise MyError.new, 'Error message here'
S Example: AllowedImplicitNamespaces: ['Gem'] (default)
L   # bad - `Foo` is not an allowed implicit namespace
L   module Foo
L     def self.foo
L       raise Exception # This is qualified to `Foo::Exception`.
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   module Gem
L     def self.foo
L       raise Exception # This is qualified to `Gem::Exception`.
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   module Foo
L     def self.foo
L       raise Foo::Exception
L     end
L   end
C Lint/RandOne
S Details
L Checks for `rand(1)` calls.
L Such calls always return `0`, and so do `rand(-1)`, `rand(1.0)`, and `rand(-1.0)`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   rand 1
L   Kernel.rand(-1)
L   rand 1.0
L   rand(-1.0)
L 
L   # good
L   0 # just use 0 instead
C Lint/RedundantCopDisableDirective
S Details
L Detects instances of rubocop:disable comments that can be
L removed without causing any offenses to be reported. It's implemented
L as a cop in that it inherits from the Cop base class and calls
L add_offense. The unusual part of its implementation is that it doesn't
L have any on_* methods or an investigate method. This means that it
L doesn't take part in the investigation phase when the other cops do
L their work. Instead, it waits until it's called in a later stage of the
L execution. The reason it can't be implemented as a normal cop is that
L it depends on the results of all other cops to do its work.
S Example
L   # bad
L   # rubocop:disable Layout/LineLength
L   x += 1
L   # rubocop:enable Layout/LineLength
L 
L   # good
L   x += 1
C Lint/RedundantCopEnableDirective
S Details
L Detects instances of rubocop:enable comments that can be
L removed.
L 
L When a comment enables all cops at once `rubocop:enable all`
L the cop checks whether any cop was actually enabled.
S Example
L   # bad
L   foo = 1
L   # rubocop:enable Layout/LineLength
L 
L   # good
L   foo = 1
L 
L   # bad
L   # rubocop:disable Style/StringLiterals
L   foo = "1"
L   # rubocop:enable Style/StringLiterals
L   baz
L   # rubocop:enable all
L 
L   # good
L   # rubocop:disable Style/StringLiterals
L   foo = "1"
L   # rubocop:enable all
L   baz
L 
L   # bad
L   foo = 1
L   # rubocop:pop
L 
L   # good
L   # rubocop:push -Style/StringLiterals
L   foo = "1"
L   # rubocop:pop
C Lint/RedundantDirGlobSort
S Details
L Sort globbed results by default in Ruby 3.0.
L This cop checks for redundant `sort` method to `Dir.glob` and `Dir[]`.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe, in case of having a file and a directory with
L   identical names, since directory will be loaded before the file, which
L   will break `exe/files.rb` that rely on `exe.rb` file.
S Example
L   # bad
L   Dir.glob('./lib/**/*.rb').sort.each do |file|
L   end
L 
L   Dir['./lib/**/*.rb'].sort.each do |file|
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   Dir.glob('./lib/**/*.rb').each do |file|
L   end
L 
L   Dir['./lib/**/*.rb'].each do |file|
L   end
C Lint/RedundantRegexpQuantifiers
S Details
L Checks for redundant quantifiers inside `Regexp` literals.
L 
L It is always allowed when interpolation is used in a regexp literal,
L because it's unknown what kind of string will be expanded as a result:
L 
L [source,ruby]
L ----
L /(?:a*#{interpolation})?/x
L ----
S Example
L   # bad
L   /(?:x+)+/
L 
L   # good
L   /(?:x)+/
L 
L   # good
L   /(?:x+)/
L 
L   # bad
L   /(?:x+)?/
L 
L   # good
L   /(?:x)*/
L 
L   # good
L   /(?:x*)/
C Lint/RedundantRequireStatement
S Details
L Checks for unnecessary `require` statement.
L 
L The following features are unnecessary `require` statement because
L they are already loaded. e.g. Ruby 2.2:
L 
L   ruby -ve 'p $LOADED_FEATURES.reject { |feature| %r|/| =~ feature }'
L   ruby 2.2.8p477 (2017-09-14 revision 59906) [x86_64-darwin13]
L   ["enumerator.so", "rational.so", "complex.so", "thread.rb"]
L 
L Below are the features that each `TargetRubyVersion` targets.
L 
L   * 2.0+ ... `enumerator`
L   * 2.1+ ... `thread`
L   * 2.2+ ... Add `rational` and `complex` above
L   * 2.7+ ... Add `ruby2_keywords` above
L   * 3.1+ ... Add `fiber` above
L   * 3.2+ ... Add `set` above
L   * 4.0+ ... Add `pathname` above
L 
L This cop target those features.
S Example
L   # bad
L   require 'unloaded_feature'
L   require 'thread'
L 
L   # good
L   require 'unloaded_feature'
C Lint/RedundantSafeNavigation
S Details
L Checks for redundant safe navigation calls.
L Use cases where a constant, named in camel case for classes and modules is `nil` are rare,
L and an offense is not detected when the receiver is a constant. The detection also applies
L to `self`, and to literal receivers, except for `nil`.
L 
L For all receivers, the `instance_of?`, `kind_of?`, `is_a?`, `eql?`, `respond_to?`,
L and `equal?` methods are checked by default.
L These are customizable with `AllowedMethods` option.
L 
L The `AllowedMethods` option specifies nil-safe methods,
L in other words, it is a method that is allowed to skip safe navigation.
L Note that the `AllowedMethod` option is not an option that specifies methods
L for which to suppress (allow) this cop's check.
L 
L In the example below, the safe navigation operator (`&.`) is unnecessary
L because `NilClass` has methods like `respond_to?` and `is_a?`.
L 
L The `InferNonNilReceiver` option specifies whether to look into previous code
L paths to infer if the receiver can't be nil. This check is unsafe because the receiver
L can be redefined between the safe navigation call and previous regular method call.
L It does the inference only in the current scope, e.g. within the same method definition etc.
L 
L The `AdditionalNilMethods` option specifies additional custom methods which are
L defined on `NilClass`. When `InferNonNilReceiver` is set, they are used to determine
L whether the receiver can be nil.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe, because autocorrection can change the return type of
L   the expression. An offending expression that previously could return `nil`
L   will be autocorrected to never return `nil`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   CamelCaseConst&.do_something
L 
L   # good
L   CamelCaseConst.do_something
L 
L   # bad
L   foo.to_s&.strip
L   foo.to_i&.zero?
L   foo.to_f&.zero?
L   foo.to_a&.size
L   foo.to_h&.size
L 
L   # good
L   foo.to_s.strip
L   foo.to_i.zero?
L   foo.to_f.zero?
L   foo.to_a.size
L   foo.to_h.size
L 
L   # bad
L   do_something if attrs&.respond_to?(:[])
L 
L   # good
L   do_something if attrs.respond_to?(:[])
L 
L   # bad
L   foo&.bar ? foo&.bar.baz : qux
L 
L   # good
L   foo&.bar ? foo.bar.baz : qux
L 
L   # bad
L   if foo&.bar
L     foo&.bar.baz
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   if foo&.bar
L     foo.bar.baz
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   while node&.is_a?(BeginNode)
L     node = node.parent
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   while node.is_a?(BeginNode)
L     node = node.parent
L   end
L 
L   # good - without `&.` this changes the return value for `nil`
L   foo&.respond_to?(:to_a)
L   foo&.respond_to?(:class)
L 
L   # bad - for `nil`s conversion methods return default values for the type
L   foo&.to_h || {}
L   foo&.to_h { |k, v| [k, v] } || {}
L   foo&.to_a || []
L   foo&.to_i || 0
L   foo&.to_f || 0.0
L   foo&.to_s || ''
L 
L   # good
L   foo.to_h
L   foo.to_h { |k, v| [k, v] }
L   foo.to_a
L   foo.to_i
L   foo.to_f
L   foo.to_s
L 
L   # bad
L   self&.foo
L 
L   # good
L   self.foo
S Example: AllowedMethods: [nil_safe_method]
L   # bad
L   do_something if attrs&.nil_safe_method(:[])
L 
L   # good
L   do_something if attrs.nil_safe_method(:[])
L   do_something if attrs&.not_nil_safe_method(:[])
S Example: InferNonNilReceiver: false (default)
L   # good
L   foo.bar
L   foo&.baz
S Example: InferNonNilReceiver: true
L   # bad
L   foo.bar
L   foo&.baz # would raise on previous line if `foo` is nil
L 
L   # good
L   foo.bar
L   foo.baz
L 
L   # bad
L   if foo.condition?
L     foo&.bar
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   if foo.condition?
L     foo.bar
L   end
L 
L   # good (different scopes)
L   def method1
L     foo.bar
L   end
L 
L   def method2
L     foo&.bar
L   end
S Example: AdditionalNilMethods: [present?]
L   # good
L   foo.present?
L   foo&.bar
C Lint/RedundantSplatExpansion
S Details
L Checks for unneeded usages of splat expansion.
S Example
L   # bad
L   a = *[1, 2, 3]
L   a = *'a'
L   a = *1
L   ['a', 'b', *%w(c d e), 'f', 'g']
L 
L   # good
L   c = [1, 2, 3]
L   a = *c
L   a, b = *c
L   a, *b = *c
L   a = *1..10
L   a = ['a']
L   ['a', 'b', 'c', 'd', 'e', 'f', 'g']
L 
L   # bad
L   do_something(*['foo', 'bar', 'baz'])
L 
L   # good
L   do_something('foo', 'bar', 'baz')
L 
L   # bad
L   begin
L     foo
L   rescue *[StandardError, ApplicationError]
L     bar
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   begin
L     foo
L   rescue StandardError, ApplicationError
L     bar
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   case foo
L   when *[1, 2, 3]
L     bar
L   else
L     baz
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   case foo
L   when 1, 2, 3
L     bar
L   else
L     baz
L   end
S Example: AllowPercentLiteralArrayArgument: true (default)
L   # good
L   do_something(*%w[foo bar baz])
S Example: AllowPercentLiteralArrayArgument: false
L   # bad
L   do_something(*%w[foo bar baz])
C Lint/RedundantStringCoercion
S Details
L Checks for string conversion in string interpolation, `print`, `puts`, and `warn` arguments,
L which is redundant.
S Example
L   # bad
L   "result is #{something.to_s}"
L   print something.to_s
L   puts something.to_s
L   warn something.to_s
L 
L   # good
L   "result is #{something}"
L   print something
L   puts something
L   warn something
C Lint/RedundantTypeConversion
S Details
L Checks for redundant uses of `to_s`, `to_sym`, `to_i`, `to_f`, `to_d`, `to_r`, `to_c`,
L `to_a`, `to_h`, and `to_set`.
L 
L When one of these methods is called on an object of the same type, that object
L is returned, making the call unnecessary. The cop detects conversion methods called
L on object literals, class constructors, class `[]` methods, and the `Kernel` methods
L `String()`, `Integer()`, `Float()`, BigDecimal(), `Rational()`, `Complex()`, and `Array()`.
L 
L Specifically, these cases are detected for each conversion method:
L 
L * `to_s` when called on a string literal, interpolated string, heredoc,
L   or with `String.new` or `String()`.
L * `to_sym` when called on a symbol literal or interpolated symbol.
L * `to_i` when called on an integer literal or with `Integer()`.
L * `to_f` when called on a float literal or with `Float()`.
L * `to_d` when called with `BigDecimal()`.
L * `to_r` when called on a rational literal or with `Rational()`.
L * `to_c` when called on a complex literal or with `Complex()`.
L * `to_a` when called on an array literal, or with `Array.new`, `Array()` or `Array[]`.
L * `to_h` when called on a hash literal, or with `Hash.new`, `Hash()` or `Hash[]`.
L * `to_set` when called on `Set.new` or `Set[]`.
L 
L In all cases, chaining one of the same `to_*` conversion methods listed above is redundant.
L 
L The cop can also register an offense for chaining conversion methods on methods that are
L expected to return a specific type regardless of receiver (eg. `foo.inspect.to_s` and
L `foo.to_json.to_s`).
S Example
L   # bad
L   "text".to_s
L   :sym.to_sym
L   42.to_i
L   8.5.to_f
L   12r.to_r
L   1i.to_c
L   [].to_a
L   {}.to_h
L   Set.new.to_set
L 
L   # good
L   "text"
L   :sym
L   42
L   8.5
L   12r
L   1i
L   []
L   {}
L   Set.new
L 
L   # bad
L   Integer(var).to_i
L 
L   # good
L   Integer(var)
L 
L   # good - chaining to a type constructor with exceptions suppressed
L   # in this case, `Integer()` could return `nil`
L   Integer(var, exception: false).to_i
L 
L   # bad
L   BigDecimal(var).to_d
L 
L   # good
L   BigDecimal(var)
L 
L   # bad - chaining the same conversion
L   foo.to_s.to_s
L 
L   # good
L   foo.to_s
L 
L   # bad - chaining a conversion to a method that is expected to return the same type
L   foo.inspect.to_s
L   foo.to_json.to_s
L 
L   # good
L   foo.inspect
L   foo.to_json
C Lint/RedundantWithIndex
S Details
L Checks for redundant `with_index`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   ary.each_with_index do |v|
L     v
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   ary.each do |v|
L     v
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   ary.each.with_index do |v|
L     v
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   ary.each do |v|
L     v
L   end
C Lint/RedundantWithObject
S Details
L Checks for redundant `with_object`.
S Safety
L   This cop's autocorrection is unsafe because the return value changes:
L   `each_with_object` returns the memo object, while the corrected `each` returns
L   the receiver. This matters when the result of the expression is used.
S Example
L   # bad
L   ary.each_with_object([]) do |v|
L     v
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   ary.each do |v|
L     v
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   ary.each.with_object([]) do |v|
L     v
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   ary.each do |v|
L     v
L   end
C Lint/RefinementImportMethods
S Details
L Checks if `include` or `prepend` is called in `refine` block.
L These methods are deprecated and should be replaced with `Refinement#import_methods`.
L 
L It emulates deprecation warnings in Ruby 3.1. Functionality has been removed in Ruby 3.2.
S Safety
L   This cop's autocorrection is unsafe because `include M` will affect the included class
L   if any changes are made to module `M`.
L   On the other hand, `import_methods M` uses a snapshot of method definitions,
L   thus it will not be affected if module `M` changes.
S Example
L   # bad
L   refine Foo do
L     include Bar
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   refine Foo do
L     prepend Bar
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   refine Foo do
L     import_methods Bar
L   end
C Lint/RegexpAsCondition
S Details
L Checks for regexp literals used as `match-current-line`.
L If a regexp literal is in condition, the regexp matches `$_` implicitly.
S Example
L   # bad
L   if /foo/
L     do_something
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   if /foo/ =~ $_
L     do_something
L   end
C Lint/RequireParentheses
S Details
L Checks for method calls with at least one argument where no parentheses
L are used around the parameter list, and the call could be misread as an
L operand of a boolean operator (`&&` or `||`). Two forms are flagged:
L 
L * a predicate method whose last argument is a `&&`/`||` expression, and
L * any method whose first argument is a ternary expression with a
L   `&&`/`||` condition.
L 
L The idea behind warning for these constructs is that the user might
L be under the impression that the return value from the method call is
L an operand of &&/||.
S Example
L   # bad
L   if day.is? :tuesday && month == :jan
L     # ...
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   if day.is?(:tuesday) && month == :jan
L     # ...
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   foo a && b ? c : d
L 
L   # good
L   foo(a && b ? c : d)
C Lint/RequireRangeParentheses
S Details
L Checks that a range literal is enclosed in parentheses when the end of the range is
L at a line break.
L 
L NOTE: The following is maybe intended for `(42..)`. But, compatible is `42..do_something`.
L So, this cop does not provide autocorrection because it is left to user.
L 
L [source,ruby]
L ----
L case condition
L when 42..
L   do_something
L end
L ----
S Example
L   # bad - Represents `(1..42)`, not endless range.
L   1..
L   42
L 
L   # good - It's incompatible, but your intentions when using endless range may be:
L   (1..)
L   42
L 
L   # good
L   1..42
L 
L   # good
L   (1..42)
L 
L   # good
L   (1..
L   42)
C Lint/RequireRelativeSelfPath
S Details
L Checks for a file requiring itself with `require_relative`.
S Example
L   # bad
L 
L   # foo.rb
L   require_relative 'foo'
L   require_relative 'bar'
L 
L   # good
L 
L   # foo.rb
L   require_relative 'bar'
C Lint/RescueException
S Details
L Checks for `rescue` blocks targeting the `Exception` class.
S Example
L   # bad
L   begin
L     do_something
L   rescue Exception
L     handle_exception
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   begin
L     do_something
L   rescue ArgumentError
L     handle_exception
L   end
C Lint/RescueType
S Details
L Checks for arguments to `rescue` that will result in a `TypeError`
L if an exception is raised.
S Example
L   # bad
L   begin
L     bar
L   rescue nil
L     baz
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   def foo
L     bar
L   rescue 1, 'a', "#{b}", 0.0, [], {}
L     baz
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   begin
L     bar
L   rescue
L     baz
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo
L     bar
L   rescue NameError
L     baz
L   end
C Lint/ReturnInVoidContext
S Details
L Checks for the use of a return with a value in a context
L where the value will be ignored. (initialize and setter methods)
S Example
L   # bad
L   def initialize
L     foo
L     return :qux if bar?
L     baz
L   end
L 
L   def foo=(bar)
L     return 42
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def initialize
L     foo
L     return if bar?
L     baz
L   end
L 
L   def foo=(bar)
L     return
L   end
C Lint/SafeNavigationChain
S Details
L The safe navigation operator returns nil if the receiver is
L nil. If you chain an ordinary method call after a safe
L navigation operator, it raises NoMethodError. We should use a
L safe navigation operator after a safe navigation operator.
L This cop checks for the problem outlined above.
S Safety
L   This cop's autocorrection is unsafe because it changes the behavior of
L   the code: `x&.foo.bar` raises `NoMethodError` when `x` or `x&.foo` is `nil`,
L   whereas the corrected `x&.foo&.bar` returns `nil` instead. The autocorrection
L   also assumes that extending safe navigation through the chain is intended,
L   while removing the first `&.` may be what was intended instead.
S Example
L   # bad
L   x&.foo.bar
L   x&.foo + bar
L   x&.foo[bar]
L 
L   # good
L   x&.foo&.bar
L   x&.foo || bar
C Lint/SafeNavigationConsistency
S Details
L Checks that if safe navigation is used in an `&&` or `||` condition,
L consistent and appropriate safe navigation, without excess or deficiency,
L is used for all method calls on the same object.
S Safety
L   Autocorrection is unsafe because if the receiver is not a local variable
L   but a method call, it may not be idempotent. For example, replacing
L   `foo&.bar` with `foo.bar` could raise `NoMethodError` if `foo` returns
L   `nil` on a subsequent call.
S Example
L   # bad
L   foo&.bar && foo&.baz
L 
L   # good
L   foo&.bar && foo.baz
L 
L   # bad
L   foo.bar && foo&.baz
L 
L   # good
L   foo.bar && foo.baz
L 
L   # bad
L   foo&.bar || foo.baz
L 
L   # good
L   foo&.bar || foo&.baz
L 
L   # bad
L   foo.bar || foo&.baz
L 
L   # good
L   foo.bar || foo.baz
L 
L   # bad
L   foo&.bar && (foobar.baz || foo&.baz)
L 
L   # good
L   foo&.bar && (foobar.baz || foo.baz)
C Lint/SafeNavigationWithEmpty
S Details
L Checks to make sure safe navigation isn't used with `empty?` in
L a conditional.
L 
L While the safe navigation operator is generally a good idea, when
L checking `foo&.empty?` in a conditional, `foo` being `nil` will actually
L do the opposite of what the author intends.
S Example
L   # bad
L   return if foo&.empty?
L   return unless foo&.empty?
L 
L   # good
L   return if foo && foo.empty?
L   return unless foo && foo.empty?
C Lint/ScriptPermission
S Details
L Checks if a file which has a shebang line as
L its first line is granted execute permission.
S Example
L   # bad
L 
L   # A file which has a shebang line as its first line is not
L   # granted execute permission.
L 
L   #!/usr/bin/env ruby
L   puts 'hello, world'
L 
L   # good
L 
L   # A file which has a shebang line as its first line is
L   # granted execute permission.
L 
L   #!/usr/bin/env ruby
L   puts 'hello, world'
L 
L   # good
L 
L   # A file which has not a shebang line as its first line is not
L   # granted execute permission.
L 
L   puts 'hello, world'
C Lint/SelfAssignment
S Details
L Checks for self-assignments.
S Example
L   # bad
L   foo = foo
L   foo, bar = foo, bar
L   Foo = Foo
L   hash['foo'] = hash['foo']
L   obj.attr = obj.attr
L 
L   # good
L   foo = bar
L   foo, bar = bar, foo
L   Foo = Bar
L   hash['foo'] = hash['bar']
L   obj.attr = obj.attr2
L 
L   # good (method calls possibly can return different results)
L   hash[foo] = hash[foo]
S Example: AllowRBSInlineAnnotation: false (default)
L   # bad
L   foo = foo #: Integer
L   foo, bar = foo, bar #: Integer
L   Foo = Foo #: Integer
L   hash['foo'] = hash['foo'] #: Integer
L   obj.attr = obj.attr #: Integer
S Example: AllowRBSInlineAnnotation: true
L   # good
L   foo = foo #: Integer
L   foo, bar = foo, bar #: Integer
L   Foo = Foo #: Integer
L   hash['foo'] = hash['foo'] #: Integer
L   obj.attr = obj.attr #: Integer
C Lint/SendWithMixinArgument
S Details
L Checks for `send`, `public_send`, and `__send__` methods
L when using mix-in.
L 
L `include` and `prepend` methods were private methods until Ruby 2.0,
L they were mixed-in via `send` method. This cop uses Ruby 2.1 or
L higher style that can be called by public methods.
L And `extend` method that was originally a public method is also targeted
L for style unification.
S Example
L   # bad
L   Foo.send(:include, Bar)
L   Foo.send(:prepend, Bar)
L   Foo.send(:extend, Bar)
L 
L   # bad
L   Foo.public_send(:include, Bar)
L   Foo.public_send(:prepend, Bar)
L   Foo.public_send(:extend, Bar)
L 
L   # bad
L   Foo.__send__(:include, Bar)
L   Foo.__send__(:prepend, Bar)
L   Foo.__send__(:extend, Bar)
L 
L   # good
L   Foo.include Bar
L   Foo.prepend Bar
L   Foo.extend Bar
C Lint/ShadowedArgument
S Details
L Checks for shadowed arguments.
L 
L This cop has `IgnoreImplicitReferences` configuration option.
L It means argument shadowing is used in order to pass parameters
L to zero arity `super` when `IgnoreImplicitReferences` is `true`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   do_something do |foo|
L     foo = 42
L     puts foo
L   end
L 
L   def do_something(foo)
L     foo = 42
L     puts foo
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   do_something do |foo|
L     foo = foo + 42
L     puts foo
L   end
L 
L   def do_something(foo)
L     foo = foo + 42
L     puts foo
L   end
L 
L   def do_something(foo)
L     puts foo
L   end
S Example: IgnoreImplicitReferences: false (default)
L   # bad
L   def do_something(foo)
L     foo = 42
L     super
L   end
L 
L   def do_something(foo)
L     foo = super
L     bar
L   end
S Example: IgnoreImplicitReferences: true
L   # good
L   def do_something(foo)
L     foo = 42
L     super
L   end
L 
L   def do_something(foo)
L     foo = super
L     bar
L   end
C Lint/ShadowedException
S Details
L Checks for a rescued exception that gets shadowed by a
L less specific exception being rescued before a more specific
L exception is rescued.
L 
L An exception is considered shadowed if it is rescued after its
L ancestor is, or if it and its ancestor are both rescued in the
L same `rescue` statement. In both cases, the more specific rescue is
L unnecessary because it is covered by rescuing the less specific
L exception. (ie. `rescue Exception, StandardError` has the same behavior
L whether `StandardError` is included or not, because all ``StandardError``s
L are rescued by `rescue Exception`).
S Example
L   # bad
L 
L   begin
L     something
L   rescue Exception
L     handle_exception
L   rescue StandardError
L     handle_standard_error
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   begin
L     something
L   rescue Exception, StandardError
L     handle_error
L   end
L 
L   # good
L 
L   begin
L     something
L   rescue StandardError
L     handle_standard_error
L   rescue Exception
L     handle_exception
L   end
L 
L   # good, however depending on runtime environment.
L   #
L   # This is a special case for system call errors.
L   # System dependent error code depends on runtime environment.
L   # For example, whether `Errno::EAGAIN` and `Errno::EWOULDBLOCK` are
L   # the same error code or different error code depends on environment.
L   # This good case is for `Errno::EAGAIN` and `Errno::EWOULDBLOCK` with
L   # the same error code.
L   begin
L     something
L   rescue Errno::EAGAIN, Errno::EWOULDBLOCK
L     handle_standard_error
L   end
C Lint/ShadowingOuterLocalVariable
S Details
L Checks for the use of local variable names from an outer scope
L in block arguments or block-local variables. This mirrors the warning
L given by `ruby -cw` prior to Ruby 2.6:
L "shadowing outer local variable - foo".
L 
L The cop is now disabled by default to match the upstream Ruby behavior.
L It's useful, however, if you'd like to avoid shadowing variables from outer
L scopes, which some people consider an anti-pattern that makes it harder
L to keep track of what's going on in a program.
L 
L NOTE: Shadowing of variables in block passed to `Ractor.new` is allowed
L because `Ractor` should not access outer variables.
L eg. following style is encouraged:
L 
L [source,ruby]
L ----
L worker_id, pipe = env
L Ractor.new(worker_id, pipe) do |worker_id, pipe|
L end
L ----
S Example
L   # bad
L   def some_method
L     foo = 1
L 
L     2.times do |foo| # shadowing outer `foo`
L       do_something(foo)
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def some_method
L     foo = 1
L 
L     2.times do |bar|
L       do_something(bar)
L     end
L   end
C Lint/SharedMutableDefault
S Details
L Checks for `Hash` creation with a mutable default value.
L Creating a `Hash` in such a way will share the default value
L across all keys, causing unexpected behavior when modifying it.
L 
L For example, when the `Hash` was created with an `Array` as the argument,
L calling `hash[:foo] << 'bar'` will also change the value of all
L other keys that have not been explicitly assigned to.
S Example
L   # bad
L   Hash.new([])
L   Hash.new({})
L   Hash.new(Array.new)
L   Hash.new(Hash.new)
L 
L   # okay -- In rare cases that intentionally have this behavior,
L   #   without disabling the cop, you can set the default explicitly.
L   h = Hash.new
L   h.default = []
L   h[:a] << 1
L   h[:b] << 2
L   h # => {:a => [1, 2], :b => [1, 2]}
L 
L   # okay -- beware this will discard mutations and only remember assignments
L   Hash.new { Array.new }
L   Hash.new { Hash.new }
L   Hash.new { {} }
L   Hash.new { [] }
L 
L   # good - frozen solution will raise an error when mutation attempted
L   Hash.new([].freeze)
L   Hash.new({}.freeze)
L 
L   # good - using a proc will create a new object for each key
L   h = Hash.new
L   h.default_proc = ->(h, k) { [] }
L   h.default_proc = ->(h, k) { {} }
L
