L   # good - using a block will create a new object for each key
L   Hash.new { |h, k| h[k] = [] }
L   Hash.new { |h, k| h[k] = {} }
C Lint/StructNewOverride
S Details
L Checks unexpected overrides of the `Struct` built-in methods
L via `Struct.new`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   Bad = Struct.new(:members, :clone, :count)
L   b = Bad.new([], true, 1)
L   b.members #=> [] (overriding `Struct#members`)
L   b.clone #=> true (overriding `Object#clone`)
L   b.count #=> 1 (overriding `Enumerable#count`)
L 
L   # good
L   Good = Struct.new(:id, :name)
L   g = Good.new(1, "foo")
L   g.members #=> [:id, :name]
L   g.clone #=> #<struct Good id=1, name="foo">
L   g.count #=> 2
C Lint/SuperArgumentMismatch
S Details
L Checks for `super` calls with explicit arguments that pass the wrong
L number of positional arguments to the overridden implementation, using
L the project index.
L 
L The check is powered by the project-wide index, so it only runs when
L `AllCops/UseProjectIndex` is enabled and the `rubydex` gem is installed.
L Without the index the cop does nothing.
L 
L Only `super` inside a plain instance method defined directly in a class
L body is considered, and only when the enclosing class resolves in the
L index and its entire ancestry is resolved, so a superclass method coming
L from a gem, the standard library, or a dynamic definition never produces
L an offense. The overridden implementation is the first ancestor after
L the enclosing class in the index's method resolution order that defines
L the method; when it is not a plain method definition (an `attr_*` or an
L alias) or its definitions disagree on arity, the call is not checked.
L Calls that forward arguments (`*`, `**`, `...`) are skipped, and bare
L `super` (which forwards the current method's parameters) is not checked.
L Methods the index attributes to `Object`, `Kernel`, or `BasicObject`
L are never used as the overridden implementation, since a `def` inside
L a block at the top level (`Struct.new do ... end`) is indexed under
L `Object` even though it defines a method somewhere else entirely.
S Example
L   # Given the project defines:
L   #   class Base
L   #     def initialize(name, size); end
L   #   end
L 
L   # bad
L   class Widget < Base
L     def initialize(name)
L       super(name)
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class Widget < Base
L     def initialize(name)
L       super(name, 0)
L     end
L   end
C Lint/SuppressedException
S Details
L Checks for `rescue` blocks with no body.
S Example
L   # bad
L   def some_method
L     do_something
L   rescue
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   begin
L     do_something
L   rescue
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def some_method
L     do_something
L   rescue
L     handle_exception
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   begin
L     do_something
L   rescue
L     handle_exception
L   end
S Example: AllowComments: true (default)
L   # good
L   def some_method
L     do_something
L   rescue
L     # do nothing
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   begin
L     do_something
L   rescue
L     # do nothing
L   end
S Example: AllowComments: false
L   # bad
L   def some_method
L     do_something
L   rescue
L     # do nothing
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   begin
L     do_something
L   rescue
L     # do nothing
L   end
S Example: AllowNil: true (default)
L   # good
L   def some_method
L     do_something
L   rescue
L     nil
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   begin
L     do_something
L   rescue
L     # do nothing
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   do_something rescue nil
S Example: AllowNil: false
L   # bad
L   def some_method
L     do_something
L   rescue
L     nil
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   begin
L     do_something
L   rescue
L     nil
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   do_something rescue nil
C Lint/SuppressedExceptionInNumberConversion
S Details
L Checks for cases where exceptions unrelated to the numeric constructors `Integer()`,
L `Float()`, `BigDecimal()`, `Complex()`, and `Rational()` may be unintentionally swallowed.
S Safety
L   The cop is unsafe for autocorrection because unexpected errors occurring in the argument
L   passed to numeric constructor (e.g., `Integer()`) can lead to incompatible behavior.
L   For example, changing it to `Integer(potential_exception_method_call, exception: false)`
L   ensures that exceptions raised by `potential_exception_method_call` are not ignored.
L 
L   [source,ruby]
L   ----
L   Integer(potential_exception_method_call) rescue nil
L   ----
S Example
L   # bad
L   Integer(arg) rescue nil
L 
L   # bad
L   begin
L     Integer(arg)
L   rescue
L     nil
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   begin
L     Integer(arg)
L   rescue
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   Integer(arg, exception: false)
C Lint/SymbolConversion
S Details
L Checks for uses of literal strings converted to
L a symbol where a literal symbol could be used instead.
L 
L There are two possible styles for this cop.
L `strict` (default) will register an offense for any incorrect usage.
L `consistent` additionally requires hashes to use the same style for
L every symbol key (ie. if any symbol key needs to be quoted it requires
L all keys to be quoted).
S Example
L   # bad
L   'string'.to_sym
L   :symbol.to_sym
L   'underscored_string'.to_sym
L   :'underscored_symbol'
L   'hyphenated-string'.to_sym
L   "string_#{interpolation}".to_sym
L 
L   # good
L   :string
L   :symbol
L   :underscored_string
L   :underscored_symbol
L   :'hyphenated-string'
L   :"string_#{interpolation}"
S Example: EnforcedStyle: strict (default)
L   # bad
L   {
L     'a': 1,
L     "b": 2,
L     'c-d': 3
L   }
L 
L   # good (don't quote keys that don't require quoting)
L   {
L     a: 1,
L     b: 2,
L     'c-d': 3
L   }
S Example: EnforcedStyle: consistent
L   # bad
L   {
L     a: 1,
L     'b-c': 2
L   }
L 
L   # good (quote all keys if any need quoting)
L   {
L     'a': 1,
L     'b-c': 2
L   }
L 
L   # good (no quoting required)
L   {
L     a: 1,
L     b: 2
L   }
C Lint/Syntax
S Details
L Repacks Parser's diagnostics/errors
L into RuboCop's offenses.
C Lint/ToEnumArguments
S Details
L Ensures that `to_enum`/`enum_for`, called for the current method,
L has correct arguments.
S Example
L   # bad
L   def foo(x, y = 1)
L     return to_enum(__callee__, x) # `y` is missing
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo(x, y = 1)
L     # Alternatives to `__callee__` are `__method__` and `:foo`.
L     return to_enum(__callee__, x, y)
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo(x, y = 1)
L     # It is also allowed if it is wrapped in some method like Sorbet.
L     return to_enum(T.must(__callee__), x, y)
L   end
C Lint/ToJSON
S Details
L Checks to make sure `#to_json` includes an optional argument.
L When overriding `#to_json`, callers may invoke JSON
L generation via `JSON.generate(your_obj)`. Since `JSON#generate` allows
L for an optional argument, your method should too.
S Example
L   # bad - incorrect arity
L   def to_json
L     JSON.generate([x, y])
L   end
L 
L   # good - preserving args
L   def to_json(*args)
L     JSON.generate([x, y], *args)
L   end
L 
L   # good - discarding args
L   def to_json(*_args)
L     JSON.generate([x, y])
L   end
C Lint/TopLevelReturnWithArgument
S Details
L Checks for top level return with arguments. If there is a
L top-level return statement with an argument, then the argument is
L always ignored. This is detected automatically since Ruby 2.7.
S Example
L   # bad
L   return 1
L 
L   # good
L   return
C Lint/TrailingCommaInAttributeDeclaration
S Details
L Checks for trailing commas in attribute declarations, such as
L `#attr_reader`. Leaving a trailing comma will nullify the next method
L definition by overriding it with a getter method.
S Example
L   # bad
L   class Foo
L     attr_reader :foo,
L 
L     def bar
L       puts "Unreachable."
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class Foo
L     attr_reader :foo
L 
L     def bar
L       puts "No problem!"
L     end
L   end
C Lint/TripleQuotes
S Details
L Checks for "triple quotes" (strings delimited by any odd number
L of quotes greater than 1).
L 
L Ruby allows multiple strings to be implicitly concatenated by just
L being adjacent in a statement (ie. `"foo""bar" == "foobar"`). This sometimes
L gives the impression that there is something special about triple quotes, but
L in fact it is just extra unnecessary quotes and produces the same string. Each
L pair of quotes produces an additional concatenated empty string, so the result
L is still only the "actual" string within the delimiters.
L 
L NOTE: Although this cop is called triple quotes, the same behavior is present
L for strings delimited by 5, 7, etc. quotation marks.
S Example
L   # bad
L   """
L     A string
L   """
L 
L   # bad
L   '''
L     A string
L   '''
L 
L   # good
L   "
L     A string
L   "
L 
L   # good
L   <<STRING
L     A string
L   STRING
L 
L   # good (but not the same spacing as the bad case)
L   'A string'
C Lint/UnderscorePrefixedVariableName
S Details
L Checks for underscore-prefixed variables that are actually
L used.
L 
L Since block keyword arguments cannot be arbitrarily named at call
L sites, the `AllowKeywordBlockArguments` will allow use of underscore-
L prefixed block keyword arguments.
S Example: AllowKeywordBlockArguments: false (default)
L   # bad
L 
L   [1, 2, 3].each do |_num|
L     do_something(_num)
L   end
L 
L   query(:sales) do |_id:, revenue:, cost:|
L     {_id: _id, profit: revenue - cost}
L   end
L 
L   # good
L 
L   [1, 2, 3].each do |num|
L     do_something(num)
L   end
L 
L   [1, 2, 3].each do |_num|
L     do_something # not using `_num`
L   end
S Example: AllowKeywordBlockArguments: true
L   # good
L 
L   query(:sales) do |_id:, revenue:, cost:|
L     {_id: _id, profit: revenue - cost}
L   end
C Lint/UnescapedBracketInRegexp
S Details
L Checks for Regexps (both literals and via `Regexp.new` / `Regexp.compile`)
L that contain unescaped `]` characters.
L 
L It emulates the following Ruby warning:
L 
L [source,ruby]
L ----
L $ ruby -e '/abc]123/'
L -e:1: warning: regular expression has ']' without escape: /abc]123/
L ----
S Example
L   # bad
L   /abc]123/
L   %r{abc]123}
L   Regexp.new('abc]123')
L   Regexp.compile('abc]123')
L 
L   # good
L   /abc\]123/
L   %r{abc\]123}
L   Regexp.new('abc\]123')
L   Regexp.compile('abc\]123')
C Lint/UnexpectedBlockArity
S Details
L Checks for a block that is known to need more positional
L block arguments than are given (by default this is configured for
L `Enumerable` methods needing 2 arguments). Optional arguments are allowed,
L although they don't generally make sense as the default value will
L be used. Blocks that have no receiver, or take splatted arguments
L (ie. `*args`) are always accepted.
L 
L Keyword arguments (including `**kwargs`) do not get counted towards
L this, as they are not used by the methods in question.
L 
L Method names and their expected arity can be configured like this:
L 
L [source,yaml]
L ----
L Methods:
L   inject: 2
L   reduce: 2
L ----
S Safety
L  This cop matches for method names only and hence cannot tell apart
L  methods with same name in different classes, which may lead to a
L  false positive.
S Example
L   # bad
L   values.reduce {}
L   values.min { |a| a }
L   values.sort { |a; b| a + b }
L 
L   # good
L   values.reduce { |memo, obj| memo << obj }
L   values.min { |a, b| a <=> b }
L   values.sort { |*x| x[0] <=> x[1] }
C Lint/UnifiedInteger
S Details
L Checks for using Fixnum or Bignum constant.
S Example
L   # bad
L   1.is_a?(Fixnum)
L   1.is_a?(Bignum)
L 
L   # good
L   1.is_a?(Integer)
C Lint/UnmodifiedReduceAccumulator
S Details
L Looks for `reduce` or `inject` blocks where the value returned (implicitly or
L explicitly) does not include the accumulator. A block is considered valid as
L long as at least one return value includes the accumulator.
L 
L If the accumulator is not included in the return value, then the entire
L block will just return a transformation of the last element value, and
L could be rewritten as such without a loop.
L 
L Also catches instances where an index of the accumulator is returned, as
L this may change the type of object being retained.
L 
L NOTE: For the purpose of reducing false positives, this cop only flags
L returns in `reduce` blocks where the element is the only variable in
L the expression (since we will not be able to tell what other variables
L relate to via static analysis).
S Example
L   # bad
L   (1..4).reduce(0) do |acc, el|
L     el * 2
L   end
L 
L   # bad, may raise a NoMethodError after the first iteration
L   %w(a b c).reduce({}) do |acc, letter|
L     acc[letter] = true
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   (1..4).reduce(0) do |acc, el|
L     acc + el * 2
L   end
L 
L   # good, element is returned but modified using the accumulator
L   values.reduce do |acc, el|
L     el << acc
L     el
L   end
L 
L   # good, returns the accumulator instead of the index
L   %w(a b c).reduce({}) do |acc, letter|
L     acc[letter] = true
L     acc
L   end
L 
L   # good, at least one branch returns the accumulator
L   values.reduce(nil) do |result, value|
L     break result if something?
L     value
L   end
L 
L   # good, recursive
L   keys.reduce(self) { |result, key| result[key] }
L 
L   # ignored as the return value cannot be determined
L   enum.reduce do |acc, el|
L     x = foo(acc, el)
L     bar(x)
L   end
C Lint/UnreachableCode
S Details
L Checks for unreachable code.
L The check is based on the presence of flow-of-control
L statements in non-final position in `begin` (implicit) blocks.
S Example
L   # bad
L   def some_method
L     return
L     do_something
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   def some_method
L     if cond
L       return
L     else
L       return
L     end
L     do_something
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def some_method
L     do_something
L   end
C Lint/UnreachableLoop
S Details
L Checks for loops that will have at most one iteration.
L 
L A loop that can never reach the second iteration is a possible error in the code.
L In rare cases where only one iteration (or at most one iteration) is intended behavior,
L the code should be refactored to use `if` conditionals.
L 
L NOTE: Block methods that are used with ``Enumerable``s are considered to be loops.
L 
L `AllowedPatterns` can be used to match against the block receiver in order to allow
L code that would otherwise be registered as an offense (eg. `times` used not in an
L `Enumerable` context).
S Example
L   # bad
L   while node
L     do_something(node)
L     node = node.parent
L     break
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   while node
L     do_something(node)
L     node = node.parent
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   def verify_list(head)
L     item = head
L     begin
L       if verify(item)
L         return true
L       else
L         return false
L       end
L     end while(item)
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def verify_list(head)
L     item = head
L     begin
L       if verify(item)
L         item = item.next
L       else
L         return false
L       end
L     end while(item)
L 
L     true
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   def find_something(items)
L     items.each do |item|
L       if something?(item)
L         return item
L       else
L         raise NotFoundError
L       end
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def find_something(items)
L     items.each do |item|
L       if something?(item)
L         return item
L       end
L     end
L     raise NotFoundError
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   2.times { raise ArgumentError }
S Example: AllowedPatterns: ['(exactly|at_least|at_most)\(\d+\)\.times'] (default)
L   # good
L   exactly(2).times { raise StandardError }
C Lint/UnreachablePatternBranch
S Details
L Checks for unreachable `in` pattern branches in `case...in` statements.
L 
L An `in` branch is unreachable when a previous branch uses an unguarded
L catch-all pattern that matches any value unconditionally. Any `in` branches
L (and `else`) that follow such a catch-all are dead code.
L 
L A catch-all pattern is one of:
L 
L * A bare variable capture (`in x`)
L * An underscore (`in _`)
L * A pattern alias where the left side is a catch-all (`in _ => y`)
L * An alternation pattern where at least one alternative is a catch-all
L   (`in _ | Integer`)
L 
L NOTE: A catch-all pattern with a guard clause (e.g., `in _ if condition`)
L does NOT make subsequent branches unreachable because the guard might
L not be satisfied.
S Example
L   # bad
L   case value
L   in Integer
L     handle_integer
L   in x
L     handle_other
L   in String
L     handle_string
L   else
L     handle_else
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   case value
L   in Integer
L     handle_integer
L   in String
L     handle_string
L   in x
L     handle_other
L   end
L 
L   # bad - else is unreachable after catch-all
L   case value
L   in Integer
L     handle_integer
L   in _
L     handle_other
L   else
L     handle_else
L   end
L 
L   # good - guard clause means catch-all might not match
L   case value
L   in x if x.positive?
L     handle_positive
L   in Integer
L     handle_integer
L   else
L     handle_other
L   end
C Lint/UnusedBlockArgument
S Details
L Checks for unused block arguments.
S Example
L   # bad
L   do_something do |used, unused|
L     puts used
L   end
L 
L   do_something do |bar|
L     puts :foo
L   end
L 
L   define_method(:foo) do |bar|
L     puts :baz
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   do_something do |used, _unused|
L     puts used
L   end
L 
L   do_something do
L     puts :foo
L   end
L 
L   define_method(:foo) do |_bar|
L     puts :baz
L   end
S Example: IgnoreEmptyBlocks: true (default)
L   # good
L   do_something { |unused| }
S Example: IgnoreEmptyBlocks: false
L   # bad
L   do_something { |unused| }
S Example: AllowUnusedKeywordArguments: false (default)
L   # bad
L   do_something do |unused: 42|
L     foo
L   end
S Example: AllowUnusedKeywordArguments: true
L   # good
L   do_something do |unused: 42|
L     foo
L   end
C Lint/UnusedMethodArgument
S Details
L Checks for unused method arguments.
S Example
L   # bad
L   def some_method(used, unused, _unused_but_allowed)
L     puts used
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def some_method(used, _unused, _unused_but_allowed)
L     puts used
L   end
S Example: AllowUnusedKeywordArguments: false (default)
L   # bad
L   def do_something(used, unused: 42)
L     used
L   end
S Example: AllowUnusedKeywordArguments: true
L   # good
L   def do_something(used, unused: 42)
L     used
L   end
S Example: IgnoreEmptyMethods: true (default)
L   # good
L   def do_something(unused)
L   end
S Example: IgnoreEmptyMethods: false
L   # bad
L   def do_something(unused)
L   end
S Example: IgnoreNotImplementedMethods: true (default)
L   # with default value of `NotImplementedExceptions: ['NotImplementedError']`
L 
L   # good
L   def do_something(unused)
L     raise NotImplementedError
L   end
L 
L   def do_something_else(unused)
L     fail "TODO"
L   end
S Example: IgnoreNotImplementedMethods: true
L   # with `NotImplementedExceptions: ['AbstractMethodError']`
L 
L   # good
L   def do_something(unused)
L     raise AbstractMethodError
L   end
S Example: IgnoreNotImplementedMethods: false
L   # bad
L   def do_something(unused)
L     raise NotImplementedError
L   end
L 
L   def do_something_else(unused)
L     fail "TODO"
L   end
C Lint/UnusedPrivateMethod
S Details
L Checks for private instance methods that are not referenced anywhere
L in the project.
L 
L The check is powered by the project-wide index, so it only runs when
L `AllCops/UseProjectIndex` is enabled and the `rubydex` gem is installed.
L Without the index the cop does nothing.
L 
L A method counts as referenced when a call with its name appears anywhere
L in the indexed project (regardless of the receiver), when it is the source
L of an `alias`, or when its name appears in the same file as a symbol or
L inside a string literal (covering `send(:name)` and declarative DSLs like
L `before_action :name`). An interpolated symbol or string with a literal
L prefix (e.g. `send(:"format_#{type}")`) counts as a reference to every
L method whose name starts with that prefix; fully dynamic names cannot
L be detected. Methods defined in classes or modules with descendants are
L not checked, since they may be invoked through `super` or inherited
L dispatch.
L 
L Methods whose names are composed by a framework (e.g. Rails
L `attribute_method_suffix` generating `attribute?` methods) can be
L excluded from the check with `AllowedNames` (exact names) or
L `AllowedPatterns` (regular expressions).
L 
L The cop is disabled by default because symbol-based references from
L *other* files (e.g. a Rails callback declared in a concern) cannot be
L detected and would be reported as false positives. It is best suited
L for occasional dead-code sweeps rather than permanent enforcement.
S Example
L   # bad - `helper` is never referenced anywhere in the project
L   class Service
L     def call
L       do_something
L     end
L 
L     private
L 
L     def helper
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class Service
L     def call
L       do_something(helper)
L     end
L 
L     private
L 
L     def helper
L     end
L   end
S Example: AllowedNames: ['attribute?']
L   # good - the name is explicitly allowed
L   class Contact
L     private
L 
L     def attribute?
L     end
L   end
S Example: AllowedPatterns: ['_hook\z']
L   # good - the name matches an allowed pattern
L   class Service
L     private
L 
L     def before_save_hook
L     end
L   end
C Lint/UriEscapeUnescape
S Details
L Identifies places where `URI.escape` can be replaced by
L `CGI.escape`, `URI.encode_www_form`, or `URI.encode_www_form_component`
L depending on your specific use case.
L Also this cop identifies places where `URI.unescape` can be replaced by
L `CGI.unescape`, `URI.decode_www_form`,
L or `URI.decode_www_form_component` depending on your specific use case.
S Example
L   # bad
L   URI.escape('http://example.com')
L   URI.encode('http://example.com')
L 
L   # good
L   CGI.escape('http://example.com')
L   URI.encode_uri_component(uri) # Since Ruby 3.1
L   URI.encode_www_form([['example', 'param'], ['lang', 'en']])
L   URI.encode_www_form(page: 10, locale: 'en')
L   URI.encode_www_form_component('http://example.com')
L 
L   # bad
L   URI.unescape(enc_uri)
L   URI.decode(enc_uri)
L 
L   # good
L   CGI.unescape(enc_uri)
L   URI.decode_uri_component(uri) # Since Ruby 3.1
L   URI.decode_www_form(enc_uri)
L   URI.decode_www_form_component(enc_uri)
C Lint/UriRegexp
S Details
L Identifies places where `URI.regexp` is obsolete and should not be used.
L 
L For Ruby 3.3 or lower, use `URI::DEFAULT_PARSER.make_regexp`.
L For Ruby 3.4 or higher, use `URI::RFC2396_PARSER.make_regexp`.
L 
L NOTE: If you need to support both Ruby 3.3 and lower as well as Ruby 3.4 and higher,
L consider manually changing the code as follows:
L 
L [source,ruby]
L ----
L defined?(URI::RFC2396_PARSER) ? URI::RFC2396_PARSER : URI::DEFAULT_PARSER
L ----
S Example
L   # bad
L   URI.regexp('http://example.com')
L 
L   # good - Ruby 3.3 or lower
L   URI::DEFAULT_PARSER.make_regexp('http://example.com')
L 
L   # good - Ruby 3.4 or higher
L   URI::RFC2396_PARSER.make_regexp('http://example.com')
C Lint/UselessAccessModifier
S Details
L Checks for redundant access modifiers, including those with no
L code, those which are repeated, those which are on top-level, and
L leading `public` modifiers in a class or module body.
L Conditionally-defined methods are considered as always being defined,
L and thus access modifiers guarding such methods are not redundant.
L 
L This cop has `ContextCreatingMethods` option. The default setting value
L is an empty array that means no method is specified.
L This setting is an array of methods which, when called, are known to
L create its own context in the module's current access context.
L 
L It also has `MethodCreatingMethods` option. The default setting value
L is an empty array that means no method is specified.
L This setting is an array of methods which, when called, are known to
L create other methods in the module's current access context.
S Example
L   # bad
L   class Foo
L     public # this is redundant (default access is public)
L 
L     def method
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   class Foo
L     # The following is redundant (methods defined on the class'
L     # singleton class are not affected by the private modifier)
L     private
L 
L     def self.method3
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   class Foo
L     protected
L 
L     define_method(:method2) do
L     end
L 
L     protected # this is redundant (repeated from previous modifier)
L 
L     [1,2,3].each do |i|
L       define_method("foo#{i}") do
L       end
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   class Foo
L     private # this is redundant (no following methods are defined)
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   private # this is useless (access modifiers have no effect on top-level)
L 
L   def method
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class Foo
L     private # this is not redundant (a method is defined)
L 
L     def method2
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class Foo
L     # The following is not redundant (conditionally defined methods are
L     # considered as always defining a method)
L     private
L 
L     if condition?
L       def method
L       end
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class Foo
L     protected # this is not redundant (a method is defined)
L 
L     define_method(:method2) do
L     end
L   end
S Example: ContextCreatingMethods: concerning
L   # Lint/UselessAccessModifier:
L   #   ContextCreatingMethods:
L   #     - concerning
L 
L   # good
L   require 'active_support/concern'
L   class Foo
L     concerning :Bar do
L       def some_public_method
L       end
L 
L       private
L 
L       def some_private_method
L       end
L     end
L 
L     # this is not redundant because `concerning` created its own context
L     private
L 
L     def some_other_private_method
L     end
L   end
S Example: MethodCreatingMethods: delegate
L   # Lint/UselessAccessModifier:
L   #   MethodCreatingMethods:
L   #     - delegate
L 
L   # good
L   require 'active_support/core_ext/module/delegation'
L   class Foo
L     # this is not redundant because `delegate` creates methods
L     private
L 
L     delegate :method_a, to: :method_b
L   end
C Lint/UselessAssignment
S Details
L Checks for every useless assignment to local variable in every
L scope.
L The basic idea for this cop was from the warning of `ruby -cw`:
L 
L [source,console]
L ----
L assigned but unused variable - foo
L ----
L 
L Currently this cop has advanced logic that detects unreferenced
L reassignments and properly handles varied cases such as branch, loop,
L rescue, ensure, etc.
L 
L This cop's autocorrection avoids cases like `a ||= 1` because removing assignment from
L operator assignment can cause `NameError` if this assignment has been used to declare
L a local variable. For example, replacing `a ||= 1` with `a || 1` may cause
L "undefined local variable or method `a' for main:Object (NameError)".
L 
L NOTE: Given the assignment `foo = 1, bar = 2`, removing unused variables
L can lead to a syntax error, so this case is not autocorrected.
S Example
L   # bad
L   def some_method
L     some_var = 1
L     do_something
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def some_method
L     some_var = 1
L     do_something(some_var)
L   end
C Lint/UselessConstantScoping
S Details
L Checks for useless constant scoping. Private constants must be defined using
L `private_constant`. Even if `private` access modifier is used, it is public scope despite
L its appearance.
L 
L It does not support autocorrection due to behavior change and multiple ways to fix it.
L Or a public constant may be intended.
L 
L Constant assignments that define classes or modules via `Class.new`, `Module.new`,
L `Struct.new`, or `Data.define` are allowed. Those forms are class and module definitions
L written with assignment syntax, and match the common practice of placing nested
L `class` / `module` bodies after `private` without intending private constant visibility.
S Example
L   # bad
L   class Foo
L     private
L     PRIVATE_CONST = 42
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class Foo
L     PRIVATE_CONST = 42
L     private_constant :PRIVATE_CONST
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class Foo
L     PUBLIC_CONST = 42 # If private scope is not intended.
L   end
L 
L   # good - class/module definitions via assignment, same as nested `class`/`module`
L   class Foo
L     private
L 
L     def some_private_method
L     end
L 
L     MyClass = Class.new
L     MyModule = Module.new
L     MyStruct = Struct.new(:name)
L     MyData = Data.define(:name)
L   end
C Lint/UselessDefaultValueArgument
S Details
L Checks for usage of method `fetch` or `Array.new` with default value argument
L and block. In such cases, block will always be used as default value.
L 
L This cop emulates Ruby warning "block supersedes default value argument" which
L applies to `Array.new`, `Array#fetch`, `Hash#fetch`, `ENV.fetch` and
L `Thread#fetch`.
L 
L A `fetch` call without a receiver is considered a custom method and does not register
L an offense.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because the receiver could have nonstandard implementation
L   of `fetch`, or be a class other than the one listed above.
L 
L   It is also unsafe because default value argument could have side effects:
L 
L   [source,ruby]
L   ----
L   def x(a) = puts "side effect"
L   Array.new(5, x(1)) { 2 }
L   ----
L 
L   so removing it would change behavior.
S Example
L   # bad
L   x.fetch(key, default_value) { block_value }
L   Array.new(size, default_value) { block_value }
L 
L   # good
L   x.fetch(key) { block_value }
L   Array.new(size) { block_value }
L 
L   # also good - in case default value argument is desired instead
L   x.fetch(key, default_value)
L   Array.new(size, default_value)
L 
L   # good - keyword arguments aren't registered as offenses
L   x.fetch(key, keyword: :arg) { block_value }
S Example: AllowedReceivers: ['Rails.cache']
L   # good
L   Rails.cache.fetch(name, options) { block }
C Lint/UselessDefined
S Details
L Checks for calls to `defined?` with strings or symbols as the argument.
L Such calls will always return `'expression'`, you probably meant to
L check for the existence of a constant, method, or variable instead.
L 
L `defined?` is part of the Ruby syntax and doesn't behave like normal methods.
L You can safely pass in what you are checking for directly, without encountering
L a `NameError`.
L 
L When interpolation is used, oftentimes it is not possible to write the
L code with `defined?`. In these cases, switch to one of the more specific methods:
L 
L * `class_variable_defined?`
L * `const_defined?`
L * `method_defined?`
L * `instance_variable_defined?`
L * `binding.local_variable_defined?`
S Example
L   # bad
L   defined?('FooBar')
L   defined?(:FooBar)
L   defined?(:foo_bar)
L   defined?('foo_bar')
L 
L   # good
L   defined?(FooBar)
L   defined?(foo_bar)
L 
L   # bad - interpolation
L   bar = 'Bar'
L   defined?("Foo::#{bar}::Baz")
L 
L   # good
L   bar = 'Bar'
L   defined?(Foo) && Foo.const_defined?(bar) && Foo.const_get(bar).const_defined?(:Baz)
C Lint/UselessElseWithoutRescue
S Details
L Checks for useless `else` in `begin..end` without `rescue`.
L 
L NOTE: This syntax is no longer valid on Ruby 2.6 or higher.
S Example
L   # bad
L   begin
L     do_something
L   else
L     do_something_else # This will never be run.
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   begin
L     do_something
L   rescue
L     handle_errors
L   else
L     do_something_else
L   end
C Lint/UselessMethodDefinition
S Details
L Checks for useless method definitions, specifically: empty constructors
L and methods just delegating to `super`.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe as it can register false positives for cases when an empty
L   constructor just overrides the parent constructor, which is bad anyway.
S Example
L   # bad
L   def initialize
L     super
L   end
L 
L   def method
L     super
L   end
L 
L   # good - with default arguments
L   def initialize(x = Object.new)
L     super
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def initialize
L     super
L     initialize_internals
L   end
L 
L   def method(*args)
L     super(:extra_arg, *args)
L   end
C Lint/UselessNumericOperation
S Details
L Certain numeric operations have no impact, being:
L Adding or subtracting 0, multiplying or dividing by 1 or raising to the power of 1.
L These are probably leftover from debugging, or are mistakes.
S Example
L   # bad
L   x + 0
L   x - 0
L   x * 1
L   x / 1
L   x ** 1
L 
L   # good
L   x
L 
L   # bad
L   x += 0
L   x -= 0
L   x *= 1
L   x /= 1
L   x **= 1
L 
L   # good
L   x = x
C Lint/UselessOr
S Details
L Checks for useless OR (`||` and `or`) expressions.
L 
L Some methods always return a truthy value, even when called
L on `nil` (e.g. `nil.to_i` evaluates to `0`). Therefore, OR expressions
L appended after these methods will never evaluate.
S Safety
L   As shown in the examples below, there are generally two possible ways to correct the
L   offense, but this cop's autocorrection always chooses the option that preserves the
L   current behavior. While this does not change how the code behaves, that option is not
L   necessarily the appropriate fix in every situation. For this reason, the autocorrection
L   provided by this cop is considered unsafe.
S Example
L   # bad
L   x.to_a || fallback
L   x.to_c || fallback
L   x.to_d || fallback
L   x.to_i || fallback
L   x.to_f || fallback
L   x.to_h || fallback
L   x.to_r || fallback
L   x.to_s || fallback
L   x.to_sym || fallback
L   x.intern || fallback
L   x.inspect || fallback
L   x.hash || fallback
L   x.object_id || fallback
L   x.__id__ || fallback
L 
L   x.to_s or fallback
L 
L   # good - if fallback is same as return value of method called on nil
L   x.to_a # nil.to_a returns []
L   x.to_c # nil.to_c returns (0+0i)
L   x.to_d # nil.to_d returns 0.0
L   x.to_i # nil.to_i returns 0
L   x.to_f # nil.to_f returns 0.0
L   x.to_h # nil.to_h returns {}
L   x.to_r # nil.to_r returns (0/1)
L   x.to_s # nil.to_s returns ''
L   x.to_sym # nil.to_sym raises an error
L   x.intern # nil.intern raises an error
L   x.inspect # nil.inspect returns "nil"
L   x.hash # nil.hash returns an Integer
L   x.object_id # nil.object_id returns an Integer
L   x.__id__ # nil.object_id returns an Integer
L 
L   # good - if the intention is not to call the method on nil
L   x&.to_a || fallback
L   x&.to_c || fallback
L   x&.to_d || fallback
L   x&.to_i || fallback
L   x&.to_f || fallback
L   x&.to_h || fallback
L   x&.to_r || fallback
L   x&.to_s || fallback
L   x&.to_sym || fallback
L   x&.intern || fallback
L   x&.inspect || fallback
L   x&.hash || fallback
L   x&.object_id || fallback
L   x&.__id__ || fallback
L 
L   x&.to_s or fallback
C Lint/UselessRescue
S Details
L Checks for useless ``rescue``s, which only reraise rescued exceptions.
S Example
L   # bad
L   def foo
L     do_something
L   rescue
L     raise
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   def foo
L     do_something
L   rescue => e
L     raise # or 'raise e', or 'raise $!', or 'raise $ERROR_INFO'
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo
L     do_something
L   rescue
L     do_cleanup
L     raise
L   end
L 
L   # bad (latest rescue)
L   def foo
L     do_something
L   rescue ArgumentError
L     # noop
L   rescue
L     raise
L   end
L 
L   # good (not the latest rescue)
L   def foo
L     do_something
L   rescue ArgumentError
L     raise
L   rescue
L     # noop
L   end
C Lint/UselessRuby2Keywords
S Details
L Looks for `ruby2_keywords` calls for methods that do not need it.
L 
L `ruby2_keywords` should only be called on methods that accept an argument splat
L (`\*args`) but do not have explicit keyword arguments (`k:` or `k: true`) or
L a keyword splat (`**kwargs`).
S Example
L   # good (splat argument without keyword arguments)
L   ruby2_keywords def foo(*args); end
L 
L   # bad (no arguments)
L   ruby2_keywords def foo; end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo; end
L 
L   # bad (positional argument)
L   ruby2_keywords def foo(arg); end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo(arg); end
L 
L   # bad (double splatted argument)
L   ruby2_keywords def foo(**args); end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo(**args); end
L 
L   # bad (keyword arguments)
L   ruby2_keywords def foo(i:, j:); end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo(i:, j:); end
L 
L   # bad (splat argument with keyword arguments)
L   ruby2_keywords def foo(*args, i:, j:); end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo(*args, i:, j:); end
L 
L   # bad (splat argument with double splat)
L   ruby2_keywords def foo(*args, **kwargs); end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo(*args, **kwargs); end
L 
L   # bad (ruby2_keywords given a symbol)
L   def foo; end
L   ruby2_keywords :foo
L 
L   # good
L   def foo; end
L 
L   # bad (ruby2_keywords with dynamic method)
L   define_method(:foo) { |arg| }
L   ruby2_keywords :foo
L 
L   # good
L   define_method(:foo) { |arg| }
C Lint/UselessSetterCall
S Details
L Checks for setter call to local variable as the final
L expression of a function definition.
S Safety
L   There are edge cases in which the local variable references a
L   value that is also accessible outside the local scope. This is not
L   detected by the cop, and it can yield a false positive.
L 
L   As well, autocorrection is unsafe because the method's
L   return value will be changed.
S Example
L   # bad
L   def something
L     x = Something.new
L     x.attr = 5
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def something
L     x = Something.new
L     x.attr = 5
L     x
L   end
C Lint/UselessTimes
S Details
L Checks for uses of `Integer#times` that will never yield
L (when the integer ``<= 0``) or that will only ever yield once
L (`1.times`).
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe as `times` returns its receiver, which is
L   *usually* OK, but might change behavior.
S Example
L   # bad
L   -5.times { do_something }
L   0.times { do_something }
L   1.times { do_something  }
L   1.times { |i| do_something(i) }
L 
L   # good
L   do_something
L   do_something(1)
C Lint/Void
S Details
L Checks for operators, variables, literals, lambda, proc and nonmutating
L methods used in void context.
L 
L `each` blocks are allowed to prevent false positives.
L For example, the expression inside the `each` block below.
L It's not void, especially when the receiver is an `Enumerator`:
L 
L [source,ruby]
L ----
L enumerator = [1, 2, 3].filter
L enumerator.each { |item| item >= 2 } #=> [2, 3]
L ----
L 
L NOTE: The last expression in an assignment method definition such as `def foo=(arg)`
L is not flagged. Ruby discards it (the method returns its argument), but the method can
L still be called directly and its return value relied upon, so flagging it would be a
L false positive for this lint.
L 
L NOTE: A constant used in a void context is flagged but not autocorrected, since
L referencing a constant can trigger autoloading side effects (e.g. forcing a file to
L load before a monkey-patch), so removing it may change behavior.
S Example: CheckForMethodsWithNoSideEffects: false (default)
L   # bad
L   def some_method
L     some_num * 10
L     do_something
L   end
L 
L   def some_method(some_var)
L     some_var
L     do_something
L   end
S Example: CheckForMethodsWithNoSideEffects: true
L   # bad
L   def some_method(some_array)
L     some_array.sort
L     do_something(some_array)
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def some_method
L     do_something
L     some_num * 10
L   end
L 
L   def some_method(some_var)
L     do_something
L     some_var
L   end
L 
L   def some_method(some_array)
L     some_array.sort!
L     do_something(some_array)
L   end
C Metrics/AbcSize
S Details
L Checks that the ABC size of methods is not higher than the
L configured maximum. The ABC size is based on assignments, branches
L (method calls), and conditions. See http://c2.com/cgi/wiki?AbcMetric
L and https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ABC_Software_Metric.
L 
L Interpreting ABC size:
L 
L * ``<= 17`` satisfactory
L * `18..30` unsatisfactory
L * `>` 30 dangerous
L 
L You can have repeated "attributes" calls count as a single "branch".
L For this purpose, attributes are any method with no argument; no attempt
L is meant to distinguish actual `attr_reader` from other methods.
S Example: CountRepeatedAttributes: false (default is true)
L    # `model` and `current_user`, referenced 3 times each,
L    # are each counted as only 1 branch each if
L    # `CountRepeatedAttributes` is set to 'false'
L 
L    def search
L      @posts = model.active.visible_by(current_user)
L                .search(params[:q])
L      @posts = model.some_process(@posts, current_user)
L      @posts = model.another_process(@posts, current_user)
L 
L      render 'pages/search/page'
L    end
L 
L This cop also takes into account `AllowedMethods` (defaults to `[]`)
L And `AllowedPatterns` (defaults to `[]`)
C Metrics/BlockLength
S Details
L Checks if the length of a block exceeds some maximum value.
L Comment lines can optionally be ignored.
L The maximum allowed length is configurable.
L The cop can be configured to ignore blocks passed to certain methods.
L 
L You can set constructs you want to fold with `CountAsOne`.
L 
L Available are: 'array', 'hash', 'heredoc', and 'method_call'.
L Each construct will be counted as one line regardless of its actual size.
L 
L NOTE: This cop does not apply for `Struct` definitions.
L 
L NOTE: The `ExcludedMethods` configuration is deprecated and only kept
L for backwards compatibility. Please use `AllowedMethods` and `AllowedPatterns`
L instead. By default, there are no allowed methods.
S Example: CountAsOne: ['array', 'hash', 'heredoc', 'method_call']
L   something do
L     array = [         # +1
L       1,
L       2
L     ]
L 
L     hash = {          # +1
L       key: 'value'
L     }
L 
L     msg = <<~HEREDOC  # +1
L       Heredoc
L       content.
L     HEREDOC
L 
L     foo(              # +1
L       1,
L       2
L     )
L   end                 # 4 points
C Metrics/BlockNesting
S Details
L Checks for excessive nesting of conditional and looping constructs.
L Deeply nested code is harder to read, understand, and maintain.
L Extracting nested logic into methods improves clarity.
L 
L You can configure if blocks are considered using the `CountBlocks` and `CountModifierForms`
L options. When both are set to `false` (the default) blocks and modifier forms are not
L counted towards the nesting level. Set them to `true` to include these in the nesting level
L calculation as well.
L 
L The maximum level of nesting allowed is configurable.
S Example: Max: 3 (default)
L   # bad
L   if condition1
L     if condition2
L       if condition3
L         if condition4
L           do_something
L         end
L       end
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   if condition1
L     if condition2
L       if condition3
L         do_something
L       end
L     end
L   end
C Metrics/ClassLength
S Details
L Checks if the length of a class exceeds some maximum value.
L Comment lines can optionally be ignored.
L The maximum allowed length is configurable.
L 
L You can set constructs you want to fold with `CountAsOne`.
L 
L Available are: 'array', 'hash', 'heredoc', and 'method_call'.
L Each construct will be counted as one line regardless of its actual size.
L 
L NOTE: This cop also applies for `Struct` definitions.
S Example: CountAsOne: ['array', 'hash', 'heredoc', 'method_call']
L   class Foo
L     ARRAY = [         # +1
L       1,
L       2
L     ]
L 
L     HASH = {          # +1
L       key: 'value'
L     }
L 
L     MSG = <<~HEREDOC  # +1
L       Heredoc
L       content.
L     HEREDOC
L 
L     foo(              # +1
L       1,
L       2
L     )
L   end                 # 4 points
C Metrics/CollectionLiteralLength
S Details
L Checks for literals with extremely many entries. This is indicative of
L configuration or data that may be better extracted somewhere else, like
L a database, fetched from an API, or read from a non-code file (CSV,
L JSON, YAML, etc.).
S Example
L   # bad
L   # Huge Array literal
L   [1, 2, '...', 999_999_999]
L 
L   # bad
L   # Huge Hash literal
L   { 1 => 1, 2 => 2, '...' => '...', 999_999_999 => 999_999_999}
L 
L   # bad
L   # Huge Set "literal"
L   Set[1, 2, '...', 999_999_999]
L 
L   # good
L   # Reasonably sized Array literal
L   [1, 2, '...', 10]
L 
L   # good
L   # Reading huge Array from external data source
L   # File.readlines('numbers.txt', chomp: true).map!(&:to_i)
L 
L   # good
L   # Reasonably sized Hash literal
L   { 1 => 1, 2 => 2, '...' => '...', 10 => 10}
L 
L   # good
L   # Reading huge Hash from external data source
L   CSV.foreach('numbers.csv', headers: true).each_with_object({}) do |row, hash|
L     hash[row["key"].to_i] = row["value"].to_i
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   # Reasonably sized Set "literal"
L   Set[1, 2, '...', 10]
L 
L   # good
L   # Reading huge Set from external data source
L   SomeFramework.config_for(:something)[:numbers].to_set
C Metrics/CyclomaticComplexity
S Details
L Checks that the cyclomatic complexity of methods is not higher
L than the configured maximum. The cyclomatic complexity is the number of
L linearly independent paths through a method. The algorithm counts
L decision points and adds one.
L 
L An if statement (or unless or ?:) increases the complexity by one. An
L else branch does not, since it doesn't add a decision point. The &&
L operator (or keyword and) can be converted to a nested if statement,
L and ||/or is shorthand for a sequence of ifs, so they also add one.
L Loops can be said to have an exit condition, so they add one.
L Blocks that are calls to builtin iteration methods
L (e.g. `ary.map{...}`) also add one, others are ignored.
S Example
L   def each_child_node(*types)               # count begins: 1
L     unless block_given?                     # unless: +1
L       return to_enum(__method__, *types)
L     end
L 
L     children.each do |child|                # each{}: +1
L       next unless child.is_a?(Node)         # unless: +1
L 
L       yield child if types.empty? ||        # if: +1, ||: +1
L                      types.include?(child.type)
L     end
L 
L     self
L   end                                       # total: 6
C Metrics/MethodLength
S Details
L Checks if the length of a method exceeds some maximum value.
L Comment lines can optionally be allowed.
L The maximum allowed length is configurable.
L 
L You can set constructs you want to fold with `CountAsOne`.
L 
L Available are: 'array', 'hash', 'heredoc', and 'method_call'.
L Each construct will be counted as one line regardless of its actual size.
L 
L NOTE: The `ExcludedMethods` and `IgnoredMethods` configuration is
L deprecated and only kept for backwards compatibility.
L Please use `AllowedMethods` and `AllowedPatterns` instead.
L By default, there are no allowed methods.
S Example: CountAsOne: ['array', 'hash', 'heredoc', 'method_call']
L   def m
L     array = [       # +1
L       1,
L       2
L     ]
L 
L     hash = {        # +1
L       key: 'value'
L     }
L 
L     <<~HEREDOC      # +1
L       Heredoc
L       content.
L     HEREDOC
L 
L     foo(            # +1
L       1,
L       2
L     )
L   end               # 4 points
C Metrics/ModuleLength
S Details
L Checks if the length of a module exceeds some maximum value.
L Comment lines can optionally be ignored.
L The maximum allowed length is configurable.
L 
L You can set constructs you want to fold with `CountAsOne`.
L 
L Available are: 'array', 'hash', 'heredoc', and 'method_call'.
L Each construct will be counted as one line regardless of its actual size.
S Example: CountAsOne: ['array', 'hash', 'heredoc', 'method_call']
L   module M
L     ARRAY = [         # +1
L       1,
L       2
L     ]
L 
L     HASH = {          # +1
L       key: 'value'
L     }
L 
L     MSG = <<~HEREDOC  # +1
L       Heredoc
L       content.
L     HEREDOC
L 
L     foo(              # +1
L       1,
L       2
L     )
L   end                 # 4 points
C Metrics/ParameterLists
S Details
L Checks for methods with too many parameters.
L 
L The maximum number of parameters is configurable.
L Keyword arguments can optionally be excluded from the total count,
L as they add less complexity than positional or optional parameters.
L 
L Any number of arguments for `initialize` method inside a block of
L `Struct.new` and `Data.define` like this is always allowed:
L 
L [source,ruby]
L ----
L Struct.new(:one, :two, :three, :four, :five, keyword_init: true) do
L   def initialize(one:, two:, three:, four:, five:)
L   end
L end
L ----
L 
L This is because checking the number of arguments of the `initialize` method
L does not make sense.
L 
L NOTE: Explicit block argument `&block` is not counted to prevent
L erroneous change that is avoided by making block argument implicit.
S Example: Max: 3
L   # good
L   def foo(a, b, c = 1)
L   end
S Example: Max: 2
L   # bad
L   def foo(a, b, c = 1)
L   end
S Example: CountKeywordArgs: true (default)
L   # counts keyword args towards the maximum
L 
L   # bad (assuming Max is 3)
L   def foo(a, b, c, d: 1)
L   end
L 
L   # good (assuming Max is 3)
L   def foo(a, b, c: 1)
L   end
S Example: CountKeywordArgs: false
L   # don't count keyword args towards the maximum
L 
L   # good (assuming Max is 3)
L   def foo(a, b, c, d: 1)
L   end
L 
L This cop also checks for the maximum number of optional parameters.
L This can be configured using the `MaxOptionalParameters` config option.
S Example: MaxOptionalParameters: 3 (default)
L   # good
L   def foo(a = 1, b = 2, c = 3)
L   end
S Example: MaxOptionalParameters: 2
L   # bad
L   def foo(a = 1, b = 2, c = 3)
L   end
C Metrics/PerceivedComplexity
S Details
L Tries to produce a complexity score that's a measure of the
L complexity the reader experiences when looking at a method. For that
L reason it considers `when` nodes as something that doesn't add as much
L complexity as an `if` or a `&&`. Except if it's one of those special
L `case`/`when` constructs where there's no expression after `case`. Then
L the cop treats it as an `if`/`elsif`/`elsif`... and lets all the `when`
L nodes count. In contrast to the CyclomaticComplexity cop, this cop
L considers `else` nodes as adding complexity.
L 
L A `case`/`in` branch whose pattern is a simple literal (e.g. `in 1`, `in "red"`, `in 1..10`)
L or a constant/type (e.g. `in Integer`) and has no guard is just as easy to read as a `when`
L branch, so it is discounted the same way. Branches with structural patterns (e.g. array,
L hash, or find patterns), bindings, alternatives, or a guard add the full complexity of
L a decision point.
S Example
L   def example_1                   # 1
L     if cond                       # 1
L       case var                    # 2 (0.8 + 4 * 0.2, rounded)
L       when 1 then func_one
L       when 2 then func_two
L       when 3 then func_three
L       when 4..10 then func_other
L       end
L     else                          # 1
L       do_something until a && b   # 2
L     end                           # ===
L   end                             # 7 complexity points
L 
L   def example_2                   # 1
L     case color                    # 1 (3 * 0.2, rounded)
L     in "red" then func_red
L     in "blue" then func_blue
L     in "green" then func_green
L     end                           # ===
L   end                             # 2 complexity points
C Migration/DepartmentName
S Details
L Checks that cop names in rubocop:disable comments are given with
L department name.
S Example
L   # bad
L   # rubocop:disable AbcSize
L 
L   # good
L   # rubocop:disable Metrics/AbcSize
C Minitest/AssertEmpty
S Details
L Enforces the test to use `assert_empty` instead of using `assert(object.empty?)`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   assert(object.empty?)
L   assert(object.empty?, 'message')
L 
L   # good
L   assert_empty(object)
L   assert_empty(object, 'message')
C Minitest/AssertEmptyLiteral
S Details
L Enforces the test to use `assert_empty`
L instead of using `assert_equal([], object)` or `assert_equal({}, object)`.
L 
L NOTE: Using `assert_empty` makes it impossible to indicate that the expected value is
L the literal `[]` or `{}`. Since this removes the ability to express the difference
L between those literals, it is disabled by default in consideration of that drawback.
L Since the role of replacing the originally intended `assert([], empty_list)` with
L `assert_equal([], empty_list)` is handled by `Minitest/AssertWithExpectedArgumentTest`,
L this cop may be removed in the future.
S Example
L   # bad
L   assert_equal([], object)
L   assert_equal({}, object)
L 
L   # good
L   assert_empty(object)
C Minitest/AssertEqual
S Details
L Enforces the use of `assert_equal(expected, actual)`
L over `assert(expected == actual)`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   assert("rubocop-minitest" == actual)
L   assert_operator("rubocop-minitest", :==, actual)
L 
L   # good
L   assert_equal("rubocop-minitest", actual)
C Minitest/AssertInDelta
S Details
L Enforces the test to use `assert_in_delta`
L instead of using `assert_equal` to compare floats.
S Example
L   # bad
L   assert_equal(0.2, actual)
L   assert_equal(0.2, actual, 'message')
L 
L   # good
L   assert_in_delta(0.2, actual)
L   assert_in_delta(0.2, actual, 0.001, 'message')
C Minitest/AssertIncludes
S Details
L Enforces the test to use `assert_includes`
L instead of using `assert(collection.include?(object))`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   assert(collection.include?(object))
L   assert(collection.include?(object), 'message')
L 
L   # good
L   assert_includes(collection, object)
L   assert_includes(collection, object, 'message')
C Minitest/AssertInstanceOf
S Details
L Enforces the test to use `assert_instance_of(Class, object)`
L over `assert(object.instance_of?(Class))`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   assert(object.instance_of?(Class))
L   assert(object.instance_of?(Class), 'message')
L 
L   # bad
L   assert_equal(Class, object.class)
L   assert_equal(Class, object.class, 'message')
L 
L   # good
L   assert_instance_of(Class, object)
L   assert_instance_of(Class, object, 'message')
C Minitest/AssertKindOf
S Details
L Enforces the test to use `assert_kind_of(Class, object)`
L over `assert(object.kind_of?(Class))`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   assert(object.kind_of?(Class))
L   assert(object.kind_of?(Class), 'message')
L 
L   # bad
L   # `is_a?` is an alias for `kind_of?`
L   assert(object.is_a?(Class))
L   assert(object.is_a?(Class), 'message')
L 
L   # good
L   assert_kind_of(Class, object)
L   assert_kind_of(Class, object, 'message')
C Minitest/AssertMatch
S Details
L Enforces the test to use `assert_match`
L instead of using `assert(matcher.match(string))`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   assert(matcher.match(string))
L   assert(matcher.match?(string))
L   assert(matcher =~ string)
L   assert_operator(matcher, :=~, string)
L   assert(matcher.match(string), 'message')
L 
L   # good
L   assert_match(regex, string)
L   assert_match(matcher, string, 'message')
C Minitest/AssertNil
S Details
L Enforces the test to use `assert_nil` instead of using
L `assert_equal(nil, something)`, `assert(something.nil?)`, or `assert_predicate(something, :nil?)`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   assert_equal(nil, actual)
L   assert_equal(nil, actual, 'message')
L   assert(object.nil?)
L   assert(object.nil?, 'message')
L   assert_predicate(object, :nil?)
L   assert_predicate(object, :nil?, 'message')
L 
L   # good
L   assert_nil(actual)
L   assert_nil(actual, 'message')
C Minitest/AssertOperator
S Details
L Enforces the use of `assert_operator(expected, :<, actual)` over `assert(expected < actual)`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   assert(expected < actual)
L 
L   # good
L   assert_operator(expected, :<, actual)
C Minitest/AssertOutput
S Details
L Checks for opportunities to use `assert_output`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   $stdout = StringIO.new
L   puts object.method
L   $stdout.rewind
L   assert_match expected, $stdout.read
L 
L   # good
L   assert_output(expected) { puts object.method }
C Minitest/AssertPathExists
S Details
L Enforces the test to use `assert_path_exists` instead of using `assert(File.exist?(path))`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   assert(File.exist?(path))
L   assert(File.exist?(path), 'message')
L 
L   # good
L   assert_path_exists(path)
L   assert_path_exists(path, 'message')
C Minitest/AssertPredicate
S Details
L Enforces the test to use `assert_predicate`
L instead of using `assert(obj.a_predicate_method?)`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   assert(obj.one?)
L   assert(obj.one?, 'message')
L 
L   # good
L   assert_predicate(obj, :one?)
L   assert_predicate(obj, :one?, 'message')
C Minitest/AssertRaisesCompoundBody
S Details
L Enforces the block body of `assert_raises { ... }` to be reduced to only the raising code.
S Example
L   # bad
L   assert_raises(MyError) do
L     foo
L     bar
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   assert_raises(MyError) do
L     foo
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   assert_raises(MyError) do
L     foo do
L       bar
L       baz
L     end
L   end
C Minitest/AssertRaisesWithRegexpArgument
S Details
L Checks for `assert_raises` with arguments of regular expression literals.
L Arguments should be exception classes.
L Optionally the last argument can be a custom message string to help explain failures.
L Either way, it's not the argument that `exception.message` is compared to.
L The raised exception is returned and can be used
L to match against a regular expression.
S Example
L   # bad
L   assert_raises FooError, /some message/ do
L     obj.occur_error
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   exception = assert_raises FooError do
L     obj.occur_error
L   end
L   assert_match(/some message/, exception.message)
C Minitest/AssertRespondTo
S Details
L Enforces the use of `assert_respond_to(object, :do_something)`
L over `assert(object.respond_to?(:do_something))`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   assert(object.respond_to?(:do_something))
L   assert(object.respond_to?(:do_something), 'message')
L   assert(respond_to?(:do_something))
L 
L   # good
L   assert_respond_to(object, :do_something)
L   assert_respond_to(object, :do_something, 'message')
L   assert_respond_to(self, :do_something)
C Minitest/AssertSame
S Details
L Enforces the use of `assert_same(expected, actual)`
L over `assert(expected.equal?(actual))`.
L 
L NOTE: Use `assert_same` only when there is a need to compare by identity.
L       Otherwise, use `assert_equal`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   assert(expected.equal?(actual))
L   assert_equal(expected.object_id, actual.object_id)
L 
L   # good
L   assert_same(expected, actual)
C Minitest/AssertSilent
S Details
L Enforces the test to use `assert_silent { ... }`
L instead of using `assert_output('', '') { ... }`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   assert_output('', '') { puts object.do_something }
L 
L   # good
L   assert_silent { puts object.do_something }
C Minitest/AssertTruthy
S Details
L Enforces the test to use `assert(actual)` instead of using `assert_equal(true, actual)`.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because true might be expected instead of truthy.
L   False positives cannot be prevented when this is a variable or method return value.
L 
L   [source,ruby]
L   ----
L   assert_equal(true, 'truthy') # failure
L   assert('truthy')             # success
L   ----
S Example
L   # bad
L   assert_equal(true, actual)
L   assert_equal(true, actual, 'message')
L 
L   # good
L   assert(actual)
L   assert(actual, 'message')
C Minitest/AssertWithExpectedArgument
S Details
L Tries to detect when a user accidentally used
L `assert` when they meant to use `assert_equal`.
L 
L NOTE: The second argument to the `assert` method named `message` and `msg` is allowed.
L       Because their names are inferred as message arguments.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because it is not possible to determine
L   whether the second argument of `assert` is a message or not.
S Example
L   # bad
L   assert(3, my_list.length)
L   assert([], empty_list)
L   assert(expected, actual)
L 
L   # good
L   assert_equal(3, my_list.length)
L   assert_equal([], empty_list)
L   assert_equal(expected, actual)
L   assert(foo, 'message')
L   assert(foo, message)
L   assert(foo, msg)
C Minitest/AssertionInLifecycleHook
S Details
L Checks for usage of assertions in lifecycle hooks.
S Example
L   # bad
L   class FooTest < Minitest::Test
L     def setup
L       assert_equal(foo, bar)
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class FooTest < Minitest::Test
L     def test_something
L       assert_equal(foo, bar)
L     end
L   end
C Minitest/DuplicateTestRun
S Details
L If a Minitest class inherits from another class,
L it will also inherit its methods causing Minitest to run the parent's tests methods twice.
L 
L This cop detects when there are two tests classes, one inherits from the other, and both have tests methods.
L This cop will add an offense to the Child class in such a case.
S Example
L   # bad
L   class ParentTest < Minitest::Test
L     def test_parent # it will run this test twice.
L     end
L   end
L 
L   class ChildTest < ParentTest
L     def test_child
L     end
L   end
L 
L 
L   # good
L   class ParentTest < Minitest::Test
L     def test_parent
L     end
L   end
L 
L   class ChildTest < Minitest::Test
L     def test_child
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class ParentTest < Minitest::Test
L   end
L 
L   class ChildTest
L     def test_child
L     end
L 
L     def test_parent
L     end
L   end
C Minitest/EmptyLineBeforeAssertionMethods
S Details
L Enforces empty line before assertion methods because it separates assertion phase.
S Example
L   # bad
L   do_something
L   assert_equal(expected, actual)
L 
L   # good
L   do_something
L 
L   assert_equal(expected, actual)
C Minitest/Focus
S Details
L Enforces tests are not focused.
S Example
L   # bad
L   focus test 'foo' do
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   focus
L   test 'foo' do
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   test 'foo' do
L   end
C Minitest/GlobalExpectations
S Details
L Checks for deprecated global expectations
L and autocorrects them to use expect format.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: any (default)
L   # bad
L   musts.must_equal expected_musts
L   wonts.wont_match expected_wonts
L   musts.must_raise TypeError
L 
L   # good
L   _(musts).must_equal expected_musts
L   _(wonts).wont_match expected_wonts
L   _ { musts }.must_raise TypeError
L 
L   expect(musts).must_equal expected_musts
L   expect(wonts).wont_match expected_wonts
L   expect { musts }.must_raise TypeError
L 
L   value(musts).must_equal expected_musts
L   value(wonts).wont_match expected_wonts
L   value { musts }.must_raise TypeError
S Example: EnforcedStyle: _
L   # bad
L   musts.must_equal expected_musts
L   wonts.wont_match expected_wonts
L   musts.must_raise TypeError
L 
L   expect(musts).must_equal expected_musts
L   expect(wonts).wont_match expected_wonts
L   expect { musts }.must_raise TypeError
L 
L   value(musts).must_equal expected_musts
L   value(wonts).wont_match expected_wonts
L   value { musts }.must_raise TypeError
L 
L   # good
L   _(musts).must_equal expected_musts
L   _(wonts).wont_match expected_wonts
L   _ { musts }.must_raise TypeError
S Example: EnforcedStyle: expect
L   # bad
L   musts.must_equal expected_musts
L   wonts.wont_match expected_wonts
L   musts.must_raise TypeError
L 
L   _(musts).must_equal expected_musts
L   _(wonts).wont_match expected_wonts
L   _ { musts }.must_raise TypeError
L 
L   value(musts).must_equal expected_musts
L   value(wonts).wont_match expected_wonts
L   value { musts }.must_raise TypeError
L 
L   # good
L   expect(musts).must_equal expected_musts
L   expect(wonts).wont_match expected_wonts
L   expect { musts }.must_raise TypeError
S Example: EnforcedStyle: value
L   # bad
L   musts.must_equal expected_musts
L   wonts.wont_match expected_wonts
L   musts.must_raise TypeError
L 
L   _(musts).must_equal expected_musts
L   _(wonts).wont_match expected_wonts
L   _ { musts }.must_raise TypeError
L 
L   expect(musts).must_equal expected_musts
L   expect(wonts).wont_match expected_wonts
L   expect { musts }.must_raise TypeError
L 
L   # good
L   value(musts).must_equal expected_musts
L   value(wonts).wont_match expected_wonts
L   value { musts }.must_raise TypeError
C Minitest/LifecycleHooksOrder
S Details
L Checks that lifecycle hooks are declared in the order in which they will be executed.
S Example
L   # bad
L   class FooTest < Minitest::Test
L     def teardown; end
L     def setup; end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class FooTest < Minitest::Test
L     def setup; end
L     def teardown; end
L   end
L 
L   # bad (after test cases)
L   class FooTest < Minitest::Test
L     def test_something
L       assert foo
L     end
L     def setup; end
L     def teardown; end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class FooTest < Minitest::Test
L     def setup; end
L     def teardown; end
L     def test_something
L       assert foo
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good (after non test case methods)
L   class FooTest < Minitest::Test
L     def do_something; end
L     def setup; end
L     def teardown; end
L   end
C Minitest/LiteralAsActualArgument
S Details
L Enforces correct order of expected and
L actual arguments for `assert_equal`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   assert_equal foo, 2
L   assert_equal foo, [1, 2]
L   assert_equal foo, [1, 2], 'message'
L 
L   # good
L   assert_equal 2, foo
L   assert_equal [1, 2], foo
L   assert_equal [1, 2], foo, 'message'
C Minitest/MultipleAssertions
S Details
L Checks if test cases contain too many assertion calls. If conditional code with assertions
L is used, the branch with maximum assertions is counted.
L The maximum allowed assertion calls is configurable.
S Example: Max: 1
L   # bad
L   class FooTest < Minitest::Test
L     def test_asserts_twice
L       assert_equal(42, do_something)
L       assert_empty(array)
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class FooTest < Minitest::Test
L     def test_asserts_once
L       assert_equal(42, do_something)
L     end
L 
L     def test_another_asserts_once
L       assert_empty(array)
L     end
L   end
C Minitest/NoAssertions
S Details
L Checks if test cases contain any assertion calls.
L 
L Matchers such as `must_equal` and `wont_match` are also treated as assertion methods.
S Example
L   # bad
L   class FooTest < Minitest::Test
L     def test_the_truth
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class FooTest < Minitest::Test
L     def test_the_truth
L       assert true
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   class FooTest < ActiveSupport::TestCase
L     describe 'foo' do
L       it 'test equal' do
L       end
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class FooTest < ActiveSupport::TestCase
L     describe 'foo' do
L       it 'test equal' do
L         musts.must_equal expected_musts
L       end
L     end
L   end
C Minitest/NoTestCases
S Details
L Checks if test class contains any test cases.
S Example
L   # bad
L   class FooTest < Minitest::Test
L     def do_something
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class FooTest < Minitest::Test
L     def test_something
L       assert true
L     end
L   end
C Minitest/NonExecutableTestMethod
S Details
L Checks for the use of test methods outside of a test class.
L 
L Test methods should be defined within a test class to ensure their execution.
L 
L NOTE: This cop assumes that classes whose superclass name includes the word
L "`Test`" are test classes, in order to prevent false positives.
S Example
L   # bad
L   class FooTest < Minitest::Test
L   end
L   def test_method_should_be_inside_test_class
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class FooTest < Minitest::Test
L     def test_method_should_be_inside_test_class
L     end
L   end
C Minitest/NonPublicTestMethod
S Details
L Detects non `public` (marked as `private` or `protected`) test methods.
L Minitest runs only test methods which are `public`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   class FooTest
L     private # or protected
L     def test_does_something
L       assert_equal 42, do_something
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class FooTest
L     def test_does_something
L       assert_equal 42, do_something
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good (not a test case name)
L   class FooTest
L     private # or protected
L     def does_something
L       assert_equal 42, do_something
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good (no assertions)
L   class FooTest
L     private # or protected
L     def test_does_something
L       do_something
L     end
L   end
C Minitest/RedundantMessageArgument
S Details
L Detects redundant message argument in assertion methods.
L The message argument `nil` is redundant because it is the default value.
S Example
L   # bad
L   assert_equal(expected, actual, nil)
L 
L   # good
L   assert_equal(expected, actual)
L   assert_equal(expected, actual, 'message')
C Minitest/RefuteEmpty
S Details
L Enforces to use `refute_empty` instead of using `refute(object.empty?)`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   refute(object.empty?)
L   refute(object.empty?, 'message')
L 
L   # good
L   refute_empty(object)
L   refute_empty(object, 'message')
C Minitest/RefuteEqual
S Details
L Enforces the use of `refute_equal(expected, object)`
L over `assert(expected != actual)` or `assert(! expected == actual)`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   assert("rubocop-minitest" != actual)
L   refute("rubocop-minitest" == actual)
L   assert_operator("rubocop-minitest", :!=, actual)
L   refute_operator("rubocop-minitest", :==, actual)
L 
L   # good
L   refute_equal("rubocop-minitest", actual)
C Minitest/RefuteFalse
S Details
L Enforces the use of `refute(object)` over `assert_equal(false, object)`.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because it cannot detect failure when second argument is `nil`.
L   False positives cannot be prevented when this is a variable or method return value.
L 
L   [source,ruby]
L   ----
L   assert_equal(false, nil) # failure
L   refute(nil)              # success
L   ----
S Example
L   # bad
L   assert_equal(false, actual)
L   assert_equal(false, actual, 'message')
L 
L   assert(!test)
L   assert(!test, 'message')
L 
L   # good
L   refute(actual)
L   refute(actual, 'message')
C Minitest/RefuteInDelta
S Details
L Enforces the test to use `refute_in_delta`
L instead of using `refute_equal` to compare floats.
S Example
L   # bad
L   refute_equal(0.2, actual)
L   refute_equal(0.2, actual, 'message')
L 
L   # good
L   refute_in_delta(0.2, actual)
L   refute_in_delta(0.2, actual, 0.001, 'message')
C Minitest/RefuteIncludes
S Details
L Enforces the test to use `refute_includes`
L instead of using `refute(collection.include?(object))`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   refute(collection.include?(object))
L   refute(collection.include?(object), 'message')
L 
L   # good
L   refute_includes(collection, object)
L   refute_includes(collection, object, 'message')
C Minitest/RefuteInstanceOf
S Details
L Enforces the use of `refute_instance_of(Class, object)`
L over `refute(object.instance_of?(Class))`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   refute(object.instance_of?(Class))
L   refute(object.instance_of?(Class), 'message')
L 
L   # bad
L   refute_equal(Class, object.class)
L   refute_equal(Class, object.class, 'message')
L 
L   # good
L   refute_instance_of(Class, object)
L   refute_instance_of(Class, object, 'message')
C Minitest/RefuteKindOf
S Details
L Enforces the use of `refute_kind_of(Class, object)`
L over `refute(object.kind_of?(Class))`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   refute(object.kind_of?(Class))
L   refute(object.kind_of?(Class), 'message')
L 
L   # bad
L   # `is_a?` is an alias for `kind_of?`
L   refute(object.is_of?(Class))
L   refute(object.is_of?(Class), 'message')
L 
L   # good
L   refute_kind_of(Class, object)
L   refute_kind_of(Class, object, 'message')
C Minitest/RefuteMatch
S Details
L Enforces the test to use `refute_match`
L instead of using `refute(matcher.match(string))`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   refute(matcher.match(string))
L   refute(matcher.match?(string))
L   refute(matcher =~ string)
L   refute_operator(matcher, :=~, string)
L   assert_operator(matcher, :!~, string)
L   refute(matcher.match(string), 'message')
L 
L   # good
L   refute_match(matcher, string)
L   refute_match(matcher, string, 'message')
C Minitest/RefuteNil
S Details
L Enforces the test to use `refute_nil` instead of using
L `refute_equal(nil, something)`, `refute(something.nil?)`, or `refute_predicate(something, :nil?)`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   refute_equal(nil, actual)
L   refute_equal(nil, actual, 'message')
L   refute(actual.nil?)
L   refute(actual.nil?, 'message')
L   refute_predicate(object, :nil?)
L   refute_predicate(object, :nil?, 'message')
L 
L   # good
L   refute_nil(actual)
L   refute_nil(actual, 'message')
C Minitest/RefuteOperator
S Details
L Enforces the use of `refute_operator(expected, :<, actual)` over `refute(expected < actual)`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   refute(expected < actual)
L 
L   # good
L   refute_operator(expected, :<, actual)
C Minitest/RefutePathExists
S Details
L Enforces the test to use `refute_path_exists` instead of using `refute(File.exist?(path))`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   refute(File.exist?(path))
L   refute(File.exist?(path), 'message')
L 
L   # good
L   refute_path_exists(path)
L   refute_path_exists(path, 'message')
C Minitest/RefutePredicate
S Details
L Enforces the test to use `refute_predicate`
L instead of using `refute(obj.a_predicate_method?)`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   refute(obj.one?)
L   refute(obj.one?, 'message')
L 
L   # good
L   refute_predicate(obj, :one?)
L   refute_predicate(obj, :one?, 'message')
C Minitest/RefuteRespondTo
S Details
L Enforces the test to use `refute_respond_to(object, :do_something)`
L over `refute(object.respond_to?(:do_something))`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   refute(object.respond_to?(:do_something))
L   refute(object.respond_to?(:do_something), 'message')
L   refute(respond_to?(:do_something))
L 
L   # good
L   refute_respond_to(object, :do_something)
L   refute_respond_to(object, :do_something, 'message')
L   refute_respond_to(self, :do_something)
C Minitest/RefuteSame
S Details
L Enforces the use of `refute_same(expected, object)`
L over `refute(expected.equal?(actual))`.
L 
L NOTE: Use `refute_same` only when there is a need to compare by identity.
L       Otherwise, use `refute_equal`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   refute(expected.equal?(actual))
L   refute_equal(expected.object_id, actual.object_id)
L 
L   # good
L   refute_same(expected, actual)
C Minitest/ReturnInTestMethod
S Details
L Enforces the use of `skip` instead of `return` in test methods.
S Example
L   # bad
L   def test_something
L     return if condition?
L     assert_equal(42, something)
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def test_something
L     skip if condition?
L     assert_equal(42, something)
L   end
C Minitest/SkipEnsure
S Details
L Checks that code in an `ensure` block does not run when the test is skipped.
L If `skip` is conditional, the `ensure` block must also be conditional,
L using the negation of the skip condition so that it runs only when the test runs.
L 
L On the other hand, `skip` used inside a `rescue` clause is accepted,
L because the `ensure` block may serve as teardown for resources created in the `begin` setup.
S Example
L   # bad
L   def test_skip
L     skip 'This test is skipped.'
L 
L     assert 'foo'.present?
L   ensure
L     do_something
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   def test_conditional_skip
L     skip 'This test is skipped.' if condition
L 
L     assert do_something
L   ensure
L     do_teardown
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def test_skip
L     skip 'This test is skipped.'
L 
L     begin
L       assert 'foo'.present?
L     ensure
L       do_something
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def test_conditional_skip
L     skip 'This test is skipped.' if condition
L 
L     assert do_something
L   ensure
L     unless condition
L       do_teardown
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def test_skip_is_used_in_rescue
L     do_setup
L     assert do_something
L   rescue
L     skip 'This test is skipped.'
L   ensure
L     do_teardown
L   end
C Minitest/SkipWithoutReason
S Details
L Checks for skipped tests missing the skipping reason.
S Example
L   # bad
L   skip
L   skip('')
L 
L   # bad
L   if condition?
L     skip
L   else
L     skip
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   skip("Reason why the test was skipped")
L 
L   # good
L   skip if condition?
C Minitest/TestFileName
S Details
L Checks if test file names start with `test_` or end with `_test.rb`.
L Files which define classes having names ending with `Test` are checked.
L Not following this convention may result in tests not being run.
S Example
L   # bad
L   my_class.rb
L 
L   # good
L   my_class_test.rb
L   test_my_class.rb
C Minitest/TestMethodName
S Details
L Enforces that test method names start with `test_` prefix.
L It aims to prevent tests that aren't executed by forgetting to start test method name with `test_`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   class FooTest < Minitest::Test
L     def does_something
L       assert_equal 42, do_something
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class FooTest < Minitest::Test
L     def test_does_something
L       assert_equal 42, do_something
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class FooTest < Minitest::Test
L     def helper_method(argument)
L     end
L   end
C Minitest/UnreachableAssertion
S Details
L Checks for `assert_raises` has an assertion method at
L the bottom of block because the assertion will be never reached.
S Example
L   # bad
L   assert_raises FooError do
L     obj.occur_error
L     assert_equal('foo', obj.bar) # Never asserted.
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   assert_raises FooError do
L     obj.occur_error
L   end
L   assert_equal('foo', obj.bar)
C Minitest/UnspecifiedException
S Details
L Checks for a specified error in `assert_raises`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   assert_raises { raise FooException }
L   assert_raises('This should have raised') { raise FooException }
L 
L   # good
L   assert_raises(FooException) { raise FooException }
L   assert_raises(FooException, 'This should have raised') { raise FooException }
C Minitest/UselessAssertion
S Details
L Detects useless assertions (assertions that either always pass or always fail).
S Example
L   # bad
L   assert true
L   assert_equal @foo, @foo
L   assert_nil [foo, bar]
L 
L   # good
L   assert something
L   assert_equal foo, bar
L   assert_nil foo
L   assert false, "My message"
C Naming/AccessorMethodName
S Details
L Avoid prefixing accessor method names with `get_` or `set_`.
L Applies to both instance and class methods.
L 
L NOTE: Method names starting with `get_` or `set_` only register an offense
L when the methods match the expected arity for getters and setters respectively.
L Getters (`get_attribute`) must have no arguments to be registered,
L and setters (`set_attribute(value)`) must have exactly one.
L 
L When `AllCops/UseProjectIndex` is enabled and the `rubydex` gem is
L installed, methods that override a method defined by an ancestor
L elsewhere in the project are not reported, since renaming an override
L breaks the inherited contract.
S Example
L   # bad
L   def set_attribute(value)
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def attribute=(value)
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   def get_attribute
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def attribute
L   end
L 
L   # accepted, incorrect arity for getter
L   def get_value(attr)
L   end
L 
L   # accepted, incorrect arity for setter
L   def set_value
L   end
C Naming/AsciiIdentifiers
S Details
L Checks for non-ascii characters in identifier and constant names.
L Identifiers are always checked and whether constants are checked
L can be controlled using AsciiConstants config.
S Example
L   # bad
L   def καλημερα # Greek alphabet (non-ascii)
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   def こんにちはと言う # Japanese character (non-ascii)
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   def hello_🍣 # Emoji (non-ascii)
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def say_hello
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   신장 = 10 # Hangul character (non-ascii)
L 
L   # good
L   height = 10
L 
L   # bad
L   params[:عرض_gteq] # Arabic character (non-ascii)
L 
L   # good
L   params[:width_gteq]
S Example: AsciiConstants: true (default)
L   # bad
L   class Foö
L   end
L 
L   FOÖ = "foo"
S Example: AsciiConstants: false
L   # good
L   class Foö
L   end
L 
L   FOÖ = "foo"
C Naming/BinaryOperatorParameterName
S Details
L Makes sure that certain binary operator methods have their
L sole parameter named `other`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   def +(amount); end
L 
L   # good
L   def +(other); end
C Naming/BlockForwarding
S Details
L In Ruby 3.1, anonymous block forwarding has been added.
L 
L This cop identifies places where `do_something(&block)` can be replaced
L by `do_something(&)`.
L 
L It also supports the opposite style by alternative `explicit` option.
L You can specify the block variable name for autocorrection with `BlockForwardingName`.
L The default variable name is `block`. If the name is already in use, it will not be
