L autocorrected.
L 
L [NOTE]
L ====
L Because of a bug in Ruby 3.3.0, when a block is referenced inside of another block,
L no offense will be registered until Ruby 3.4:
L 
L [source,ruby]
L ----
L def foo(&block)
L   # Using an anonymous block would be a syntax error on Ruby 3.3.0
L   block_method { bar(&block) }
L end
L ----
L ====
S Example: EnforcedStyle: anonymous (default)
L   # bad
L   def foo(&block)
L     bar(&block)
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo(&)
L     bar(&)
L   end
S Example: EnforcedStyle: explicit
L   # bad
L   def foo(&)
L     bar(&)
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo(&block)
L     bar(&block)
L   end
C Naming/BlockParameterName
S Details
L Checks block parameter names for how descriptive they
L are. It is highly configurable.
L 
L The `MinNameLength` config option takes an integer. It represents
L the minimum amount of characters the name must be. Its default is 1.
L The `AllowNamesEndingInNumbers` config option takes a boolean. When
L set to false, this cop will register offenses for names ending with
L numbers. Its default is false. The `AllowedNames` config option
L takes an array of permitted names that will never register an
L offense. The `ForbiddenNames` config option takes an array of
L restricted names that will always register an offense.
S Example
L   # bad
L   bar do |varOne, varTwo|
L     varOne + varTwo
L   end
L 
L   # With `AllowNamesEndingInNumbers` set to false
L   foo { |num1, num2| num1 * num2 }
L 
L   # With `MinNameLength` set to number greater than 1
L   baz { |a, b, c| do_stuff(a, b, c) }
L 
L   # good
L   bar do |thud, fred|
L     thud + fred
L   end
L 
L   foo { |speed, distance| speed * distance }
L 
L   baz { |age, height, gender| do_stuff(age, height, gender) }
C Naming/ClassAndModuleCamelCase
S Details
L Checks for class and module names with
L an underscore in them.
L 
L `AllowedNames` config takes an array of permitted names.
L Its default value is `['module_parent']`.
L These names can be full class/module names or part of the name.
L eg. Adding `my_class` to the `AllowedNames` config will allow names like
L `my_class`, `my_class::User`, `App::my_class`, `App::my_class::User`, etc.
S Example
L   # bad
L   class My_Class
L   end
L   module My_Module
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class MyClass
L   end
L   module MyModule
L   end
L   class module_parent::MyModule
L   end
C Naming/ConstantName
S Details
L Checks whether constant names are written using
L SCREAMING_SNAKE_CASE.
L 
L To avoid false positives, it ignores cases in which we cannot know
L for certain the type of value that would be assigned to a constant.
S Example
L   # bad
L   InchInCm = 2.54
L   INCHinCM = 2.54
L   Inch_In_Cm = 2.54
L 
L   # good
L   INCH_IN_CM = 2.54
C Naming/FileName
S Details
L Makes sure that Ruby source files have snake_case
L names. Ruby scripts (i.e. source files with a shebang in the
L first line) are ignored.
L 
L The cop also ignores `.gemspec` files, because Bundler
L recommends using dashes to separate namespaces in nested gems
L (i.e. `bundler-console` becomes `Bundler::Console`). As such, the
L gemspec is supposed to be named `bundler-console.gemspec`.
L 
L When `ExpectMatchingDefinition` (default: `false`) is `true`, the cop requires
L each file to have a class, module or `Struct` defined in it that matches
L the filename. This can be further configured using
L `CheckDefinitionPathHierarchy` (default: `true`) to determine whether the
L path should match the namespace of the above definition.
L 
L When `IgnoreExecutableScripts` (default: `true`) is `true`, files that start
L with a shebang line are not considered by the cop.
L 
L When `Regex` is set, the cop will flag any filename that does not match
L the regular expression.
S Example
L   # bad
L   lib/layoutManager.rb
L 
L   anything/usingCamelCase
L 
L   # good
L   lib/layout_manager.rb
L 
L   anything/using_snake_case.rake
C Naming/HeredocDelimiterCase
S Details
L Checks that your heredocs are using the configured case.
L By default it is configured to enforce uppercase heredocs.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: uppercase (default)
L   # bad
L   <<-sql
L     SELECT * FROM foo
L   sql
L 
L   # good
L   <<-SQL
L     SELECT * FROM foo
L   SQL
S Example: EnforcedStyle: lowercase
L   # bad
L   <<-SQL
L     SELECT * FROM foo
L   SQL
L 
L   # good
L   <<-sql
L     SELECT * FROM foo
L   sql
C Naming/HeredocDelimiterNaming
S Details
L Checks that your heredocs are using meaningful delimiters.
L By default it disallows `END` and `EO*`, and can be configured through
L forbidden listing additional delimiters.
S Example
L   # good
L   <<-SQL
L     SELECT * FROM foo
L   SQL
L 
L   # bad
L   <<-END
L     SELECT * FROM foo
L   END
L 
L   # bad
L   <<-EOS
L     SELECT * FROM foo
L   EOS
C Naming/InclusiveLanguage
S Details
L Recommends the use of inclusive language instead of problematic terms.
L The cop can check the following locations for offenses:
L 
L - identifiers
L - constants
L - variables
L - strings
L - symbols
L - comments
L - file paths
L 
L Each of these locations can be individually enabled/disabled via configuration,
L for example CheckIdentifiers = true/false.
L 
L Flagged terms are configurable for the cop. For each flagged term an optional
L Regex can be specified to identify offenses. Suggestions for replacing a flagged term can
L be configured and will be displayed as part of the offense message.
L An AllowedRegex can be specified for a flagged term to exempt allowed uses of the term.
L `WholeWord: true` can be set on a flagged term to indicate the cop should only match when
L a term matches the whole word (partial matches will not be offenses).
L 
L The cop supports autocorrection when there is only one suggestion. When there are multiple
L suggestions, the best suggestion cannot be identified and will not be autocorrected.
S Example: FlaggedTerms: { whitelist: { Suggestions: ['allowlist'] } }
L   # Suggest replacing identifier whitelist with allowlist
L 
L   # bad
L   whitelist_users = %w(user1 user1)
L 
L   # good
L   allowlist_users = %w(user1 user2)
S Example: FlaggedTerms: { master: { Suggestions: ['main', 'primary', 'leader'] } }
L   # Suggest replacing master in an instance variable name with main, primary, or leader
L 
L   # bad
L   @master_node = 'node1.example.com'
L 
L   # good
L   @primary_node = 'node1.example.com'
S Example: FlaggedTerms: { whitelist: { Regex: !ruby/regexp '/white[-_\s]?list' } }
L   # Identify problematic terms using a Regexp
L 
L   # bad
L   white_list = %w(user1 user2)
L 
L   # good
L   allow_list = %w(user1 user2)
S Example: FlaggedTerms: { master: { AllowedRegex: 'master\'?s degree' } }
L   # Specify allowed uses of the flagged term as a string or regexp.
L 
L   # bad
L   # They had a masters
L 
L   # good
L   # They had a master's degree
S Example: FlaggedTerms: { slave: { WholeWord: true } }
L   # Specify that only terms that are full matches will be flagged.
L 
L   # bad
L   Slave
L 
L   # good (won't be flagged despite containing `slave`)
L   TeslaVehicle
C Naming/MemoizedInstanceVariableName
S Details
L Checks for memoized methods whose instance variable name
L does not match the method name. Applies to both regular methods
L (defined with `def`) and dynamic methods (defined with
L `define_method` or `define_singleton_method`).
L 
L This cop can be configured with the EnforcedStyleForLeadingUnderscores
L directive. It can be configured to allow for memoized instance variables
L prefixed with an underscore. Prefixing ivars with an underscore is a
L convention that is used to implicitly indicate that an ivar should not
L be set or referenced outside of the memoization method.
S Safety
L   This cop relies on the pattern `@instance_var ||= ...`,
L   but this is sometimes used for other purposes than memoization
L   so this cop is considered unsafe. Also, its autocorrection is unsafe
L   because it may conflict with instance variable names already in use.
S Example: EnforcedStyleForLeadingUnderscores: disallowed (default)
L   # bad
L   # Method foo is memoized using an instance variable that is
L   # not `@foo`. This can cause confusion and bugs.
L   def foo
L     @something ||= calculate_expensive_thing
L   end
L 
L   def foo
L     return @something if defined?(@something)
L     @something = calculate_expensive_thing
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def _foo
L     @foo ||= calculate_expensive_thing
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo
L     @foo ||= calculate_expensive_thing
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo
L     @foo ||= begin
L       calculate_expensive_thing
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo
L     helper_variable = something_we_need_to_calculate_foo
L     @foo ||= calculate_expensive_thing(helper_variable)
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   define_method(:foo) do
L     @foo ||= calculate_expensive_thing
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   define_method(:foo) do
L     return @foo if defined?(@foo)
L     @foo = calculate_expensive_thing
L   end
S Example: EnforcedStyleForLeadingUnderscores: required
L   # bad
L   def foo
L     @something ||= calculate_expensive_thing
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   def foo
L     @foo ||= calculate_expensive_thing
L   end
L 
L   def foo
L     return @foo if defined?(@foo)
L     @foo = calculate_expensive_thing
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo
L     @_foo ||= calculate_expensive_thing
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def _foo
L     @_foo ||= calculate_expensive_thing
L   end
L 
L   def foo
L     return @_foo if defined?(@_foo)
L     @_foo = calculate_expensive_thing
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   define_method(:foo) do
L     @_foo ||= calculate_expensive_thing
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   define_method(:foo) do
L     return @_foo if defined?(@_foo)
L     @_foo = calculate_expensive_thing
L   end
S Example: EnforcedStyleForLeadingUnderscores: optional
L   # bad
L   def foo
L     @something ||= calculate_expensive_thing
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo
L     @foo ||= calculate_expensive_thing
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo
L     @_foo ||= calculate_expensive_thing
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def _foo
L     @_foo ||= calculate_expensive_thing
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo
L     return @_foo if defined?(@_foo)
L     @_foo = calculate_expensive_thing
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   define_method(:foo) do
L     @foo ||= calculate_expensive_thing
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   define_method(:foo) do
L     @_foo ||= calculate_expensive_thing
L   end
C Naming/MethodName
S Details
L Makes sure that all methods use the configured style,
L snake_case or camelCase, for their names.
L 
L Method names matching patterns are always allowed.
L 
L The cop can be configured with `AllowedPatterns` to allow certain regexp patterns:
L 
L [source,yaml]
L ----
L Naming/MethodName:
L   AllowedPatterns:
L     - '\AonSelectionBulkChange\z'
L     - '\AonSelectionCleared\z'
L ----
L 
L As well, you can also forbid specific method names or regexp patterns
L using `ForbiddenIdentifiers` or `ForbiddenPatterns`:
L 
L [source,yaml]
L ----
L Naming/MethodName:
L   ForbiddenIdentifiers:
L     - 'def'
L     - 'super'
L   ForbiddenPatterns:
L     - '_v1\z'
L     - '_gen1\z'
L ----
S Example: EnforcedStyle: snake_case (default)
L   # bad
L   def fooBar; end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo_bar; end
L 
L   # bad
L   define_method :fooBar do
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   define_method :foo_bar do
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   Struct.new(:fooBar)
L 
L   # good
L   Struct.new(:foo_bar)
L 
L   # bad
L   alias_method :fooBar, :some_method
L 
L   # good
L   alias_method :foo_bar, :some_method
S Example: EnforcedStyle: camelCase
L   # bad
L   def foo_bar; end
L 
L   # good
L   def fooBar; end
L 
L   # bad
L   define_method :foo_bar do
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   define_method :fooBar do
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   Struct.new(:foo_bar)
L 
L   # good
L   Struct.new(:fooBar)
L 
L   # bad
L   alias_method :foo_bar, :some_method
L 
L   # good
L   alias_method :fooBar, :some_method
S Example: ForbiddenIdentifiers: ['def', 'super']
L   # bad
L   def def; end
L   def super; end
S Example: ForbiddenPatterns: ['_v1\z', '_gen1\z']
L   # bad
L   def release_v1; end
L   def api_gen1; end
C Naming/MethodParameterName
S Details
L Checks method parameter names for how descriptive they
L are. It is highly configurable.
L 
L The `MinNameLength` config option takes an integer. It represents
L the minimum amount of characters the name must be. Its default is 3.
L The `AllowNamesEndingInNumbers` config option takes a boolean. When
L set to false, this cop will register offenses for names ending with
L numbers. Its default is false. The `AllowedNames` config option
L takes an array of permitted names that will never register an
L offense. The `ForbiddenNames` config option takes an array of
L restricted names that will always register an offense.
S Example
L   # bad
L   def bar(varOne, varTwo)
L     varOne + varTwo
L   end
L 
L   # With `AllowNamesEndingInNumbers` set to false
L   def foo(num1, num2)
L     num1 * num2
L   end
L 
L   # With `MinNameLength` set to number greater than 1
L   def baz(a, b, c)
L     do_stuff(a, b, c)
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def bar(thud, fred)
L     thud + fred
L   end
L 
L   def foo(speed, distance)
L     speed * distance
L   end
L 
L   def baz(age_a, height_b, gender_c)
L     do_stuff(age_a, height_b, gender_c)
L   end
C Naming/PredicateMethod
S Details
L Checks that predicate methods end with `?` and non-predicate methods do not.
L 
L The names of predicate methods (methods that return a boolean value) should end
L in a question mark. Methods that don't return a boolean shouldn't
L end in a question mark.
L 
L The cop assesses a predicate method as one that returns boolean values. Likewise,
L a method that only returns literal values is assessed as non-predicate. Other predicate
L method calls are assumed to return boolean values. The cop does not make an assessment
L if the return type is unknown (non-predicate method calls, variables, etc.).
L 
L NOTE: The `initialize` method and operator methods (`def ==`, etc.) are ignored.
L 
L By default, the cop runs in `conservative` mode, which allows a method to be named
L with a question mark as long as at least one return value is boolean. In `aggressive`
L mode, methods with a question mark will register an offense if any known non-boolean
L return values are detected.
L 
L The cop also has `AllowedMethods` configuration in order to prevent the cop from
L registering an offense from a method name that does not conform to the naming
L guidelines. By default, `call` is allowed. The cop also has `AllowedPatterns`
L configuration to allow method names by regular expression.
L 
L Although returning a call to another predicate method is treated as a boolean value,
L certain method names can be known to not return a boolean, despite ending in a `?`
L (for example, `Numeric#nonzero?` returns `self` or `nil`). These methods can be
L configured using `NonBooleanPredicates`.
L 
L The cop can furthermore be configured to allow all bang methods (method names
L ending with `!`), with `AllowBangMethods: true` (default false).
S Example: Mode: conservative (default)
L   # bad
L   def foo
L     bar == baz
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo?
L     bar == baz
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   def foo?
L     5
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo
L     5
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   def foo
L     x == y
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo?
L     x == y
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   def foo
L     !x
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo?
L     !x
L   end
L 
L   # bad - returns the value of another predicate method
L   def foo
L     bar?
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo?
L     bar?
L   end
L 
L   # good - operator method
L   def ==(other)
L     hash == other.hash
L   end
L 
L   # good - at least one return value is boolean
L   def foo?
L     return unless bar?
L     true
L   end
L 
L   # ok - return type is not known
L   def foo?
L     bar
L   end
L 
L   # ok - return type is not known
L   def foo
L     bar?
L   end
S Example: Mode: aggressive
L   # bad - the method returns nil in some cases
L   def foo?
L     return unless bar?
L     true
L   end
S Example: AllowedMethods: [call] (default)
L   # good
L   def call
L     foo == bar
L   end
S Example: AllowedPatterns: [\Afoo]
L   # good
L   def foo?
L     'foo'
L   end
S Example: AllowBangMethods: false (default)
L   # bad
L   def save!
L     true
L   end
S Example: AllowBangMethods: true
L   # good
L   def save!
L     true
L   end
S Example: WaywardPredicates: ['infinite?', 'nonzero?'] (default)
L   # good
L   def non_predicate_method(num)
L     num.infinite?
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def non_predicate_method(num)
L     num.nonzero?
L   end
C Naming/PredicatePrefix
S Details
L Checks that predicate method names end with a question mark and
L do not start with a forbidden prefix.
L 
L A method is determined to be a predicate method if its name starts with
L one of the prefixes listed in the `NamePrefix` configuration. The list
L defaults to `is_`, `has_`, and `have_` but may be overridden.
L 
L Predicate methods must end with a question mark.
L 
L When `ForbiddenPrefixes` is also set (as it is by default), predicate
L methods which begin with a forbidden prefix are not allowed, even if
L they end with a `?`. These methods should be changed to remove the
L prefix.
L 
L When `UseSorbetSigs` is set to true (optional), the cop will only report
L offenses if the method has a Sorbet `sig` with a return type of
L `T::Boolean`. Dynamic methods are not supported with this configuration.
L 
L When `AllCops/UseProjectIndex` is enabled and the `rubydex` gem is
L installed, methods that override a method defined by an ancestor
L elsewhere in the project are not reported, since renaming an override
L breaks the inherited contract.
S Example: NamePrefix: ['is_', 'has_', 'have_'] (default)
L   # bad
L   def is_even(value)
L   end
L 
L   # When ForbiddenPrefixes: ['is_', 'has_', 'have_'] (default)
L   # good
L   def even?(value)
L   end
L 
L   # When ForbiddenPrefixes: []
L   # good
L   def is_even?(value)
L   end
S Example: NamePrefix: ['seems_to_be_']
L   # bad
L   def seems_to_be_even(value)
L   end
L 
L   # When ForbiddenPrefixes: ['seems_to_be_']
L   # good
L   def even?(value)
L   end
L 
L   # When ForbiddenPrefixes: []
L   # good
L   def seems_to_be_even?(value)
L   end
S Example: AllowedMethods: ['is_a?'] (default)
L   # Despite starting with the `is_` prefix, this method is allowed
L   # good
L   def is_a?(value)
L   end
S Example: AllowedMethods: ['is_even?']
L   # good
L   def is_even?(value)
L   end
S Example: UseSorbetSigs: false (default)
L   # bad
L   sig { returns(String) }
L   def is_this_thing_on
L     "yes"
L   end
L 
L   # good - Sorbet signature is not evaluated
L   sig { returns(String) }
L   def is_this_thing_on?
L     "yes"
L   end
S Example: UseSorbetSigs: true
L   # bad
L   sig { returns(T::Boolean) }
L   def odd(value)
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   sig { returns(T::Boolean) }
L   def odd?(value)
L   end
S Example: MethodDefinitionMacros: ['define_method', 'define_singleton_method'] (default)
L   # bad
L   define_method(:is_even) { |value| }
L 
L   # good
L   define_method(:even?) { |value| }
S Example: MethodDefinitionMacros: ['def_node_matcher']
L   # bad
L   def_node_matcher(:is_even) { |value| }
L 
L   # good
L   def_node_matcher(:even?) { |value| }
C Naming/RescuedExceptionsVariableName
S Details
L Makes sure that rescued exception variables are named as
L expected.
L 
L The `PreferredName` config option takes a `String`. It represents
L the required name of the variable. Its default is `e`.
L 
L NOTE: This cop does not consider nested rescues because it cannot
L guarantee that the variable from the outer rescue is not used within
L the inner rescue (in which case, changing the inner variable would
L shadow the outer variable).
S Example: PreferredName: e (default)
L   # bad
L   begin
L     # do something
L   rescue MyException => exception
L     # do something
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   begin
L     # do something
L   rescue MyException => e
L     # do something
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   begin
L     # do something
L   rescue MyException => _e
L     # do something
L   end
S Example: PreferredName: exception
L   # bad
L   begin
L     # do something
L   rescue MyException => e
L     # do something
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   begin
L     # do something
L   rescue MyException => exception
L     # do something
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   begin
L     # do something
L   rescue MyException => _exception
L     # do something
L   end
C Naming/VariableName
S Details
L Checks that the configured style (snake_case or camelCase) is used for all variable names.
L This includes local variables, instance variables, class variables, method arguments
L (positional, keyword, rest or block), and block arguments.
L 
L The cop can also be configured to forbid using specific names for variables, using
L `ForbiddenIdentifiers` or `ForbiddenPatterns`. In addition to the above, this applies
L to global variables as well.
L 
L Method definitions and method calls are not affected by this cop.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: snake_case (default)
L   # bad
L   fooBar = 1
L 
L   # good
L   foo_bar = 1
S Example: EnforcedStyle: camelCase
L   # bad
L   foo_bar = 1
L 
L   # good
L   fooBar = 1
S Example: AllowedIdentifiers: ['fooBar']
L   # good (with EnforcedStyle: snake_case)
L   fooBar = 1
S Example: AllowedPatterns: ['_v\d+\z']
L   # good (with EnforcedStyle: camelCase)
L   release_v1 = true
S Example: ForbiddenIdentifiers: ['fooBar']
L   # bad (in all cases)
L   fooBar = 1
L   @fooBar = 1
L   @@fooBar = 1
L   $fooBar = 1
S Example: ForbiddenPatterns: ['_v\d+\z']
L   # bad (in all cases)
L   release_v1 = true
L   @release_v1 = true
L   @@release_v1 = true
L   $release_v1 = true
C Naming/VariableNumber
S Details
L Makes sure that all numbered variables use the
L configured style, snake_case, normalcase, or non_integer,
L for their numbering.
L 
L Additionally, `CheckMethodNames` and `CheckSymbols` configuration options
L can be used to specify whether method names and symbols should be checked.
L Both are enabled by default.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: normalcase (default)
L   # bad
L   :some_sym_1
L   variable_1 = 1
L 
L   def some_method_1; end
L 
L   def some_method1(arg_1); end
L 
L   # good
L   :some_sym1
L   variable1 = 1
L 
L   def some_method1; end
L 
L   def some_method1(arg1); end
S Example: EnforcedStyle: snake_case
L   # bad
L   :some_sym1
L   variable1 = 1
L 
L   def some_method1; end
L 
L   def some_method_1(arg1); end
L 
L   # good
L   :some_sym_1
L   variable_1 = 1
L 
L   def some_method_1; end
L 
L   def some_method_1(arg_1); end
S Example: EnforcedStyle: non_integer
L   # bad
L   :some_sym1
L   :some_sym_1
L 
L   variable1 = 1
L   variable_1 = 1
L 
L   def some_method1; end
L 
L   def some_method_1; end
L 
L   def some_methodone(arg1); end
L   def some_methodone(arg_1); end
L 
L   # good
L   :some_symone
L   :some_sym_one
L 
L   variableone = 1
L   variable_one = 1
L 
L   def some_methodone; end
L 
L   def some_method_one; end
L 
L   def some_methodone(argone); end
L   def some_methodone(arg_one); end
L 
L   # In the following examples, we assume `EnforcedStyle: normalcase` (default).
S Example: CheckMethodNames: true (default)
L   # bad
L   def some_method_1; end
S Example: CheckMethodNames: false
L   # good
L   def some_method_1; end
S Example: CheckSymbols: true (default)
L   # bad
L   :some_sym_1
S Example: CheckSymbols: false
L   # good
L   :some_sym_1
S Example: AllowedIdentifiers: [capture3]
L   # good
L   expect(Open3).to receive(:capture3)
S Example: AllowedPatterns: ['_v\d+\z']
L   # good
L   :some_sym_v1
C Performance/AncestorsInclude
S Details
L Identifies usages of `ancestors.include?` and change them to use `<=` instead.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because it can't tell whether the receiver is a class or an object.
L   e.g. the false positive was for `Nokogiri::XML::Node#ancestors`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   A.ancestors.include?(B)
L 
L   # good
L   A <= B
C Performance/ArraySemiInfiniteRangeSlice
S Details
L Identifies places where slicing arrays with semi-infinite ranges
L can be replaced by `Array#take` and `Array#drop`.
L This cop was created due to a mistake in microbenchmark and hence is disabled by default.
L Refer https://github.com/rubocop/rubocop-performance/pull/175#issuecomment-731892717
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe for string slices because strings do not have `#take` and `#drop` methods.
S Example
L   # bad
L   array[..2]
L   array[...2]
L   array[2..]
L   array[2...]
L   array.slice(..2)
L 
L   # good
L   array.take(3)
L   array.take(2)
L   array.drop(2)
L   array.drop(2)
L   array.take(3)
C Performance/BigDecimalWithNumericArgument
S Details
L Identifies places where a float argument to BigDecimal should be converted to a string.
L Initializing from String is faster than from Float for BigDecimal.
L 
L Also identifies places where an integer string argument to BigDecimal should be converted to
L an integer. Initializing from Integer is faster than from String for BigDecimal.
L 
L NOTE: This cop is disabled by default because the performance of initializing with a String
L and Number differ between versions. Additionally, performance depends on the size of the Number,
L and if it is an Integer or a Float. Since this is very specific to `bigdecimal` internals,
L suggestions from this cop are not unlikely to result in code that performs worse than before.
S Example
L   # bad
L   BigDecimal(1.2, 3, exception: true)
L   4.5.to_d(6, exception: true)
L 
L   # good
L   BigDecimal('1.2', 3, exception: true)
L   BigDecimal('4.5', 6, exception: true)
L 
L   # bad
L   BigDecimal('1', 2)
L   BigDecimal('4', 6)
L 
L   # good
L   BigDecimal(1, 2)
L   4.to_d(6)
C Performance/BindCall
S Details
L In Ruby 2.7, `UnboundMethod#bind_call` has been added.
L 
L This cop identifies places where `bind(obj).call(args, ...)`
L can be replaced by `bind_call(obj, args, ...)`.
L 
L The `bind_call(obj, args, ...)` method is faster than
L `bind(obj).call(args, ...)`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   umethod.bind(obj).call(foo, bar)
L   umethod.bind(obj).(foo, bar)
L 
L   # good
L   umethod.bind_call(obj, foo, bar)
C Performance/BlockGivenWithExplicitBlock
S Details
L Identifies unnecessary use of a `block_given?` where explicit check
L of block argument would suffice.
L 
L NOTE: This cop produces code with significantly worse performance when a
L block is being passed to the method and as such should not be enabled.
S Example
L   # bad
L   def method(&block)
L     do_something if block_given?
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def method(&block)
L     do_something if block
L   end
L 
L   # good - block is reassigned
L   def method(&block)
L     block ||= -> { do_something }
L     warn "Using default ..." unless block_given?
L     # ...
L   end
C Performance/Caller
S Details
L Identifies places where `caller[n]` can be replaced by `caller(n..n).first`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   caller[1]
L   caller.first
L   caller_locations[1]
L   caller_locations.first
L 
L   # good
L   caller(2..2).first
L   caller(1..1).first
L   caller_locations(2..2).first
L   caller_locations(1..1).first
C Performance/CaseWhenSplat
S Details
L Reordering `when` conditions with a splat to the end
L of the `when` branches can improve performance.
L 
L Ruby has to allocate memory for the splat expansion every time
L that the `case` `when` statement is run. Since Ruby does not support
L fall through inside of `case` `when`, like some other languages do,
L the order of the `when` branches should not matter. By placing any
L splat expansions at the end of the list of `when` branches we will
L reduce the number of times that memory has to be allocated for
L the expansion. The exception to this is if multiple of your `when`
L conditions can be true for any given condition. A likely scenario for
L this defining a higher level when condition to override a condition
L that is inside of the splat expansion.
S Safety
L   This cop is not unsafe autocorrection because it is not a guaranteed
L   performance improvement. If the data being processed by the `case` condition is
L   normalized in a manner that favors hitting a condition in the splat expansion,
L   it is possible that moving the splat condition to the end will use more memory,
L   and run slightly slower.
L   See for more details: https://github.com/rubocop/rubocop/pull/6163
S Example
L   # bad
L   case foo
L   when *condition
L     bar
L   when baz
L     foobar
L   end
L 
L   case foo
L   when *[1, 2, 3, 4]
L     bar
L   when 5
L     baz
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   case foo
L   when baz
L     foobar
L   when *condition
L     bar
L   end
L 
L   case foo
L   when 1, 2, 3, 4
L     bar
L   when 5
L     baz
L   end
C Performance/Casecmp
S Details
L Identifies places where a case-insensitive string comparison
L can better be implemented using `casecmp`.
L 
L This cop is disabled by default because `String#casecmp` only works with
L ASCII characters. See https://github.com/rubocop/rubocop/issues/9753.
L 
L If you are working only with ASCII characters, then this cop can be
L safely enabled.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because `String#casecmp` and `String#casecmp?` behave
L   differently when using Non-ASCII characters.
S Example
L   # bad
L   str.downcase == 'abc'
L   str.upcase.eql? 'ABC'
L   'abc' == str.downcase
L   'ABC'.eql? str.upcase
L   str.downcase == str.downcase
L 
L   # good
L   str.casecmp('ABC').zero?
L   'abc'.casecmp(str).zero?
C Performance/ChainArrayAllocation
S Details
L Identifies usages of `array.compact.flatten.map { |x| x.downcase }`.
L Each of these methods (`compact`, `flatten`, `map`) will generate a new intermediate array
L that is promptly thrown away. Instead it is faster to mutate when we know it's safe.
S Example
L   # bad
L   array = ["a", "b", "c"]
L   array.compact.flatten.map { |x| x.downcase }
L 
L   # good
L   array = ["a", "b", "c"]
L   array.compact!
L   array.flatten!
L   array.map! { |x| x.downcase }
L   array
C Performance/CollectionLiteralInLoop
S Details
L Identifies places where Array and Hash literals are used within loops.
L It is better to extract them into a local variable or constant
L to avoid unnecessary allocations on each iteration.
L 
L You can set the minimum number of elements to consider
L an offense with `MinSize`.
L 
L NOTE: Since Ruby 3.4, certain simple arguments to `Array#include?` are
L optimized directly in Ruby. This avoids allocations without changing the
L code, as such no offense will be registered in those cases. Currently that
L includes: strings, `self`, local variables, instance variables, and method
L calls without arguments. Additionally, any number of methods can be chained:
L `[1, 2, 3].include?(@foo)` and `[1, 2, 3].include?(@foo.bar.baz)` both avoid
L the array allocation.
S Example
L   # bad
L   users.select do |user|
L     %i[superadmin admin].include?(user.role)
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   admin_roles = %i[superadmin admin]
L   users.select do |user|
L     admin_roles.include?(user.role)
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   ADMIN_ROLES = %i[superadmin admin]
L   ...
L   users.select do |user|
L     ADMIN_ROLES.include?(user.role)
L   end
C Performance/CompareWithBlock
S Details
L Identifies places where `sort { |a, b| a.foo <=> b.foo }`
L can be replaced by `sort_by(&:foo)`.
L This cop also checks `sort!`, `min`, `max` and `minmax` methods.
S Example
L   # bad
L   array.sort   { |a, b| a.foo <=> b.foo }
L   array.sort!  { |a, b| a.foo <=> b.foo }
L   array.max    { |a, b| a.foo <=> b.foo }
L   array.min    { |a, b| a.foo <=> b.foo }
L   array.minmax { |a, b| a.foo <=> b.foo }
L   array.sort   { |a, b| a[:foo] <=> b[:foo] }
L 
L   # good
L   array.sort_by(&:foo)
L   array.sort_by!(&:foo)
L   array.sort_by { |v| v.foo }
L   array.sort_by do |var|
L     var.foo
L   end
L   array.max_by(&:foo)
L   array.min_by(&:foo)
L   array.minmax_by(&:foo)
L   array.sort_by { |a| a[:foo] }
C Performance/ConcurrentMonotonicTime
S Details
L Identifies places where `Concurrent.monotonic_time`
L can be replaced by `Process.clock_gettime(Process::CLOCK_MONOTONIC)`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   Concurrent.monotonic_time
L 
L   # good
L   Process.clock_gettime(Process::CLOCK_MONOTONIC)
C Performance/ConstantRegexp
S Details
L Finds regular expressions with dynamic components that are all constants.
L 
L Ruby allocates a new Regexp object every time it executes a code containing such
L a regular expression. It is more efficient to extract it into a constant,
L memoize it, or add an `/o` option to perform `#{}` interpolation only once and
L reuse that Regexp object.
S Example
L   # bad
L   def tokens(pattern)
L     pattern.scan(TOKEN).reject { |token| token.match?(/\A#{SEPARATORS}\Z/) }
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   ALL_SEPARATORS = /\A#{SEPARATORS}\Z/
L   def tokens(pattern)
L     pattern.scan(TOKEN).reject { |token| token.match?(ALL_SEPARATORS) }
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def tokens(pattern)
L     pattern.scan(TOKEN).reject { |token| token.match?(/\A#{SEPARATORS}\Z/o) }
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def separators
L     @separators ||= /\A#{SEPARATORS}\Z/
L   end
C Performance/Count
S Details
L Identifies usages of `count` on an `Enumerable` that
L follow calls to `select`, `find_all`, `filter` or `reject`. Querying logic can instead be
L passed to the `count` call.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because it has known compatibility issues with `ActiveRecord` and other
L   frameworks. Before Rails 5.1, `ActiveRecord` will ignore the block that is passed to `count`.
L   Other methods, such as `select`, will convert the association to an
L   array and then run the block on the array. A simple work around to
L   make `count` work with a block is to call `to_a.count {...}`.
L 
L   For example:
L 
L   [source,ruby]
L   ----
L   Model.where(id: [1, 2, 3]).select { |m| m.method == true }.size
L   ----
L 
L   becomes:
L 
L   [source,ruby]
L   ----
L   Model.where(id: [1, 2, 3]).to_a.count { |m| m.method == true }
L   ----
S Example
L   # bad
L   [1, 2, 3].select { |e| e > 2 }.size
L   [1, 2, 3].reject { |e| e > 2 }.size
L   [1, 2, 3].select { |e| e > 2 }.length
L   [1, 2, 3].reject { |e| e > 2 }.length
L   [1, 2, 3].select { |e| e > 2 }.count { |e| e.odd? }
L   [1, 2, 3].reject { |e| e > 2 }.count { |e| e.even? }
L   array.select(&:value).count
L 
L   # good
L   [1, 2, 3].count { |e| e > 2 }
L   [1, 2, 3].count { |e| e < 2 }
L   [1, 2, 3].count { |e| e > 2 && e.odd? }
L   [1, 2, 3].count { |e| e < 2 && e.even? }
L   Model.select('field AS field_one').count
L   Model.select(:value).count
C Performance/DeletePrefix
S Details
L In Ruby 2.5, `String#delete_prefix` has been added.
L 
L This cop identifies places where `gsub(/\Aprefix/, '')` and `sub(/\Aprefix/, '')`
L can be replaced by `delete_prefix('prefix')`.
L 
L This cop has `SafeMultiline` configuration option that `true` by default because
L `^prefix` is unsafe as it will behave incompatible with `delete_prefix`
L for receiver is multiline string.
L 
L The `delete_prefix('prefix')` method is faster than `gsub(/\Aprefix/, '')`.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because `Pathname` has `sub` but not `delete_prefix`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   str.gsub(/\Aprefix/, '')
L   str.gsub!(/\Aprefix/, '')
L 
L   str.sub(/\Aprefix/, '')
L   str.sub!(/\Aprefix/, '')
L 
L   # good
L   str.delete_prefix('prefix')
L   str.delete_prefix!('prefix')
S Example: SafeMultiline: true (default)
L   # good
L   str.gsub(/^prefix/, '')
L   str.gsub!(/^prefix/, '')
L   str.sub(/^prefix/, '')
L   str.sub!(/^prefix/, '')
S Example: SafeMultiline: false
L   # bad
L   str.gsub(/^prefix/, '')
L   str.gsub!(/^prefix/, '')
L   str.sub(/^prefix/, '')
L   str.sub!(/^prefix/, '')
C Performance/DeleteSuffix
S Details
L In Ruby 2.5, `String#delete_suffix` has been added.
L 
L This cop identifies places where `gsub(/suffix\z/, '')` and `sub(/suffix\z/, '')`
L can be replaced by `delete_suffix('suffix')`.
L 
L This cop has `SafeMultiline` configuration option that `true` by default because
L `suffix$` is unsafe as it will behave incompatible with `delete_suffix?`
L for receiver is multiline string.
L 
L The `delete_suffix('suffix')` method is faster than `gsub(/suffix\z/, '')`.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because `Pathname` has `sub` but not `delete_suffix`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   str.gsub(/suffix\z/, '')
L   str.gsub!(/suffix\z/, '')
L 
L   str.sub(/suffix\z/, '')
L   str.sub!(/suffix\z/, '')
L 
L   # good
L   str.delete_suffix('suffix')
L   str.delete_suffix!('suffix')
S Example: SafeMultiline: true (default)
L   # good
L   str.gsub(/suffix$/, '')
L   str.gsub!(/suffix$/, '')
L   str.sub(/suffix$/, '')
L   str.sub!(/suffix$/, '')
S Example: SafeMultiline: false
L   # bad
L   str.gsub(/suffix$/, '')
L   str.gsub!(/suffix$/, '')
L   str.sub(/suffix$/, '')
L   str.sub!(/suffix$/, '')
C Performance/Detect
S Details
L Identifies usages of `first`, `last`, `[0]` or `[-1]`
L chained to `select`, `find_all` or `filter` and change them to use
L `detect` instead.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because it assumes that the receiver is an
L   `Array` or equivalent, but can't reliably detect it. For example,
L   if the receiver is a `Hash`, it may report a false positive.
S Example
L   # bad
L   [].select { |item| true }.first
L   [].select { |item| true }.last
L   [].find_all { |item| true }.first
L   [].find_all { |item| true }.last
L   [].filter { |item| true }.first
L   [].filter { |item| true }.last
L   [].filter { |item| true }[0]
L   [].filter { |item| true }[-1]
L 
L   # good
L   [].detect { |item| true }
L   [].reverse.detect { |item| true }
C Performance/DoubleStartEndWith
S Details
L Checks for consecutive `#start_with?` or `#end_with?` calls.
L These methods accept multiple arguments, so in some cases like when
L they are separated by `||`, they can be combined into a single method call.
L 
L `IncludeActiveSupportAliases` configuration option is used to check for
L `starts_with?` and `ends_with?`. These methods are defined by Active Support.
S Example
L   # bad
L   str.start_with?("a") || str.start_with?(Some::CONST)
L   str.start_with?("a", "b") || str.start_with?("c")
L   !str.start_with?(foo) && !str.start_with?(bar)
L   str.end_with?(var1) || str.end_with?(var2)
L 
L   # good
L   str.start_with?("a", Some::CONST)
L   str.start_with?("a", "b", "c")
L   !str.start_with?(foo, bar)
L   str.end_with?(var1, var2)
S Example: IncludeActiveSupportAliases: false (default)
L   # good
L   str.starts_with?("a", "b") || str.starts_with?("c")
L   str.ends_with?(var1) || str.ends_with?(var2)
L 
L   str.starts_with?("a", "b", "c")
L   str.ends_with?(var1, var2)
S Example: IncludeActiveSupportAliases: true
L   # bad
L   str.starts_with?("a", "b") || str.starts_with?("c")
L   str.ends_with?(var1) || str.ends_with?(var2)
L 
L   # good
L   str.starts_with?("a", "b", "c")
L   str.ends_with?(var1, var2)
C Performance/EndWith
S Details
L Identifies unnecessary use of a regex where `String#end_with?` would suffice.
L 
L This cop has `SafeMultiline` configuration option that `true` by default because
L `end$` is unsafe as it will behave incompatible with `end_with?`
L for receiver is multiline string.
S Safety
L   This will change to a new method call which isn't guaranteed to be on the
L   object. Switching these methods has to be done with knowledge of the types
L   of the variables which rubocop doesn't have.
S Example
L   # bad
L   'abc'.match?(/bc\Z/)
L   /bc\Z/.match?('abc')
L   'abc' =~ /bc\Z/
L   /bc\Z/ =~ 'abc'
L   'abc'.match(/bc\Z/)
L   /bc\Z/.match('abc')
L 
L   # good
L   'abc'.end_with?('bc')
S Example: SafeMultiline: true (default)
L   # good
L   'abc'.match?(/bc$/)
L   /bc$/.match?('abc')
L   'abc' =~ /bc$/
L   /bc$/ =~ 'abc'
L   'abc'.match(/bc$/)
L   /bc$/.match('abc')
S Example: SafeMultiline: false
L   # bad
L   'abc'.match?(/bc$/)
L   /bc$/.match?('abc')
L   'abc' =~ /bc$/
L   /bc$/ =~ 'abc'
L   'abc'.match(/bc$/)
L   /bc$/.match('abc')
C Performance/FixedSize
S Details
L Do not compute the size of statically sized objects.
S Example
L   # String methods
L   # bad
L   'foo'.size
L   %q[bar].count
L   %(qux).length
L 
L   # Symbol methods
L   # bad
L   :fred.size
L   :'baz'.length
L 
L   # Array methods
L   # bad
L   [1, 2, thud].count
L   %W(1, 2, bar).size
L 
L   # Hash methods
L   # bad
L   { a: corge, b: grault }.length
L 
L   # good
L   foo.size
L   bar.count
L   qux.length
L 
L   # good
L   :"#{fred}".size
L   CONST = :baz.length
L 
L   # good
L   [1, 2, *thud].count
L   garply = [1, 2, 3]
L   garply.size
L 
L   # good
L   { a: corge, **grault }.length
L   waldo = { a: corge, b: grault }
L   waldo.size
C Performance/FlatMap
S Details
L Identifies usages of `map { ... }.flatten` and
L change them to use `flat_map { ... }` instead.
S Example
L   # bad
L   [1, 2, 3, 4].map { |e| [e, e] }.flatten(1)
L   [1, 2, 3, 4].collect { |e| [e, e] }.flatten(1)
L 
L   # good
L   [1, 2, 3, 4].flat_map { |e| [e, e] }
L   [1, 2, 3, 4].map { |e| [e, e] }.flatten
L   [1, 2, 3, 4].collect { |e| [e, e] }.flatten
C Performance/InefficientHashSearch
S Details
L Checks for inefficient searching of keys and values within
L hashes.
L 
L `Hash#keys.include?` is less efficient than `Hash#key?` because
L the former allocates a new array and then performs an O(n) search
L through that array, while `Hash#key?` does not allocate any array and
L performs a faster O(1) search for the key.
L 
L `Hash#values.include?` is less efficient than `Hash#value?`. While they
L both perform an O(n) search through all of the values, calling `values`
L allocates a new array while using `value?` does not.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because it can't tell whether the receiver is a hash object.
S Example
L   # bad
L   { a: 1, b: 2 }.keys.include?(:a)
L   { a: 1, b: 2 }.keys.include?(:z)
L   h = { a: 1, b: 2 }; h.keys.include?(100)
L 
L   # good
L   { a: 1, b: 2 }.key?(:a)
L   { a: 1, b: 2 }.has_key?(:z)
L   h = { a: 1, b: 2 }; h.key?(100)
L 
L   # bad
L   { a: 1, b: 2 }.values.include?(2)
L   { a: 1, b: 2 }.values.include?('garbage')
L   h = { a: 1, b: 2 }; h.values.include?(nil)
L 
L   # good
L   { a: 1, b: 2 }.value?(2)
L   { a: 1, b: 2 }.has_value?('garbage')
L   h = { a: 1, b: 2 }; h.value?(nil)
C Performance/IoReadlines
S Details
L Identifies places where inefficient `readlines` method
L can be replaced by `each_line` to avoid fully loading file content into memory.
S Example
L   # bad
L   File.readlines('testfile').each { |l| puts l }
L   IO.readlines('testfile', chomp: true).each { |l| puts l }
L 
L   conn.readlines(10).map { |l| l.size }
L   file.readlines.find { |l| l.start_with?('#') }
L   file.readlines.each { |l| puts l }
L 
L   # good
L   File.open('testfile', 'r').each_line { |l| puts l }
L   IO.open('testfile').each_line(chomp: true) { |l| puts l }
L 
L   conn.each_line(10).map { |l| l.size }
L   file.each_line.find { |l| l.start_with?('#') }
L   file.each_line { |l| puts l }
C Performance/MapCompact
S Details
L In Ruby 2.7, `Enumerable#filter_map` has been added.
L 
L This cop identifies places where `map { ... }.compact` can be replaced by `filter_map`.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because `map { ... }.compact` might yield
L   different results than `filter_map`. As illustrated in the example, `filter_map`
L   also filters out falsey values, while `compact` only gets rid of `nil`.
L 
L [source,ruby]
L ----
L [true, false, nil].compact              #=> [true, false]
L [true, false, nil].filter_map(&:itself) #=> [true]
L ----
S Example
L   # bad
L   ary.map(&:foo).compact
L   ary.collect(&:foo).compact
L 
L   # good
L   ary.filter_map(&:foo)
L   ary.map(&:foo).compact!
L   ary.compact.map(&:foo)
C Performance/MapMethodChain
S Details
L Checks if the map method is used in a chain.
L 
L Autocorrection is not supported because an appropriate block variable name cannot be determined automatically.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because false positives occur if the number of times the first method is executed
L   affects the return value of subsequent methods.
L 
L [source,ruby]
L ----
L class X
L   def initialize
L     @@num = 0
L   end
L 
L   def foo
L     @@num += 1
L     self
L   end
L 
L   def bar
L     @@num * 2
L   end
L end
L 
L [X.new, X.new].map(&:foo).map(&:bar) # => [4, 4]
L [X.new, X.new].map { |x| x.foo.bar } # => [2, 4]
L ----
S Example
L   # bad
L   array.map(&:foo).map(&:bar)
L 
L   # good
L   array.map { |item| item.foo.bar }
C Performance/MethodObjectAsBlock
S Details
L Identifies places where methods are converted to blocks, with the
L use of `&method`, and passed as arguments to method calls.
L It is faster to replace those with explicit blocks, calling those methods inside.
S Example
L   # bad
L   array.map(&method(:do_something))
L   [1, 2, 3].each(&out.method(:puts))
L 
L   # good
L   array.map { |x| do_something(x) }
L   [1, 2, 3].each { |x| out.puts(x) }
C Performance/OpenStruct
S Details
L Checks for `OpenStruct.new` calls.
L Instantiation of an `OpenStruct` invalidates
L Ruby global method cache as it causes dynamic method
L definition during program runtime.
L This could have an effect on performance,
L especially in case of single-threaded
L applications with multiple `OpenStruct` instantiations.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because `OpenStruct.new` and `Struct.new`
L   are not equivalent.
S Example
L   # bad
L   class MyClass
L     def my_method
L       OpenStruct.new(my_key1: 'my_value1', my_key2: 'my_value2')
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class MyClass
L     MyStruct = Struct.new(:my_key1, :my_key2)
L     def my_method
L       MyStruct.new('my_value1', 'my_value2')
L     end
L   end
C Performance/RangeInclude
S Details
L Identifies uses of `Range#include?` and `Range#member?`, which iterates over each
L item in a `Range` to see if a specified item is there. In contrast,
L `Range#cover?` simply compares the target item with the beginning and
L end points of the `Range`. In a great majority of cases, this is what
L is wanted.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because `Range#include?` (or `Range#member?`) and `Range#cover?`
L   are not equivalent behavior.
L   Example of a case where `Range#cover?` may not provide the desired result:
L 
L   [source,ruby]
L   ----
L   ('a'..'z').cover?('yellow') # => true
L   ----
S Example
L   # bad
L   ('a'..'z').include?('b') # => true
L   ('a'..'z').member?('b')  # => true
L 
L   # good
L   ('a'..'z').cover?('b') # => true
C Performance/RedundantBlockCall
S Details
L Identifies the use of a `&block` parameter and `block.call`
L where `yield` would do just as well.
S Example
L   # bad
L   def method(&block)
L     block.call
L   end
L   def another(&func)
L     func.call 1, 2, 3
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def method
L     yield
L   end
L   def another
L     yield 1, 2, 3
L   end
C Performance/RedundantEqualityComparisonBlock
S Details
L Checks for uses `Enumerable#all?`, `Enumerable#any?`, `Enumerable#one?`,
L and `Enumerable#none?` are compared with `===` or similar methods in block.
L 
L By default, `Object#===` behaves the same as `Object#==`, but this
L behavior is appropriately overridden in subclass. For example,
L `Range#===` returns `true` when argument is within the range.
L 
L This cop has `AllowRegexpMatch` option and it is true by default because
L `regexp.match?('string')` often used in block changes to the opposite result:
L 
L [source,ruby]
L ----
L [/pattern/].all? { |regexp| regexp.match?('pattern') } # => true
L [/pattern/].all? { |regexp| regexp =~ 'pattern' }      # => true
L [/pattern/].all?('pattern')                            # => false
L ----
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because `===` and `==` do not always behave the same.
S Example
L   # bad
L   items.all? { |item| pattern === item }
L   items.all? { |item| item == other }
L   items.all? { |item| item.is_a?(Klass) }
L   items.all? { |item| item.kind_of?(Klass) }
L 
L   # good
L   items.all?(pattern)
L   items.all?(Klass)
S Example: AllowRegexpMatch: true (default)
L   # good
L   items.all? { |item| item =~ pattern }
L   items.all? { |item| item.match?(pattern) }
S Example: AllowRegexpMatch: false
L   # bad
L   items.all? { |item| item =~ pattern }
L   items.all? { |item| item.match?(pattern) }
C Performance/RedundantMatch
S Details
L Identifies the use of `Regexp#match` or `String#match`, which
L returns `#<MatchData>`/`nil`. The return value of `=~` is an integral
L index/`nil` and is more performant.
S Example
L   # bad
L   do_something if str.match(/regex/)
L   while regex.match('str')
L     do_something
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   method(str =~ /regex/)
L   return value unless regex =~ 'str'
C Performance/RedundantMerge
S Details
L Identifies places where `Hash#merge!` can be replaced by `Hash#[]=`.
L You can set the maximum number of key-value pairs to consider
L an offense with `MaxKeyValuePairs`.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because RuboCop cannot determine if the
L   receiver of `merge!` is actually a hash or not.
S Example
L   # bad
L   hash.merge!(a: 1)
L   hash.merge!({'key' => 'value'})
L 
L   # good
L   hash[:a] = 1
L   hash['key'] = 'value'
S Example: MaxKeyValuePairs: 2 (default)
L   # bad
L   hash.merge!(a: 1, b: 2)
L 
L   # good
L   hash[:a] = 1
L   hash[:b] = 2
C Performance/RedundantSortBlock
S Details
L Identifies places where `sort { |a, b| a <=> b }` can be replaced with `sort`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   array.sort { |a, b| a <=> b }
L 
L   # good
L   array.sort
C Performance/RedundantSplitRegexpArgument
S Details
L Identifies places where `split` argument can be replaced from
L a deterministic regexp to a string.
S Example
L   # bad
L   'a,b,c'.split(/,/)
L 
L   # good
L   'a,b,c'.split(',')
C Performance/RedundantStringChars
S Details
L Checks for redundant `String#chars`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   str.chars[0..2]
L   str.chars.slice(0..2)
L   str.chars.last
L 
L   # good
L   str[0..2].chars
L 
L   # bad
L   str.chars.first
L   str.chars.first(2)
L 
L   # good
L   str[0]
L   str[0...2].chars
L   str[-1]
L 
L   # bad
L   str.chars.take(2)
L   str.chars.length
L   str.chars.size
L   str.chars.empty?
L 
L   # good
L   str[0...2].chars
L   str.length
L   str.size
L   str.empty?
L 
L   # For example, if the receiver is an empty string, it will be incompatible.
L   # If a negative value is specified for the receiver, `nil` is returned.
L   str.chars.last(2) # Incompatible with `str[-2..-1].chars`.
L   str.chars.drop(2) # Incompatible with `str[2..-1].chars`.
C Performance/RegexpMatch
S Details
L In Ruby 2.4, `String#match?`, `Regexp#match?`, and `Symbol#match?`
L have been added. The methods are faster than `match`.
L Because the methods avoid creating a `MatchData` object or saving
L backref.
L So, when `MatchData` is not used, use `match?` instead of `match`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   def foo
L     if x =~ /re/
L       do_something
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   def foo
L     if x !~ /re/
L       do_something
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   def foo
L     if x.match(/re/)
L       do_something
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   def foo
L     if /re/ === x
L       do_something
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo
L     if x.match?(/re/)
L       do_something
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo
L     if !x.match?(/re/)
L       do_something
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo
L     if x =~ /re/
L       do_something(Regexp.last_match)
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo
L     if x.match(/re/)
L       do_something($~)
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo
L     if /re/ === x
L       do_something($~)
L     end
L   end
C Performance/ReverseEach
S Details
L Identifies usages of `reverse.each` and change them to use `reverse_each` instead.
L 
L If the return value is used, it will not be detected because the result will be different.
L 
L [source,ruby]
L ----
L [1, 2, 3].reverse.each {} #=> [3, 2, 1]
L [1, 2, 3].reverse_each {} #=> [1, 2, 3]
L ----
S Example
L   # bad
L   items.reverse.each
L 
L   # good
L   items.reverse_each
C Performance/ReverseFirst
S Details
L Identifies places where `reverse.first(n)` and `reverse.first`
L can be replaced by `last(n).reverse` and `last`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   array.reverse.first(5)
L   array.reverse.first
L 
L   # good
L   array.last(5).reverse
L   array.last
C Performance/SelectMap
S Details
L In Ruby 2.7, `Enumerable#filter_map` has been added.
L 
L This cop identifies places where `select.map` can be replaced by `filter_map`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   ary.select(&:foo).map(&:bar)
L   ary.filter(&:foo).map(&:bar)
L 
L   # good
L   ary.filter_map { |o| o.bar if o.foo }
C Performance/Size
S Details
L Identifies usages of `count` on an `Array` and `Hash` and change them to `size`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   [1, 2, 3].count
L   (1..3).to_a.count
L   Array[*1..3].count
L   Array(1..3).count
L 
L   # bad
L   {a: 1, b: 2, c: 3}.count
L   [[:foo, :bar], [1, 2]].to_h.count
L   Hash[*('a'..'z')].count
L   Hash(key: :value).count
L 
L   # good
L   [1, 2, 3].size
L   (1..3).to_a.size
L   Array[*1..3].size
L   Array(1..3).size
L 
L   # good
L   {a: 1, b: 2, c: 3}.size
L   [[:foo, :bar], [1, 2]].to_h.size
L   Hash[*('a'..'z')].size
L   Hash(key: :value).size
L 
L   # good
L   [1, 2, 3].count { |e| e > 2 }
L TODO: Add advanced detection of variables that could
L have been assigned to an array or a hash.
C Performance/SortReverse
S Details
L Identifies places where `sort { |a, b| b <=> a }`
L can be replaced by a faster `sort.reverse`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   array.sort { |a, b| b <=> a }
L 
L   # good
L   array.sort.reverse
C Performance/Squeeze
S Details
L Identifies places where `gsub(/a+/, 'a')` and `gsub!(/a+/, 'a')`
L can be replaced by `squeeze('a')` and `squeeze!('a')`.
L 
L The `squeeze('a')` method is faster than `gsub(/a+/, 'a')`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   str.gsub(/a+/, 'a')
L   str.gsub!(/a+/, 'a')
L 
L   # good
L   str.squeeze('a')
L   str.squeeze!('a')
C Performance/StartWith
S Details
L Identifies unnecessary use of a regex where `String#start_with?` would suffice.
L 
L This cop has `SafeMultiline` configuration option that `true` by default because
L `^start` is unsafe as it will behave incompatible with `start_with?`
L for receiver is multiline string.
S Safety
L   This will change to a new method call which isn't guaranteed to be on the
L   object. Switching these methods has to be done with knowledge of the types
L   of the variables which rubocop doesn't have.
S Example
L   # bad
L   'abc'.match?(/\Aab/)
L   /\Aab/.match?('abc')
L   'abc' =~ /\Aab/
L   /\Aab/ =~ 'abc'
L   'abc'.match(/\Aab/)
L   /\Aab/.match('abc')
L 
L   # good
L   'abc'.start_with?('ab')
S Example: SafeMultiline: true (default)
L   # good
L   'abc'.match?(/^ab/)
L   /^ab/.match?('abc')
L   'abc' =~ /^ab/
L   /^ab/ =~ 'abc'
L   'abc'.match(/^ab/)
L   /^ab/.match('abc')
S Example: SafeMultiline: false
L   # bad
L   'abc'.match?(/^ab/)
L   /^ab/.match?('abc')
L   'abc' =~ /^ab/
L   /^ab/ =~ 'abc'
L   'abc'.match(/^ab/)
L   /^ab/.match('abc')
C Performance/StringBytesize
S Details
L Checks for calls to `#bytes` counting method and suggests using `bytesize` instead.
L The `bytesize` method is more efficient and directly returns the size in bytes,
L avoiding the intermediate array allocation that `bytes.size` incurs.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because it assumes that the receiver
L   responds to `#bytesize` method.
S Example
L   # bad
L   string_var.bytes.count
L   "foobar".bytes.size
L 
L   # good
L   string_var.bytesize
L   "foobar".bytesize
C Performance/StringIdentifierArgument
S Details
L Identifies places where string identifier argument can be replaced
L by symbol identifier argument.
L It prevents the redundancy of the internal string-to-symbol conversion.
L 
L This cop targets methods that take identifier (e.g. method name) argument
L and the following examples are parts of it.
S Example
L   # bad
L   send('do_something')
L   attr_accessor 'do_something'
L   instance_variable_get('@ivar')
L 
L   # good
L   send(:do_something)
L   attr_accessor :do_something
L   instance_variable_get(:@ivar)
L 
L   # good - these methods don't support namespaced symbols
L   const_get("#{module_path}::Base")
L   const_source_location("#{module_path}::Base")
L   const_defined?("#{module_path}::Base")
L 
L   # good - using a symbol when string interpolation is involved causes a performance regression.
L   respond_to?("string_#{interpolation}")
C Performance/StringInclude
S Details
L Identifies unnecessary use of a regex where `String#include?` would suffice.
S Safety
L   This cop's offenses are not safe to autocorrect if a receiver is nil or a Symbol.
S Example
L   # bad
L   str.match?(/ab/)
L   /ab/.match?(str)
L   str =~ /ab/
L   /ab/ =~ str
L   str.match(/ab/)
L   /ab/.match(str)
L   /ab/ === str
L 
L   # good
L   str.include?('ab')
C Performance/StringReplacement
S Details
L Identifies places where `gsub` can be replaced by `tr` or `delete`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   'abc'.gsub('b', 'd')
L   'abc'.gsub('a', '')
L   'abc'.gsub(/a/, 'd')
L   'abc'.gsub!('a', 'd')
L 
L   # good
L   'abc'.gsub(/.*/, 'a')
L   'abc'.gsub(/a+/, 'd')
L   'abc'.tr('b', 'd')
L   'a b c'.delete(' ')
C Performance/Sum
S Details
L Identifies places where custom code finding the sum of elements
L in some Enumerable object can be replaced by `Enumerable#sum` method.
S Safety
L   Autocorrections are unproblematic wherever an initial value is provided explicitly:
L 
L   [source,ruby]
L   ----
L   [1, 2, 3].reduce(4, :+) # => 10
L   [1, 2, 3].sum(4) # => 10
L 
L   [].reduce(4, :+) # => 4
L   [].sum(4) # => 4
L   ----
L 
L   This also holds true for non-numeric types which implement a `:+` method:
L 
L   [source,ruby]
L   ----
L   ['l', 'o'].reduce('Hel', :+) # => "Hello"
L   ['l', 'o'].sum('Hel') # => "Hello"
L   ----
L 
L   When no initial value is provided though, `Enumerable#reduce` will pick the first enumerated value
L   as initial value and successively add all following values to it, whereas
L   `Enumerable#sum` will set an initial value of `0` (`Integer`) which can lead to a `TypeError`:
L 
L   [source,ruby]
L   ----
L   [].reduce(:+) # => nil
L   [1, 2, 3].reduce(:+) # => 6
L   ['H', 'e', 'l', 'l', 'o'].reduce(:+) # => "Hello"
L 
L   [].sum # => 0
L   [1, 2, 3].sum # => 6
L   ['H', 'e', 'l', 'l', 'o'].sum # => in `+': String can't be coerced into Integer (TypeError)
L   ----
S Example: OnlySumOrWithInitialValue: false (default)
L   # bad
L   [1, 2, 3].inject(:+)                        # Autocorrections for cases without initial value are unsafe
L   [1, 2, 3].inject(&:+)                       # and will only be performed when using the `-A` option.
L   [1, 2, 3].reduce { |acc, elem| acc + elem } # They can be prohibited completely using `SafeAutoCorrect: true`.
L   [1, 2, 3].reduce(10, :+)
L   [1, 2, 3].map { |elem| elem ** 2 }.sum
L   [1, 2, 3].collect(&:count).sum(10)
L 
L   # good
L   [1, 2, 3].sum
L   [1, 2, 3].sum(10)
L   [1, 2, 3].sum { |elem| elem ** 2 }
L   [1, 2, 3].sum(10, &:count)
S Example: OnlySumOrWithInitialValue: true
L   # bad
L   [1, 2, 3].reduce(10, :+)
L   [1, 2, 3].map { |elem| elem ** 2 }.sum
L   [1, 2, 3].collect(&:count).sum(10)
L 
L   # good
L   [1, 2, 3].sum(10)
L   [1, 2, 3].sum { |elem| elem ** 2 }
L   [1, 2, 3].sum(10, &:count)
C Performance/TimesMap
S Details
L Checks for .times.map calls.
L In most cases such calls can be replaced
L with an explicit array creation.
S Safety
L   This cop's autocorrection is unsafe because `Integer#times` does nothing if receiver is 0
L   or less. However, `Array.new` raises an error if argument is less than 0.
L 
L   For example:
L 
L   [source,ruby]
L   ----
L   -1.times{}    # does nothing
L   Array.new(-1) # ArgumentError: negative array size
L   ----
S Example
L   # bad
L   9.times.map do |i|
L     i.to_s
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   Array.new(9) do |i|
L     i.to_s
L   end
C Performance/UnfreezeString
S Details
L In Ruby 2.3 or later, use unary plus operator to unfreeze a string
L literal instead of `String#dup` and `String.new`.
L Unary plus operator is faster than `String#dup`.
S Safety
L   This cop's autocorrection is unsafe because `String.new` (without operator) is not
L   exactly the same as `+''`. These differ in encoding. `String.new.encoding` is always
L   `ASCII-8BIT`. However, `(+'').encoding` is the same as script encoding(e.g. `UTF-8`).
L   if you expect `ASCII-8BIT` encoding, disable this cop.
S Example
L   # bad
L   ''.dup          # when Ruby 3.2 or lower
L   "something".dup # when Ruby 3.2 or lower
L   String.new
L   String.new('')
L   String.new('something')
L 
L   # good
L   +'something'
L   +''
C Performance/UriDefaultParser
S Details
L Identifies places where `URI::Parser.new` can be replaced by `URI::DEFAULT_PARSER`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   URI::Parser.new
L 
L   # good
L   URI::DEFAULT_PARSER
C Performance/ZipWithoutBlock
S Details
L Checks for `map { |id| [id] }` and suggests replacing it with `zip`.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe for novel definitions of `map` and `collect`
L   on non-Enumerable objects that do not respond to `zip`.
L   To make your object enumerable, define an `each` method
L   as described in https://ruby-doc.org/core/Enumerable.html
S Example
L   # bad
L   [1, 2, 3].map { |id| [id] }
L 
L   # good
L   [1, 2, 3].zip
C RSpec/AlignLeftLetBrace
S Details
L Checks that left braces for adjacent single line lets are aligned.
S Example
L   # bad
L   let(:foobar) { blahblah }
L   let(:baz) { bar }
L   let(:a) { b }
L 
L   # good
L   let(:foobar) { blahblah }
L   let(:baz)    { bar }
L   let(:a)      { b }
C RSpec/AlignRightLetBrace
S Details
L Checks that right braces for adjacent single line lets are aligned.
S Example
L   # bad
L   let(:foobar) { blahblah }
L   let(:baz)    { bar }
L   let(:a)      { b }
L 
L   # good
L   let(:foobar) { blahblah }
L   let(:baz)    { bar      }
L   let(:a)      { b        }
C RSpec/AnyInstance
S Details
L Check that instances are not being stubbed globally.
L 
L Prefer instance doubles over stubbing any instance of a class
S Example
L   # bad
L   describe MyClass do
L     before { allow_any_instance_of(MyClass).to receive(:foo) }
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   describe MyClass do
L     let(:my_instance) { instance_double(MyClass) }
L 
L     before do
L       allow(MyClass).to receive(:new).and_return(my_instance)
L       allow(my_instance).to receive(:foo)
L     end
L   end
C RSpec/AroundBlock
S Details
L Checks that around blocks actually run the test.
S Example
L   # bad
L   around do
L     some_method
L   end
L 
L   around do |test|
L     some_method
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   around do |test|
L     some_method
L     test.call
L   end
L 
L   around do |test|
L     some_method
L     test.run
L   end
C RSpec/Be
S Details
L Check for expectations where `be` is used without argument.
L 
L The `be` matcher is too generic, as it pass on everything that is not
L nil or false. If that is the exact intend, use `be_truthy`. In all other
L cases it's better to specify what exactly is the expected value.
S Example
L   # bad
L   expect(foo).to be
L 
L   # good
L   expect(foo).to be_truthy
L   expect(foo).to be 1.0
L   expect(foo).to be(true)
C RSpec/BeEmpty
S Details
L Prefer using `be_empty` when checking for an empty array.
S Example
L   # bad
L   expect(array).to contain_exactly
L   expect(array).to match_array([])
L 
L   # good
L   expect(array).to be_empty
C RSpec/BeEq
S Details
L Check for expectations where `be(...)` can replace `eq(...)`.
L 
L The `be` matcher compares by identity while the `eq` matcher compares
L using `==`. Booleans and nil can be compared by identity and therefore
L the `be` matcher is preferable as it is a more strict test.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because it changes how values are compared.
S Example
L   # bad
L   expect(foo).to eq(true)
L   expect(foo).to eq(false)
L   expect(foo).to eq(nil)
L 
L   # good
L   expect(foo).to be(true)
L   expect(foo).to be(false)
L   expect(foo).to be(nil)
C RSpec/BeEql
S Details
L Check for expectations where `be(...)` can replace `eql(...)`.
L 
L The `be` matcher compares by identity while the `eql` matcher
L compares using `eql?`. Integers, floats, booleans, symbols, and nil
L can be compared by identity and therefore the `be` matcher is
L preferable as it is a more strict test.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because it changes how values are compared.
S Example
L   # bad
L   expect(foo).to eql(1)
L   expect(foo).to eql(1.0)
L   expect(foo).to eql(true)
L   expect(foo).to eql(false)
L   expect(foo).to eql(:bar)
L   expect(foo).to eql(nil)
L 
L   # good
L   expect(foo).to be(1)
L   expect(foo).to be(1.0)
L   expect(foo).to be(true)
L   expect(foo).to be(false)
L   expect(foo).to be(:bar)
L   expect(foo).to be(nil)
L 
L This cop only looks for instances of `expect(...).to eql(...)`. We
L do not check `to_not` or `not_to` since `!eql?` is more strict
L than `!equal?`. We also do not try to flag `eq` because if
L `a == b`, and `b` is comparable by identity, `a` is still not
L necessarily the same type as `b` since the `#==` operator can
L coerce objects for comparison.
C RSpec/BeNil
S Details
L Ensures a consistent style is used when matching `nil`.
L 
L You can either use the more specific `be_nil` matcher, or the more
L generic `be` matcher with a `nil` argument.
L 
L This cop can be configured using the `EnforcedStyle` option
S Example: `EnforcedStyle: be_nil` (default)
L   # bad
L   expect(foo).to be(nil)
L 
L   # good
L   expect(foo).to be_nil
S Example: `EnforcedStyle: be`
L   # bad
L   expect(foo).to be_nil
L 
L   # good
L   expect(foo).to be(nil)
C RSpec/BeforeAfterAll
S Details
L Check that before/after(:all/:context) isn't being used.
S Example
L   # bad - Faster but risk of state leaking between examples
L   describe MyClass do
L     before(:all) { Widget.create }
L     after(:context) { Widget.delete_all }
L   end
L 
L   # good - Slower but examples are properly isolated
L   describe MyClass do
L     before(:each) { Widget.create }
L     after(:each) { Widget.delete_all }
L   end
C RSpec/ChangeByZero
S Details
L Prefer negated matchers over `to change.by(0)`.
L 
L In the case of composite expectations, cop suggest using the
L negation matchers of `RSpec::Matchers#change`.
L 
L By default the cop does not support autocorrect of
L compound expectations, but if you set the
L negated matcher for `change`, e.g. `not_change` with
L the `NegatedMatcher` option, the cop will perform the autocorrection.
S Example: NegatedMatcher: ~ (default)
L   # bad
L   expect { run }.to change(Foo, :bar).by(0)
L   expect { run }.to change { Foo.bar }.by(0)
L 
L   # bad - compound expectations (does not support autocorrection)
L   expect { run }
L     .to change(Foo, :bar).by(0)
L     .and change(Foo, :baz).by(0)
L   expect { run }
L     .to change { Foo.bar }.by(0)
L     .and change { Foo.baz }.by(0)
L 
L   # good
L   expect { run }.not_to change(Foo, :bar)
L   expect { run }.not_to change { Foo.bar }
L 
L   # good - compound expectations
L   define_negated_matcher :not_change, :change
L   expect { run }
L     .to not_change(Foo, :bar)
L     .and not_change(Foo, :baz)
L   expect { run }
L     .to not_change { Foo.bar }
L     .and not_change { Foo.baz }
S Example: NegatedMatcher: not_change
L   # bad (support autocorrection to good case)
L   expect { run }
L     .to change(Foo, :bar).by(0)
L     .and change(Foo, :baz).by(0)
L   expect { run }
L     .to change { Foo.bar }.by(0)
L     .and change { Foo.baz }.by(0)
L 
L   # good
L   define_negated_matcher :not_change, :change
L   expect { run }
L     .to not_change(Foo, :bar)
L     .and not_change(Foo, :baz)
L   expect { run }
L     .to not_change { Foo.bar }
L     .and not_change { Foo.baz }
C RSpec/ClassCheck
S Details
L Enforces consistent use of `be_a` or `be_kind_of`.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: be_a (default)
L   # bad
L   expect(object).to be_kind_of(String)
L   expect(object).to be_a_kind_of(String)
L 
L   # good
L   expect(object).to be_a(String)
L   expect(object).to be_an(String)
S Example: EnforcedStyle: be_kind_of
L   # bad
L   expect(object).to be_a(String)
L   expect(object).to be_an(String)
L 
L   # good
L   expect(object).to be_kind_of(String)
L   expect(object).to be_a_kind_of(String)
C RSpec/ContainExactly
S Details
L Checks where `contain_exactly` is used.
L 
L This cop checks for the following:
L 
L - Prefer `match_array` when matching array values.
L - Prefer `be_empty` when using `contain_exactly` with no arguments.
S Example
L   # bad
L   it { is_expected.to contain_exactly(*array) }
L 
L   # good
L   it { is_expected.to match_array(array) }
L 
L   # good
L   it { is_expected.to contain_exactly(*array1, *array2) }
L 
L   # good
L   it { is_expected.to contain_exactly(content, *array) }
C RSpec/ContextMethod
S Details
L `context` should not be used for specifying methods.
S Example
L   # bad
L   context '#foo_bar' do
L     # ...
L   end
L 
L   context '.foo_bar' do
L     # ...
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   describe '#foo_bar' do
L     # ...
L   end
L 
L   describe '.foo_bar' do
L     # ...
L   end
C RSpec/ContextWording
S Details
L Checks that `context` docstring starts with an allowed prefix.
L 
L The default list of prefixes is minimal. Users are encouraged to tailor
L the configuration to meet project needs. Other acceptable prefixes may
L include `if`, `unless`, `for`, `before`, `after`, or `during`.
L They may consist of multiple words if desired.
S See: http://www.betterspecs.org/#contexts
L If both `Prefixes` and `AllowedPatterns` are empty, this cop will always
L report an offense. So you need to set at least one of them.
S Example: `Prefixes` configuration
L   # .rubocop.yml
L   # RSpec/ContextWording:
L   #   Prefixes:
L   #     - when
L   #     - with
L   #     - without
L   #     - if
L   #     - unless
L   #     - for
S Example
L   # bad
L   context 'the display name not present' do
L     # ...
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   context 'when the display name is not present' do
L     # ...
L   end
L 
L This cop can be customized allowed context description pattern
L with `AllowedPatterns`. By default, there are no checking by pattern.
S Example: `AllowedPatterns` configuration
L   # .rubocop.yml
L   # RSpec/ContextWording:
L   #   AllowedPatterns:
L   #     - とき$
S Example
L   # bad
L   context '条件を満たす' do
L     # ...
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   context '条件を満たすとき' do
L     # ...
L   end
C RSpec/DescribeClass
S Details
L Check that the first argument to the top-level describe is a constant.
L 
L It can be configured to ignore strings when certain metadata is passed.
L 
L Ignores Rails and Aruba `type` metadata by default.
S Example: `IgnoredMetadata` configuration
L   # .rubocop.yml
L   # RSpec/DescribeClass:
L   #   IgnoredMetadata:
L   #     type:
L   #       - request
L   #       - controller
S Example
L   # bad
L   describe 'Do something' do
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   describe TestedClass do
L     subject { described_class }
L   end
L 
L   describe 'TestedClass::VERSION' do
L     subject { Object.const_get(self.class.description) }
L   end
L 
L   describe "A feature example", type: :feature do
L   end
C RSpec/DescribeMethod
S Details
L Checks that the second argument to `describe` specifies a method.
S Example
L   # bad
L   describe MyClass, 'do something' do
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   describe MyClass, '#my_instance_method' do
L   end
L 
L   describe MyClass, '.my_class_method' do
L   end
C RSpec/DescribeSymbol
S Details
L Avoid describing symbols.
S Example
L   # bad
L   describe :my_method do
L     # ...
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   describe '#my_method' do
L     # ...
L   end
C RSpec/DescribedClass
S Details
L Checks that tests use `described_class`.
L 
L If the first argument of describe is a class, the class is exposed to
L each example via described_class.
L 
L This cop can be configured using the `EnforcedStyle`, `SkipBlocks`
L and `OnlyStaticConstants` options.
L `OnlyStaticConstants` is only relevant when `EnforcedStyle` is
L `described_class`.
L 
L There's a known caveat with rspec-rails's `controller` helper that
L runs its block in a different context, and `described_class` is not
L available to it. `SkipBlocks` option excludes detection in all
L non-RSpec related blocks.
L 
L To narrow down this setting to only a specific directory, it is
L possible to use an overriding configuration file local to that
L directory.
S Safety
L   Autocorrection is unsafe when `SkipBlocks: false` because
L   `described_class` might not be available within the block (for
L   example, in rspec-rails's `controller` helper).
S Example: `EnforcedStyle: described_class` (default)
L   # bad
L   describe MyClass do
L     subject { MyClass.do_something }
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   describe MyClass do
L     subject { described_class.do_something }
L   end
S Example: `OnlyStaticConstants: true` (default)
L   # good
L   describe MyClass do
L     subject { MyClass::CONSTANT }
L   end
S Example: `OnlyStaticConstants: false`
L   # bad
L   describe MyClass do
L     subject { MyClass::CONSTANT }
L   end
S Example: `EnforcedStyle: explicit`
L   # bad
L   describe MyClass do
L     subject { described_class.do_something }
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   describe MyClass do
L     subject { MyClass.do_something }
L   end
S Example: `SkipBlocks: true`
L   # spec/controllers/.rubocop.yml
L   # RSpec/DescribedClass:
L   #   SkipBlocks: true
L 
L   # acceptable
L   describe MyConcern do
L     controller(ApplicationController) do
L       include MyConcern
L     end
L   end
C RSpec/DescribedClassModuleWrapping
S Details
L Avoid opening modules and defining specs within them.
S Example
L   # bad
L   module MyModule
L     RSpec.describe MyClass do
L       # ...
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   RSpec.describe MyModule::MyClass do
L     # ...
L   end
C RSpec/Dialect
S Details
L Enforces custom RSpec dialects.
L 
L A dialect can be based on the following RSpec methods:
L 
L - describe, context, feature, example_group
L - xdescribe, xcontext, xfeature
L - fdescribe, fcontext, ffeature
L - shared_examples, shared_examples_for, shared_context
L - it, specify, example, scenario, its
L - fit, fspecify, fexample, fscenario, focus
L - xit, xspecify, xexample, xscenario, skip
L - pending
L - prepend_before, before, append_before,
L - around
L - prepend_after, after, append_after
L - let, let!
L - subject, subject!
L - expect, is_expected, expect_any_instance_of
L - raise_error, raise_exception
L 
L By default all of the RSpec methods and aliases are allowed. By setting
L a config like:
L 
L   RSpec/Dialect:
L     PreferredMethods:
L       context: describe
L 
L If you were previously using the `RSpec/Capybara/FeatureMethods` cop and
L want to keep disabling all Capybara-specific methods that have the same
L native RSpec method (e.g. are just aliases), use the following config:
L 
L   RSpec/Dialect:
L     PreferredMethods:
L       background: :before
L       scenario:   :it
L       xscenario:  :xit
L       given:      :let
L       given!:     :let!
L       feature:    :describe
L 
L You can expect the following behavior:
S Example
L   # bad
L   context 'display name presence' do
L     # ...
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   describe 'display name presence' do
L     # ...
L   end
C RSpec/DiscardedMatcher
S Details
L Checks for matchers that are used in void context.
L 
L Matcher calls like `change`, `receive`, etc. that appear as
L standalone expressions have their result silently discarded.
L This usually means a missing `.and` to chain compound matchers.
L 
L The list of matcher methods can be configured
L with `CustomMatcherMethods`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   specify do
L     expect { result }
L       .to change { obj.foo }.from(1).to(2)
L       change { obj.bar }.from(3).to(4)
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   specify do
L     expect { result }
L       .to change { obj.foo }.from(1).to(2)
L       .and change { obj.bar }.from(3).to(4)
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   specify do
L     expect { result }.to change { obj.foo }.from(1).to(2)
L   end
C RSpec/DuplicatedMetadata
S Details
L Avoid duplicated metadata.
S Example
L   # bad
L   describe 'Something', :a, :a
L 
L   # good
L   describe 'Something', :a
C RSpec/EmptyExampleGroup
S Details
L Checks if an example group does not include any tests.
S Example: usage
L   # bad
L   describe Bacon do
L     let(:bacon)      { Bacon.new(chunkiness) }
L     let(:chunkiness) { false                 }
L 
L     context 'extra chunky' do   # flagged by rubocop
L       let(:chunkiness) { true }
L     end
L 
L     it 'is chunky' do
L       expect(bacon.chunky?).to be_truthy
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   describe Bacon do
L     let(:bacon)      { Bacon.new(chunkiness) }
L     let(:chunkiness) { false                 }
L 
L     it 'is chunky' do
L       expect(bacon.chunky?).to be_truthy
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   describe Bacon do
L     pending 'will add tests later'
L   end
C RSpec/EmptyHook
S Details
L Checks for empty before and after hooks.
S Example
L   # bad
L   before {}
L   after do; end
L   before(:all) do
L   end
L   after(:all) { }
L 
L   # good
L   before { create_users }
L   after do
L     cleanup_users
L   end
L   before(:all) do
L     create_feed
L   end
L   after(:all) { cleanup_feed }
C RSpec/EmptyLineAfterExample
S Details
L Checks if there is an empty line after example blocks.
S Example
L   # bad
L   RSpec.describe Foo do
L     it 'does this' do
L     end
L     it 'does that' do
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   RSpec.describe Foo do
L     it 'does this' do
L     end
L 
L     it 'does that' do
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # fair - it's ok to have non-separated one-liners
L   RSpec.describe Foo do
L     it { one }
L     it { two }
L   end
S Example: with AllowConsecutiveOneLiners configuration
L   # rubocop.yml
L   # RSpec/EmptyLineAfterExample:
L   #   AllowConsecutiveOneLiners: false
L 
L   # bad
L   RSpec.describe Foo do
L     it { one }
L     it { two }
L   end
C RSpec/EmptyLineAfterExampleGroup
S Details
L Checks if there is an empty line after example group blocks.
S Example
L   # bad
L   RSpec.describe Foo do
L     describe '#bar' do
L     end
L     describe '#baz' do
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   RSpec.describe Foo do
L     describe '#bar' do
L     end
L 
L     describe '#baz' do
L     end
L   end
C RSpec/EmptyLineAfterFinalLet
S Details
L Checks if there is an empty line after the last let block.
S Example
L   # bad
L   let(:foo) { bar }
L   let(:something) { other }
L   it { does_something }
L 
L   # good
L   let(:foo) { bar }
L   let(:something) { other }
L 
L   it { does_something }
C RSpec/EmptyLineAfterHook
S Details
L Checks if there is an empty line after hook blocks.
L 
L `AllowConsecutiveOneLiners` configures whether adjacent
L one-line definitions are considered an offense.
S Example
L   # bad
L   before { do_something }
L   it { does_something }
L 
L   # bad
L   after { do_something }
L   it { does_something }
L 
L   # bad
L   around { |test| test.run }
L   it { does_something }
L 
L   # good
L   after { do_something }
L 
L   it { does_something }
L 
L   # fair - it's ok to have non-separated one-liners hooks
L   around { |test| test.run }
L   after { do_something }
L 
L   it { does_something }
S Example: with AllowConsecutiveOneLiners configuration
L   # rubocop.yml
L   # RSpec/EmptyLineAfterHook:
L   #   AllowConsecutiveOneLiners: false
L 
L   # bad
L   around { |test| test.run }
L   after { do_something }
L 
L   it { does_something }
L 
L   # good
L   around { |test| test.run }
L 
L   after { do_something }
L 
L   it { does_something }
C RSpec/EmptyLineAfterSubject
S Details
L Checks if there is an empty line after subject block.
S Example
L   # bad
L   subject(:obj) { described_class }
L   let(:foo) { bar }
L 
L   # good
L   subject(:obj) { described_class }
L 
L   let(:foo) { bar }
C RSpec/EmptyMetadata
S Details
L Avoid empty metadata hash.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: symbol (default)
L   # bad
L   describe 'Something', {}
L 
L   # good
L   describe 'Something'
C RSpec/EmptyOutput
S Details
L Check that the `output` matcher is not called with an empty string.
S Example
L   # bad
L   expect { foo }.to output('').to_stdout
L   expect { bar }.not_to output('').to_stderr
L 
L   # good
