L   class User < ApplicationRecord
L     attribute :configuration, default: -> { {} }
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class User < ApplicationRecord
L     attribute :role, :string, default: :customer
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class User < ApplicationRecord
L     attribute :activated, :boolean, default: false
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class User < ApplicationRecord
L     attribute :login_count, :integer, default: 0
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class User < ApplicationRecord
L     FOO = 123
L     attribute :custom_attribute, :integer, default: FOO
L   end
C Rails/BelongsTo
S Details
L Looks for belongs_to associations where we control whether the
L association is required via the deprecated `required` option instead.
L 
L Since Rails 5, belongs_to associations are required by default and this
L can be controlled through the use of `optional: true`.
L 
L From the release notes:
L 
L     belongs_to will now trigger a validation error by default if the
L     association is not present. You can turn this off on a
L     per-association basis with optional: true. Also deprecate required
L     option in favor of optional for belongs_to. (Pull Request)
L 
L In the case that the developer is doing `required: false`, we
L definitely want to autocorrect to `optional: true`.
L 
L However, without knowing whether they've set overridden the default
L value of `config.active_record.belongs_to_required_by_default`, we
L can't say whether it's safe to remove `required: true` or whether we
L should replace it with `optional: false` (or, similarly, remove a
L superfluous `optional: false`). Therefore, in the cases we're using
L `required: true`, we'll simply invert it to `optional: false` and the
L user can remove depending on their defaults.
S Example
L   # bad
L   class Post < ApplicationRecord
L     belongs_to :blog, required: false
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class Post < ApplicationRecord
L     belongs_to :blog, optional: true
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   class Post < ApplicationRecord
L     belongs_to :blog, required: true
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class Post < ApplicationRecord
L     belongs_to :blog, optional: false
L   end
C Rails/Blank
S Details
L Checks for code that can be written with simpler conditionals
L using `Object#blank?` defined by Active Support.
L 
L Interaction with `Style/UnlessElse`:
L The configuration of `NotPresent` will not produce an offense in the
L context of `unless else` if `Style/UnlessElse` is enabled. This is
L to prevent interference between the autocorrection of the two cops.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe autocorrection, because `' '.empty?` returns false,
L   but `' '.blank?` returns true. Therefore, autocorrection is not compatible
L   if the receiver is a non-empty blank string, tab, or newline meta characters.
S Example: NilOrEmpty: true (default)
L   # Converts usages of `nil? || empty?` to `blank?`
L 
L   # bad
L   foo.nil? || foo.empty?
L   foo == nil || foo.empty?
L 
L   # good
L   foo.blank?
S Example: NotPresent: true (default)
L   # Converts usages of `!present?` to `blank?`
L 
L   # bad
L   !foo.present?
L 
L   # good
L   foo.blank?
S Example: UnlessPresent: true (default)
L   # Converts usages of `unless present?` to `if blank?`
L 
L   # bad
L   something unless foo.present?
L 
L   # good
L   something if foo.blank?
L 
L   # bad
L   unless foo.present?
L     something
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   if foo.blank?
L     something
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def blank?
L     !present?
L   end
C Rails/BulkChangeTable
S Details
L Checks whether alter queries are combinable.
L If combinable queries are detected, it suggests to you
L to use `change_table` with `bulk: true` instead.
L This option causes the migration to generate a single
L ALTER TABLE statement combining multiple column alterations.
L 
L The `bulk` option is only supported on the MySQL and
L the PostgreSQL (5.2 later) adapter; thus it will
L automatically detect an adapter from `development` environment
L in `config/database.yml` or the environment variable `DATABASE_URL`
L when the `Database` option is not set.
L If the adapter is not `mysql2`, `trilogy`, `postgresql`, or `postgis`,
L this Cop ignores offenses.
S Example
L   # bad
L   def change
L     add_column :users, :name, :string, null: false
L     add_column :users, :nickname, :string
L 
L     # ALTER TABLE `users` ADD `name` varchar(255) NOT NULL
L     # ALTER TABLE `users` ADD `nickname` varchar(255)
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def change
L     change_table :users, bulk: true do |t|
L       t.string :name, null: false
L       t.string :nickname
L     end
L 
L     # ALTER TABLE `users` ADD `name` varchar(255) NOT NULL,
L     #                     ADD `nickname` varchar(255)
L   end
S Example
L   # bad
L   def change
L     change_table :users do |t|
L       t.string :name, null: false
L       t.string :nickname
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def change
L     change_table :users, bulk: true do |t|
L       t.string :name, null: false
L       t.string :nickname
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   # When you don't want to combine alter queries.
L   def change
L     change_table :users, bulk: false do |t|
L       t.string :name, null: false
L       t.string :nickname
L     end
L   end
C Rails/CompactBlank
S Details
L Checks if collection can be blank-compacted with `compact_blank`.
S Safety
L   It is unsafe by default because false positives may occur in the
L   blank check of block arguments to the receiver object.
L 
L   For example, `[[1, 2], [3, nil]].reject { |first, second| second.blank? }` and
L   `[[1, 2], [3, nil]].compact_blank` are not compatible. The same is true for `blank?`.
L   This will work fine when the receiver is a hash object.
L 
L   And `compact_blank!` has different implementations for `Array`, `Hash`, and
L   `ActionController::Parameters`.
L   `Array#compact_blank!`, `Hash#compact_blank!` are equivalent to `delete_if(&:blank?)`.
L   If the cop makes a mistake, autocorrected code may get unexpected behavior.
S Example
L   # bad
L   collection.reject(&:blank?)
L   collection.reject { |_k, v| v.blank? }
L   collection.select(&:present?)
L   collection.select { |_k, v| v.present? }
L   collection.filter(&:present?)
L   collection.filter { |_k, v| v.present? }
L 
L   # good
L   collection.compact_blank
L 
L   # bad
L   collection.delete_if(&:blank?)            # Same behavior as `Array#compact_blank!` and `Hash#compact_blank!`
L   collection.delete_if { |_k, v| v.blank? } # Same behavior as `Array#compact_blank!` and `Hash#compact_blank!`
L   collection.keep_if(&:present?)            # Same behavior as `Array#compact_blank!` and `Hash#compact_blank!`
L   collection.keep_if { |_k, v| v.present? } # Same behavior as `Array#compact_blank!` and `Hash#compact_blank!`
L 
L   # good
L   collection.compact_blank!
C Rails/ContentTag
S Details
L Checks legacy syntax usage of `tag`
L 
L NOTE: Allow `tag` when the first argument is a variable because
L `tag(name)` is simpler rather than `tag.public_send(name)`.
L And this cop will be renamed to something like `LegacyTag` in the future. (e.g. RuboCop Rails 3.0)
S Example
L  # bad
L  tag(:p)
L  tag(:br, class: 'classname')
L 
L  # good
L  tag.p
L  tag.br(class: 'classname')
L  tag(name, class: 'classname')
C Rails/CreateTableWithTimestamps
S Details
L Checks the migration for which timestamps are not included when creating a new table.
L In many cases, timestamps are useful information and should be added.
L 
L NOTE: Allow `timestamps` not written when `id: false` because this emphasizes respecting
L user's editing intentions.
S Example
L   # bad
L   create_table :users
L 
L   # bad
L   create_table :users do |t|
L     t.string :name
L     t.string :email
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   create_table :users do |t|
L     t.string :name
L     t.string :email
L 
L     t.timestamps
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   create_table :users do |t|
L     t.string :name
L     t.string :email
L 
L     t.datetime :created_at, default: -> { 'CURRENT_TIMESTAMP' }
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   create_table :users do |t|
L     t.string :name
L     t.string :email
L 
L     t.datetime :updated_at, default: -> { 'CURRENT_TIMESTAMP' }
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   create_table :users, articles, id: false do |t|
L     t.integer :user_id
L     t.integer :article_id
L   end
C Rails/DangerousColumnNames
S Details
L Avoid dangerous column names.
L 
L Some column names are considered dangerous because they would overwrite methods already defined.
S Example
L   # bad
L   add_column :users, :save
L 
L   # good
L   add_column :users, :saved
C Rails/Date
S Details
L Checks for the correct use of Date methods,
L such as Date.today, Date.current etc.
L 
L Using `Date.today` is dangerous, because it doesn't know anything about
L Rails time zone. You must use `Time.zone.today` instead.
L 
L The cop also reports warnings when you are using `to_time` method,
L because it doesn't know about Rails time zone either.
L 
L Two styles are supported for this cop. When `EnforcedStyle` is `strict`
L then the Date methods `today`, `current`, `yesterday`, and `tomorrow`
L are prohibited and the usage of both `to_time`
L and `to_time_in_current_zone` are reported as warning.
L 
L When `EnforcedStyle` is `flexible` then only `Date.today` is prohibited.
L 
L And you can set a warning for `to_time` with `AllowToTime: false`.
L `AllowToTime` is `true` by default to prevent false positive on `DateTime` object.
S Safety
L   This cop's autocorrection is unsafe because it may change handling time.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: flexible (default)
L   # bad
L   Date.today
L 
L   # good
L   Time.zone.today
L   Time.zone.today - 1.day
L   Date.current
L   Date.yesterday
L   date.in_time_zone
S Example: EnforcedStyle: strict
L   # bad
L   Date.current
L   Date.yesterday
L   Date.today
L 
L   # good
L   Time.zone.today
L   Time.zone.today - 1.day
S Example: AllowToTime: true (default)
L   # good
L   date.to_time
S Example: AllowToTime: false
L   # bad
L   date.to_time
C Rails/DefaultScope
S Details
L Looks for uses of `default_scope`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   default_scope -> { where(hidden: false) }
L 
L   # good
L   scope :published, -> { where(hidden: false) }
L 
L   # bad
L   def self.default_scope
L     where(hidden: false)
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def self.published
L     where(hidden: false)
L   end
C Rails/Delegate
S Details
L Looks for delegations that could have been created
L automatically with the `delegate` method.
L 
L Safe navigation `&.` is ignored because Rails' `allow_nil`
L option checks not just for nil but also delegates if nil
L responds to the delegated method.
L 
L The `EnforceForPrefixed` option (defaulted to `true`) means that
L using the target object as a prefix of the method name
L without using the `delegate` method will be a violation.
L When set to `false`, this case is legal.
L 
L It is disabled for controllers in order to keep controller actions
L explicitly defined.
S Example
L   # bad
L   def bar
L     foo.bar
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   delegate :bar, to: :foo
L 
L   # bad
L   def bar
L     self.bar
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   delegate :bar, to: :self
L 
L   # good
L   def bar
L     foo&.bar
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   private
L   def bar
L     foo.bar
L   end
S Example: EnforceForPrefixed: true (default)
L   # bad
L   def foo_bar
L     foo.bar
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   delegate :bar, to: :foo, prefix: true
S Example: EnforceForPrefixed: false
L   # good
L   def foo_bar
L     foo.bar
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   delegate :bar, to: :foo, prefix: true
C Rails/DelegateAllowBlank
S Details
L Looks for delegations that pass :allow_blank as an option
L instead of :allow_nil. :allow_blank is not a valid option to pass
L to ActiveSupport#delegate.
S Example
L   # bad
L   delegate :foo, to: :bar, allow_blank: true
L 
L   # good
L   delegate :foo, to: :bar, allow_nil: true
C Rails/DeprecatedActiveModelErrorsMethods
S Details
L Checks direct manipulation of ActiveModel#errors as hash.
L These operations are deprecated in Rails 6.1 and will not work in Rails 7.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because it can report `errors` manipulation on non-ActiveModel,
L   which is obviously valid.
L   The cop has no way of knowing whether a variable is an ActiveModel or not.
S Example
L   # bad
L   user.errors[:name] << 'msg'
L   user.errors.messages[:name] << 'msg'
L 
L   # good
L   user.errors.add(:name, 'msg')
L 
L   # bad
L   user.errors[:name].clear
L   user.errors.messages[:name].clear
L 
L   # good
L   user.errors.delete(:name)
L 
L   # bad
L   user.errors.keys.include?(:attr)
L 
L   # good
L   user.errors.attribute_names.include?(:attr)
C Rails/DotSeparatedKeys
S Details
L Enforces the use of dot-separated locale keys instead of specifying the `:scope` option
L with an array or a single symbol in `I18n` translation methods.
L Dot-separated notation is easier to read and trace the hierarchy.
S Example
L   # bad
L   I18n.t :record_invalid, scope: [:activerecord, :errors, :messages]
L   I18n.t :title, scope: :invitation
L 
L   # good
L   I18n.t 'activerecord.errors.messages.record_invalid'
L   I18n.t :record_invalid, scope: 'activerecord.errors.messages'
C Rails/DuplicateAssociation
S Details
L Looks for associations that have been defined multiple times in the same file.
L 
L When an association is defined multiple times on a model, Active Record overrides the
L previously defined association with the new one. Because of this, this cop's autocorrection
L simply keeps the last of any duplicates and discards the rest.
S Example
L   # bad
L   belongs_to :foo
L   belongs_to :bar
L   has_one :foo
L 
L   # good
L   belongs_to :bar
L   has_one :foo
L 
L   # bad
L   has_many :foo, class_name: 'Foo'
L   has_many :bar, class_name: 'Foo'
L   has_one :baz
L 
L   # good
L   has_many :bar, class_name: 'Foo'
L   has_one :foo
C Rails/DuplicateScope
S Details
L Checks for multiple scopes in a model that have the same expression. This
L often means you copy/pasted a scope, updated the name, and forgot to change the condition.
S Example
L   # bad
L   scope :visible, -> { where(visible: true) }
L   scope :hidden, -> { where(visible: true) }
L 
L   # good
L   scope :visible, -> { where(visible: true) }
L   scope :hidden, -> { where(visible: false) }
C Rails/DurationArithmetic
S Details
L Checks if a duration is added to or subtracted from `Time.current`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   Time.current - 1.minute
L   Time.current + 2.days
L 
L   # good - using relative would make it harder to express and read
L   Date.yesterday + 3.days
L   created_at - 1.minute
L   3.days - 1.hour
L 
L   # good
L   1.minute.ago
L   2.days.from_now
C Rails/DynamicFindBy
S Details
L Checks dynamic `find_by_*` methods.
L Use `find_by` instead of dynamic method.
L See. https://rails.rubystyle.guide#find_by
S Safety
L   It is certainly unsafe when not configured properly, i.e. user-defined `find_by_xxx`
L   method is not added to cop's `AllowedMethods`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   User.find_by_name(name)
L   User.find_by_name_and_email(name)
L   User.find_by_email!(name)
L 
L   # good
L   User.find_by(name: name)
L   User.find_by(name: name, email: email)
L   User.find_by!(email: email)
S Example: AllowedMethods: ['find_by_sql', 'find_by_token_for'] (default)
L   # bad
L   User.find_by_query(users_query)
L   User.find_by_token_for(:password_reset, token)
L 
L   # good
L   User.find_by_sql(users_sql)
L   User.find_by_token_for(:password_reset, token)
S Example: AllowedReceivers: ['Gem::Specification', 'page'] (default)
L   # bad
L   Specification.find_by_name('backend').gem_dir
L   page.find_by_id('a_dom_id').click
L 
L   # good
L   Gem::Specification.find_by_name('backend').gem_dir
L   page.find_by_id('a_dom_id').click
C Rails/EagerEvaluationLogMessage
S Details
L Checks that blocks are used for interpolated strings passed to
L `Rails.logger.debug`.
L 
L By default, Rails production environments use the `:info` log level.
L At the `:info` log level, `Rails.logger.debug` statements do not result
L in log output. However, Ruby must eagerly evaluate interpolated string
L arguments passed as method arguments. Passing a block to
L `Rails.logger.debug` prevents costly evaluation of interpolated strings
L when no output would be produced anyway.
S Example
L   # bad
L   Rails.logger.debug "The time is #{Time.zone.now}."
L 
L   # good
L   Rails.logger.debug { "The time is #{Time.zone.now}." }
C Rails/EnumHash
S Details
L Looks for enums written with array syntax.
L 
L When using array syntax, adding an element in a
L position other than the last causes all previous
L definitions to shift. Explicitly specifying the
L value for each key prevents this from happening.
S Example
L   # bad
L   enum :status, [:active, :archived]
L 
L   # good
L   enum :status, { active: 0, archived: 1 }
L 
L   # bad
L   enum status: [:active, :archived]
L 
L   # good
L   enum status: { active: 0, archived: 1 }
C Rails/EnumSyntax
S Details
L Looks for enums written with keyword arguments syntax.
L 
L Defining enums with keyword arguments syntax is deprecated and will be removed in Rails 8.0.
L Positional arguments should be used instead:
S Example
L   # bad
L   enum status: { active: 0, archived: 1 }, _prefix: true
L 
L   # good
L   enum :status, { active: 0, archived: 1 }, prefix: true
C Rails/EnumUniqueness
S Details
L Looks for duplicate values in enum declarations.
S Example
L   # bad
L   enum :status, { active: 0, archived: 0 }
L 
L   # good
L   enum :status, { active: 0, archived: 1 }
L 
L   # bad
L   enum :status, [:active, :archived, :active]
L 
L   # good
L   enum :status, [:active, :archived]
L 
L   # bad
L   enum status: { active: 0, archived: 0 }
L 
L   # good
L   enum status: { active: 0, archived: 1 }
L 
L   # bad
L   enum status: [:active, :archived, :active]
L 
L   # good
L   enum status: [:active, :archived]
C Rails/Env
S Details
L Checks for usage of `Rails.env` which can be replaced with Feature Flags
L 
L The cop does not flag `Rails.env.local?`, the built-in alias for
L "development or test" introduced in Rails 7.1. Unlike per-environment
L predicates such as `development?` or `production?`, `local?` expresses
L the intent of guarding code that must only ever run in development or
L test (sanity checks, devtools, seed data) rather than gating an
L environment rollout, so a Feature Flag is not a suitable replacement.
S Example
L   # bad
L   Rails.env.production? || Rails.env.development?
L 
L   # good
L   if FeatureFlag.enabled?(:new_feature)
L     # new feature code
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   raise 'This should never run in production' unless Rails.env.local?
C Rails/EnvLocal
S Details
L Checks for usage of `Rails.env.development? || Rails.env.test?` which
L can be replaced with `Rails.env.local?`, introduced in Rails 7.1.
S Example
L   # bad
L   Rails.env.development? || Rails.env.test?
L 
L   # good
L   Rails.env.local?
C Rails/EnvironmentComparison
S Details
L Checks that `Rails.env` is compared using `.production?`-like
L methods instead of equality against a string or symbol.
S Example
L   # bad
L   Rails.env == 'production'
L   Rails.env.to_sym == :production
L 
L   # bad, always returns false
L   Rails.env == :test
L 
L   # good
L   Rails.env.production?
C Rails/EnvironmentVariableAccess
S Details
L Looks for direct access to environment variables through the
L `ENV` variable within the application code. This can lead to runtime
L errors due to misconfiguration that could have been discovered at boot
L time if the environment variables were loaded as part of initialization
L and copied into the application's configuration or secrets. The cop can
L be configured to allow either reads or writes if required.
S Example
L   # good
L   Rails.application.config.foo
L   Rails.application.config.x.foo.bar
L   Rails.application.secrets.foo
L   Rails.application.config.foo = "bar"
S Example: AllowReads: false (default)
L   # bad
L   ENV["FOO"]
L   ENV.fetch("FOO")
S Example: AllowReads: true
L   # good
L   ENV["FOO"]
L   ENV.fetch("FOO")
S Example: AllowWrites: false (default)
L   # bad
L   ENV["FOO"] = "bar"
S Example: AllowWrites: true
L   # good
L   ENV["FOO"] = "bar"
C Rails/Exit
S Details
L Enforces that `exit` and `abort` calls are not used within a rails app.
L Valid options are instead to raise an error, break, return, or some
L other form of stopping execution of current request.
L 
L There are two obvious cases where `exit` is particularly harmful:
L 
L * Usage in library code for your application. Even though Rails will
L rescue from a `SystemExit` and continue on, unit testing that library
L code will result in specs exiting (potentially silently if `exit(0)`
L is used.)
L * Usage in application code outside of the web process could result in
L the program exiting, which could result in the code failing to run and
L do its job.
S Example
L   # bad
L   exit(0)
L 
L   # good
L   raise 'a bad error has happened'
C Rails/ExpandedDateRange
S Details
L Checks for expanded date range. It only compatible `..` range is targeted.
L Incompatible `...` range is ignored.
S Example
L   # bad
L   date.beginning_of_day..date.end_of_day
L   date.beginning_of_week..date.end_of_week
L   date.beginning_of_month..date.end_of_month
L   date.beginning_of_quarter..date.end_of_quarter
L   date.beginning_of_year..date.end_of_year
L 
L   # good
L   date.all_day
L   date.all_week
L   date.all_month
L   date.all_quarter
L   date.all_year
C Rails/FilePath
S Details
L Identifies usages of file path joining process to use `Rails.root.join` clause.
L It is used to add uniformity when joining paths.
L 
L NOTE: This cop ignores leading slashes in string literal arguments for `Rails.root.join`
L       and multiple slashes in string literal arguments for `Rails.root.join` and `File.join`.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: slashes (default)
L   # bad
L   Rails.root.join('app', 'models', 'goober')
L 
L   # good
L   Rails.root.join('app/models/goober')
L 
L   # bad
L   File.join(Rails.root, 'app/models/goober')
L   "#{Rails.root}/app/models/goober"
L 
L   # good
L   Rails.root.join('app/models/goober').to_s
S Example: EnforcedStyle: arguments
L   # bad
L   Rails.root.join('app/models/goober')
L 
L   # good
L   Rails.root.join('app', 'models', 'goober')
L 
L   # bad
L   File.join(Rails.root, 'app/models/goober')
L   "#{Rails.root}/app/models/goober"
L 
L   # good
L   Rails.root.join('app', 'models', 'goober').to_s
C Rails/FindBy
S Details
L Identifies usages of `where.take` and change them to use `find_by` instead.
L 
L And `where(...).first` can return different results from `find_by`.
L (They order records differently, so the "first" record can be different.)
L 
L If you also want to detect `where.first`, you can set `IgnoreWhereFirst` to false.
S Example
L   # bad
L   User.where(name: 'Bruce').take
L 
L   # good
L   User.find_by(name: 'Bruce')
S Example: IgnoreWhereFirst: true (default)
L   # good
L   User.where(name: 'Bruce').first
S Example: IgnoreWhereFirst: false
L   # bad
L   User.where(name: 'Bruce').first
C Rails/FindById
S Details
L Enforces that `ActiveRecord#find` is used instead of
L `where.take!`, `find_by!`, and `find_by_id!` to retrieve a single record
L by primary key when you expect it to be found.
S Example
L   # bad
L   User.where(id: id).take!
L   User.find_by_id!(id)
L   User.find_by!(id: id)
L 
L   # good
L   User.find(id)
C Rails/FindByOrAssignmentMemoization
S Details
L Avoid memoizing `find_by` results with `||=`.
L 
L It is common to see code that attempts to memoize `find_by` result by `||=`,
L but `find_by` may return `nil`, in which case it is not memoized as intended.
L 
L NOTE: Respecting the object shapes introduced in Ruby 3.2, instance variables used
L for memoization that are initialized at object creation are ignored.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because detected `find_by` may not be Active Record's method,
L   or the code may have a different purpose than memoization.
S Example
L   # bad - exclusively doing memoization
L   def current_user
L     @current_user ||= User.find_by(id: session[:user_id])
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def current_user
L     return @current_user if defined?(@current_user)
L 
L     @current_user = User.find_by(id: session[:user_id])
L   end
L 
L   # bad - method contains other code
L   def current_user
L     @current_user ||= User.find_by(id: session[:user_id])
L     @current_user.do_something
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def current_user
L     if defined?(@current_user)
L       @current_user
L     else
L       @current_user = User.find_by(id: session[:user_id])
L     end
L     @current_user.do_something
L   end
C Rails/FindEach
S Details
L Identifies usages of `all.each` and change them to use `all.find_each` instead.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe if the receiver object is not an Active Record object.
L   Also, `all.each` returns an `Array` instance and `all.find_each` returns nil,
L   so the return values are different.
S Example
L   # bad
L   User.all.each
L 
L   # good
L   User.all.find_each
S Example: AllowedMethods: ['order']
L   # good
L   User.order(:foo).each
S Example: AllowedPattern: ['order']
L   # good
L   User.order(:foo).each
C Rails/FreezeTime
S Details
L Identifies usages of `travel_to` with an argument of the current time and
L change them to use `freeze_time` instead.
S Safety
L   This cop’s autocorrection is unsafe because `freeze_time` just delegates to
L   `travel_to` with a default `Time.now`, it is not strictly equivalent to `Time.now`
L   if the argument of `travel_to` is the current time considering time zone.
S Example
L   # bad
L   travel_to(Time.now)
L   travel_to(Time.new)
L   travel_to(DateTime.now)
L   travel_to(Time.current)
L   travel_to(Time.zone.now)
L   travel_to(Time.now.in_time_zone)
L   travel_to(Time.current.to_time)
L 
L   # good
L   freeze_time
C Rails/HasAndBelongsToMany
S Details
L Checks for the use of the has_and_belongs_to_many macro.
S Example
L   # bad
L   # has_and_belongs_to_many :ingredients
L 
L   # good
L   # has_many :ingredients, through: :recipe_ingredients
C Rails/HasManyOrHasOneDependent
S Details
L Looks for `has_many` or `has_one` associations that don't
L specify a `:dependent` option.
L 
L It doesn't register an offense if `:through` or `dependent: nil`
L is specified, or if the model is read-only.
S Example
L   # bad
L   class User < ActiveRecord::Base
L     has_many :comments
L     has_one :avatar
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class User < ActiveRecord::Base
L     has_many :comments, dependent: :restrict_with_exception
L     has_one :avatar, dependent: :destroy
L     has_many :articles, dependent: nil
L     has_many :patients, through: :appointments
L   end
L 
L   class User < ActiveRecord::Base
L     has_many :comments
L     has_one :avatar
L 
L     def readonly?
L       true
L     end
L   end
C Rails/HelperInstanceVariable
S Details
L Checks for use of the helper methods which reference
L instance variables.
L 
L Relying on instance variables makes it difficult to reuse helper
L methods.
L 
L If it seems awkward to explicitly pass in each dependent
L variable, consider moving the behavior elsewhere, for
L example to a model, decorator or presenter.
L 
L Provided that an instance variable belongs to a class,
L an offense will not be registered.
S Example
L   # bad
L   def welcome_message
L     "Hello #{@user.name}"
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def welcome_message(user)
L     "Hello #{user.name}"
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   module ButtonHelper
L     class Welcome
L       def initialize(text:)
L         @text = text
L       end
L     end
L 
L     def welcome(**)
L       render Welcome.new(**)
L     end
L   end
C Rails/HttpPositionalArguments
S Details
L Identifies usages of http methods like `get`, `post`,
L `put`, `patch` without the usage of keyword arguments in your tests and
L change them to use keyword args. This cop only applies to Rails >= 5.
L If you are running Rails < 5 you should disable the
L Rails/HttpPositionalArguments cop or set your TargetRailsVersion in your
L .rubocop.yml file to 4.2.
L 
L NOTE: It does not detect any cases where `include Rack::Test::Methods` is used
L which makes the http methods incompatible behavior.
S Example
L   # bad
L   get :new, { user_id: 1}
L 
L   # good
L   get :new, params: { user_id: 1 }
L   get :new, **options
C Rails/HttpStatus
S Details
L Enforces use of symbolic or numeric value to define HTTP status.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: symbolic (default)
L   # bad
L   render :foo, status: 200
L   render :foo, status: '200'
L   render json: { foo: 'bar' }, status: 200
L   render plain: 'foo/bar', status: 304
L   redirect_to root_url, status: 301
L   head 200
L   assert_response 200
L   assert_redirected_to '/some/path', status: 301
L 
L   # good
L   render :foo, status: :ok
L   render json: { foo: 'bar' }, status: :ok
L   render plain: 'foo/bar', status: :not_modified
L   redirect_to root_url, status: :moved_permanently
L   head :ok
L   assert_response :ok
L   assert_redirected_to '/some/path', status: :moved_permanently
S Example: EnforcedStyle: numeric
L   # bad
L   render :foo, status: :ok
L   render json: { foo: 'bar' }, status: :not_found
L   render plain: 'foo/bar', status: :not_modified
L   redirect_to root_url, status: :moved_permanently
L   head :ok
L   assert_response :ok
L   assert_redirected_to '/some/path', status: :moved_permanently
L 
L   # good
L   render :foo, status: 200
L   render json: { foo: 'bar' }, status: 404
L   render plain: 'foo/bar', status: 304
L   redirect_to root_url, status: 301
L   head 200
L   assert_response 200
L   assert_redirected_to '/some/path', status: 301
C Rails/HttpStatusNameConsistency
S Details
L Enforces consistency by using the current HTTP status names.
S Example
L   # bad
L   render json: { error: "Invalid data" }, status: :unprocessable_entity
L   head :payload_too_large
L 
L   # good
L   render json: { error: "Invalid data" }, status: :unprocessable_content
L   head :content_too_large
C Rails/I18nLazyLookup
S Details
L Checks for places where I18n "lazy" lookup can be used.
L 
L This cop has two different enforcement modes. When the EnforcedStyle
L is `lazy` (the default), explicit lookups are added as offenses.
L 
L When the EnforcedStyle is `explicit` then lazy lookups are added as
L offenses.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: lazy (default)
L   # en.yml
L   # en:
L   #   books:
L   #     create:
L   #       success: Book created!
L 
L   # bad
L   class BooksController < ApplicationController
L     def create
L       # ...
L       redirect_to books_url, notice: t('books.create.success')
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class BooksController < ApplicationController
L     def create
L       # ...
L       redirect_to books_url, notice: t('.success')
L     end
L   end
S Example: EnforcedStyle: explicit
L   # bad
L   class BooksController < ApplicationController
L     def create
L       # ...
L       redirect_to books_url, notice: t('.success')
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class BooksController < ApplicationController
L     def create
L       # ...
L       redirect_to books_url, notice: t('books.create.success')
L     end
L   end
C Rails/I18nLocaleAssignment
S Details
L Checks for the use of `I18n.locale=` method.
L 
L The `locale` attribute persists for the rest of the Ruby runtime, potentially causing
L unexpected behavior at a later time.
L Using `I18n.with_locale` ensures the code passed in the block is the only place `I18n.locale` is affected.
L It eliminates the possibility of a `locale` sticking around longer than intended.
S Example
L   # bad
L   I18n.locale = :fr
L 
L   # good
L   I18n.with_locale(:fr) do
L   end
C Rails/I18nLocaleTexts
S Details
L Enforces use of I18n and locale files instead of locale specific strings.
S Example
L   # bad
L   class User < ApplicationRecord
L     validates :email, presence: { message: "must be present" }
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   # config/locales/en.yml
L   # en:
L   #   activerecord:
L   #     errors:
L   #       models:
L   #         user:
L   #           blank: "must be present"
L 
L   class User < ApplicationRecord
L     validates :email, presence: true
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   class PostsController < ApplicationController
L     def create
L       # ...
L       redirect_to root_path, notice: "Post created!"
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   # config/locales/en.yml
L   # en:
L   #   posts:
L   #     create:
L   #       success: "Post created!"
L 
L   class PostsController < ApplicationController
L     def create
L       # ...
L       redirect_to root_path, notice: t(".success")
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   class PostsController < ApplicationController
L     def update
L       # ...
L       redirect_back_or_to root_path, alert: "Failed to update!"
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   # config/locales/en.yml
L   # en:
L   #   posts:
L   #     update:
L   #       failure: "Failed to update!"
L 
L   class PostsController < ApplicationController
L     def update
L       # ...
L       redirect_back_or_to root_path, alert: t(".failure")
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   class UserMailer < ApplicationMailer
L     def welcome(user)
L       mail(to: user.email, subject: "Welcome to My Awesome Site")
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   # config/locales/en.yml
L   # en:
L   #   user_mailer:
L   #     welcome:
L   #       subject: "Welcome to My Awesome Site"
L 
L   class UserMailer < ApplicationMailer
L     def welcome(user)
L       mail(to: user.email)
L     end
L   end
C Rails/IgnoredColumnsAssignment
S Details
L Looks for assignments of `ignored_columns` that may override previous
L assignments.
L 
L Overwriting previous assignments is usually a mistake, since it will
L un-ignore the first set of columns. Since duplicate column names is not
L a problem, it is better to simply append to the list.
S Example
L   # bad
L   class User < ActiveRecord::Base
L     self.ignored_columns = [:one]
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   class User < ActiveRecord::Base
L     self.ignored_columns = [:one, :two]
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class User < ActiveRecord::Base
L     self.ignored_columns += [:one, :two]
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class User < ActiveRecord::Base
L     self.ignored_columns += [:one]
L     self.ignored_columns += [:two]
L   end
C Rails/IgnoredSkipActionFilterOption
S Details
L Checks that `if` and `only` (or `except`) are not used together
L as options of `skip_*` action filter.
L 
L The `if` option will be ignored when `if` and `only` are used together.
L Similarly, the `except` option will be ignored when `if` and `except`
L are used together.
S Example
L   # bad
L   class MyPageController < ApplicationController
L     skip_before_action :login_required,
L       only: :show, if: :trusted_origin?
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class MyPageController < ApplicationController
L     skip_before_action :login_required,
L       if: -> { trusted_origin? && action_name == "show" }
L   end
S Example
L   # bad
L   class MyPageController < ApplicationController
L     skip_before_action :login_required,
L       except: :admin, if: :trusted_origin?
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class MyPageController < ApplicationController
L     skip_before_action :login_required,
L       if: -> { trusted_origin? && action_name != "admin" }
L   end
C Rails/IndexBy
S Details
L Looks for uses of `each_with_object({}) { ... }`,
L `map { ... }.to_h`, and `Hash[map { ... }]` that are transforming
L an enumerable into a hash where the values are the original elements.
L Rails provides the `index_by` method for this purpose.
S Example
L   # bad
L   [1, 2, 3].each_with_object({}) { |el, h| h[foo(el)] = el }
L   [1, 2, 3].to_h { |el| [foo(el), el] }
L   [1, 2, 3].map { |el| [foo(el), el] }.to_h
L   Hash[[1, 2, 3].collect { |el| [foo(el), el] }]
L 
L   # good
L   [1, 2, 3].index_by { |el| foo(el) }
C Rails/IndexWith
S Details
L Looks for uses of `each_with_object({}) { ... }`,
L `map { ... }.to_h`, and `Hash[map { ... }]` that are transforming
L an enumerable into a hash where the keys are the original elements.
L Rails provides the `index_with` method for this purpose.
S Safety
L   This cop is marked as unsafe autocorrection, because `nil.to_h` returns {}
L   but `nil.with_index` throws `NoMethodError`. Therefore, autocorrection is not
L   compatible if the receiver is nil.
S Example
L   # bad
L   [1, 2, 3].each_with_object({}) { |el, h| h[el] = foo(el) }
L   [1, 2, 3].to_h { |el| [el, foo(el)] }
L   [1, 2, 3].map { |el| [el, foo(el)] }.to_h
L   Hash[[1, 2, 3].collect { |el| [el, foo(el)] }]
L 
L   # good
L   [1, 2, 3].index_with { |el| foo(el) }
C Rails/Inquiry
S Details
L Checks that Active Support's `inquiry` method is not used.
S Example
L   # bad - String#inquiry
L   ruby = 'two'.inquiry
L   ruby.two?
L 
L   # good
L   ruby = 'two'
L   ruby == 'two'
L 
L   # bad - Array#inquiry
L   pets = %w(cat dog).inquiry
L   pets.gopher?
L 
L   # good
L   pets = %w(cat dog)
L   pets.include? 'cat'
C Rails/InverseOf
S Details
L Looks for has_(one|many) and belongs_to associations where
L Active Record can't automatically determine the inverse association
L because of a scope or the options used. Using the blog with order scope
L example below, traversing the a Blog's association in both directions
L with `blog.posts.first.blog` would cause the `blog` to be loaded from
L the database twice.
L 
L `:inverse_of` must be manually specified for Active Record to use the
L associated object in memory, or set to `false` to opt-out. Note that
L setting `nil` does not stop Active Record from trying to determine the
L inverse automatically, and is not considered a valid value for this.
S Example
L   # good
L   class Blog < ApplicationRecord
L     has_many :posts
L   end
L 
L   class Post < ApplicationRecord
L     belongs_to :blog
L   end
S Example
L   # bad
L   class Blog < ApplicationRecord
L     has_many :posts, -> { order(published_at: :desc) }
L   end
L 
L   class Post < ApplicationRecord
L     belongs_to :blog
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class Blog < ApplicationRecord
L     has_many(:posts,
L              -> { order(published_at: :desc) },
L              inverse_of: :blog)
L   end
L 
L   class Post < ApplicationRecord
L     belongs_to :blog
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class Blog < ApplicationRecord
L     with_options inverse_of: :blog do
L       has_many :posts, -> { order(published_at: :desc) }
L     end
L   end
L 
L   class Post < ApplicationRecord
L     belongs_to :blog
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   # When you don't want to use the inverse association.
L   class Blog < ApplicationRecord
L     has_many(:posts,
L              -> { order(published_at: :desc) },
L              inverse_of: false)
L   end
S Example
L   # bad
L   class Picture < ApplicationRecord
L     belongs_to :imageable, polymorphic: true
L   end
L 
L   class Employee < ApplicationRecord
L     has_many :pictures, as: :imageable
L   end
L 
L   class Product < ApplicationRecord
L     has_many :pictures, as: :imageable
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class Picture < ApplicationRecord
L     belongs_to :imageable, polymorphic: true
L   end
L 
L   class Employee < ApplicationRecord
L     has_many :pictures, as: :imageable, inverse_of: :imageable
L   end
L 
L   class Product < ApplicationRecord
L     has_many :pictures, as: :imageable, inverse_of: :imageable
L   end
S Example
L   # bad
L   # However, RuboCop can not detect this pattern...
L   class Physician < ApplicationRecord
L     has_many :appointments
L     has_many :patients, through: :appointments
L   end
L 
L   class Appointment < ApplicationRecord
L     belongs_to :physician
L     belongs_to :patient
L   end
L 
L   class Patient < ApplicationRecord
L     has_many :appointments
L     has_many :physicians, through: :appointments
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class Physician < ApplicationRecord
L     has_many :appointments
L     has_many :patients, through: :appointments
L   end
L 
L   class Appointment < ApplicationRecord
L     belongs_to :physician, inverse_of: :appointments
L     belongs_to :patient, inverse_of: :appointments
L   end
L 
L   class Patient < ApplicationRecord
L     has_many :appointments
L     has_many :physicians, through: :appointments
L   end
S Example: IgnoreScopes: false (default)
L   # bad
L   class Blog < ApplicationRecord
L     has_many :posts, -> { order(published_at: :desc) }
L   end
S Example: IgnoreScopes: true
L   # good
L   class Blog < ApplicationRecord
L     has_many :posts, -> { order(published_at: :desc) }
L   end
C Rails/LexicallyScopedActionFilter
S Details
L Checks that methods specified in the filter's `only` or
L `except` options are defined within the same class or module.
S Safety
L   You can technically specify methods of superclass or methods added by
L   mixins on the filter, but these can confuse developers. If you specify
L   methods that are defined in other classes or modules, you should
L   define the filter in that class or module.
L 
L   If you rely on behavior defined in the superclass actions, you must
L   remember to invoke `super` in the subclass actions.
S Example
L   # bad
L   class LoginController < ApplicationController
L     before_action :require_login, only: %i[index settings logout]
L 
L     def index
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class LoginController < ApplicationController
L     before_action :require_login, only: %i[index settings logout]
L 
L     def index
L     end
L 
L     def settings
L     end
L 
L     def logout
L     end
L   end
S Example
L   # bad
L   module FooMixin
L     extend ActiveSupport::Concern
L 
L     included do
L       before_action proc { authenticate }, only: :foo
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   module FooMixin
L     extend ActiveSupport::Concern
L 
L     included do
L       before_action proc { authenticate }, only: :foo
L     end
L 
L     def foo
L       # something
L     end
L   end
S Example
L   class ContentController < ApplicationController
L     def update
L       @content.update(content_attributes)
L     end
L   end
L 
L   class ArticlesController < ContentController
L     before_action :load_article, only: [:update]
L 
L     # the cop requires this method, but it relies on behavior defined
L     # in the superclass, so needs to invoke `super`
L     def update
L       super
L     end
L 
L     private
L 
L     def load_article
L       @content = Article.find(params[:article_id])
L     end
L   end
C Rails/LinkToBlank
S Details
L Checks for calls to `link_to`, `link_to_if`, and `link_to_unless` methods that contain a
L `target: '_blank'` but no `rel: 'noopener'`. This can be a security
L risk as the loaded page will have control over the previous page
L and could change its location for phishing purposes.
L 
L The option `rel: 'noreferrer'` also blocks this behavior
L and removes the http-referrer header.
S Example
L   # bad
L   link_to 'Click here', url, target: '_blank'
L 
L   # good
L   link_to 'Click here', url, target: '_blank', rel: 'noopener'
L 
L   # good
L   link_to 'Click here', url, target: '_blank', rel: 'noreferrer'
C Rails/MailerName
S Details
L Enforces that mailer names end with `Mailer` suffix.
L 
L Without the `Mailer` suffix it isn't immediately apparent what's a mailer
L and which views are related to the mailer.
S Safety
L   This cop's autocorrection is unsafe because renaming a constant is
L   always an unsafe operation.
S Example
L   # bad
L   class User < ActionMailer::Base
L   end
L 
L   class User < ApplicationMailer
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class UserMailer < ActionMailer::Base
L   end
L 
L   class UserMailer < ApplicationMailer
L   end
C Rails/MatchRoute
S Details
L Identifies places where defining routes with `match`
L can be replaced with a specific HTTP method.
L 
L Don't use `match` to define any routes unless there is a need to map multiple request types
L among [:get, :post, :patch, :put, :delete] to a single action using the `:via` option.
S Example
L   # bad
L   match ':controller/:action/:id'
L   match 'photos/:id', to: 'photos#show', via: :get
L 
L   # good
L   get ':controller/:action/:id'
L   get 'photos/:id', to: 'photos#show'
L   match 'photos/:id', to: 'photos#show', via: [:get, :post]
L   match 'photos/:id', to: 'photos#show', via: :all
C Rails/MigrationClassName
S Details
L Makes sure that each migration file defines a migration class
L whose name matches the file name.
L (e.g. `20220224111111_create_users.rb` should define `CreateUsers` class.)
S Example
L   # db/migrate/20220224111111_create_users.rb
L 
L   # bad
L   class SellBooks < ActiveRecord::Migration[7.0]
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class CreateUsers < ActiveRecord::Migration[7.0]
L   end
C Rails/MultipleRoutePaths
S Details
L Checks for mapping a route with multiple paths, which is deprecated and will be removed in Rails 8.1.
S Example
L   # bad
L   get '/users', '/other_path', to: 'users#index'
L 
L   # good
L   get '/users', to: 'users#index'
L   get '/other_path', to: 'users#index'
C Rails/NegateInclude
S Details
L Enforces the use of `collection.exclude?(obj)`
L over `!collection.include?(obj)`.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because false positive will occur for
L   receiver objects that do not have an `exclude?` method. (e.g. `IPAddr`)
S Example
L   # bad
L   !array.include?(2)
L   !hash.include?(:key)
L 
L   # good
L   array.exclude?(2)
L   hash.exclude?(:key)
C Rails/NotNullColumn
S Details
L Checks for add_column calls with a NOT NULL constraint without a default
L value.
L 
L This cop only applies when adding a column to an existing table, since
L existing records will not have a value for the new column. New tables
L can freely use NOT NULL columns without defaults, since there are no
L records that could violate the constraint.
L 
L If you need to add a NOT NULL column to an existing table, you must add
L it as nullable first, back-fill the data, and then use
L `change_column_null`. Alternatively, you could add the column with a
L default first to have the database automatically backfill existing rows,
L and then use `change_column_default` to remove the default.
L 
L `TEXT` cannot have a default value in MySQL.
L The cop will automatically detect an adapter from `development`
L environment in `config/database.yml` or the environment variable
L `DATABASE_URL` when the `Database` option is not set. If the database
L is MySQL, this cop ignores offenses for `TEXT` columns.
S Example
L   # bad
L   add_column :users, :name, :string, null: false
L   add_reference :products, :category, null: false
L   change_table :users do |t|
L     t.string :name, null: false
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   add_column :users, :name, :string, null: true
L   add_column :users, :name, :string, null: false, default: ''
L   change_table :users do |t|
L     t.string :name, null: false, default: ''
L   end
L   add_reference :products, :category
L   change_column_null :products, :category_id, false
C Rails/OrderArguments
S Details
L Prefer symbol arguments over strings in `order` method.
S Safety
L   Cop is unsafe because the receiver might not be an Active Record query.
S Example
L   # bad
L   User.order('name')
L   User.order('name DESC')
L 
L   # good
L   User.order(:name)
L   User.order(name: :desc)
C Rails/OrderById
S Details
L Checks for places where ordering by `id` column is used.
L 
L Don't use the `id` column for ordering. The sequence of ids is not guaranteed
L to be in any particular order, despite often (incidentally) being chronological.
L Use a timestamp column to order chronologically. As a bonus the intent is clearer.
L 
L NOTE: Make sure the changed order column does not introduce performance
L bottlenecks and appropriate database indexes are added.
S Example
L   # bad
L   scope :chronological, -> { order(id: :asc) }
L   scope :chronological, -> { order(primary_key => :asc) }
L 
L   # good
L   scope :chronological, -> { order(created_at: :asc) }
C Rails/Output
S Details
L Checks for the use of output calls like puts and print
S Safety
L   This cop's autocorrection is unsafe because depending on the Rails log level configuration,
L   changing from `puts` to `Rails.logger.debug` could result in no output being shown.
S Example
L   # bad
L   puts 'A debug message'
L   pp 'A debug message'
L   print 'A debug message'
L 
L   # good
L   Rails.logger.debug 'A debug message'
C Rails/OutputSafety
S Details
L Checks for the use of output safety calls like `html_safe`,
L `raw`, and `safe_concat`. These methods do not escape content. They
L simply return a SafeBuffer containing the content as is. Instead,
L use `safe_join` to join content and escape it and concat to
L concatenate content and escape it, ensuring its safety.
S Example
L   user_content = "<b>hi</b>"
L 
L   # bad
L   "<p>#{user_content}</p>".html_safe
L   # => ActiveSupport::SafeBuffer "<p><b>hi</b></p>"
L 
L   # good
L   content_tag(:p, user_content)
L   # => ActiveSupport::SafeBuffer "<p>&lt;b&gt;hi&lt;/b&gt;</p>"
L 
L   # bad
L   out = ""
L   out << "<li>#{user_content}</li>"
L   out << "<li>#{user_content}</li>"
L   out.html_safe
L   # => ActiveSupport::SafeBuffer "<li><b>hi</b></li><li><b>hi</b></li>"
L 
L   # good
L   out = []
L   out << content_tag(:li, user_content)
L   out << content_tag(:li, user_content)
L   safe_join(out)
L   # => ActiveSupport::SafeBuffer
L   #    "<li>&lt;b&gt;hi&lt;/b&gt;</li><li>&lt;b&gt;hi&lt;/b&gt;</li>"
L 
L   # bad
L   out = "<h1>trusted content</h1>".html_safe
L   out.safe_concat(user_content)
L   # => ActiveSupport::SafeBuffer "<h1>trusted_content</h1><b>hi</b>"
L 
L   # good
L   out = "<h1>trusted content</h1>".html_safe
L   out.concat(user_content)
L   # => ActiveSupport::SafeBuffer
L   #    "<h1>trusted_content</h1>&lt;b&gt;hi&lt;/b&gt;"
L 
L   # safe, though maybe not good style
L   out = "trusted content"
L   result = out.concat(user_content)
L   # => String "trusted content<b>hi</b>"
L   # because when rendered in ERB the String will be escaped:
L   # <%= result %>
L   # => trusted content&lt;b&gt;hi&lt;/b&gt;
L 
L   # bad
L   (user_content + " " + content_tag(:span, user_content)).html_safe
L   # => ActiveSupport::SafeBuffer "<b>hi</b> <span><b>hi</b></span>"
L 
L   # good
L   safe_join([user_content, " ", content_tag(:span, user_content)])
L   # => ActiveSupport::SafeBuffer
L   #    "&lt;b&gt;hi&lt;/b&gt; <span>&lt;b&gt;hi&lt;/b&gt;</span>"
C Rails/Pick
S Details
L Enforces the use of `pick` over `pluck(...).first`.
L 
L Using `pluck` followed by `first` creates an intermediate array, which
L `pick` avoids. When called on an Active Record relation, `pick` adds a
L limit to the query so that only one value is fetched from the database.
L 
L Note that when `pick` is added to a relation with an existing limit, it
L causes a subquery to be added. In most cases this is undesirable, and
L care should be taken while resolving this violation.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because `pluck` is defined on both `ActiveRecord::Relation` and `Enumerable`,
L   whereas `pick` is only defined on `ActiveRecord::Relation` in Rails 6.0. This was addressed
L   in Rails 6.1 via rails/rails#38760, at which point the cop is safe.
L 
L   See: https://github.com/rubocop/rubocop-rails/pull/249
S Example
L   # bad
L   Model.pluck(:a).first
L   [{ a: :b, c: :d }].pluck(:a, :b).first
L 
L   # good
L   Model.pick(:a)
L   [{ a: :b, c: :d }].pick(:a, :b)
C Rails/Pluck
S Details
L Enforces the use of `pluck` over `map`.
L 
L `pluck` can be used instead of `map` to extract a single key from each
L element in an enumerable. When called on an Active Record relation, it
L results in a more efficient query that only selects the necessary key.
L 
L NOTE: If the receiver's relation is not loaded and `pluck` is used inside an iteration,
L it may result in N+1 queries because `pluck` queries the database on each iteration.
L This cop ignores offenses for `map/collect` when they are suspected to be part of an iteration
L to prevent such potential issues.
L 
L [source,ruby]
L ----
L users = User.all
L 5.times do
L   users.map { |user| user[:foo] } # Only one query is executed
L end
L 
L users = User.all
L 5.times do
L   users.pluck(:id) # A query is executed on every iteration
L end
L ----
L 
L If a method call has no receiver, like `do_something { users.map { |user| user[:foo] }`,
L it is not considered part of an iteration and will be detected.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because model can use column aliases.
L 
L   [source,ruby]
L   ----
L   # Original code
L   User.select('name AS nickname').map { |user| user[:nickname] } # => array of nicknames
L 
L   # After autocorrection
L   User.select('name AS nickname').pluck(:nickname) # => raises ActiveRecord::StatementInvalid
L   ----
S Example
L   # bad
L   Post.published.map { |post| post[:title] }
L   [{ a: :b, c: :d }].collect { |el| el[:a] }
L 
L   # good
L   Post.published.pluck(:title)
L   [{ a: :b, c: :d }].pluck(:a)
C Rails/PluckId
S Details
L Enforces the use of `ids` over `pluck(:id)` and `pluck(primary_key)`.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe if the receiver object is not an Active Record object.
S Example
L   # bad
L   User.pluck(:id)
L   user.posts.pluck(:id)
L 
L   def self.user_ids
L     pluck(primary_key)
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   User.ids
L   user.posts.ids
L 
L   def self.user_ids
L     ids
L   end
C Rails/PluckInWhere
S Details
L Identifies places where `pluck` is used in `where` query methods
L and can be replaced with `select`.
L 
L Since `pluck` is an eager method and hits the database immediately,
L using `select` helps to avoid additional database queries by running as
L a subquery.
L 
L This cop has two modes of enforcement. When the `EnforcedStyle` is set
L to `conservative` (the default), only calls to `pluck` on a constant
L (e.g. a model class) within `where` are considered offenses.
S Safety
L   When `EnforcedStyle` is set to `aggressive`, all calls to `pluck`
L   within `where` are considered offenses. This might lead to false
L   positives because the check cannot distinguish between calls to
L   `pluck` on an `ActiveRecord::Relation` instance and calls to `pluck`
L   on an `Array` instance.
L 
L   Additionally, when using a subquery with the SQL `IN` operator,
L   databases like PostgreSQL and MySQL can't optimize complex queries as
L   well. They need to scan all records of the outer table against the
L   subquery result sequentially, rather than using an index. This can
L   cause significant performance issues compared to writing the query
L   differently or using `pluck`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   Post.where(user_id: User.active.pluck(:id))
L   Post.where(user_id: User.active.ids)
L   Post.where.not(user_id: User.active.pluck(:id))
L 
L   # good
L   Post.where(user_id: User.active.select(:id))
L   Post.where(user_id: active_users.select(:id))
L   Post.where.not(user_id: active_users.select(:id))
S Example: EnforcedStyle: conservative (default)
L   # good
L   Post.where(user_id: active_users.pluck(:id))
S Example: EnforcedStyle: aggressive
L   # bad
L   Post.where(user_id: active_users.pluck(:id))
C Rails/PluralizationGrammar
S Details
L Checks for correct grammar when using ActiveSupport's
L core extensions to the numeric classes.
S Example
L   # bad
L   3.day.ago
L   1.months.ago
L   5.megabyte
L   1.gigabytes
L 
L   # good
L   3.days.ago
L   1.month.ago
L   5.megabytes
L   1.gigabyte
C Rails/Presence
S Details
L Checks code that can be written more easily using
L `Object#presence` defined by Active Support.
S Example
L   # bad
L   a.present? ? a : nil
L 
L   # bad
L   !a.present? ? nil : a
L 
L   # bad
L   a.blank? ? nil : a
L 
L   # bad
L   !a.blank? ? a : nil
L 
L   # good
L   a.presence
S Example
L   # bad
L   a.present? ? a : b
L 
L   # bad
L   !a.present? ? b : a
L 
L   # bad
L   a.blank? ? b : a
L 
L   # bad
L   !a.blank? ? a : b
L 
L   # good
L   a.presence || b
S Example
L   # bad
L   a.present? ? a.foo : nil
L 
L   # bad
L   !a.present? ? nil : a.foo
L 
L   # bad
L   a.blank? ? nil : a.foo
L 
L   # bad
L   !a.blank? ? a.foo : nil
L 
L   # good
L   a.presence&.foo
S Example
L   # good
L   a.present? ? a[1] : nil
L 
L   # good
L   a[:key] = value if a.present?
L 
L   # good
L   a.present? ? a > 1 : nil
L 
L   # good
L   a <= 0 if a.present?
L 
L   # good
L   a << "bar" if a.present?
C Rails/Present
S Details
L Checks for code that can be written with simpler conditionals
L using `Object#present?` defined by Active Support.
L 
L Interaction with `Style/UnlessElse`:
L The configuration of `NotBlank` will not produce an offense in the
L context of `unless else` if `Style/UnlessElse` is enabled. This is
L to prevent interference between the autocorrection of the two cops.
S Example: NotNilAndNotEmpty: true (default)
L   # Converts usages of `!nil? && !empty?` to `present?`
L 
L   # bad
L   !foo.nil? && !foo.empty?
L 
L   # bad
L   foo != nil && !foo.empty?
L 
L   # good
L   foo.present?
S Example: NotBlank: true (default)
L   # Converts usages of `!blank?` to `present?`
L 
L   # bad
L   !foo.blank?
L 
L   # bad
L   not foo.blank?
L 
L   # good
L   foo.present?
S Example: UnlessBlank: true (default)
L   # Converts usages of `unless blank?` to `if present?`
L 
L   # bad
L   something unless foo.blank?
L 
L   # good
L   something if foo.present?
C Rails/RakeEnvironment
S Details
L Checks for Rake tasks without the `:environment` task
L dependency. The `:environment` task loads application code for other
L Rake tasks. Without it, tasks cannot make use of application code like
L models.
L 
L You can ignore the offense if the task satisfies at least one of the
L following conditions:
L 
L * The task does not need application code.
L * The task invokes the `:environment` task.
S Safety
L   Probably not a problem in most cases, but it is possible that calling `:environment` task
L   will break a behavior. It's also slower. E.g. some task that only needs one gem to be
L   loaded to run will run significantly faster without loading the whole application.
S Example
L   # bad
L   task :foo do
L     do_something
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   task foo: :environment do
L     do_something
L   end
C Rails/ReadWriteAttribute
S Details
L Checks for the use of the `read_attribute` or `write_attribute`
L methods and recommends square brackets instead.
L 
L If an attribute is missing from the instance (for example, when
L initialized by a partial `select`) then `read_attribute`
L will return nil, but square brackets will raise
L an `ActiveModel::MissingAttributeError`.
L 
L Explicitly raising an error in this situation is preferable, and that
L is why rubocop recommends using square brackets.
S Example
L   # bad
L   x = read_attribute(:attr)
L   write_attribute(:attr, val)
L 
L   # good
L   x = self[:attr]
L   self[:attr] = val
L 
L When called from within a method with the same name as the attribute,
L `read_attribute` and `write_attribute` must be used to prevent an
L infinite loop:
S Example
L   # good
L   def foo
L     bar || read_attribute(:foo)
L   end
C Rails/RedirectBackOrTo
S Details
L Checks for uses of `redirect_back(fallback_location: ...)` and
L suggests using `redirect_back_or_to(...)` instead.
L 
L `redirect_back(fallback_location: ...)` was soft deprecated in Rails 7.0 and
L `redirect_back_or_to` was introduced as a replacement.
S Example
L   # bad
L   redirect_back(fallback_location: root_path)
L 
L   # good
L   redirect_back_or_to(root_path)
L 
L   # bad
L   redirect_back(fallback_location: root_path, allow_other_host: false)
L 
L   # good
L   redirect_back_or_to(root_path, allow_other_host: false)
C Rails/RedundantActiveRecordAllMethod
S Details
L Detect redundant `all` used as a receiver for Active Record query methods.
L 
L For the methods `delete_all` and `destroy_all`, this cop will only check cases where the receiver is a model.
L It will ignore cases where the receiver is an association (e.g., `user.articles.all.delete_all`).
L This is because omitting `all` from an association changes the methods
L from `ActiveRecord::Relation` to `ActiveRecord::Associations::CollectionProxy`,
L which can affect their behavior.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because false positives will occur if the receiver is not an Active Record object.
S Example
L   # bad
L   User.all.find(id)
L   User.all.order(:created_at)
L   users.all.where(id: ids)
L   user.articles.all.order(:created_at)
L 
L   # good
L   User.find(id)
L   User.order(:created_at)
L   users.where(id: ids)
L   user.articles.order(:created_at)
S Example: AllowedReceivers: ['ActionMailer::Preview', 'ActiveSupport::TimeZone'] (default)
L   # good
L   ActionMailer::Preview.all.first
L   ActiveSupport::TimeZone.all.first
C Rails/RedundantAllowNil
S Details
L Checks Rails model validations for a redundant `allow_nil` when
L `allow_blank` is present.
S Example
L   # bad
L   validates :x, length: { is: 5 }, allow_nil: true, allow_blank: true
L 
L   # bad
L   validates :x, length: { is: 5 }, allow_nil: false, allow_blank: true
L 
L   # bad
L   validates :x, length: { is: 5 }, allow_nil: false, allow_blank: false
L 
L   # good
L   validates :x, length: { is: 5 }, allow_blank: true
L 
L   # good
L   validates :x, length: { is: 5 }, allow_blank: false
L 
L   # good
L   # Here, `nil` is valid but `''` is not
L   validates :x, length: { is: 5 }, allow_nil: true, allow_blank: false
C Rails/RedundantForeignKey
S Details
L Detects cases where the `:foreign_key` option on associations
L is redundant.
S Example
L   # bad
L   class Post
L     has_many :comments, foreign_key: 'post_id'
L   end
L 
L   class Comment
L     belongs_to :post, foreign_key: 'post_id'
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class Post
L     has_many :comments
L   end
L 
L   class Comment
L     belongs_to :author, foreign_key: 'user_id'
L   end
C Rails/RedundantPresenceValidationOnBelongsTo
S Details
L Since Rails 5.0 the default for `belongs_to` is `optional: false`
L unless `config.active_record.belongs_to_required_by_default` is
L explicitly set to `false`. The presence validator is added
L automatically, and explicit presence validation is redundant.
S Safety
L   This cop's autocorrection is unsafe because it changes the default error message
L   from "can't be blank" to "must exist".
S Example
L   # bad
L   belongs_to :user
L   validates :user, presence: true
L 
L   # bad
L   belongs_to :user
L   validates :user_id, presence: true
L 
L   # bad
L   belongs_to :author, foreign_key: :user_id
L   validates :user_id, presence: true
L 
L   # good
L   belongs_to :user
L 
L   # good
L   belongs_to :author, foreign_key: :user_id
C Rails/RedundantReceiverInWithOptions
S Details
L Checks for redundant receiver in `with_options`.
L Receiver is implicit from Rails 4.2 or higher.
S Example
L   # bad
L   class Account < ApplicationRecord
L     with_options dependent: :destroy do |assoc|
L       assoc.has_many :customers
L       assoc.has_many :products
L       assoc.has_many :invoices
L       assoc.has_many :expenses
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class Account < ApplicationRecord
L     with_options dependent: :destroy do
L       has_many :customers
L       has_many :products
L       has_many :invoices
L       has_many :expenses
L     end
L   end
S Example
L   # bad
L   with_options options: false do |merger|
L     merger.invoke(merger.something)
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   with_options options: false do
L     invoke(something)
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   client = Client.new
L   with_options options: false do |merger|
L     client.invoke(merger.something, something)
L   end
L 
L   # ok
L   # When `with_options` includes a block, all scoping scenarios
L   # cannot be evaluated. Thus, it is ok to include the explicit
L   # receiver.
L   with_options options: false do |merger|
L     merger.invoke
L     with_another_method do |another_receiver|
L       merger.invoke(another_receiver)
L     end
L   end
C Rails/RedundantTravelBack
S Details
L Checks for redundant `travel_back` calls.
L Since Rails 5.2, `travel_back` is automatically called at the end of the test.
S Example
L   # bad
L   def teardown
L     do_something
L     travel_back
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def teardown
L     do_something
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   after do
L     do_something
L     travel_back
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   after do
L     do_something
L   end
C Rails/ReflectionClassName
S Details
L Checks if the value of the option `class_name`, in
L the definition of a reflection is a string.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because it cannot be determined whether
L   constant or method return value specified to `class_name` is a string.
S Example
L   # bad
L   has_many :accounts, class_name: Account
L   has_many :accounts, class_name: Account.name
L 
L   # good
L   has_many :accounts, class_name: 'Account'
C Rails/RefuteMethods
S Details
L Use `assert_not` methods instead of `refute` methods.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: assert_not (default)
L   # bad
L   refute false
L   refute_empty [1, 2, 3]
L   refute_equal true, false
L 
L   # good
L   assert_not false
L   assert_not_empty [1, 2, 3]
L   assert_not_equal true, false
S Example: EnforcedStyle: refute
L   # bad
L   assert_not false
L   assert_not_empty [1, 2, 3]
L   assert_not_equal true, false
L 
L   # good
L   refute false
L   refute_empty [1, 2, 3]
L   refute_equal true, false
C Rails/RelativeDateConstant
S Details
L Checks whether constant value isn't relative date.
L Because the relative date will be evaluated only once.
S Safety
L   This cop's autocorrection is unsafe because its dependence on the constant is not corrected.
S Example
L   # bad
L   class SomeClass
L     EXPIRED_AT = 1.week.since
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class SomeClass
L     EXPIRES = 1.week
L 
L     def self.expired_at
L       EXPIRES.since
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class SomeClass
L     def self.expired_at
L       1.week.since
L     end
L   end
C Rails/RenderInline
S Details
L Looks for inline rendering within controller actions.
S Example
L   # bad
L   class ProductsController < ApplicationController
L     def index
L       render inline: "<% products.each do |p| %><p><%= p.name %></p><% end %>", type: :erb
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   # app/views/products/index.html.erb
L   # <% products.each do |p| %>
L   #   <p><%= p.name %></p>
L   # <% end %>
L 
L   class ProductsController < ApplicationController
L     def index
L     end
L   end
C Rails/RenderPlainText
S Details
L Identifies places where `render text:` can be
L replaced with `render plain:`.
S Example
L   # bad - explicit MIME type to `text/plain`
L   render text: 'Ruby!', content_type: 'text/plain'
L 
L   # good - short and precise
L   render plain: 'Ruby!'
L 
L   # good - explicit MIME type not to `text/plain`
L   render text: 'Ruby!', content_type: 'text/html'
S Example: ContentTypeCompatibility: true (default)
L   # good - sets MIME type to `text/html`
L   render text: 'Ruby!'
S Example: ContentTypeCompatibility: false
L   # bad - sets MIME type to `text/html`
L   render text: 'Ruby!'
C Rails/RequestReferer
S Details
L Checks for consistent uses of `request.referer` or
L `request.referrer`, depending on the cop's configuration.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: referer (default)
L   # bad
L   request.referrer
L 
L   # good
L   request.referer
S Example: EnforcedStyle: referrer
L   # bad
L   request.referer
L 
L   # good
L   request.referrer
C Rails/RequireDependency
S Details
L Checks for the usage of `require_dependency`.
L 
L `require_dependency` is an obsolete method for Rails applications running in Zeitwerk mode.
L In Zeitwerk mode, the semantics should match Ruby's and no need to be defensive with load order,
L just refer to classes and modules normally.
L If the constant name is dynamic, camelize if needed, and constantize.
L 
L Applications running in Zeitwerk mode should not use `require_dependency`.
L 
L NOTE: This cop is disabled by default. Please enable it if you are using Zeitwerk mode.
S Example
L   # bad
L   require_dependency 'some_lib'
C Rails/ResponseParsedBody
S Details
L Prefer `response.parsed_body` to custom parsing logic for `response.body`.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because Content-Type may not be `application/json` or `text/html`.
L   For example, the proprietary Content-Type provided by corporate entities such as
L   `application/vnd.github+json` is not supported at `response.parsed_body` by default,
L   so you still have to use `JSON.parse(response.body)` there.
S Example
L   # bad
L   JSON.parse(response.body)
L   Nokogiri::HTML(response.body)
L   Nokogiri::HTML4(response.body)
L   Nokogiri::HTML5(response.body)
L   Nokogiri::HTML.parse(response.body)
L   Nokogiri::HTML4.parse(response.body)
L   Nokogiri::HTML5.parse(response.body)
L   Nokogiri::HTML::Document.parse(response.body)
L   Nokogiri::HTML4::Document.parse(response.body)
L   Nokogiri::HTML5::Document.parse(response.body)
L 
L   # good
L   response.parsed_body
C Rails/ReversibleMigration
S Details
L Checks whether the change method of the migration file is
L reversible.
S Example
L   # bad
L   def change
L     change_table :users do |t|
L       t.remove :name
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def change
L     change_table :users do |t|
L       t.remove :name, type: :string
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def change
L     create_table :users do |t|
L       t.string :name
L     end
L   end
S Example
L   # drop_table
L 
L   # bad
L   def change
L     drop_table :users
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def change
L     drop_table :users do |t|
L       t.string :name
L     end
L   end
S Example
L   # change_column_default
L 
L   # bad
L   def change
L     change_column_default(:suppliers, :qualification, 'new')
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def change
L     change_column_default(:posts, :state, from: nil, to: "draft")
L   end
S Example
L   # remove_column
L 
L   # bad
L   def change
L     remove_column(:suppliers, :qualification)
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def change
L     remove_column(:suppliers, :qualification, :string)
L   end
S Example
L   # remove_foreign_key
L 
L   # bad
L   def change
L     remove_foreign_key :accounts, column: :owner_id
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def change
L     remove_foreign_key :accounts, :branches
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def change
L     remove_foreign_key :accounts, to_table: :branches
L   end
S Example
L   # change_table
L 
L   # bad
L   def change
L     change_table :users do |t|
L       t.remove :name
L       t.change_default :authorized, 1
L       t.change :price, :string
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def change
L     change_table :users do |t|
L       t.string :name
L     end
L   end
S Example
L   # remove_columns
L 
L   # bad
L   def change
L     remove_columns :users, :name, :email
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def change
L     reversible do |dir|
L       dir.up do
L         remove_columns :users, :name, :email
L       end
L 
L       dir.down do
L         add_column :users, :name, :string
L         add_column :users, :email, :string
L       end
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good (Rails >= 6.1, see https://github.com/rails/rails/pull/36589)
L   def change
L     remove_columns :users, :name, :email, type: :string
L   end
S Example
L   # remove_index
L 
L   # bad
L   def change
L     remove_index :users, name: :index_users_on_email
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def change
L     remove_index :users, :email
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def change
L     remove_index :users, column: :email
L   end
C Rails/ReversibleMigrationMethodDefinition
S Details
L Checks whether the migration implements
L either a `change` method or both an `up` and a `down`
L method.
S Example
L   # bad
L   class SomeMigration < ActiveRecord::Migration[6.0]
L     def up
L       # up migration
L     end
L 
L     # <----- missing down method
L   end
L 
L   class SomeMigration < ActiveRecord::Migration[6.0]
L     # <----- missing up method
L 
L     def down
L       # down migration
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class SomeMigration < ActiveRecord::Migration[6.0]
L     def change
L       # reversible migration
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class SomeMigration < ActiveRecord::Migration[6.0]
L     def up
L       # up migration
L     end
L 
L     def down
L       # down migration
L     end
L   end
C Rails/RootJoinChain
S Details
L Use a single `#join` instead of chaining on `Rails.root` or `Rails.public_path`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   Rails.root.join('db').join('schema.rb')
L   Rails.root.join('db').join(migrate).join('migration.rb')
L   Rails.public_path.join('path').join('file.pdf')
L   Rails.public_path.join('path').join(to).join('file.pdf')
L 
L   # good
L   Rails.root.join('db', 'schema.rb')
L   Rails.root.join('db', migrate, 'migration.rb')
L   Rails.public_path.join('path', 'file.pdf')
L   Rails.public_path.join('path', to, 'file.pdf')
C Rails/RootPathnameMethods
S Details
L Use `Rails.root` IO methods instead of passing it to `File`.
L 
L `Rails.root` is an instance of `Pathname`
L so we can apply many IO methods directly.
L 
L This cop works best when used together with
L `Style/FileRead`, `Style/FileWrite` and `Rails/RootJoinChain`.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe for autocorrection because ``Dir``'s `children`, `each_child`, `entries`, and `glob`
L   methods return string element, but these methods of `Pathname` return `Pathname` element.
S Example
L   # bad
L   File.open(Rails.root.join('db', 'schema.rb'))
L   File.open(Rails.root.join('db', 'schema.rb'), 'w')
L   File.read(Rails.root.join('db', 'schema.rb'))
L   File.binread(Rails.root.join('db', 'schema.rb'))
L   File.write(Rails.root.join('db', 'schema.rb'), content)
L   File.binwrite(Rails.root.join('db', 'schema.rb'), content)
L   Dir.glob(Rails.root.join('db', 'schema.rb'))
L   Dir[Rails.root.join('db', 'schema.rb')]
L 
L   # good
L   Rails.root.join('db', 'schema.rb').open
L   Rails.root.join('db', 'schema.rb').open('w')
L   Rails.root.join('db', 'schema.rb').read
L   Rails.root.join('db', 'schema.rb').binread
L   Rails.root.join('db', 'schema.rb').write(content)
L   Rails.root.join('db', 'schema.rb').binwrite(content)
L   Rails.root.glob("db/schema.rb")
C Rails/RootPublicPath
S Details
L Favor `Rails.public_path` over `Rails.root` with `'public'`
S Example
L   # bad
L   Rails.root.join('public')
L   Rails.root.join('public/file.pdf')
L   Rails.root.join('public', 'file.pdf')
L 
L   # good
L   Rails.public_path
L   Rails.public_path.join('file.pdf')
L   Rails.public_path.join('file.pdf')
C Rails/SafeNavigation
S Details
L Converts usages of `try!` to `&.`. It can also be configured
L to convert `try`. It will convert code to use safe navigation
L if the target Ruby version is set to 2.3+
S Example: ConvertTry: false (default)
L   # bad
L   foo.try!(:bar)
L   foo.try!(:bar, baz)
L   foo.try!(:bar) { |e| e.baz }
L   foo.try!(&:bar)
L 
L   foo.try!(:[], 0)
L   foo.try!(:==, baz)
L 
L   # good
L   foo.try(:bar)
L   foo.try(:bar, baz)
L   foo.try(:bar) { |e| e.baz }
L 
L   foo&.bar
L   foo&.bar(baz)
L   foo&.bar { |e| e.baz }
L   foo&.[](0)
L   foo&.==(baz)
S Example: ConvertTry: true
L   # bad
L   foo.try!(:bar)
L   foo.try!(:bar, baz)
L   foo.try!(:bar) { |e| e.baz }
L   foo.try!(&:bar)
L   foo.try(:bar)
L   foo.try(:bar, baz)
L   foo.try(:bar) { |e| e.baz }
L   foo.try(&:bar)
L   foo.try(:[], 0)
L   foo.try(:==, baz)
L 
L   # good
L   foo&.bar
L   foo&.bar(baz)
L   foo&.bar { |e| e.baz }
L   foo&.[](0)
L   foo&.==(baz)
C Rails/SafeNavigationWithBlank
S Details
L Checks to make sure safe navigation isn't used with `blank?` in
L a conditional.
S Safety
L   While the safe navigation operator is generally a good idea, when
L   checking `foo&.blank?` in a conditional, `foo` being `nil` will actually
L   do the opposite of what the author intends.
L 
L   For example:
L 
L   [source,ruby]
L   ----
L   foo&.blank? #=> nil
L   foo.blank? #=> true
L   ----
S Example
L   # bad
L   do_something if foo&.blank?
L   do_something unless foo&.blank?
L 
L   # good
L   do_something if foo.blank?
L   do_something unless foo.blank?
C Rails/SaveBang
S Details
L Identifies possible cases where Active Record save! or related
L should be used instead of save because the model might have failed to
L save and an exception is better than unhandled failure.
L 
L This will allow:
L 
L * update or save calls, assigned to a variable,
L   or used as a condition in an if/unless/case statement.
L * create calls, assigned to a variable that then has a
L   call to `persisted?`, or whose return value is checked by
L   `persisted?` immediately
L * calls if the result is explicitly returned from methods and blocks,
L   or provided as arguments.
L * calls whose signature doesn't look like an ActiveRecord
L   persistence method.
L 
L By default it will also allow implicit returns from methods and blocks.
L that behavior can be turned off with `AllowImplicitReturn: false`.
L 
L You can permit receivers that are giving false positives with
L `AllowedReceivers: []`
S Safety
L   This cop's autocorrection is unsafe because a custom `update` method call would be changed to `update!`,
L   but the method name in the definition would be unchanged.
L 
L   [source,ruby]
L   ----
L   # Original code
L   def update_attributes
L   end
L 
L   update_attributes
L 
L   # After running rubocop --safe-autocorrect
L   def update_attributes
L   end
L 
L   update
L   ----
S Example
L   # bad
L   user.save
L   user.update(name: 'Joe')
L   user.find_or_create_by(name: 'Joe')
L   user.destroy
L 
L   # good
L   unless user.save
L     # ...
L   end
L   user.save!
L   user.update!(name: 'Joe')
L   user.find_or_create_by!(name: 'Joe')
L   user.destroy!
L 
L   user = User.find_or_create_by(name: 'Joe')
L   unless user.persisted?
L     # ...
L   end
L 
L   def save_user
L     return user.save
L   end
S Example: AllowImplicitReturn: true (default)
L   # good
L   users.each { |u| u.save }
L 
L   def save_user
L     user.save
L   end
S Example: AllowImplicitReturn: false
L   # bad
L   users.each { |u| u.save }
L   def save_user
L     user.save
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   users.each { |u| u.save! }
L 
L   def save_user
L     user.save!
L   end
L 
L   def save_user
L     return user.save
L   end
S Example: AllowedReceivers: ['merchant.customers', 'Service::Mailer']
L   # bad
L   merchant.create
L   customers.builder.save
L   Mailer.create
L 
L   module Service::Mailer
L     self.create
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   merchant.customers.create
L   MerchantService.merchant.customers.destroy
L   Service::Mailer.update(message: 'Message')
L   ::Service::Mailer.update
L   Services::Service::Mailer.update(message: 'Message')
L   Service::Mailer::update
C Rails/SchemaComment
S Details
L Enforces the use of the `comment` option when adding a new table or column
L to the database during a migration.
S Example
L   # bad (no comment for a new column or table)
L   add_column :table, :column, :integer
L 
L   create_table :table do |t|
L     t.type :column
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   add_column :table, :column, :integer, comment: 'Number of offenses'
L 
L   create_table :table, comment: 'Table of offenses data' do |t|
L     t.type :column, comment: 'Number of offenses'
L   end
C Rails/ScopeArgs
S Details
L Checks for scope calls where it was passed
L a method (usually a scope) instead of a lambda/proc.
S Example
L   # bad
L   scope :something, where(something: true)
L 
L   # good
L   scope :something, -> { where(something: true) }
C Rails/SelectMap
S Details
L Checks for uses of `select(:column_name)` with `map(&:column_name)`.
L These can be replaced with `pluck(:column_name)`.
L 
L There also should be some performance improvement since it skips instantiating the model class for matches.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because the model might override the attribute getter.
L   Additionally, the model's `after_initialize` hooks are skipped when using `pluck`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   Model.select(:column_name).map(&:column_name)
L 
L   # good
L   Model.pluck(:column_name)
C Rails/ShortI18n
S Details
L Enforces that short forms of `I18n` methods are used:
L `t` instead of `translate` and `l` instead of `localize`.
L 
L This cop has two different enforcement modes. When the EnforcedStyle
L is conservative (the default) then only `I18n.translate` and `I18n.localize`
L calls are added as offenses.
L 
L When the EnforcedStyle is aggressive then all `translate` and `localize` calls
L without a receiver are added as offenses.
S Example
L   # bad
