L   I18n.translate :key
L   I18n.localize Time.now
L 
L   # good
L   I18n.t :key
L   I18n.l Time.now
S Example: EnforcedStyle: conservative (default)
L   # good
L   translate :key
L   localize Time.now
L   t :key
L   l Time.now
S Example: EnforcedStyle: aggressive
L   # bad
L   translate :key
L   localize Time.now
L 
L   # good
L   t :key
L   l Time.now
C Rails/SkipsModelValidations
S Details
L Checks for the use of methods which skip
L validations which are listed in
L https://guides.rubyonrails.org/active_record_validations.html#skipping-validations
L 
L Methods may be ignored from this rule by configuring a `AllowedMethods`.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe if the receiver object is not an Active Record object.
S Example
L   # bad
L   Article.first.decrement!(:view_count)
L   DiscussionBoard.decrement_counter(:post_count, 5)
L   Article.first.increment!(:view_count)
L   DiscussionBoard.increment_counter(:post_count, 5)
L   person.toggle :active
L   product.touch
L   Billing.update_all("category = 'authorized', author = 'David'")
L   user.update_attribute(:website, 'example.com')
L   user.update_columns(last_request_at: Time.current)
L   Post.update_counters 5, comment_count: -1, action_count: 1
L 
L   # good
L   user.update(website: 'example.com')
L   FileUtils.touch('file')
S Example: AllowedMethods: ["touch"]
L   # bad
L   DiscussionBoard.decrement_counter(:post_count, 5)
L   DiscussionBoard.increment_counter(:post_count, 5)
L   person.toggle :active
L 
L   # good
L   user.touch
C Rails/SquishedSQLHeredocs
S Details
L Checks SQL heredocs to use `.squish`.
S Safety
L   Some SQL syntax (e.g. PostgreSQL comments and functions) requires newlines
L   to be preserved in order to work, thus autocorrection for this cop is not safe.
S Example
L   # bad
L   <<-SQL
L     SELECT * FROM posts;
L   SQL
L 
L   <<-SQL
L     SELECT * FROM posts
L       WHERE id = 1
L   SQL
L 
L   execute(<<~SQL, "Post Load")
L     SELECT * FROM posts
L       WHERE post_id = 1
L   SQL
L 
L   # good
L   <<-SQL.squish
L     SELECT * FROM posts;
L   SQL
L 
L   <<~SQL.squish
L     SELECT * FROM table
L       WHERE id = 1
L   SQL
L 
L   execute(<<~SQL.squish, "Post Load")
L     SELECT * FROM posts
L       WHERE post_id = 1
L   SQL
C Rails/StripHeredoc
S Details
L Enforces the use of squiggly heredoc over `strip_heredoc`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   <<EOS.strip_heredoc
L     some text
L   EOS
L 
L   # bad
L   <<-EOS.strip_heredoc
L     some text
L   EOS
L 
L   # good
L   <<~EOS
L     some text
L   EOS
C Rails/StrongParametersExpect
S Details
L Enforces the use of `ActionController::Parameters#expect` as a method for strong parameter handling.
L 
L In the following cases, `params[:key]` is treated as a key that is expected to be passed from the HTTP client,
L and the cop detects it using the `expect` method.
L 
L - Method calls on `params[:key]` without comparison methods, methods that are safe to call
L   on `nil` (such as `to_i`, `to_s`, or `is_a?`), key-check methods such as `key?`,
L   collection methods such as `keys`, `merge`, or `slice`, or block-style calls such as
L   `params[:key].each { ... }` or `params[:key].map(&:to_s)`
L - Passing `params[:key]` as an argument to finder methods that raise on missing records
L - Strong parameter methods using `require` or `permit`
L 
L Other cases are not detected, as they are cases where `params[:key]` may not be passed from the HTTP client.
S Safety
L   This cop's autocorrection is considered unsafe because there are cases where the HTTP status may change
L   from 500 to 400 when handling invalid parameters. This change, however, reflects an intentional
L   incompatibility introduced for valid reasons by the `expect` method, which aligns better with
L   strong parameter conventions.
L 
L   It is also unsafe because `expect` is stricter about the structure of the parameters than
L   `require`/`permit`. Nested attributes that hold an array of records need an extra array wrapper,
L   such as `expect(user: [{ pets_attributes: [[:name]] }])`. The cop cannot tell a single nested hash
L   from an array of nested hashes, so it always generates the single-hash form, which can turn
L   a previously successful request into a failure.
L 
L   It is also unsafe when `params[:key]` is passed to a finder method such as `find`, because
L   `find` accepts an array of IDs. `Model.find(params[:id])` loads every record for an array of IDs,
L   but the corrected `Model.find(params.expect(:id))` raises `ActionController::ParameterMissing`
L   for an array value, since `expect` requires a scalar. The cop cannot tell a scalar ID from
L   an array of IDs, so the autocorrection can turn a previously successful request into a failure.
S Example
L   # bad
L   params[:key].do_something
L 
L   # good
L   params.expect(:key).do_something
L 
L   # bad
L   Model.find(params[:id])
L 
L   # good
L   Model.find(params.expect(:id))
L 
L   # bad
L   Model.find_by!(key: params[:key])
L 
L   # good
L   Model.find_by!(key: params.expect(:key))
L 
L   # bad
L   params.require(:user).permit(:name, :age)
L   params.permit(user: [:name, :age]).require(:user)
L 
L   # good
L   params.expect(user: [:name, :age])
C Rails/TableNameAssignment
S Details
L Enforces the absence of explicit table name assignment.
L 
L `self.table_name=` should only be used for very good reasons,
L such as not having control over the database, or working
L on a legacy project.
L 
L If you need to change how your model's name is translated to
L a table name, you may want to look at Inflections:
L https://api.rubyonrails.org/classes/ActiveSupport/Inflector/Inflections.html
L 
L If you wish to add a prefix in front of your model, or wish to change
L the default prefix, `self.table_name_prefix` might better suit your needs:
L https://api.rubyonrails.org/classes/ActiveRecord/ModelSchema.html#method-c-table_name_prefix-3D
L 
L STI base classes named `Base` are ignored by this cop.
L For more information: https://api.rubyonrails.org/classes/ActiveRecord/Inheritance.html
S Example
L   # bad
L   self.table_name = 'some_table_name'
L   self.table_name = :some_other_name
C Rails/ThreeStateBooleanColumn
S Details
L Enforces that boolean columns are created with default values (`false` or `true`) and
L `NOT NULL` constraint.
S Example
L   # bad
L   add_column :users, :active, :boolean
L   t.column :active, :boolean
L   t.boolean :active
L 
L   # good
L   add_column :users, :active, :boolean, default: true, null: false
L   t.column :active, :boolean, default: true, null: false
L   t.boolean :active, default: true, null: false
C Rails/TimeZone
S Details
L Checks for the use of Time methods without zone.
L 
L Built on top of Ruby on Rails style guide (https://rails.rubystyle.guide#time)
L and the article http://danilenko.org/2012/7/6/rails_timezones/
L 
L Two styles are supported for this cop. When `EnforcedStyle` is 'strict'
L then only use of `Time.zone` is allowed.
L 
L When EnforcedStyle is 'flexible' then it's also allowed
L to use `Time#in_time_zone`.
S Safety
L   This cop's autocorrection is unsafe because it may change handling time.
S Example
L   # bad
L   Time.now
L   Time.parse('2015-03-02T19:05:37')
L   '2015-03-02T19:05:37'.to_time
L 
L   # good
L   Time.current
L   Time.zone.now
L   Time.zone.parse('2015-03-02T19:05:37')
L   Time.zone.parse('2015-03-02T19:05:37Z') # Respect ISO 8601 format with timezone specifier.
L   Time.parse('2015-03-02T19:05:37Z') # Also respects ISO 8601
L   '2015-03-02T19:05:37Z'.to_time # Also respects ISO 8601
S Example: EnforcedStyle: flexible (default)
L   # `flexible` allows usage of `in_time_zone` instead of `zone`.
L 
L   # good
L   Time.at(timestamp).in_time_zone
S Example: EnforcedStyle: strict
L   # `strict` means that `Time` should be used with `zone`.
L 
L   # bad
L   Time.at(timestamp).in_time_zone
C Rails/TimeZoneAssignment
S Details
L Checks for the use of `Time.zone=` method.
L 
L The `zone` attribute persists for the rest of the Ruby runtime, potentially causing
L unexpected behavior at a later time.
L Using `Time.use_zone` ensures the code passed in the block is the only place Time.zone is affected.
L It eliminates the possibility of a `zone` sticking around longer than intended.
S Example
L   # bad
L   Time.zone = 'EST'
L 
L   # good
L   Time.use_zone('EST') do
L   end
C Rails/ToFormattedS
S Details
L Checks for consistent uses of `to_fs` or `to_formatted_s`,
L depending on the cop's configuration.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: to_fs (default)
L   # bad
L   time.to_formatted_s(:db)
L 
L   # good
L   time.to_fs(:db)
S Example: EnforcedStyle: to_formatted_s
L   # bad
L   time.to_fs(:db)
L 
L   # good
L   time.to_formatted_s(:db)
C Rails/ToSWithArgument
S Details
L Identifies passing any argument to `#to_s`.
S Safety
L   This cop is marked as unsafe because it may detect `#to_s` calls
L   that are not related to Active Support implementation.
S Example
L   # bad
L   obj.to_s(:delimited)
L 
L   # good
L   obj.to_formatted_s(:delimited)
C Rails/TopLevelHashWithIndifferentAccess
S Details
L Identifies top-level `HashWithIndifferentAccess`.
L This has been soft-deprecated since Rails 5.1.
S Example
L   # bad
L   HashWithIndifferentAccess.new(foo: 'bar')
L 
L   # good
L   ActiveSupport::HashWithIndifferentAccess.new(foo: 'bar')
C Rails/TransactionExitStatement
S Details
L Checks for the use of exit statements (namely `return`,
L `break` and `throw`) in transactions. This is due to the eventual
L unexpected behavior when using ActiveRecord >= 7, where transactions
L exited using these statements are being rollbacked rather than
L committed (pre ActiveRecord 7 behavior).
L 
L As alternatives, it would be more intuitive to explicitly raise an
L error when rollback is desired, and to use `next` when commit is
L desired.
L 
L If you are defining custom transaction methods, you can configure it with `TransactionMethods`.
L 
L NOTE: This cop is disabled on Rails >= 7.2 because transactions were restored
L to their historical behavior. In Rails 7.1, the behavior is controlled with
L the config `active_record.commit_transaction_on_non_local_return`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   ApplicationRecord.transaction do
L     return if user.active?
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   ApplicationRecord.transaction do
L     break if user.active?
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   ApplicationRecord.transaction do
L     throw if user.active?
L   end
L 
L   # bad, as `with_lock` implicitly opens a transaction too
L   user.with_lock do
L     throw if user.active?
L   end
L 
L   # bad, as `with_lock` implicitly opens a transaction too
L   ApplicationRecord.with_lock do
L     break if user.active?
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   ApplicationRecord.transaction do
L     # Rollback
L     raise "User is active" if user.active?
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   ApplicationRecord.transaction do
L     # Commit
L     next if user.active?
L   end
S Example: TransactionMethods: ["custom_transaction"]
L   # bad
L   CustomModel.custom_transaction do
L     return if user.active?
L   end
C Rails/UniqBeforePluck
S Details
L Prefer using `distinct` before `pluck` instead of `uniq` after `pluck`.
L 
L The use of distinct before pluck is preferred because it executes by
L the database.
L 
L This cop has two different enforcement modes. When the EnforcedStyle
L is `conservative` (the default), then only calls to `pluck` on a constant
L (i.e. a model class) before `uniq` are added as offenses.
L 
L When the EnforcedStyle is `aggressive` then all calls to `pluck` before
L distinct are added as offenses. This may lead to false positives
L as the cop cannot distinguish between calls to `pluck` on an
L ActiveRecord::Relation vs a call to pluck on an
L ActiveRecord::Associations::CollectionProxy.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe for autocorrection because the behavior may change
L   depending on the database collation.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: conservative (default)
L   # bad - redundantly fetches duplicate values
L   Album.pluck(:band_name).uniq
L 
L   # good
L   Album.distinct.pluck(:band_name)
S Example: EnforcedStyle: aggressive
L   # bad - redundantly fetches duplicate values
L   Album.pluck(:band_name).uniq
L 
L   # bad - redundantly fetches duplicate values
L   Album.where(year: 1985).pluck(:band_name).uniq
L 
L   # bad - redundantly fetches duplicate values
L   customer.favourites.pluck(:color).uniq
L 
L   # good
L   Album.distinct.pluck(:band_name)
L   Album.distinct.where(year: 1985).pluck(:band_name)
L   customer.favourites.distinct.pluck(:color)
C Rails/UniqueValidationWithoutIndex
S Details
L When you define a uniqueness validation in Active Record model,
L you also should add a unique index for the column. There are two reasons.
L First, duplicated records may occur even if Active Record's validation
L is defined.
L Second, it will cause slow queries. The validation executes a `SELECT`
L statement with the target column when inserting/updating a record.
L If the column does not have an index and the table is large,
L the query will be heavy.
L 
L Note that the cop does nothing if db/schema.rb does not exist.
S Example
L   # bad - if the schema does not have a unique index
L   validates :account, uniqueness: true
L 
L   # good - if the schema has a unique index
L   validates :account, uniqueness: true
L 
L   # good - even if the schema does not have a unique index
L   validates :account, length: { minimum: MIN_LENGTH }
C Rails/UnknownEnv
S Details
L Checks that environments called with `Rails.env` predicates
L exist.
L By default the cop allows three environments which Rails ships with:
L `development`, `test`, and `production`.
L More can be added to the `Environments` config parameter.
S Example
L   # bad
L   Rails.env.proudction?
L   Rails.env == 'proudction'
L   Rails.env != 'proudction'
L 
L   # good
L   Rails.env.production?
L   Rails.env == 'production'
L   Rails.env != 'production'
S Example
L   # bad
L   case Rails.env
L   when 'proudction'
L     do_something
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   case Rails.env
L   when 'production'
L     do_something
L   end
C Rails/UnusedIgnoredColumns
S Details
L Suggests you remove a column that does not exist in the schema from `ignored_columns`.
L `ignored_columns` is necessary to drop a column from RDBMS, but you don't need it after the migration
L to drop the column. You avoid forgetting to remove `ignored_columns` by this cop.
L 
L IMPORTANT: This cop can't be used to effectively check for unused columns because the development
L and production schema can be out of sync until the migration has been run on production. As such,
L this cop can cause `ignored_columns` to be removed even though the production schema still contains
L the column, which can lead to downtime when the migration is actually executed. Only enable this cop
L if you know your migrations will be run before any of your Rails applications boot with the modified code.
S Example
L   # bad
L   class User < ApplicationRecord
L     self.ignored_columns = [:already_removed_column]
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class User < ApplicationRecord
L     self.ignored_columns = [:still_existing_column]
L   end
C Rails/UnusedRenderContent
S Details
L If you try to render content along with a non-content status code (100-199, 204, 205, or 304),
L it will be dropped from the response.
L 
L This cop checks for uses of `render` which specify both body content and a non-content status.
S Example
L   # bad
L   render 'foo', status: :continue
L   render status: 100, plain: 'Ruby!'
L 
L   # good
L   head :continue
L   head 100
C Rails/Validation
S Details
L Checks for the use of old-style attribute validation macros.
S Example
L   # bad
L   validates_acceptance_of :foo
L   validates_comparison_of :foo
L   validates_confirmation_of :foo
L   validates_exclusion_of :foo
L   validates_format_of :foo
L   validates_inclusion_of :foo
L   validates_length_of :foo
L   validates_numericality_of :foo
L   validates_presence_of :foo
L   validates_absence_of :foo
L   validates_size_of :foo
L   validates_uniqueness_of :foo
L 
L   # good
L   validates :foo, acceptance: true
L   validates :foo, confirmation: true
L   validates :foo, comparison: true
L   validates :foo, exclusion: true
L   validates :foo, format: true
L   validates :foo, inclusion: true
L   validates :foo, length: true
L   validates :foo, numericality: true
L   validates :foo, presence: true
L   validates :foo, absence: true
L   validates :foo, length: true
L   validates :foo, uniqueness: true
C Rails/WhereEquals
S Details
L Identifies places where manually constructed SQL
L in `where` and `where.not` can be replaced with
L `where(attribute: value)` and `where.not(attribute: value)`.
S Safety
L   This cop's autocorrection is unsafe because is may change SQL.
L   See: https://github.com/rubocop/rubocop-rails/issues/403
S Example
L   # bad
L   User.where('name = ?', 'Gabe')
L   User.where.not('name = ?', 'Gabe')
L   User.where('name = :name', name: 'Gabe')
L   User.where('name IS NULL')
L   User.where('name IN (?)', ['john', 'jane'])
L   User.where('name IN (:names)', names: ['john', 'jane'])
L   User.where('users.name = :name', name: 'Gabe')
L 
L   # good
L   User.where(name: 'Gabe')
L   User.where.not(name: 'Gabe')
L   User.where(name: nil)
L   User.where(name: ['john', 'jane'])
L   User.where(users: { name: 'Gabe' })
C Rails/WhereExists
S Details
L Enforces consistent style when using `exists?`.
L 
L Two styles are supported for this cop. When EnforcedStyle is 'exists'
L then the cop enforces `exists?(...)` over `where(...).exists?`.
L 
L When EnforcedStyle is 'where' then the cop enforces
L `where(...).exists?` over `exists?(...)`.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe for autocorrection because the behavior may change on the following case:
L 
L   [source,ruby]
L   ----
L   Author.includes(:articles).where(articles: {id: id}).exists?
L   #=> Perform `eager_load` behavior (`LEFT JOIN` query) and get result.
L 
L   Author.includes(:articles).exists?(articles: {id: id})
L   #=> Perform `preload` behavior and `ActiveRecord::StatementInvalid` error occurs.
L   ----
S Example: EnforcedStyle: exists (default)
L   # bad
L   User.where(name: 'john').exists?
L   User.where(['name = ?', 'john']).exists?
L   User.where('name = ?', 'john').exists?
L   user.posts.where(published: true).exists?
L 
L   # good
L   User.exists?(name: 'john')
L   User.where('length(name) > 10').exists?
L   user.posts.exists?(published: true)
S Example: EnforcedStyle: where
L   # bad
L   User.exists?(name: 'john')
L   User.exists?(['name = ?', 'john'])
L   user.posts.exists?(published: true)
L 
L   # good
L   User.where(name: 'john').exists?
L   User.where(['name = ?', 'john']).exists?
L   User.where('name = ?', 'john').exists?
L   user.posts.where(published: true).exists?
L   User.where('length(name) > 10').exists?
C Rails/WhereMissing
S Details
L Use `where.missing(...)` to find missing relationship records.
L 
L This cop is enabled in Rails 6.1 or higher.
S Example
L   # bad
L   Post.left_joins(:author).where(authors: { id: nil })
L 
L   # good
L   Post.where.missing(:author)
C Rails/WhereNot
S Details
L Identifies places where manually constructed SQL
L in `where` can be replaced with `where.not(...)`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   User.where('name != ?', 'Gabe')
L   User.where('name != :name', name: 'Gabe')
L   User.where('name <> ?', 'Gabe')
L   User.where('name <> :name', name: 'Gabe')
L   User.where('name IS NOT NULL')
L   User.where('name NOT IN (?)', ['john', 'jane'])
L   User.where('name NOT IN (:names)', names: ['john', 'jane'])
L   User.where('users.name != :name', name: 'Gabe')
L 
L   # good
L   User.where.not(name: 'Gabe')
L   User.where.not(name: nil)
L   User.where.not(name: ['john', 'jane'])
L   User.where.not(users: { name: 'Gabe' })
C Rails/WhereNotWithMultipleConditions
S Details
L Identifies calls to `where.not` with multiple hash arguments.
L 
L The behavior of `where.not` changed in Rails 6.1. Prior to the change,
L `.where.not(trashed: true, role: 'admin')` evaluated to
L `WHERE trashed != TRUE AND role != 'admin'`.
L From Rails 6.1 onwards, this executes the query
L `WHERE NOT (trashed == TRUE AND roles == 'admin')`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   User.where.not(trashed: true, role: 'admin')
L   User.where.not(trashed: true, role: ['moderator', 'admin'])
L   User.joins(:posts).where.not(posts: { trashed: true, title: 'Rails' })
L 
L   # good
L   User.where.not(trashed: true)
L   User.where.not(role: ['moderator', 'admin'])
L   User.where.not(trashed: true).where.not(role: ['moderator', 'admin'])
L   User.where.not('trashed = ? OR role = ?', true, 'admin')
C Rails/WhereRange
S Details
L Identifies places where manually constructed SQL
L in `where` can be replaced with ranges.
S Safety
L   This cop's autocorrection is unsafe because it can change the query
L   by explicitly attaching the column to the wrong table.
L   For example, `Booking.joins(:events).where('end_at < ?', Time.current)` will correctly
L   implicitly attach the `end_at` column to the `events` table. But when autocorrected to
L   `Booking.joins(:events).where(end_at: ...Time.current)`, it will now be incorrectly
L   explicitly attached to the `bookings` table.
S Example
L   # bad
L   User.where('age >= ?', 18)
L   User.where.not('age >= ?', 18)
L   User.where('age < ?', 18)
L   User.where('age >= ? AND age < ?', 18, 21)
L   User.where('age >= :start', start: 18)
L   User.where('users.age >= ?', 18)
L 
L   # good
L   User.where(age: 18..)
L   User.where.not(age: 18..)
L   User.where(age: ...18)
L   User.where(age: 18...21)
L   User.where(users: { age: 18.. })
L 
L   # good
L   # There are no beginless ranges in ruby.
L   User.where('age > ?', 18)
C Security/CompoundHash
S Details
L Checks for implementations of the `hash` method which combine
L values using custom logic instead of delegating to `Array#hash`.
L 
L Manually combining hashes is error prone and hard to follow, especially
L when there are many values. Poor implementations may also introduce
L performance or security concerns if they are prone to collisions.
L Delegating to `Array#hash` is clearer and safer, although it might be slower
L depending on the use case.
S Safety
L   This cop may be unsafe if the application logic depends on the hash
L   value, however this is inadvisable anyway.
S Example
L   # bad
L   def hash
L     @foo ^ @bar
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def hash
L     [@foo, @bar].hash
L   end
C Security/Eval
S Details
L Checks for the use of `Kernel#eval` and `Binding#eval` with
L dynamic strings as arguments. Evaluating non-literal strings
L can enable code injection attacks and makes it difficult to
L reason about what code will actually be executed.
L 
L Calls to `eval` with literal strings are not flagged by this cop,
L as they do not pose the same injection risk.
S Example
L   # bad
L   eval(something)
L   binding.eval(something)
L   Kernel.eval(something)
L 
L   # good - use safer alternatives
L   obj.public_send(method_name)
L   obj.send(method_name, *args)
L 
L   # good - literal strings are allowed
L   eval("1 + 1")
L   binding.eval("foo")
C Security/IoMethods
S Details
L Checks for the first argument to `IO.read`, `IO.binread`, `IO.write`, `IO.binwrite`,
L `IO.foreach`, and `IO.readlines`.
L 
L If argument starts with a pipe character (`'|'`) and the receiver is the `IO` class,
L a subprocess is created in the same way as `Kernel#open`, and its output is returned.
L `Kernel#open` may allow unintentional command injection, which is the reason these
L `IO` methods are a security risk.
L Consider using `File.read` to disable the behavior of subprocess invocation.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because false positive will occur if the variable passed as
L   the first argument is a command that is not a file path.
S Example
L   # bad
L   IO.read(path)
L   IO.read('path')
L 
L   # good
L   File.read(path)
L   File.read('path')
L   IO.read('| command') # Allow intentional command invocation.
C Security/JSONLoad
S Details
L Checks for the use of JSON class methods which have potential
L security issues.
L 
L `JSON.load` and similar methods allow deserialization of arbitrary ruby objects:
L 
L [source,ruby]
L ----
L require 'json/add/string'
L result = JSON.load('{ "json_class": "String", "raw": [72, 101, 108, 108, 111] }')
L pp result # => "Hello"
L ----
L 
L Never use `JSON.load` for untrusted user input. Prefer `JSON.parse` unless you have
L a concrete use-case for `JSON.load`.
L 
L NOTE: Starting with `json` gem version 2.8.0, triggering this behavior without explicitly
L passing the `create_additions` keyword argument emits a deprecation warning, with the
L goal of being secure by default in the next major version 3.0.0.
L 
L NOTE: Before `json` gem version 2.17.0, `JSON.load` only takes options as its third
L argument. A hash passed as the second argument is taken as the `proc` argument, so
L its `create_additions` option is ignored. Which `json` version runs can't be told
L statically (the lockfile may resolve a newer one than the default gem loaded in
L production), so options passed as the second argument are always reported. Pass
L `nil` as the second argument instead.
S Safety
L   This cop's autocorrection is unsafe because it's potentially dangerous.
L   If using a stream, like `JSON.load(open('file'))`, you will need to call
L   `#read` manually, like `JSON.parse(open('file').read)`.
L   Other similar issues may apply.
S Example
L   # bad
L   JSON.load('{}')
L   JSON.restore('{}')
L 
L   # bad - ignored by `json` older than 2.17.0
L   JSON.load('{}', create_additions: false)
L 
L   # good
L   JSON.parse('{}')
L   JSON.unsafe_load('{}')
L 
L   # good - explicit use of `create_additions` option
L   JSON.load('{}', nil, create_additions: true)
L   JSON.load('{}', nil, create_additions: false)
C Security/MarshalLoad
S Details
L Checks for the use of Marshal class methods which have
L potential security issues leading to remote code execution when
L loading from an untrusted source.
S Example
L   # bad
L   Marshal.load("{}")
L   Marshal.restore("{}")
L 
L   # good
L   Marshal.dump("{}")
L 
L   # okish - deep copy hack
L   Marshal.load(Marshal.dump({}))
C Security/Open
S Details
L Checks for the use of `Kernel#open` and `URI.open` with dynamic
L data.
L 
L `Kernel#open` and `URI.open` enable not only file access but also process
L invocation by prefixing a pipe symbol (e.g., `open("| ls")`).
L So, it may lead to a serious security risk by using variable input to
L the argument of `Kernel#open` and `URI.open`. It would be better to use
L `File.open`, `IO.popen` or `URI.parse#open` explicitly.
L 
L NOTE: `open` and `URI.open` with literal strings are not flagged by this
L cop.
S Safety
L   This cop could register false positives if `open` is redefined
L   in a class and then used without a receiver in that class.
S Example
L   # bad
L   open(something)
L   open("| #{something}")
L   open("| foo")
L   URI.open(something)
L 
L   # good
L   File.open(something)
L   IO.popen(something)
L   URI.parse(something).open
L 
L   # good (literal strings)
L   open("foo.text")
L   URI.open("http://example.com")
L   URI.parse(url).open
C Security/YAMLLoad
S Details
L Checks for the use of YAML class methods which have
L potential security issues leading to remote code execution when
L loading from an untrusted source.
L 
L NOTE: Ruby 3.1+ (Psych 4) uses `Psych.load` as `Psych.safe_load` by default.
S Safety
L   The behavior of the code might change depending on what was
L   in the YAML payload, since `YAML.safe_load` is more restrictive.
S Example
L   # bad
L   YAML.load("--- !ruby/object:Foo {}") # Psych 3 is unsafe by default
L 
L   # good
L   YAML.safe_load("--- !ruby/object:Foo {}", [Foo])                    # Ruby 2.5  (Psych 3)
L   YAML.safe_load("--- !ruby/object:Foo {}", permitted_classes: [Foo]) # Ruby 3.0- (Psych 3)
L   YAML.load("--- !ruby/object:Foo {}", permitted_classes: [Foo])      # Ruby 3.1+ (Psych 4)
L   YAML.dump(foo)
C Style/AccessModifierDeclarations
S Details
L Access modifiers should be declared to apply to a group of methods
L or inline before each method, depending on configuration.
L EnforcedStyle config covers only method definitions.
L Applications of visibility methods to symbols can be controlled
L using AllowModifiersOnSymbols config.
L Also, the visibility of `attr*` methods can be controlled using
L AllowModifiersOnAttrs config.
L 
L In Ruby 3.0, `attr*` methods now return an array of defined method names
L as symbols. So we can write the modifier and `attr*` in inline style.
L AllowModifiersOnAttrs config allows `attr*` methods to be written in
L inline style without modifying applications that have been maintained
L for a long time in group style. Furthermore, developers who are not very
L familiar with Ruby may know that the modifier applies to `def`, but they
L may not know that it also applies to `attr*` methods. It would be easier
L to understand if we could write `attr*` methods in inline style.
S Safety
L   Autocorrection is not safe, because the visibility of dynamically
L   defined methods can vary depending on the state determined by
L   the group access modifier.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: group (default)
L   # bad
L   class Foo
L 
L     private def bar; end
L     private def baz; end
L 
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class Foo
L 
L     private
L 
L     def bar; end
L     def baz; end
L 
L   end
S Example: EnforcedStyle: inline
L   # bad
L   class Foo
L 
L     private
L 
L     def bar; end
L     def baz; end
L 
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class Foo
L 
L     private def bar; end
L     private def baz; end
L 
L   end
S Example: AllowModifiersOnSymbols: true (default)
L   # good
L   class Foo
L 
L     private :bar, :baz
L     private *%i[qux quux]
L     private *METHOD_NAMES
L     private *private_methods
L 
L   end
S Example: AllowModifiersOnSymbols: false
L   # bad
L   class Foo
L 
L     private :bar, :baz
L     private *%i[qux quux]
L     private *METHOD_NAMES
L     private *private_methods
L 
L   end
S Example: AllowModifiersOnAttrs: true (default)
L   # good
L   class Foo
L 
L     public attr_reader :bar
L     protected attr_writer :baz
L     private attr_accessor :qux
L     private attr :quux
L 
L     def public_method; end
L 
L     private
L 
L     def private_method; end
L 
L   end
S Example: AllowModifiersOnAttrs: false
L   # bad
L   class Foo
L 
L     public attr_reader :bar
L     protected attr_writer :baz
L     private attr_accessor :qux
L     private attr :quux
L 
L   end
S Example: AllowModifiersOnAliasMethod: true (default)
L   # good
L   class Foo
L 
L     public alias_method :bar, :foo
L     protected alias_method :baz, :foo
L     private alias_method :qux, :foo
L 
L   end
S Example: AllowModifiersOnAliasMethod: false
L   # bad
L   class Foo
L 
L     public alias_method :bar, :foo
L     protected alias_method :baz, :foo
L     private alias_method :qux, :foo
L 
L   end
C Style/AccessorGrouping
S Details
L Checks for grouping of accessors in `class` and `module` bodies.
L By default it enforces accessors to be placed in grouped
L declarations, reducing boilerplate. It can also be configured
L to enforce separating them into individual declarations for
L easier diffing and per-attribute documentation.
L 
L NOTE: If there is a method call before the accessor method it is always allowed
L as it might be intended like Sorbet.
L 
L NOTE: If there is a RBS::Inline annotation comment just after the accessor method
L it is always allowed.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: grouped (default)
L   # bad
L   class Foo
L     attr_reader :bar
L     attr_reader :bax
L     attr_reader :baz
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class Foo
L     attr_reader :bar, :bax, :baz
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class Foo
L     # may be intended comment for bar.
L     attr_reader :bar
L 
L     sig { returns(String) }
L     attr_reader :bax
L 
L     may_be_intended_annotation :baz
L     attr_reader :baz
L   end
S Example: EnforcedStyle: separated
L   # bad
L   class Foo
L     attr_reader :bar, :baz
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class Foo
L     attr_reader :bar
L     attr_reader :baz
L   end
C Style/Alias
S Details
L Enforces the use of either `#alias` or `#alias_method`
L depending on configuration. Consistent use of one or the
L other prevents confusion about their different semantics
L (e.g., `alias` is resolved at parse time, while `alias_method`
L is resolved at runtime).
L It also flags uses of `alias :symbol` rather than `alias bareword`.
L 
L However, it will always enforce `alias_method` when `alias` is used
L in an instance method definition and in a singleton method definition.
L If used in a block, always enforce `alias_method`
L unless it is an `instance_eval` block.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: prefer_alias (default)
L   # bad
L   alias_method :bar, :foo
L   alias :bar :foo
L 
L   # good
L   alias bar foo
S Example: EnforcedStyle: prefer_alias_method
L   # bad
L   alias :bar :foo
L   alias bar foo
L 
L   # good
L   alias_method :bar, :foo
C Style/AmbiguousEndlessMethodDefinition
S Details
L Looks for endless methods inside operations of lower precedence (`and`, `or`, and
L modifier forms of `if`, `unless`, `while`, `until`) that are ambiguous due to
L lack of parentheses. This may lead to unexpected behavior as the code may appear
L to use these keywords as part of the method but in fact they modify
L the method definition itself.
L 
L In these cases, using a normal method definition is more clear.
S Example
L   # bad
L   def foo = true if bar
L 
L   # good - using a non-endless method is more explicit
L   def foo
L     true
L   end if bar
L 
L   # ok - method body is explicit
L   def foo = (true if bar)
L 
L   # ok - method definition is explicit
L   (def foo = true) if bar
C Style/AndOr
S Details
L Checks for uses of `and` and `or`, and suggests using `&&` and
L `||` instead. It can be configured to check only in conditions or in
L all contexts.
S Safety
L   Autocorrection is unsafe because there is a different operator precedence
L   between logical operators (`&&` and `||`) and semantic operators (`and` and `or`),
L   and that might change the behavior.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: conditionals (default)
L   # bad
L   if foo and bar
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   foo.save && return
L 
L   # good
L   foo.save and return
L 
L   # good
L   if foo && bar
L   end
S Example: EnforcedStyle: always
L   # bad
L   foo.save and return
L 
L   # bad
L   if foo and bar
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   foo.save && return
L 
L   # good
L   if foo && bar
L   end
C Style/ArgumentsForwarding
S Details
L In Ruby 2.7, arguments forwarding has been added.
L 
L This cop identifies places where `do_something(*args, &block)`
L can be replaced by `do_something(...)`.
L 
L In Ruby 3.1, anonymous block forwarding has been added.
L 
L This cop identifies places where `do_something(&block)` can be replaced
L by `do_something(&)`; if desired, this functionality can be disabled
L by setting `UseAnonymousForwarding: false`.
L 
L In Ruby 3.2, anonymous args/kwargs forwarding has been added.
L 
L This cop also identifies places where `+use_args(*args)+`/`+use_kwargs(**kwargs)+` can be
L replaced by `+use_args(*)+`/`+use_kwargs(**)+`; if desired, this functionality can be
L disabled by setting `UseAnonymousForwarding: false`.
L 
L And this cop has `RedundantRestArgumentNames`, `RedundantKeywordRestArgumentNames`,
L and `RedundantBlockArgumentNames` options. This configuration is a list of redundant names
L that are sufficient for anonymizing meaningless naming.
L 
L Meaningless names that are commonly used can be anonymized by default:
L e.g., `+*args+`, `+**options+`, `&block`, and so on.
L 
L Names not on this list are likely to be meaningful and are allowed by default.
L 
L This cop handles not only method forwarding but also forwarding to `super`.
L 
L [NOTE]
L ====
L Because of a bug in Ruby 3.3.0, when a block is referenced inside of another block,
L no offense will be registered until Ruby 3.4:
L 
L [source,ruby]
L ----
L def foo(&block)
L   # Using an anonymous block would be a syntax error on Ruby 3.3.0
L   block_method { bar(&block) }
L end
L ----
L ====
S Example
L   # bad
L   def foo(*args, &block)
L     bar(*args, &block)
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   def foo(*args, **kwargs, &block)
L     bar(*args, **kwargs, &block)
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo(...)
L     bar(...)
L   end
S Example: UseAnonymousForwarding: true (default, only relevant for Ruby >= 3.2)
L   # bad
L   def foo(*args, **kwargs, &block)
L     args_only(*args)
L     kwargs_only(**kwargs)
L     block_only(&block)
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo(*, **, &)
L     args_only(*)
L     kwargs_only(**)
L     block_only(&)
L   end
S Example: UseAnonymousForwarding: false (only relevant for Ruby >= 3.2)
L   # good
L   def foo(*args, **kwargs, &block)
L     args_only(*args)
L     kwargs_only(**kwargs)
L     block_only(&block)
L   end
S Example: AllowOnlyRestArgument: true (default, only relevant for Ruby < 3.2)
L   # good
L   def foo(*args)
L     bar(*args)
L   end
L 
L   def foo(**kwargs)
L     bar(**kwargs)
L   end
S Example: AllowOnlyRestArgument: false (only relevant for Ruby < 3.2)
L   # bad
L   # The following code can replace the arguments with `...`,
L   # but it will change the behavior. Because `...` forwards block also.
L   def foo(*args)
L     bar(*args)
L   end
L 
L   def foo(**kwargs)
L     bar(**kwargs)
L   end
S Example: RedundantRestArgumentNames: ['args', 'arguments'] (default)
L   # bad
L   def foo(*args)
L     bar(*args)
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo(*)
L     bar(*)
L   end
S Example: RedundantKeywordRestArgumentNames: ['kwargs', 'options', 'opts'] (default)
L   # bad
L   def foo(**kwargs)
L     bar(**kwargs)
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo(**)
L     bar(**)
L   end
S Example: RedundantBlockArgumentNames: ['blk', 'block', 'proc'] (default)
L   # bad - But it is good with `EnforcedStyle: explicit` set for `Naming/BlockForwarding`.
L   def foo(&block)
L     bar(&block)
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo(&)
L     bar(&)
L   end
C Style/ArrayCoercion
S Details
L Enforces the use of `Array()` instead of explicit `Array` check or `[*var]`.
L 
L The cop is disabled by default due to safety concerns.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because a false positive may occur if
L   the argument of `Array()` is (or could be) nil or depending
L   on how the argument is handled by `Array()` (which can be
L   different than just wrapping the argument in an array).
L 
L   For example:
L 
L   [source,ruby]
L   ----
L   [nil]             #=> [nil]
L   Array(nil)        #=> []
L 
L   [{a: 'b'}]        #= [{a: 'b'}]
L   Array({a: 'b'})   #=> [[:a, 'b']]
L 
L   [Time.now]        #=> [#<Time ...>]
L   Array(Time.now)   #=> [14, 16, 14, 16, 9, 2021, 4, 259, true, "EDT"]
L   ----
S Example
L   # bad
L   paths = [paths] unless paths.is_a?(Array)
L   paths.each { |path| do_something(path) }
L 
L   # bad (always creates a new Array instance)
L   [*paths].each { |path| do_something(path) }
L 
L   # good (and a bit more readable)
L   Array(paths).each { |path| do_something(path) }
C Style/ArrayFirstLast
S Details
L Identifies usages of `arr[0]` and `arr[-1]` and suggests to change
L them to use `arr.first` and `arr.last` instead.
L 
L The cop is disabled by default due to safety concerns.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because `[0]` or `[-1]` can be called on a Hash,
L   which returns a value for `0` or `-1` key, but changing these to use
L   `.first` or `.last` will return first/last tuple instead. Also, String
L   does not implement `first`/`last` methods.
S Example
L   # bad
L   arr[0]
L   arr[-1]
L 
L   # good
L   arr.first
L   arr.last
L   arr[0] = 2
L   arr[0][-2]
C Style/ArrayIntersect
S Details
L In Ruby 3.1, `Array#intersect?` has been added.
L 
L This cop identifies places where:
L 
L * `(array1 & array2).any?`
L * `(array1.intersection(array2)).any?`
L * `array1.any? { |elem| array2.member?(elem) }`
L * `(array1 & array2).count > 0`
L * `(array1 & array2).size > 0`
L 
L can be replaced with `array1.intersect?(array2)`.
L 
L `array1.intersect?(array2)` is faster and more readable.
L 
L In cases like the following, compatibility is not ensured,
L so it will not be detected when using block argument.
L 
L [source,ruby]
L ----
L ([1] & [1,2]).any? { |x| false }    # => false
L [1].intersect?([1,2]) { |x| false } # => true
L ----
L 
L NOTE: Although `Array#intersection` can take zero or multiple arguments,
L only cases where exactly one argument is provided can be replaced with
L `Array#intersect?` and are handled by this cop.
L 
L NOTE: In the block form, `include?` is only detected when its receiver is
L an array literal, because `include?` is defined with different semantics
L on many non-array classes (e.g. `String#include?` checks for substrings).
L `member?` does not have this restriction.
S Safety
L   This cop cannot guarantee that `array1` and `array2` are
L   actually arrays while method `intersect?` is for arrays only.
S Example
L   # bad
L   (array1 & array2).any?
L   (array1 & array2).empty?
L   (array1 & array2).none?
L 
L   # bad
L   array1.intersection(array2).any?
L   array1.intersection(array2).empty?
L   array1.intersection(array2).none?
L 
L   # bad
L   array1.any? { |elem| array2.member?(elem) }
L   array1.none? { |elem| [1, 2].include?(elem) }
L 
L   # good
L   array1.intersect?(array2)
L   !array1.intersect?(array2)
L 
L   # bad
L   (array1 & array2).count > 0
L   (array1 & array2).count.positive?
L   (array1 & array2).count != 0
L 
L   (array1 & array2).count == 0
L   (array1 & array2).count.zero?
L 
L   # good
L   array1.intersect?(array2)
L 
L   !array1.intersect?(array2)
S Example: AllCops:ActiveSupportExtensionsEnabled: false (default)
L   # good
L   (array1 & array2).present?
L   (array1 & array2).blank?
S Example: AllCops:ActiveSupportExtensionsEnabled: true
L   # bad
L   (array1 & array2).present?
L   (array1 & array2).blank?
L 
L   # good
L   array1.intersect?(array2)
L   !array1.intersect?(array2)
C Style/ArrayIntersectWithSingleElement
S Details
L Use `include?(element)` instead of `intersect?([element])`.
S Safety
L   The receiver might not be an array.
S Example
L   # bad
L   array.intersect?([element])
L 
L   # good
L   array.include?(element)
C Style/ArrayJoin
S Details
L Checks for uses of `*` as a substitute for `Array#join`.
L Using `join` is clearer about intent and more readable than
L overloading the `*` operator for string conversion.
L 
L Not all cases can be reliably checked, due to Ruby's dynamic
L types, so we consider only cases when the first argument is an
L array literal or the second is a string literal.
S Example
L   # bad
L   %w(foo bar baz) * ","
L 
L   # good
L   %w(foo bar baz).join(",")
C Style/AsciiComments
S Details
L Checks for non-ascii (non-English) characters
L in comments. Non-ascii characters can cause issues with
L portability and encoding across different environments
L and editors. You could set an array of allowed non-ascii
L chars in `AllowedChars` attribute (copyright notice "©"
L by default).
S Example
L   # bad
L   # Translates from English to 日本語。
L 
L   # good
L   # Translates from English to Japanese
C Style/Attr
S Details
L Checks for uses of `Module#attr`. The `attr` method has confusing
L behavior: with a single argument it creates a reader (like `attr_reader`),
L but with a second boolean argument it creates an accessor (deprecated in
L Ruby 1.9). Use `attr_reader` or `attr_accessor` to make intent explicit.
S Example
L   # bad
L   attr :something, true
L   attr :one, :two, :three # behaves as attr_reader
L 
L   # good
L   attr_accessor :something
L   attr_reader :one, :two, :three
C Style/AutoResourceCleanup
S Details
L Checks for cases when you could use a block
L accepting version of a method that does automatic
L resource cleanup.
S Example
L   # bad
L   f = File.open('file')
L 
L   # good
L   File.open('file') do |f|
L     # ...
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   f = Tempfile.open('temp')
L 
L   # good
L   Tempfile.open('temp') do |f|
L     # ...
L   end
C Style/BarePercentLiterals
S Details
L Checks if usage of `%()` or `%Q()` matches configuration.
L Consistent use of one style makes the codebase easier
L to read.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: bare_percent (default)
L   # bad
L   %Q(He said: "#{greeting}")
L   %q{She said: 'Hi'}
L 
L   # good
L   %(He said: "#{greeting}")
L   %{She said: 'Hi'}
S Example: EnforcedStyle: percent_q
L   # bad
L   %|He said: "#{greeting}"|
L   %/She said: 'Hi'/
L 
L   # good
L   %Q|He said: "#{greeting}"|
L   %q/She said: 'Hi'/
C Style/BeginBlock
S Details
L Checks for `BEGIN` blocks. They are Perl-style constructs that execute
L code before the rest of the file is parsed, making the control flow
L harder to follow and reason about.
S Example
L   # bad
L   BEGIN { test }
C Style/BisectedAttrAccessor
S Details
L Checks for places where `attr_reader` and `attr_writer`
L for the same method can be combined into single `attr_accessor`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   class Foo
L     attr_reader :bar
L     attr_writer :bar
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class Foo
L     attr_accessor :bar
L   end
C Style/BitwisePredicate
S Details
L Prefer bitwise predicate methods over direct comparison operations.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe, as it can produce false positives if the receiver
L   is not an `Integer` object.
S Example
L   # bad - checks any set bits
L   (variable & flags).positive?
L 
L   # good
L   variable.anybits?(flags)
L 
L   # bad - checks all set bits
L   (variable & flags) == flags
L 
L   # good
L   variable.allbits?(flags)
L 
L   # bad - checks no set bits
L   (variable & flags).zero?
L 
L   # good
L   variable.nobits?(flags)
C Style/BlockComments
S Details
L Looks for uses of block comments (=begin...=end).
S Example
L   # bad
L   =begin
L   Multiple lines
L   of comments...
L   =end
L 
L   # good
L   # Multiple lines
L   # of comments...
C Style/BlockDelimiters
S Details
L Checks for uses of braces or do/end around single line or
L multi-line blocks.
L 
L Methods that can be either procedural or functional and cannot be
L categorised from their usage alone is ignored.
L `lambda`, `proc`, and `it` are their defaults.
L Additional methods can be added to the `AllowedMethods`.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: line_count_based (default)
L   # bad - single line block
L   items.each do |item| item / 5 end
L 
L   # good - single line block
L   items.each { |item| item / 5 }
L 
L   # bad - multi-line block
L   things.map { |thing|
L     something = thing.some_method
L     process(something)
L   }
L 
L   # good - multi-line block
L   things.map do |thing|
L     something = thing.some_method
L     process(something)
L   end
S Example: EnforcedStyle: semantic
L   # Prefer `do...end` over `{...}` for procedural blocks.
L 
L   # return value is used/assigned
L   # bad
L   foo = map do |x|
L     x
L   end
L   puts (map do |x|
L     x
L   end)
L 
L   # return value is not used out of scope
L   # good
L   map do |x|
L     x
L   end
L 
L   # Prefer `{...}` over `do...end` for functional blocks.
L 
L   # return value is not used out of scope
L   # bad
L   each { |x|
L     x
L   }
L 
L   # return value is used/assigned
L   # good
L   foo = map { |x|
L     x
L   }
L   map { |x|
L     x
L   }.inspect
L 
L   # The AllowBracesOnProceduralOneLiners option is allowed unless the
L   # EnforcedStyle is set to `semantic`. If so:
L 
L   # If the AllowBracesOnProceduralOneLiners option is unspecified, or
L   # set to `false` or any other falsey value, then semantic purity is
L   # maintained, so one-line procedural blocks must use do-end, not
L   # braces.
L 
L   # bad
L   collection.each { |element| puts element }
L 
L   # good
L   collection.each do |element| puts element end
L 
L   # If the AllowBracesOnProceduralOneLiners option is set to `true`, or
L   # any other truthy value, then one-line procedural blocks may use
L   # either style. (There is no setting for requiring braces on them.)
L 
L   # good
L   collection.each { |element| puts element }
L 
L   # also good
L   collection.each do |element| puts element end
S Example: EnforcedStyle: braces_for_chaining
L   # bad
L   words.each do |word|
L     word.flip.flop
L   end.join("-")
L 
L   # good
L   words.each { |word|
L     word.flip.flop
L   }.join("-")
S Example: EnforcedStyle: always_braces
L   # bad
L   words.each do |word|
L     word.flip.flop
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   words.each { |word|
L     word.flip.flop
L   }
S Example: BracesRequiredMethods: ['sig']
L   # Methods listed in the BracesRequiredMethods list, such as 'sig'
L   # in this example, will require `{...}` braces. This option takes
L   # precedence over all other configurations except AllowedMethods.
L 
L   # bad
L   sig do
L     params(
L       foo: string,
L     ).void
L   end
L   def bar(foo)
L     puts foo
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   sig {
L     params(
L       foo: string,
L     ).void
L   }
L   def bar(foo)
L     puts foo
L   end
S Example: AllowedMethods: ['lambda', 'proc', 'it' ] (default)
L   # good
L   foo = lambda do |x|
L     puts "Hello, #{x}"
L   end
L 
L   foo = lambda do |x|
L     x * 100
L   end
S Example: AllowedPatterns: [] (default)
L   # bad
L   things.map { |thing|
L     something = thing.some_method
L     process(something)
L   }
S Example: AllowedPatterns: ['map']
L   # good
L   things.map { |thing|
L     something = thing.some_method
L     process(something)
L   }
C Style/CaseEquality
S Details
L Checks for uses of the case equality operator (`===`).
L The `===` operator has different behavior depending on the
L receiver and its use outside of `case`/`when` is confusing.
L Prefer more explicit alternatives like `is_a?`, `include?`,
L or `match?`.
L 
L If `AllowOnConstant` option is enabled, the cop will ignore violations when the receiver of
L the case equality operator is a constant.
L 
L If `AllowOnSelfClass` option is enabled, the cop will ignore violations when the receiver of
L the case equality operator is `self.class`. Note intermediate variables are not accepted.
L 
L NOTE: Regexp case equality (`/regexp/ === var`) is allowed because changing it to
L `/regexp/.match?(var)` needs to take into account `Regexp.last_match?`, `$~`, `$1`, etc.
L This potentially incompatible transformation is handled by `Performance/RegexpMatch` cop.
S Example
L   # bad
L   (1..100) === 7
L 
L   # good
L   (1..100).include?(7)
S Example: AllowOnConstant: false (default)
L   # bad
L   Array === something
L 
L   # good
L   something.is_a?(Array)
S Example: AllowOnConstant: true
L   # good
L   Array === something
L   something.is_a?(Array)
S Example: AllowOnSelfClass: false (default)
L   # bad
L   self.class === something
S Example: AllowOnSelfClass: true
L   # good
L   self.class === something
C Style/CaseLikeIf
S Details
L Identifies places where `if-elsif` constructions
L can be replaced with `case-when`.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe. `case` statements use `===` for equality,
L   so if the original conditional used a different equality operator, the
L   behavior may be different.
S Example: MinBranchesCount: 3 (default)
L   # bad
L   if status == :active
L     perform_action
L   elsif status == :inactive || status == :hibernating
L     check_timeout
L   elsif status == :invalid
L     report_invalid
L   else
L     final_action
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   case status
L   when :active
L     perform_action
L   when :inactive, :hibernating
L     check_timeout
L   when :invalid
L     report_invalid
L   else
L     final_action
L   end
S Example: MinBranchesCount: 4
L   # good
L   if status == :active
L     perform_action
L   elsif status == :inactive || status == :hibernating
L     check_timeout
L   elsif status == :invalid
L     report_invalid
L   else
L     final_action
L   end
C Style/CharacterLiteral
S Details
L Checks for uses of the character literal ?x.
L Starting with Ruby 1.9 character literals are
L essentially one-character strings, so this syntax
L is mostly redundant at this point.
L 
L A `?` character literal can be used to express meta and control characters.
L That's a good use case of a `?` literal so it doesn't count as an offense.
S Example
L   # bad
L   ?x
L 
L   # good
L   'x'
L 
L   # good - control & meta escapes
L   ?\C-\M-d
L   "\C-\M-d" # same as above
C Style/ClassAndModuleChildren
S Details
L Checks that namespaced classes and modules are defined with a consistent style.
L 
L With `nested` style, classes and modules should be defined separately (one constant
L on each line, without `::`). With `compact` style, classes and modules should be
L defined with fully qualified names (using `::` for namespaces).
L 
L NOTE: The style chosen will affect `Module.nesting` for the class or module. Using
L `nested` style will result in each level being added, whereas `compact` style will
L only include the fully qualified class or module name.
L 
L By default, `EnforcedStyle` applies to both classes and modules. If desired, separate
L styles can be defined for classes and modules by using `EnforcedStyleForClasses` and
L `EnforcedStyleForModules` respectively. If not set, or set to nil, the `EnforcedStyle`
L value will be used.
S Safety
L   Autocorrection is unsafe.
L 
L   Moving from `compact` to `nested` children requires knowledge of whether the
L   outer parent is a module or a class. Moving from `nested` to `compact` requires
L   verification that the outer parent is defined elsewhere. By default RuboCop does
L   not have the knowledge to perform either operation safely and thus requires
L   manual oversight.
L 
L   When `AllCops/UseProjectIndex` is enabled and the `rubydex` gem is installed,
L   the project-wide index is consulted to resolve whether the outer parent is a
L   class or a module, and compacting is skipped when the outer parent is not
L   defined elsewhere.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: nested (default)
L   # bad
L   class Foo::Bar
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class Foo
L     class Bar
L     end
L   end
S Example: EnforcedStyle: compact
L   # bad
L   class Foo
L     class Bar
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class Foo::Bar
L   end
L 
L The compact style is only forced for classes/modules with one child.
C Style/ClassCheck
S Details
L Enforces consistent use of `Object#is_a?` or `Object#kind_of?`.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: is_a? (default)
L   # bad
L   var.kind_of?(Date)
L   var.kind_of?(Integer)
L 
L   # good
L   var.is_a?(Date)
L   var.is_a?(Integer)
S Example: EnforcedStyle: kind_of?
L   # bad
L   var.is_a?(Time)
L   var.is_a?(String)
L 
L   # good
L   var.kind_of?(Time)
L   var.kind_of?(String)
C Style/ClassEqualityComparison
S Details
L Enforces the use of `Object#instance_of?` instead of class comparison
L for equality.
L `==`, `equal?`, and `eql?` custom method definitions are allowed by default.
L These are customizable with `AllowedMethods` option.
S Safety
L   This cop's autocorrection is unsafe because there is no guarantee that
L   the constant `Foo` exists when autocorrecting `var.class.name == 'Foo'` to
L   `var.instance_of?(Foo)`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   var.class == Date
L   var.class.equal?(Date)
L   var.class.eql?(Date)
L   var.class.name == 'Date'
L 
L   # good
L   var.instance_of?(Date)
S Example: AllowedMethods: ['==', 'equal?', 'eql?'] (default)
L   # good
L   def ==(other)
L     self.class == other.class && name == other.name
L   end
L 
L   def equal?(other)
L     self.class.equal?(other.class) && name.equal?(other.name)
L   end
L 
L   def eql?(other)
L     self.class.eql?(other.class) && name.eql?(other.name)
L   end
S Example: AllowedPatterns: [] (default)
L   # bad
L   def eq(other)
L     self.class.eq(other.class) && name.eq(other.name)
L   end
S Example: AllowedPatterns: ['eq']
L   # good
L   def eq(other)
L     self.class.eq(other.class) && name.eq(other.name)
L   end
C Style/ClassMethods
S Details
L Checks for uses of the class/module name instead of
L self, when defining class/module methods.
S Example
L   # bad
L   class SomeClass
L     def SomeClass.class_method
L       # ...
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class SomeClass
L     def self.class_method
L       # ...
L     end
L   end
C Style/ClassMethodsDefinitions
S Details
L Enforces using `def self.method_name` or `class << self` to define class methods.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: def_self (default)
L   # bad
L   class SomeClass
L     class << self
L       attr_accessor :class_accessor
L 
L       def class_method
L         # ...
L       end
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class SomeClass
L     def self.class_method
L       # ...
L     end
L 
L     class << self
L       attr_accessor :class_accessor
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good - contains private method
L   class SomeClass
L     class << self
L       attr_accessor :class_accessor
L 
L       private
L 
L       def private_class_method
L         # ...
L       end
L     end
L   end
S Example: EnforcedStyle: self_class
L   # bad
L   class SomeClass
L     def self.class_method
L       # ...
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class SomeClass
L     class << self
L       def class_method
L         # ...
L       end
L     end
L   end
C Style/ClassVars
S Details
L Checks for uses of class variables. Offenses
L are signaled only on assignment to class variables to
L reduce the number of offenses that would be reported.
L 
L You have to be careful when setting a value for a class
L variable; if a class has been inherited, changing the
L value of a class variable also affects the inheriting
L classes. This means that it's almost always better to
L use a class instance variable instead.
S Example
L   # bad
L   class A
L     @@test = 10
L   end
L 
L   class A
L     def self.test(name, value)
L       class_variable_set("@@#{name}", value)
L     end
L   end
L 
L   class A; end
L   A.class_variable_set(:@@test, 10)
L 
L   # good
L   class A
L     @test = 10
L   end
L 
L   class A
L     def test
L       @@test # you can access class variable without offense
L     end
L   end
L 
L   class A
L     def self.test(name)
L       class_variable_get("@@#{name}") # you can access without offense
L     end
L   end
C Style/CollectionCompact
S Details
L Checks for places where custom logic on rejection nils from arrays
L and hashes can be replaced with `{Array,Hash}#{compact,compact!}`.
S Safety
L   It is unsafe by default because false positives may occur in the
L   `nil` check of block arguments to the receiver object. Additionally,
L   we can't know the type of the receiver object for sure, which may
L   result in false positives as well.
L 
L   For example, `[[1, 2], [3, nil]].reject { |first, second| second.nil? }`
L   and `[[1, 2], [3, nil]].compact` are not compatible. This will work fine
L   when the receiver is a hash object.
S Example
L   # bad
L   array.reject(&:nil?)
L   array.reject { |e| e.nil? }
L   array.select { |e| !e.nil? }
L   array.filter { |e| !e.nil? }
L   array.grep_v(nil)
L   array.grep_v(NilClass)
L 
L   # good
L   array.compact
L 
L   # bad
L   hash.reject!(&:nil?)
L   hash.reject! { |k, v| v.nil? }
L   hash.select! { |k, v| !v.nil? }
L   hash.filter! { |k, v| !v.nil? }
L 
L   # good
L   hash.compact!
S Example: AllowedReceivers: ['params']
L   # good
L   params.reject(&:nil?)
C Style/CollectionMethods
S Details
L Enforces the use of consistent method names
L from the Enumerable module.
L 
L You can customize the mapping from undesired method to desired method.
L 
L e.g. to use `detect` over `find`:
L 
L   Style/CollectionMethods:
L     PreferredMethods:
L       find: detect
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because it finds methods by name, without actually
L   being able to determine if the receiver is an Enumerable or not, so
L   this cop may register false positives.
S Example
L   # These examples are based on the default mapping for `PreferredMethods`.
L 
L   # bad
L   items.collect
L   items.collect!
L   items.collect_concat
L   items.inject
L   items.detect
L   items.find_all
L   items.member?
L 
L   # good
L   items.map
L   items.map!
L   items.flat_map
L   items.reduce
L   items.find
L   items.select
L   items.include?
C Style/CollectionQuerying
S Details
L Prefer `Enumerable` predicate methods over expressions with `count`.
L 
L The cop checks calls to `count` without arguments, or with a
L block. It doesn't register offenses for `count` with a positional
L argument because its behavior differs from predicate methods (`count`
L matches the argument using `==`, while `any?`, `none?` and `one?` use
L `===`).
L 
L NOTE: This cop doesn't check `length` and `size` methods because they
L would yield false positives. For example, `String` implements `length`
L and `size`, but it doesn't include `Enumerable`.
L 
L When `count` is used without a block, it measures the collection's
L size, so the offense is corrected to `empty?` or `!empty?`. The
L predicate methods `any?`, `none?` and `one?` test element truthiness
L instead and would return different results for collections with falsey
L elements (e.g. `[false].count.zero?` is `false`, but `[false].none?`
L is `true`). For the same reason, `count == 1` and `count > 1` without
L a block are not flagged.
S Safety
L   The cop is unsafe because receiver might not include `Enumerable`, or
L   it has nonstandard implementation of `count` or any replacement
L   methods.
L 
L   Blockless `count` is corrected to `empty?`, which `Enumerable` doesn't
L   define. `Range`, `Enumerator` (including lazy ones), `Struct`, `StringIO`
L   and any class that just includes `Enumerable` respond to `count` but not
L   to `empty?`, so `(1...1).count.zero?` is `true` while `(1...1).empty?`
L   raises `NoMethodError`.
L 
L   Autocorrection is unsafe when replacement methods don't iterate over
L   every element in collection and the given block runs side effects:
L 
L   [source,ruby]
L   ----
L   x.count(&:method_with_side_effects).positive?
L   # calls `method_with_side_effects` on every element
L 
L   x.any?(&:method_with_side_effects)
L   # calls `method_with_side_effects` until first element returns a truthy value
L   ----
S Example
L   # bad
L   x.count.positive?
L   x.count > 0
L   x.count != 0
L 
L   # good
L   !x.empty?
L 
L   # bad
L   x.count(&:foo?).positive?
L   x.count { |item| item.foo? }.positive?
L 
L   # good
L   x.any?(&:foo?)
L   x.any? { |item| item.foo? }
L 
L   # bad
L   x.count.zero?
L   x.count == 0
L 
L   # good
L   x.empty?
L 
L   # bad
L   x.count(&:foo?).zero?
L   x.count(&:foo?) == 0
L 
L   # good
L   x.none?(&:foo?)
L 
L   # bad
L   x.count(&:foo?) == 1
L 
L   # good
L   x.one?(&:foo?)
S Example: AllCops:ActiveSupportExtensionsEnabled: false (default)
L   # good
L   x.count(&:foo?) > 1
S Example: AllCops:ActiveSupportExtensionsEnabled: true
L   # bad
L   x.count(&:foo?) > 1
L 
L   # good
L   x.many?(&:foo?)
C Style/ColonMethodCall
S Details
L Checks for methods invoked via the `::` operator instead
L of the `.` operator (like `FileUtils::rmdir` instead of
L `FileUtils.rmdir`). The `::` operator is conventionally used to
L reference constants, so using it for method calls can be misleading.
S Example
L   # bad
L   Timeout::timeout(500) { do_something }
L   FileUtils::rmdir(dir)
L   Marshal::dump(obj)
L 
L   # good
L   Timeout.timeout(500) { do_something }
L   FileUtils.rmdir(dir)
L   Marshal.dump(obj)
C Style/ColonMethodDefinition
S Details
L Checks for class methods that are defined using the `::`
L operator instead of the `.` operator.
S Example
L   # bad
L   class Foo
L     def self::bar
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class Foo
L     def self.bar
L     end
L   end
C Style/CombinableDefined
S Details
L Checks for multiple `defined?` calls joined by `&&` that can be combined
L into a single `defined?`.
L 
L When checking that a nested constant or chained method is defined, it is
L not necessary to check each ancestor or component of the chain.
S Example
L   # bad
L   defined?(Foo) && defined?(Foo::Bar) && defined?(Foo::Bar::Baz)
L 
L   # good
L   defined?(Foo::Bar::Baz)
L 
L   # bad
L   defined?(foo) && defined?(foo.bar) && defined?(foo.bar.baz)
L 
L   # good
L   defined?(foo.bar.baz)
C Style/CombinableLoops
S Details
L Checks for places where multiple consecutive loops over the same data
L can be combined into a single loop. It is very likely that combining them
L will make the code more efficient and more concise.
L 
L NOTE: Autocorrection is not applied when the block variable names differ in separate loops,
L as it is impossible to determine which variable name should be prioritized.
S Safety
L   The cop is unsafe, because the first loop might modify state that the
L   second loop depends on; these two aren't combinable.
S Example
L   # bad
L   def method
L     items.each do |item|
L       do_something(item)
L     end
L 
L     items.each do |item|
L       do_something_else(item)
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def method
L     items.each do |item|
L       do_something(item)
L       do_something_else(item)
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   def method
L     for item in items do
L       do_something(item)
L     end
L 
L     for item in items do
L       do_something_else(item)
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def method
L     for item in items do
L       do_something(item)
L       do_something_else(item)
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def method
L     each_slice(2) { |slice| do_something(slice) }
L     each_slice(3) { |slice| do_something(slice) }
L   end
C Style/CommandLiteral
S Details
L Enforces using `` or %x around command literals.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: backticks (default)
L   # bad
L   folders = %x(find . -type d).split
L 
L   # bad
L   %x(
L     ln -s foo.example.yml foo.example
L     ln -s bar.example.yml bar.example
L   )
L 
L   # good
L   folders = `find . -type d`.split
L 
L   # good
L   `
L     ln -s foo.example.yml foo.example
L     ln -s bar.example.yml bar.example
L   `
S Example: EnforcedStyle: mixed
L   # bad
L   folders = %x(find . -type d).split
L 
L   # bad
L   `
L     ln -s foo.example.yml foo.example
L     ln -s bar.example.yml bar.example
L   `
L 
L   # good
L   folders = `find . -type d`.split
L 
L   # good
L   %x(
L     ln -s foo.example.yml foo.example
L     ln -s bar.example.yml bar.example
L   )
S Example: EnforcedStyle: percent_x
L   # bad
L   folders = `find . -type d`.split
L 
L   # bad
L   `
L     ln -s foo.example.yml foo.example
L     ln -s bar.example.yml bar.example
L   `
L 
L   # good
L   folders = %x(find . -type d).split
L 
L   # good
L   %x(
L     ln -s foo.example.yml foo.example
L     ln -s bar.example.yml bar.example
L   )
S Example: AllowInnerBackticks: false (default)
L   # If `false`, the cop will always recommend using `%x` if one or more
L   # backticks are found in the command string.
L 
L   # bad
L   `echo \`ls\``
L 
L   # good
L   %x(echo `ls`)
S Example: AllowInnerBackticks: true
L   # good
L   `echo \`ls\``
C Style/CommentAnnotation
S Details
L Checks that comment annotation keywords are written according
L to guidelines.
L 
L Annotation keywords can be specified by overriding the cop's `Keywords`
L configuration. Keywords are allowed to be single words or phrases.
L 
L NOTE: With a multiline comment block (where each line is only a
L comment), only the first line will be able to register an offense, even
L if an annotation keyword starts another line. This is done to prevent
L incorrect registering of keywords (eg. `review`) inside a paragraph as an
L annotation.
S Example: RequireColon: true (default)
L   # bad
L   # TODO make better
L 
L   # good
L   # TODO: make better
L 
L   # bad
L   # TODO:make better
L 
L   # good
L   # TODO: make better
L 
L   # bad
L   # fixme: does not work
L 
L   # good
L   # FIXME: does not work
L 
L   # bad
L   # Optimize does not work
L 
L   # good
L   # OPTIMIZE: does not work
S Example: RequireColon: false
L   # bad
L   # TODO: make better
L 
L   # good
L   # TODO make better
L 
L   # bad
L   # fixme does not work
L 
L   # good
L   # FIXME does not work
L 
L   # bad
L   # Optimize does not work
L 
L   # good
L   # OPTIMIZE does not work
C Style/CommentedKeyword
S Details
L Checks for comments put on the same line as some keywords.
L These keywords are: `class`, `module`, `def`, `begin`, `end`.
L 
L Note that some comments
L (`:nodoc:`, `:yields:`, `rubocop:disable` and `rubocop:todo`),
L RBS::Inline annotation, and Steep annotation (`steep:ignore`) are allowed.
L 
L Autocorrection removes comments from `end` keyword and keeps comments
L for `class`, `module`, `def` and `begin` above the keyword.
S Safety
L   Autocorrection is unsafe because it may remove a comment that is
L   meaningful.
S Example
L   # bad
L   if condition
L     statement
L   end # end if
L 
L   # bad
L   class X # comment
L     statement
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   def x; end # comment
L 
L   # good
L   if condition
L     statement
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class X # :nodoc:
L     y
L   end
C Style/ComparableBetween
S Details
L Checks for logical comparison which can be replaced with `Comparable#between?`.
L 
L NOTE: `Comparable#between?` is on average slightly slower than logical comparison,
L although the difference generally isn't observable. If you require maximum
L performance, consider using logical comparison.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because the receiver may not respond to `between?`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   x >= min && x <= max
L 
L   # bad
L   x <= max && x >= min
L 
L   # good
L   x.between?(min, max)
C Style/ComparableClamp
S Details
L Enforces the use of `Comparable#clamp` instead of comparison by minimum and maximum.
L 
L This cop supports autocorrection for `if/elsif/else` bad style only.
L Because `ArgumentError` occurs if the minimum and maximum of `clamp` arguments are reversed.
L When these are variables, it is not possible to determine which is the minimum and maximum:
L 
L [source,ruby]
L ----
L [1, [2, 3].max].min # => 1
L 1.clamp(3, 1)       # => min argument must be smaller than max argument (ArgumentError)
L ----
S Example
L   # bad
L   [[x, low].max, high].min
L 
L   # bad
L   if x < low
L     low
L   elsif high < x
L     high
L   else
L     x
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   x.clamp(low, high)
C Style/ConcatArrayLiterals
S Details
L Enforces the use of `Array#push(item)` instead of `Array#concat([item])`
L to avoid redundant array literals.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe, as it can produce false positives if the receiver
L   is not an `Array` object.
S Example
L   # bad
L   list.concat([foo])
L   list.concat([bar, baz])
L   list.concat([qux, quux], [corge])
L 
L   # good
L   list.push(foo)
L   list.push(bar, baz)
L   list.push(qux, quux, corge)
C Style/ConditionalAssignment
S Details
L Checks for `if` and `case` statements where each branch is used for
L both the assignment and comparison of the same variable
L when using the return of the condition can be used instead.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: assign_to_condition (default)
L   # bad
L   if foo
L     bar = 1
L   else
L     bar = 2
L   end
L 
L   case foo
L   when 'a'
L     bar += 1
L   else
L     bar += 2
L   end
L 
L   if foo
L     some_method
L     bar = 1
L   else
L     some_other_method
L     bar = 2
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   bar = if foo
L           1
L         else
L           2
L         end
L 
L   bar += case foo
L          when 'a'
L            1
L          else
L            2
L          end
L 
L   bar << if foo
L            some_method
L            1
L          else
L            some_other_method
L            2
L          end
S Example: EnforcedStyle: assign_inside_condition
L   # bad
L   bar = if foo
L           1
L         else
L           2
L         end
L 
L   bar += case foo
L          when 'a'
L            1
L          else
L            2
L          end
L 
L   bar << if foo
L            some_method
L            1
L          else
L            some_other_method
L            2
L          end
L 
L   # good
L   if foo
L     bar = 1
L   else
L     bar = 2
L   end
L 
L   case foo
L   when 'a'
L     bar += 1
L   else
L     bar += 2
L   end
L 
L   if foo
L     some_method
L     bar = 1
L   else
L     some_other_method
L     bar = 2
L   end
C Style/ConstantVisibility
S Details
L Checks that constants defined in classes and modules have
L an explicit visibility declaration. By default, Ruby makes all class-
L and module constants public, which litters the public API of the
L class or module. Explicitly declaring a visibility makes intent more
L clear, and prevents outside actors from touching private state.
S Example
L   # bad
L   class Foo
L     BAR = 42
L     BAZ = 43
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class Foo
L     BAR = 42
L     private_constant :BAR
L 
L     BAZ = 43
L     public_constant :BAZ
L   end
S Example: IgnoreModules: false (default)
L   # bad
L   class Foo
L     MyClass = Struct.new
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class Foo
L     MyClass = Struct.new
L     public_constant :MyClass
L   end
S Example: IgnoreModules: true
L   # good
L   class Foo
L     MyClass = Struct.new
L   end
C Style/Copyright
S Details
L Checks that a copyright notice was given in each source file.
L 
L The default regexp for an acceptable copyright notice can be found in
L config/default.yml. The default can be changed as follows:
L 
L [source,yaml]
L ----
L Style/Copyright:
L   Notice: '^Copyright (\(c\) )?2\d{3} Acme Inc'
L ----
L 
L This regex string is treated as an unanchored regex. For each file
L that RuboCop scans, a comment that matches this regex must be found or
L an offense is reported.
C Style/DataInheritance
S Details
L Checks for inheritance from `Data.define` to avoid creating the anonymous parent class.
L Inheriting from `Data.define` adds a superfluous level in inheritance tree.
S Safety
L   Autocorrection is unsafe because it will change the inheritance
L   tree (e.g. return value of `Module#ancestors`) of the constant.
L 
L   It is also unsafe because constants that the class body resolves through its
L   ancestors (e.g. one provided by an included module) fall out of scope inside
L   the block.
S Example
L   # bad
L   class Person < Data.define(:first_name, :last_name)
L     def age
L       42
L     end
L   end
L 
L   Person.ancestors
L   # => [Person, #<Class:0x000000010b4e14a0>, Data, (...)]
L 
L   # good
L   Person = Data.define(:first_name, :last_name) do
L     def age
L       42
L     end
L   end
L 
L   Person.ancestors
L   # => [Person, Data, (...)]
C Style/DateTime
S Details
L Checks for consistent usage of the `Time` class over the
L `DateTime` class. This cop is disabled by default since these classes,
L although highly overlapping, have particularities that make them not
L replaceable in certain situations when dealing with multiple timezones
L and/or DST.
S Safety
L   Autocorrection is not safe, because `DateTime` and `Time` do not have
L   exactly the same behavior, although in most cases the autocorrection
L   will be fine.
S Example
L   # bad - uses `DateTime` for current time
L   DateTime.now
L 
L   # good - uses `Time` for current time
L   Time.now
L 
L   # bad - uses `DateTime` for modern date
L   DateTime.iso8601('2016-06-29')
L 
L   # good - uses `Time` for modern date
L   Time.iso8601('2016-06-29')
L 
L   # good - uses `DateTime` with start argument for historical date
L   DateTime.iso8601('1751-04-23', Date::ENGLAND)
S Example: AllowCoercion: false (default)
L   # bad - coerces to `DateTime`
L   something.to_datetime
L 
L   # good - coerces to `Time`
L   something.to_time
S Example: AllowCoercion: true
L   # good
L   something.to_datetime
L 
L   # good
L   something.to_time
C Style/DefWithParentheses
S Details
L Checks for parentheses in the definition of a method,
L that does not take any arguments. Both instance and
L class/singleton methods are checked.
S Example
L   # bad
L   def foo()
L     do_something
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo
L     do_something
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   def foo() = do_something
L 
L   # good
L   def foo = do_something
L 
L   # good - without parentheses it's a syntax error
L   def foo() do_something end
L   def foo()=do_something
L 
L   # bad
L   def Baz.foo()
L     do_something
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def Baz.foo
L     do_something
L   end
C Style/DigChain
S Details
L Checks for chained `dig` calls that can be collapsed into a single `dig`.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because it cannot be guaranteed that the receiver
L   is an `Enumerable` or does not have a nonstandard implementation
L   of `dig`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   x.dig(:foo).dig(:bar).dig(:baz)
L   x.dig(:foo, :bar).dig(:baz)
L   x.dig(:foo, :bar)&.dig(:baz)
L 
L   # good
L   x.dig(:foo, :bar, :baz)
L 
L   # good - `dig`s cannot be combined
L   x.dig(:foo).bar.dig(:baz)
C Style/Dir
S Details
L Checks for places where the `#\_\_dir\_\_` method can replace more
L complex constructs to retrieve a canonicalized absolute path to the
L current file.
S Example
L   # bad
L   path = File.expand_path(File.dirname(__FILE__))
L 
L   # bad
L   path = File.dirname(File.realpath(__FILE__))
L 
L   # good
L   path = __dir__
C Style/DirEmpty
S Details
L Prefer to use `Dir.empty?('path/to/dir')` when checking if a directory is empty.
S Example
L   # bad
L   Dir.entries('path/to/dir').size == 2
L   Dir.children('path/to/dir').empty?
L   Dir.children('path/to/dir').size == 0
L   Dir.each_child('path/to/dir').none?
L 
L   # good
L   Dir.empty?('path/to/dir')
C Style/DirectiveScope
S Details
L Checks for directive scopes that can be expressed with the tighter
L next-statement forms: a `disable`/`enable` pair, an
L `enable`/`disable` pair, or a `push`/`pop` with signed arguments
L wrapping exactly one statement. A statement-scoped directive cannot
L drift as the surrounding code changes and needs no closing boundary.
S Safety
L   The autocorrection is unsafe because the suppression scope shrinks
L   from the whole region to the statement: offenses of the suppressed
L   cops located on the directive lines themselves resurface.
S Example
L   # bad
L   # rubocop:disable Metrics/AbcSize
L   def foo
L   end
L   # rubocop:enable Metrics/AbcSize
L 
L   # good
L   # rubocop:disable-next Metrics/AbcSize
L   def foo
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   # rubocop:push -Metrics/AbcSize
L   def foo
L   end
L   # rubocop:pop
L 
L   # good
L   # rubocop:disable-next Metrics/AbcSize
L   def foo
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   # rubocop:disable Metrics/AbcSize
L   # rubocop:push +Metrics/AbcSize
L   def foo
L   end
L   # rubocop:pop
L   # rubocop:enable Metrics/AbcSize
L 
L   # good
L   # rubocop:disable Metrics/AbcSize
L   # rubocop:enable-next Metrics/AbcSize
L   def foo
L   end
L   # rubocop:enable Metrics/AbcSize
L 
L   # good - the region spans more than one statement
L   # rubocop:disable Metrics/AbcSize
L   def foo
L   end
L 
L   def bar
L   end
L   # rubocop:enable Metrics/AbcSize
C Style/DisableCopsWithinSourceCodeDirective
S Details
L Detects comments to enable/disable RuboCop.
L This is useful if you want to make sure that every RuboCop error gets fixed
L and not quickly disabled with a comment.
L 
L Specific cops can be allowed with the `AllowedCops` configuration. Note that
L if this configuration is set, `rubocop:disable all` is still disallowed.
L 
L `AllowedCops` and `DisallowedCops` accept cop names and department names
L alike. Listing a cop does not cover its whole department: a directive
L disabling `Metrics` is broader than one disabling `Metrics/AbcSize`.
L 
L Alternatively, specific cops can be disallowed with the `DisallowedCops`
L configuration. When `DisallowedCops` is set, only directives for the listed
L cops (and `all`) will be flagged. This is useful when you want
L to protect a small set of critical cops from being disabled rather than
L allowlisting all other cops. `AllowedCops` and `DisallowedCops` should not
L both be set at the same time; if `DisallowedCops` is set, it takes precedence.
L 
L `AllowedDirectives` names directive forms - `disable`, `todo`,
L `disable-next`, `todo-next`, `push`, `next` - and exempts them from the
L cop entirely. `rubocop:todo` is the usual candidate, since
L `--disable-uncorrectable` generates those and they record debt rather
L than a decision someone should be asked to justify.
L 
L With `AllowWithReason` set to `true`, a disable directive carrying
L a `--` trailing justification comment is allowed, so a team can require
