L every disable to be documented instead of banning them outright. Enable
L directives are not checked in this mode, since they end a suppression
L rather than start one. Offenses in this mode are not autocorrected,
L since only a human can supply the missing justification.
L 
L This cop cannot be disabled via directive comments when it is explicitly
L enabled with `Enabled: true`. This prevents users from bypassing the cop
L with `# rubocop:disable Style/DisableCopsWithinSourceCodeDirective`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   # rubocop:disable Metrics/AbcSize
L   def foo
L   end
L   # rubocop:enable Metrics/AbcSize
L 
L   # good
L   def foo
L   end
S Example: AllowedCops: [Metrics/AbcSize]
L   # good
L   # rubocop:disable Metrics/AbcSize
L   def foo
L   end
L   # rubocop:enable Metrics/AbcSize
S Example: DisallowedCops: [Lint/Void]
L   # bad
L   # rubocop:disable Lint/Void
L   foo
L   # rubocop:enable Lint/Void
L 
L   # good
L   # rubocop:disable Metrics/AbcSize
L   foo
L   # rubocop:enable Metrics/AbcSize
S Example: AllowedDirectives: [todo]
L   # good - the cop does not look at `todo` directives at all
L   x = 0 # rubocop:todo Layout/SpaceAroundOperators
L 
L   # bad
L   x = 0 # rubocop:disable Layout/SpaceAroundOperators
S Example: AllowedCops: [Metrics]
L   # good - every cop the directive disables is in an exempt department
L   # rubocop:disable Metrics/AbcSize
L   def foo
L   end
L   # rubocop:enable Metrics/AbcSize
S Example: AllowWithReason: true
L   # bad
L   x = 0 # rubocop:disable Layout/SpaceAroundOperators
L 
L   # good
L   x = 0 # rubocop:disable Layout/SpaceAroundOperators -- would misalign the table
C Style/DocumentDynamicEvalDefinition
S Details
L When using `class_eval` (or other `eval`) with string interpolation,
L add a comment block showing its appearance if interpolated (a practice used in Rails code).
S Example
L   # from activesupport/lib/active_support/core_ext/string/output_safety.rb
L 
L   # bad
L   UNSAFE_STRING_METHODS.each do |unsafe_method|
L     if 'String'.respond_to?(unsafe_method)
L       class_eval <<-EOT, __FILE__, __LINE__ + 1
L         def #{unsafe_method}(*params, &block)
L           to_str.#{unsafe_method}(*params, &block)
L         end
L 
L         def #{unsafe_method}!(*params)
L           @dirty = true
L           super
L         end
L       EOT
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good, inline comments in heredoc
L   UNSAFE_STRING_METHODS.each do |unsafe_method|
L     if 'String'.respond_to?(unsafe_method)
L       class_eval <<-EOT, __FILE__, __LINE__ + 1
L         def #{unsafe_method}(*params, &block)       # def capitalize(*params, &block)
L           to_str.#{unsafe_method}(*params, &block)  #   to_str.capitalize(*params, &block)
L         end                                         # end
L 
L         def #{unsafe_method}!(*params)              # def capitalize!(*params)
L           @dirty = true                             #   @dirty = true
L           super                                     #   super
L         end                                         # end
L       EOT
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good, block comments in heredoc
L   class_eval <<-EOT, __FILE__, __LINE__ + 1
L     # def capitalize!(*params)
L     #   @dirty = true
L     #   super
L     # end
L 
L     def #{unsafe_method}!(*params)
L       @dirty = true
L       super
L     end
L   EOT
L 
L   # good, block comments before heredoc
L   class_eval(
L     # def capitalize!(*params)
L     #   @dirty = true
L     #   super
L     # end
L 
L     <<-EOT, __FILE__, __LINE__ + 1
L       def #{unsafe_method}!(*params)
L         @dirty = true
L         super
L       end
L     EOT
L   )
L 
L   # bad - interpolated string without comment
L   class_eval("def #{unsafe_method}!(*params); end")
L 
L   # good - with inline comment or replace it with block comment using heredoc
L   class_eval("def #{unsafe_method}!(*params); end # def capitalize!(*params); end")
C Style/Documentation
S Details
L Checks for missing top-level documentation of classes and
L modules. Classes with no body are exempt from the check and so are
L namespace modules - modules that have nothing in their bodies except
L classes, other modules, constant definitions or constant visibility
L declarations.
L 
L The documentation requirement is annulled if the class or module has
L a `#:nodoc:` comment next to it. Likewise, `#:nodoc: all` does the
L same for all its children.
L 
L When `AllCops/UseProjectIndex` is enabled and the `rubydex` gem is
L installed, a reopened class or module is not reported when any of its
L other definition sites (in the same or another file) carries a
L documentation comment.
S Example
L   # bad
L   class Person
L     # ...
L   end
L 
L   module Math
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   # Description/Explanation of Person class
L   class Person
L     # ...
L   end
L 
L   # allowed
L   # Class without body
L   class Person
L   end
L 
L   # Namespace - A namespace can be a class or a module
L   # Containing a class
L   module Namespace
L     # Description/Explanation of Person class
L     class Person
L       # ...
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # Containing constant visibility declaration
L   module Namespace
L     class Private
L     end
L 
L     private_constant :Private
L   end
L 
L   # Containing constant definition
L   module Namespace
L     Public = Class.new
L   end
L 
L   # Macro calls
L   module Namespace
L     extend Foo
L   end
S Example: AllowedConstants: ['ClassMethods']
L   # good
L   module A
L     module ClassMethods
L       # ...
L     end
L   end
C Style/DocumentationMethod
S Details
L Checks for missing documentation comment for public methods.
L It can optionally be configured to also require documentation for
L non-public methods.
L 
L NOTE: This cop allows `initialize` method because `initialize` is
L a special method called from `new`. In some programming languages
L they are called constructor to distinguish it from method.
S Example
L   # bad
L 
L   class Foo
L     def bar
L       puts baz
L     end
L   end
L 
L   module Foo
L     def bar
L       puts baz
L     end
L   end
L 
L   def foo.bar
L     puts baz
L   end
L 
L   # good
L 
L   class Foo
L     # Documentation
L     def bar
L       puts baz
L     end
L   end
L 
L   module Foo
L     # Documentation
L     def bar
L       puts baz
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # Documentation
L   def foo.bar
L     puts baz
L   end
S Example: RequireForNonPublicMethods: false (default)
L   # good
L   class Foo
L     protected
L     def do_something
L     end
L   end
L 
L   class Foo
L     private
L     def do_something
L     end
L   end
S Example: RequireForNonPublicMethods: true
L   # bad
L   class Foo
L     protected
L     def do_something
L     end
L   end
L 
L   class Foo
L     private
L     def do_something
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class Foo
L     protected
L     # Documentation
L     def do_something
L     end
L   end
L 
L   class Foo
L     private
L     # Documentation
L     def do_something
L     end
L   end
S Example: AllowedMethods: ['method_missing', 'respond_to_missing?']
L   # good
L   class Foo
L     def method_missing(name, *args)
L     end
L 
L     def respond_to_missing?(symbol, include_private)
L     end
L   end
C Style/DoubleNegation
S Details
L Checks for uses of double negation (`!!`) to convert something to a boolean value.
L 
L When using `EnforcedStyle: allowed_in_returns`, allow double negation in contexts
L that use boolean as a return value. When using `EnforcedStyle: forbidden`, double negation
L should be forbidden always.
L 
L NOTE: when `something` is a boolean value
L `!!something` and `!something.nil?` are not the same thing.
L As you're unlikely to write code that can accept values of any type
L this is rarely a problem in practice.
S Safety
L   Autocorrection is unsafe when the value is `false`, because the result
L   of the expression will change.
L 
L   [source,ruby]
L   ----
L   !!false     #=> false
L   !false.nil? #=> true
L   ----
S Example
L   # bad
L   !!something
L 
L   # good
L   !something.nil?
S Example: EnforcedStyle: allowed_in_returns (default)
L   # good
L   def foo?
L     !!return_value
L   end
L 
L   define_method :foo? do
L     !!return_value
L   end
L 
L   define_singleton_method :foo? do
L     !!return_value
L   end
S Example: EnforcedStyle: forbidden
L   # bad
L   def foo?
L     !!return_value
L   end
L 
L   define_method :foo? do
L     !!return_value
L   end
L 
L   define_singleton_method :foo? do
L     !!return_value
L   end
C Style/EachForSimpleLoop
S Details
L Checks for loops which iterate a constant number of times,
L using a `Range` literal and `#each`. This can be done more readably using
L `Integer#times`.
L 
L This check only applies if the block takes no parameters.
S Example
L   # bad
L   (1..5).each { }
L 
L   # good
L   5.times { }
L 
L   # bad
L   (0...10).each {}
L 
L   # good
L   10.times {}
C Style/EachWithObject
S Details
L Looks for inject / reduce calls where the passed in object is
L returned at the end and so could be replaced by each_with_object without
L the need to return the object at the end.
L 
L However, we can't replace with each_with_object if the accumulator
L parameter is assigned to within the block.
S Example
L   # bad
L   [1, 2].inject({}) { |a, e| a[e] = e; a }
L 
L   # good
L   [1, 2].each_with_object({}) { |e, a| a[e] = e }
C Style/EmptyBlockParameter
S Details
L Checks for pipes for empty block parameters. Pipes for empty
L block parameters do not cause syntax errors, but they are redundant.
S Example
L   # bad
L   a do ||
L     do_something
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   a { || do_something }
L 
L   # good
L   a do
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   a { do_something }
C Style/EmptyCaseCondition
S Details
L Checks for case statements with an empty condition.
S Example
L   # bad:
L   case
L   when x == 0
L     puts 'x is 0'
L   when y == 0
L     puts 'y is 0'
L   else
L     puts 'neither is 0'
L   end
L 
L   # good:
L   if x == 0
L     puts 'x is 0'
L   elsif y == 0
L     puts 'y is 0'
L   else
L     puts 'neither is 0'
L   end
L 
L   # good: (the case condition node is not empty)
L   case n
L   when 0
L     puts 'zero'
L   when 1
L     puts 'one'
L   else
L     puts 'more'
L   end
C Style/EmptyClassDefinition
S Details
L Enforces consistent style for empty class definitions.
L 
L This cop can enforce either a standard class definition or `Class.new`
L for classes with no body.
L 
L The supported styles are:
L 
L * class_keyword (default) - prefer standard class definition over `Class.new`
L * class_new - prefer `Class.new` over class definition
L 
L One difference between the two styles is that the `Class.new` form does not make
L the subclass name available to the base class's `inherited` callback.
L For this reason, `EnforcedStyle: class_keyword` is set as the default style.
L Class definitions without a superclass, which are not involved in inheritance,
L are not detected. This ensures safe detection regardless of the applied style.
L This avoids overlapping responsibilities with the `Lint/EmptyClass` cop.
L 
L Use `AllowedParentClasses` to permit both styles for specific parent classes.
L For example, adding `StandardError` allows both `Error = Class.new(StandardError)`
L and `class Error < StandardError; end` regardless of the enforced style.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: class_keyword (default)
L   # bad
L   FooError = Class.new(StandardError)
L 
L   # okish
L   class FooError < StandardError; end
L 
L   # good
L   class FooError < StandardError
L   end
S Example: EnforcedStyle: class_new
L   # bad
L   class FooError < StandardError
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   class FooError < StandardError; end
L 
L   # good
L   FooError = Class.new(StandardError)
S Example: AllowedParentClasses: ['StandardError']
L   # good - allowed regardless of EnforcedStyle
L   FooError = Class.new(StandardError)
L 
L   # good - allowed regardless of EnforcedStyle
L   class FooError < StandardError
L   end
C Style/EmptyElse
S Details
L Checks for empty else-clauses, possibly including comments and/or an
L explicit `nil` depending on the EnforcedStyle.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: both (default)
L   # warn on empty else and else with nil in it
L 
L   # bad
L   if condition
L     statement
L   else
L     nil
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   if condition
L     statement
L   else
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   if condition
L     statement
L   else
L     statement
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   if condition
L     statement
L   end
S Example: EnforcedStyle: empty
L   # warn only on empty else
L 
L   # bad
L   if condition
L     statement
L   else
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   if condition
L     statement
L   else
L     nil
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   if condition
L     statement
L   else
L     statement
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   if condition
L     statement
L   end
S Example: EnforcedStyle: nil
L   # warn on else with nil in it
L 
L   # bad
L   if condition
L     statement
L   else
L     nil
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   if condition
L     statement
L   else
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   if condition
L     statement
L   else
L     statement
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   if condition
L     statement
L   end
S Example: AllowComments: false (default)
L   # bad
L   if condition
L     statement
L   else
L     # something comment
L     nil
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   if condition
L     statement
L   else
L     # something comment
L   end
S Example: AllowComments: true
L   # good
L   if condition
L     statement
L   else
L     # something comment
L     nil
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   if condition
L     statement
L   else
L     # something comment
L   end
C Style/EmptyHeredoc
S Details
L Checks for using empty heredoc to reduce redundancy.
S Example
L   # bad
L   <<~EOS
L   EOS
L 
L   <<-EOS
L   EOS
L 
L   <<EOS
L   EOS
L 
L   # good
L   ''
L 
L   # bad
L   do_something(<<~EOS)
L   EOS
L 
L   do_something(<<-EOS)
L   EOS
L 
L   do_something(<<EOS)
L   EOS
L 
L   # good
L   do_something('')
C Style/EmptyLambdaParameter
S Details
L Checks for parentheses for empty lambda parameters. Parentheses
L for empty lambda parameters do not cause syntax errors, but they are
L redundant.
S Example
L   # bad
L   -> () { do_something }
L 
L   # good
L   -> { do_something }
L 
L   # good
L   -> (arg) { do_something(arg) }
C Style/EmptyLiteral
S Details
L Checks for the use of a method, the result of which
L would be a literal, like an empty array, hash, or string.
L 
L NOTE: When frozen string literals are enabled, `String.new`
L isn't corrected to an empty string since the former is
L mutable and the latter would be frozen.
S Example
L   # bad
L   a = Array.new
L   a = Array[]
L   h = Hash.new
L   h = Hash[]
L   s = String.new
L 
L   # good
L   a = []
L   h = {}
L   s = ''
C Style/EmptyMethod
S Details
L Checks for the formatting of empty method definitions.
L By default it enforces empty method definitions to go on a single
L line (compact style), but it can be configured to enforce the `end`
L to go on its own line (expanded style).
L 
L NOTE: A method definition is not considered empty if it contains
L comments.
L 
L NOTE: Autocorrection will not be applied for the `compact` style
L if the resulting code is longer than the `Max` configuration for
L `Layout/LineLength`, but an offense will still be registered.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: compact (default)
L   # bad
L   def foo(bar)
L   end
L 
L   def self.foo(bar)
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo(bar); end
L 
L   def foo(bar)
L     # baz
L   end
L 
L   def self.foo(bar); end
S Example: EnforcedStyle: expanded
L   # bad
L   def foo(bar); end
L 
L   def self.foo(bar); end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo(bar)
L   end
L 
L   def self.foo(bar)
L   end
C Style/EmptyStringInsideInterpolation
S Details
L Checks for empty strings being assigned inside string interpolation.
L 
L Empty strings are a meaningless outcome inside of string interpolation, so we remove them.
L Alternatively, when configured to do so, we prioritise using empty strings.
L 
L While this cop would also apply to variables that are only going to be used as strings,
L RuboCop can't detect that, so we only check inside of string interpolation.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: trailing_conditional (default)
L   # bad
L   "#{condition ? 'foo' : ''}"
L 
L   # good
L   "#{'foo' if condition}"
L 
L   # bad
L   "#{condition ? '' : 'foo'}"
L 
L   # good
L   "#{'foo' unless condition}"
S Example: EnforcedStyle: ternary
L   # bad
L   "#{'foo' if condition}"
L 
L   # good
L   "#{condition ? 'foo' : ''}"
L 
L   # bad
L   "#{'foo' unless condition}"
L 
L   # good
L   "#{condition ? '' : 'foo'}"
C Style/Encoding
S Details
L Checks that source files have no utf-8 encoding comments.
L Since Ruby 2.0, UTF-8 is the default source encoding, so
L these comments are no longer necessary and just add noise.
S Example
L   # bad
L   # encoding: UTF-8
L   # coding: UTF-8
L   # -*- coding: UTF-8 -*-
L 
L   # good
L   # No encoding comment needed
C Style/EndBlock
S Details
L Checks for `END` blocks. `END` blocks are Perl-style constructs
L and `Kernel#at_exit` is the idiomatic Ruby alternative, as it's
L explicit and can be used anywhere.
S Example
L   # bad
L   END { puts 'Goodbye!' }
L 
L   # good
L   at_exit { puts 'Goodbye!' }
C Style/EndlessMethod
S Details
L Checks for endless methods.
L 
L It can enforce endless method definitions whenever possible or with single line methods.
L It can also disallow multiline endless method definitions or all endless definitions.
L 
L `require_single_line` style enforces endless method definitions for single line methods.
L `require_always` style enforces endless method definitions for single statement methods.
L 
L Other method definition types are not considered by this cop.
L 
L The supported styles are:
L 
L * allow_single_line (default) - only single line endless method definitions are allowed.
L * allow_always - all endless method definitions are allowed.
L * disallow - all endless method definitions are disallowed.
L * require_single_line - endless method definitions are required for single line methods.
L * require_always - all endless method definitions are required.
L 
L NOTE: Incorrect endless method definitions will always be
L corrected to a multi-line definition.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: allow_single_line (default)
L   # bad, multi-line endless method
L   def my_method = x.foo
L                    .bar
L                    .baz
L 
L   # good
L   def my_method
L     x
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def my_method = x
L 
L   # good
L   def my_method
L     x.foo
L      .bar
L      .baz
L   end
S Example: EnforcedStyle: allow_always
L   # good
L   def my_method
L     x
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def my_method = x
L 
L   # good
L   def my_method = x.foo
L                    .bar
L                    .baz
L 
L   # good
L   def my_method
L     x.foo
L      .bar
L      .baz
L   end
S Example: EnforcedStyle: disallow
L   # bad
L   def my_method = x
L 
L   # bad
L   def my_method = x.foo
L                    .bar
L                    .baz
L 
L   # good
L   def my_method
L     x
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def my_method
L     x.foo
L      .bar
L      .baz
L   end
S Example: EnforcedStyle: require_single_line
L   # bad
L   def my_method
L     x
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   def my_method = x.foo
L                    .bar
L                    .baz
L 
L   # good
L   def my_method = x
L 
L   # good
L   def my_method
L     x.foo
L      .bar
L      .baz
L   end
S Example: EnforcedStyle: require_always
L   # bad
L   def my_method
L     x
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   def my_method
L     x.foo
L      .bar
L      .baz
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def my_method = x
L 
L   # good
L   def my_method = x.foo
L                    .bar
L                    .baz
C Style/EnvHome
S Details
L Checks for consistent usage of `ENV['HOME']`. If `nil` is used as
L the second argument of `ENV.fetch`, it is treated as a bad case like `ENV[]`.
S Safety
L   The cop is unsafe because the result when `nil` is assigned to `ENV['HOME']` changes:
L 
L   [source,ruby]
L   ----
L   ENV['HOME'] = nil
L   ENV['HOME'] # => nil
L   Dir.home    # => '/home/foo'
L   ----
S Example
L   # bad
L   ENV['HOME']
L   ENV.fetch('HOME', nil)
L 
L   # good
L   Dir.home
L 
L   # good
L   ENV.fetch('HOME', default)
C Style/EvalWithLocation
S Details
L Ensures that eval methods (`eval`, `instance_eval`, `class_eval`
L and `module_eval`) are given filename and line number values (`+__FILE__+`
L and `+__LINE__+`). This data is used to ensure that any errors raised
L within the evaluated code will be given the correct identification
L in a backtrace.
L 
L The cop also checks that the line number given relative to `+__LINE__+` is
L correct.
L 
L This cop will autocorrect incorrect or missing filename and line number
L values. However, if `eval` is called without a binding argument, the cop
L will not attempt to automatically add a binding, or add filename and
L line values.
L 
L NOTE: This cop works only when a string literal is given as a code string.
L No offense is reported if a string variable is given as below:
L 
L [source,ruby]
L ----
L code = <<-RUBY
L   def do_something
L   end
L RUBY
L eval code # not checked.
L ----
S Example
L   # bad
L   eval <<-RUBY
L     def do_something
L     end
L   RUBY
L 
L   # bad
L   C.class_eval <<-RUBY
L     def do_something
L     end
L   RUBY
L 
L   # good
L   eval <<-RUBY, binding, __FILE__, __LINE__ + 1
L     def do_something
L     end
L   RUBY
L 
L   # good
L   C.class_eval <<-RUBY, __FILE__, __LINE__ + 1
L     def do_something
L     end
L   RUBY
C Style/EvenOdd
S Details
L Checks for places where `Integer#even?` or `Integer#odd?`
L can be used.
S Example
L   # bad
L   if x % 2 == 0
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   if x.even?
L   end
C Style/ExactRegexpMatch
S Details
L Checks for exact regexp match inside `Regexp` literals.
L 
L `=~` returns an index and `match` returns `MatchData`, so they are only
L autocorrected when their value is used as a condition. The same goes for
L safe navigation calls, which return `nil` for a `nil` receiver.
S Example
L   # bad
L   string =~ /\Astring\z/
L   /\Astring\z/ === string
L   string.match(/\Astring\z/)
L   string.match?(/\Astring\z/)
L 
L   # good
L   string == 'string'
L 
L   # bad
L   string !~ /\Astring\z/
L 
L   # good
L   string != 'string'
C Style/ExpandPathArguments
S Details
L Checks for use of the `File.expand_path` arguments.
L Likewise, it also checks for the `Pathname.new` argument.
L 
L Contrastive bad case and good case are alternately shown in
L the following examples.
S Example
L   # bad
L   File.expand_path('..', __FILE__)
L 
L   # good
L   File.expand_path(__dir__)
L 
L   # bad
L   File.expand_path('../..', __FILE__)
L 
L   # good
L   File.expand_path('..', __dir__)
L 
L   # bad
L   File.expand_path('.', __FILE__)
L 
L   # good
L   File.expand_path(__FILE__)
L 
L   # bad
L   Pathname(__FILE__).parent.expand_path
L 
L   # good
L   Pathname(__dir__).expand_path
L 
L   # bad
L   Pathname.new(__FILE__).parent.expand_path
L 
L   # good
L   Pathname.new(__dir__).expand_path
C Style/ExplicitBlockArgument
S Details
L Enforces the use of explicit block argument to avoid writing
L block literal that just passes its arguments to another block.
L 
L NOTE: This cop only registers an offense if the block args match the
L yield args exactly.
S Example
L   # bad
L   def with_tmp_dir
L     Dir.mktmpdir do |tmp_dir|
L       Dir.chdir(tmp_dir) { |dir| yield dir } # block just passes arguments
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   def nine_times
L     9.times { yield }
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def with_tmp_dir(&block)
L     Dir.mktmpdir do |tmp_dir|
L       Dir.chdir(tmp_dir, &block)
L     end
L   end
L 
L   with_tmp_dir do |dir|
L     puts "dir is accessible as a parameter and pwd is set: #{dir}"
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def nine_times(&block)
L     9.times(&block)
L   end
C Style/ExponentialNotation
S Details
L Enforces consistency when using exponential notation
L for numbers in the code (eg 1.2e4). Different styles are supported:
L 
L * `scientific` which enforces a mantissa between 1 (inclusive) and 10 (exclusive).
L * `engineering` which enforces the exponent to be a multiple of 3 and the mantissa
L   to be between 0.1 (inclusive) and 1000 (exclusive).
L * `integral` which enforces the mantissa to always be a whole number without
L   trailing zeroes.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: scientific (default)
L   # Enforces a mantissa between 1 (inclusive) and 10 (exclusive).
L 
L   # bad
L   10e6
L   0.3e4
L   11.7e5
L   3.14e0
L 
L   # good
L   1e7
L   3e3
L   1.17e6
L   3.14
S Example: EnforcedStyle: engineering
L   # Enforces using multiple of 3 exponents,
L   # mantissa should be between 0.1 (inclusive) and 1000 (exclusive)
L 
L   # bad
L   3.2e7
L   0.1e5
L   12e5
L   1232e6
L 
L   # good
L   32e6
L   10e3
L   1.2e6
L   1.232e9
S Example: EnforcedStyle: integral
L   # Enforces the mantissa to have no decimal part and no
L   # trailing zeroes.
L 
L   # bad
L   3.2e7
L   0.1e5
L   120e4
L 
L   # good
L   32e6
L   1e4
L   12e5
C Style/FetchEnvVar
S Details
L Suggests `ENV.fetch` for the replacement of `ENV[]`.
L `ENV[]` silently fails and returns `nil` when the environment variable is unset,
L which may cause unexpected behaviors when the developer forgets to set it.
L On the other hand, `ENV.fetch` raises `KeyError` or returns the explicitly
L specified default value.
S Example: DefaultToNil: true (default)
L   # bad
L   ENV['X']
L   x = ENV['X']
L 
L   # good
L   ENV.fetch('X', nil)
L   x = ENV.fetch('X', nil)
L 
L   # also good
L   !ENV['X']
L   ENV['X'].some_method # (e.g. `.nil?`)
S Example: DefaultToNil: false
L   # bad
L   ENV['X']
L   x = ENV['X']
L 
L   # good
L   ENV.fetch('X')
L   x = ENV.fetch('X')
L 
L   # also good
L   !ENV['X']
L   ENV['X'].some_method # (e.g. `.nil?`)
C Style/FileEmpty
S Details
L Prefer to use `File.empty?('path/to/file')` when checking if a file is empty.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe, because `File.size`, `File.read`, and `File.binread`
L   raise `ENOENT` exception when there is no file corresponding to the path,
L   while `File.empty?` does not raise an exception.
S Example
L   # bad
L   File.zero?('path/to/file')
L   File.size('path/to/file') == 0
L   File.size('path/to/file') >= 0
L   File.size('path/to/file').zero?
L   File.read('path/to/file').empty?
L   File.binread('path/to/file') == ''
L   FileTest.zero?('path/to/file')
L 
L   # good
L   File.empty?('path/to/file')
L   FileTest.empty?('path/to/file')
C Style/FileNull
S Details
L Use `File::NULL` instead of hardcoding the null device (`/dev/null` on Unix-like
L OSes, `NUL` or `NUL:` on Windows), so that code is platform independent.
L Only looks for full string matches, substrings within a longer string are not
L considered.
L 
L However, only files that use the string `'/dev/null'` are targeted for detection.
L This is because the string `'NUL'` is not limited to the null device.
L This behavior results in false negatives when the `'/dev/null'` string is not used,
L but it is a trade-off to avoid false positives. `NULL:`
L Unlike `'NUL'`, `'NUL:'` is regarded as something like `C:` and is always detected.
L 
L NOTE: Uses inside arrays and hashes are ignored.
S Safety
L   It is possible for a string value to be changed if code is being run
L   on multiple platforms and was previously hardcoded to a specific null device.
L 
L   For example, the following string will change on Windows when changed to
L   `File::NULL`:
L 
L   [source,ruby]
L   ----
L   path = "/dev/null"
L   ----
S Example
L   # bad
L   '/dev/null'
L   'NUL'
L   'NUL:'
L 
L   # good
L   File::NULL
L 
L   # ok - inside an array
L   null_devices = %w[/dev/null nul]
L 
L   # ok - inside a hash
L   { unix: "/dev/null", windows: "nul" }
C Style/FileOpen
S Details
L Checks for `File.open` without a block, which can leak file descriptors.
L 
L When `File.open` is called without a block, the caller is responsible
L for closing the file descriptor. If it is not explicitly closed, it
L will only be closed when the garbage collector runs, which may lead
L to resource exhaustion. Using the block form ensures the file is
L automatically closed when the block exits.
L 
L This cop only registers an offense when the result of `File.open` is
L assigned to a variable or has a method chained on it, as those are the
L clearest indicators that the block form should be used instead. When
L `File.open` is used as a return value or passed as an argument, the
L caller is likely managing the file descriptor intentionally.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because it relies on syntax heuristics and cannot
L   verify whether the file descriptor is safely managed. For example, it
L   still flags intentional one-shot reads (`File.open("f").read`) where
L   the file descriptor is closed by the garbage collector.
S Example
L   # bad
L   f = File.open('file')
L 
L   # bad
L   File.open('file').read
L 
L   # good
L   File.open('file') do |f|
L     f.read
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   File.open('file', &:read)
L 
L   # good - pass an open file object to an API that manages its lifecycle
L   process(io: File.open('file'))
L 
L   # good - return an open file object for the caller to manage
L   def json_key_io
L     File.open('file')
L   end
L 
L   # good - use File.read for one-shot reads
L   File.read('file')
C Style/FileRead
S Details
L Favor `File.(bin)read` convenience methods.
S Example
L   # bad - text mode
L   File.open(filename).read
L   File.open(filename, &:read)
L   File.open(filename) { |f| f.read }
L   File.open(filename) do |f|
L     f.read
L   end
L   File.open(filename, 'r').read
L   File.open(filename, 'r', &:read)
L   File.open(filename, 'r') do |f|
L     f.read
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   File.read(filename)
L 
L   # bad - binary mode
L   File.open(filename, 'rb').read
L   File.open(filename, 'rb', &:read)
L   File.open(filename, 'rb') do |f|
L     f.read
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   File.binread(filename)
C Style/FileTouch
S Details
L Checks for usage of `File.open` in append mode with empty block.
L 
L Such a usage only creates a new file, but it doesn't update
L timestamps for an existing file, which might have been the intention.
L 
L For example, for an existing file `foo.txt`:
L 
L   ruby -e "puts File.mtime('foo.txt')"
L   # 2024-11-26 12:17:23 +0100
L 
L   ruby -e "File.open('foo.txt', 'a') {}"
L 
L   ruby -e "puts File.mtime('foo.txt')"
L   # 2024-11-26 12:17:23 +0100 -> unchanged
L 
L If the intention was to update timestamps, `FileUtils.touch('foo.txt')`
L should be used instead.
S Safety
L   Autocorrection is unsafe for this cop because unlike `File.open`,
L   `FileUtils.touch` updates an existing file's timestamps.
S Example
L   # bad
L   File.open(filename, 'a') {}
L   File.open(filename, 'a+') {}
L 
L   # good
L   FileUtils.touch(filename)
C Style/FileWrite
S Details
L Favor `File.(bin)write` convenience methods.
L 
L NOTE: There are different method signatures between `File.write` (class method)
L and `File#write` (instance method). The following case will be allowed because
L static analysis does not know the contents of the splat argument:
L 
L [source,ruby]
L ----
L File.open(filename, 'w') do |f|
L   f.write(*objects)
L end
L ----
S Example
L   # bad - text mode
L   File.open(filename, 'w').write(content)
L   File.open(filename, 'w') do |f|
L     f.write(content)
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   File.write(filename, content)
L 
L   # bad - binary mode
L   File.open(filename, 'wb').write(content)
L   File.open(filename, 'wb') do |f|
L     f.write(content)
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   File.binwrite(filename, content)
C Style/FloatDivision
S Details
L Checks for division with integers coerced to floats.
L It is recommended to either always use `fdiv` or coerce one side only.
L This cop also provides other options for code consistency.
L 
L For `Regexp.last_match` and nth reference (e.g., `$1`), it assumes that the value
L is a string matched by a regular expression, and allows conversion with `#to_f`.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe, because if the operand variable is a string object
L   then `#to_f` will be removed and an error will occur.
L 
L   [source,ruby]
L   ----
L   a = '1.2'
L   b = '3.4'
L   a.to_f / b.to_f # Both `to_f` calls are required here
L   ----
S Example: EnforcedStyle: single_coerce (default)
L   # bad
L   a.to_f / b.to_f
L 
L   # good
L   a.to_f / b
L   a / b.to_f
S Example: EnforcedStyle: left_coerce
L   # bad
L   a / b.to_f
L   a.to_f / b.to_f
L 
L   # good
L   a.to_f / b
S Example: EnforcedStyle: right_coerce
L   # bad
L   a.to_f / b
L   a.to_f / b.to_f
L 
L   # good
L   a / b.to_f
S Example: EnforcedStyle: fdiv
L   # bad
L   a / b.to_f
L   a.to_f / b
L   a.to_f / b.to_f
L 
L   # good
L   a.fdiv(b)
C Style/For
S Details
L Looks for uses of the `for` keyword or `each` method. The
L preferred alternative is set in the EnforcedStyle configuration
L parameter. An `each` call with a block on a single line is always
L allowed.
L 
L NOTE: `each` is preferred in idiomatic Ruby because `for` leaks
L its loop variable into the surrounding scope.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: each (default)
L   # bad
L   def foo
L     for n in [1, 2, 3] do
L       puts n
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo
L     [1, 2, 3].each do |n|
L       puts n
L     end
L   end
S Example: EnforcedStyle: for
L   # bad
L   def foo
L     [1, 2, 3].each do |n|
L       puts n
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo
L     for n in [1, 2, 3] do
L       puts n
L     end
L   end
S Safety
L   This cop's autocorrection is unsafe because the scope of
L   variables is different between `each` and `for`.
C Style/FormatString
S Details
L Enforces the use of a single string formatting utility.
L Valid options include `Kernel#format`, `Kernel#sprintf`, and `String#%`.
L 
L The detection of `String#%` cannot be implemented in a reliable
L manner for all cases, so only two scenarios are considered -
L if the first argument is a string literal and if the second
L argument is an array literal.
L 
L Autocorrection will be applied when the argument is a literal or uses a known
L built-in conversion method such as `to_d`, `to_f`, `to_h`, `to_i`, `to_r`, `to_s`,
L and `to_sym` on variables, provided that their return value is not an array.
L For example, when using `to_s`,
L `'%s' % [1, 2, 3].to_s` can be autocorrected without any incompatibility:
L 
L [source,ruby]
L ----
L '%s' % [1, 2, 3]        #=> '1'
L format('%s', [1, 2, 3]) #=> '[1, 2, 3]'
L '%s' % [1, 2, 3].to_s   #=> '[1, 2, 3]'
L ----
S Example: EnforcedStyle: format (default)
L   # bad
L   puts sprintf('%10s', 'foo')
L   puts '%10s' % 'foo'
L 
L   # good
L   puts format('%10s', 'foo')
S Example: EnforcedStyle: sprintf
L   # bad
L   puts format('%10s', 'foo')
L   puts '%10s' % 'foo'
L 
L   # good
L   puts sprintf('%10s', 'foo')
S Example: EnforcedStyle: percent
L   # bad
L   puts format('%10s', 'foo')
L   puts sprintf('%10s', 'foo')
L 
L   # good
L   puts '%10s' % 'foo'
C Style/FormatStringToken
S Details
L Use a consistent style for tokens within a format string.
L 
L By default, all strings are evaluated. In some cases, this may be undesirable,
L as they could be used as arguments to a method that does not consider
L them to be tokens, but rather other identifiers or just part of the string.
L 
L `AllowedMethods` or `AllowedPatterns` can be configured with in order to mark specific
L methods as always allowed, thereby avoiding an offense from the cop. By default, there
L are no allowed methods.
L 
L Additionally, the cop can be made conservative by configuring it with
L `Mode: conservative` (default `aggressive`). In this mode, tokens (regardless
L of `EnforcedStyle`) are only considered if used in the format string argument to the
L methods `printf`, `sprintf`, `format` and `%`.
L 
L NOTE: In `aggressive` mode, offenses are registered for all strings containing tokens,
L but autocorrection is only applied when the string appears in a known formatting context
L (`format`, `sprintf`, `printf`, or `%`). This is done in order to prevent false
L autocorrections for strings that are not actually format strings.
L 
L NOTE: Tokens in the `unannotated` style (eg. `%s`) are always treated as if
L configured with `Conservative: true`. This is done in order to prevent false positives,
L because this format is very similar to encoded URLs or Date/Time formatting strings.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: annotated (default)
L   # bad
L   format('%{greeting}', greeting: 'Hello')
L   format('%s', 'Hello')
L 
L   # good
L   format('%<greeting>s', greeting: 'Hello')
S Example: EnforcedStyle: template
L   # bad
L   format('%<greeting>s', greeting: 'Hello')
L   format('%s', 'Hello')
L 
L   # good
L   format('%{greeting}', greeting: 'Hello')
S Example: EnforcedStyle: unannotated
L   # bad
L   format('%<greeting>s', greeting: 'Hello')
L   format('%{greeting}', greeting: 'Hello')
L 
L   # good
L   format('%s', 'Hello')
L 
L It is allowed to contain unannotated token
L if the number of them is less than or equals to
L `MaxUnannotatedPlaceholdersAllowed`.
S Example: MaxUnannotatedPlaceholdersAllowed: 0
L   # bad
L   format('%06d', 10)
L   format('%s %s.', 'Hello', 'world')
L 
L   # good
L   format('%<number>06d', number: 10)
S Example: MaxUnannotatedPlaceholdersAllowed: 1 (default)
L   # bad
L   format('%s %s.', 'Hello', 'world')
L 
L   # good
L   format('%06d', 10)
S Example: AllowedMethods: [] (default)
L   # bad
L   redirect('foo/%{bar_id}')
S Example: AllowedMethods: [redirect]
L   # good
L   redirect('foo/%{bar_id}')
S Example: AllowedPatterns: [] (default)
L   # bad
L   redirect('foo/%{bar_id}')
S Example: AllowedPatterns: ['redirect']
L   # good
L   redirect('foo/%{bar_id}')
S Example: Mode: aggressive (default), EnforcedStyle: annotated
L   # bad
L   "%{greeting}"
L   foo("%{greeting}")
L 
L   # bad
L   format("%{greeting}", greeting: 'Hello')
L   printf("%{greeting}", greeting: 'Hello')
L   sprintf("%{greeting}", greeting: 'Hello')
L   "%{greeting}" % { greeting: 'Hello' }
L 
L   # good
L   format("%<greeting>s", greeting: 'Hello')
L   printf("%<greeting>s", greeting: 'Hello')
L   sprintf("%<greeting>s", greeting: 'Hello')
L   "%<greeting>s" % { greeting: 'Hello' }
S Example: Mode: conservative, EnforcedStyle: annotated
L   # good
L   "%{greeting}"
L   foo("%{greeting}")
L 
L   # bad
L   format("%{greeting}", greeting: 'Hello')
L   printf("%{greeting}", greeting: 'Hello')
L   sprintf("%{greeting}", greeting: 'Hello')
L   "%{greeting}" % { greeting: 'Hello' }
L 
L   # good
L   format("%<greeting>s", greeting: 'Hello')
L   printf("%<greeting>s", greeting: 'Hello')
L   sprintf("%<greeting>s", greeting: 'Hello')
L   "%<greeting>s" % { greeting: 'Hello' }
C Style/FrozenStringLiteralComment
S Details
L Helps you transition from mutable string literals
L to frozen string literals.
L It will add the `# frozen_string_literal: true` magic comment to the top
L of files to enable frozen string literals. Frozen string literals may be
L default in future Ruby. The comment will be added below a shebang and
L encoding comment. The frozen string literal comment is only valid in Ruby 2.3+.
L 
L Note that the cop will accept files where the comment exists but is set
L to `false` instead of `true`.
L 
L To require a blank line after this comment, please see
L `Layout/EmptyLineAfterMagicComment` cop.
S Safety
L  This cop's autocorrection is unsafe since any strings mutations will
L  change from being accepted to raising `FrozenError`, as all strings
L  will become frozen by default, and will need to be manually refactored.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: always (default)
L   # The `always` style will always add the frozen string literal comment
L   # to a file, regardless of the Ruby version or if `freeze` or `<<` are
L   # called on a string literal.
L   # bad
L   module Bar
L     # ...
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   # frozen_string_literal: true
L 
L   module Bar
L     # ...
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   # frozen_string_literal: false
L 
L   module Bar
L     # ...
L   end
S Example: EnforcedStyle: never
L   # The `never` will enforce that the frozen string literal comment does
L   # not exist in a file.
L   # bad
L   # frozen_string_literal: true
L 
L   module Baz
L     # ...
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   module Baz
L     # ...
L   end
S Example: EnforcedStyle: always_true
L   # The `always_true` style enforces that the frozen string literal
L   # comment is set to `true`. This is a stricter option than `always`
L   # and forces projects to use frozen string literals.
L   # bad
L   # frozen_string_literal: false
L 
L   module Baz
L     # ...
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   module Baz
L     # ...
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   # frozen_string_literal: true
L 
L   module Bar
L     # ...
L   end
C Style/GlobalStdStream
S Details
L Enforces the use of `$stdout/$stderr/$stdin` instead of `STDOUT/STDERR/STDIN`.
L `STDOUT/STDERR/STDIN` are constants, and while you can actually
L reassign (possibly to redirect some stream) constants in Ruby, you'll get
L an interpreter warning if you do so.
L 
L Additionally, `$stdout/$stderr/$stdin` can safely be accessed in a Ractor because they
L are ractor-local, while `STDOUT/STDERR/STDIN` will raise `Ractor::IsolationError`.
S Safety
L   Autocorrection is unsafe because `STDOUT` and `$stdout` may point to different
L   objects, for example.
S Example
L   # bad
L   STDOUT.puts('hello')
L 
L   hash = { out: STDOUT, key: value }
L 
L   def m(out = STDOUT)
L     out.puts('hello')
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   $stdout.puts('hello')
L 
L   hash = { out: $stdout, key: value }
L 
L   def m(out = $stdout)
L     out.puts('hello')
L   end
C Style/GlobalVars
S Details
L Looks for uses of global variables. Global variables introduce
L shared mutable state that makes code harder to test, debug,
L and reason about, since any part of the program can read or modify them.
L 
L It does not report offenses for built-in global variables.
L Built-in global variables are allowed by default. Additionally
L users can allow additional variables via the AllowedVariables option.
L 
L Note that backreferences like $1, $2, etc are not global variables.
S Example
L   # bad
L   $foo = 2
L   bar = $foo + 5
L 
L   # good
L   FOO = 2
L   foo = 2
L   $stdin.read
C Style/GuardClause
S Details
L Use a guard clause instead of wrapping the code inside a conditional
L expression
L 
L A condition with an `elsif` or `else` branch is allowed unless
L one of `return`, `break`, `next`, `raise`, or `fail` is used
L in the body of the conditional expression.
L 
L NOTE: Autocorrect works in most cases except with if-else statements
L   that contain logical operators such as `foo || raise('exception')`
S Example
L   # bad
L   def test
L     if something
L       work
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def test
L     return unless something
L 
L     work
L   end
L 
L   # also good
L   def test
L     work if something
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   if something
L     raise 'exception'
L   else
L     ok
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   raise 'exception' if something
L   ok
L 
L   # bad
L   define_method(:test) do
L     if something
L       work
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   define_method(:test) do
L     return unless something
L 
L     work
L   end
L 
L   # also good
L   define_method(:test) do
L     work if something
L   end
S Example: AllowConsecutiveConditionals: false (default)
L   # bad
L   def test
L     if foo?
L       work
L     end
L 
L     if bar?  # <- reports an offense
L       work
L     end
L   end
S Example: AllowConsecutiveConditionals: true
L   # good
L   def test
L     if foo?
L       work
L     end
L 
L     if bar?
L       work
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   def test
L     if foo?
L       work
L     end
L 
L     do_something
L 
L     if bar?  # <- reports an offense
L       work
L     end
L   end
C Style/HashAsLastArrayItem
S Details
L Checks for presence or absence of braces around hash literal as a last
L array item depending on configuration.
L 
L NOTE: This cop will ignore arrays where multiple items are all hashes,
L regardless of `EnforcedStyle`.
L 
L [source,ruby]
L ----
L [{ one: 1 }, { two: 2 }]
L ----
S Example: EnforcedStyle: braces (default)
L   # bad
L   [1, 2, one: 1, two: 2]
L 
L   # good
L   [1, 2, { one: 1, two: 2 }]
L 
L   # bad
L   [one: 1, two: 2]
L 
L   # good
L   [{ one: 1, two: 2 }]
S Example: EnforcedStyle: no_braces
L   # bad
L   [1, 2, { one: 1, two: 2 }]
L 
L   # good
L   [1, 2, one: 1, two: 2]
L 
L   # bad
L   [{ one: 1, two: 2 }]
L 
L   # good
L   [one: 1, two: 2]
C Style/HashConversion
S Details
L Checks the usage of pre-2.1 `Hash[args]` method of converting enumerables and
L sequences of values to hashes.
L 
L Correction code from splat argument (`Hash[*ary]`) is not simply determined. For example,
L `Hash[*ary]` can be replaced with `ary.each_slice(2).to_h` but it will be complicated.
L So, `AllowSplatArgument` option is true by default to allow splat argument for simple code.
S Safety
L   This cop's autocorrection is unsafe because `ArgumentError` occurs
L   if the number of elements is odd:
L 
L   [source,ruby]
L   ----
L   Hash[[[1, 2], [3]]] #=> {1=>2, 3=>nil}
L   [[1, 2], [5]].to_h  #=> wrong array length at 1 (expected 2, was 1) (ArgumentError)
L   ----
S Example
L   # bad
L   Hash[ary]
L 
L   # good
L   ary.to_h
L 
L   # bad
L   Hash[key1, value1, key2, value2]
L 
L   # good
L   {key1 => value1, key2 => value2}
S Example: AllowSplatArgument: true (default)
L   # good
L   Hash[*ary]
S Example: AllowSplatArgument: false
L   # bad
L   Hash[*ary]
C Style/HashEachMethods
S Details
L Checks for uses of `each_key` and `each_value` `Hash` methods.
L 
L NOTE: If you have an array of two-element arrays, you can put
L   parentheses around the block arguments to indicate that you're not
L   working with a hash, and suppress RuboCop offenses.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because it cannot be guaranteed that the receiver
L   is a `Hash`. The `AllowedReceivers` configuration can mitigate,
L   but not fully resolve, this safety issue.
S Example
L   # bad
L   hash.keys.each { |k| p k }
L   hash.each { |k, unused_value| p k }
L 
L   # good
L   hash.each_key { |k| p k }
L 
L   # bad
L   hash.values.each { |v| p v }
L   hash.each { |unused_key, v| p v }
L 
L   # good
L   hash.each_value { |v| p v }
S Example: AllowedReceivers: ['execute']
L   # good
L   execute(sql).keys.each { |v| p v }
L   execute(sql).values.each { |v| p v }
C Style/HashExcept
S Details
L Checks for usages of `Hash#reject`, `Hash#select`, and `Hash#filter` methods
L that can be replaced with `Hash#except` method.
L 
L This cop should only be enabled on Ruby version 3.0 or higher.
L (`Hash#except` was added in Ruby 3.0.)
L 
L For safe detection, it is limited to commonly used string and symbol comparisons
L when using `==` or `!=`.
L 
L This cop doesn't check for `Hash#delete_if` and `Hash#keep_if` because they
L modify the receiver.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because it cannot be guaranteed that the receiver
L   is a `Hash` or responds to the replacement method.
S Example
L   # bad
L   {foo: 1, bar: 2, baz: 3}.reject {|k, v| k == :bar }
L   {foo: 1, bar: 2, baz: 3}.select {|k, v| k != :bar }
L   {foo: 1, bar: 2, baz: 3}.filter {|k, v| k != :bar }
L   {foo: 1, bar: 2, baz: 3}.reject {|k, v| k.eql?(:bar) }
L 
L   # bad
L   {foo: 1, bar: 2, baz: 3}.reject {|k, v| %i[bar].include?(k) }
L   {foo: 1, bar: 2, baz: 3}.select {|k, v| !%i[bar].include?(k) }
L   {foo: 1, bar: 2, baz: 3}.filter {|k, v| !%i[bar].include?(k) }
L 
L   # good
L   {foo: 1, bar: 2, baz: 3}.except(:bar)
S Example: AllCops:ActiveSupportExtensionsEnabled: false (default)
L   # good
L   {foo: 1, bar: 2, baz: 3}.reject {|k, v| !%i[bar].exclude?(k) }
L   {foo: 1, bar: 2, baz: 3}.select {|k, v| %i[bar].exclude?(k) }
L 
L   # good
L   {foo: 1, bar: 2, baz: 3}.reject {|k, v| k.in?(%i[bar]) }
L   {foo: 1, bar: 2, baz: 3}.select {|k, v| !k.in?(%i[bar]) }
S Example: AllCops:ActiveSupportExtensionsEnabled: true
L   # bad
L   {foo: 1, bar: 2, baz: 3}.reject {|k, v| !%i[bar].exclude?(k) }
L   {foo: 1, bar: 2, baz: 3}.select {|k, v| %i[bar].exclude?(k) }
L 
L   # bad
L   {foo: 1, bar: 2, baz: 3}.reject {|k, v| k.in?(%i[bar]) }
L   {foo: 1, bar: 2, baz: 3}.select {|k, v| !k.in?(%i[bar]) }
L 
L   # good
L   {foo: 1, bar: 2, baz: 3}.except(:bar)
C Style/HashFetchChain
S Details
L Use `Hash#dig` instead of chaining potentially null `fetch` calls.
L 
L When `fetch(identifier, nil)` calls are chained on a hash, the expectation
L is that each step in the chain returns either `nil` or another hash,
L and in both cases, these can be simplified with a single call to `dig` with
L multiple arguments.
L 
L If the 2nd parameter is `{}` or `Hash.new`, an offense will also be registered,
L as long as the final call in the chain is a nil value. If a non-nil value is given,
L the chain will not be registered as an offense, as the default value cannot be safely
L given with `dig`.
L 
L NOTE: See `Style/DigChain` for replacing chains of `dig` calls with
L a single method call.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because it cannot be guaranteed that the receiver
L   is a `Hash` or that `fetch` or `dig` have the expected standard implementation.
S Example
L   # bad
L   hash.fetch('foo', nil)&.fetch('bar', nil)
L 
L   # bad
L   # earlier members of the chain can return `{}` as long as the final `fetch`
L   # has `nil` as a default value
L   hash.fetch('foo', {}).fetch('bar', nil)
L 
L   # good
L   hash.dig('foo', 'bar')
L 
L   # ok - not handled by the cop since the final `fetch` value is non-nil
L   hash.fetch('foo', {}).fetch('bar', {})
C Style/HashLikeCase
S Details
L Checks for places where `case-when` represents a simple 1:1
L mapping and can be replaced with a hash lookup.
S Example: MinBranchesCount: 3 (default)
L   # bad
L   case country
L   when 'europe'
L     'http://eu.example.com'
L   when 'america'
L     'http://us.example.com'
L   when 'australia'
L     'http://au.example.com'
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   SITES = {
L     'europe'    => 'http://eu.example.com',
L     'america'   => 'http://us.example.com',
L     'australia' => 'http://au.example.com'
L   }
L   SITES[country]
S Example: MinBranchesCount: 4
L   # good
L   case country
L   when 'europe'
L     'http://eu.example.com'
L   when 'america'
L     'http://us.example.com'
L   when 'australia'
L     'http://au.example.com'
L   end
C Style/HashLookupMethod
S Details
L Enforces the use of either `Hash#[]` or `Hash#fetch` for hash lookup.
L 
L This cop can be configured to prefer either bracket-style (`[]`)
L or fetch-style lookup. It is disabled by default.
L 
L When enforcing `fetch` style, only single-argument bracket access is flagged.
L When enforcing `brackets` style, only `fetch` calls with a single key
L argument are flagged (not those with default values or blocks).
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because `Hash#[]` and `Hash#fetch` have different
L   semantics. `Hash#[]` returns `nil` for missing keys, while `Hash#fetch`
L   raises a `KeyError`. Replacing one with the other can change program
L   behavior in cases where the key is missing.
L 
L   Additionally, it cannot be guaranteed that the receiver is a `Hash`
L   or responds to the replacement method.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: brackets (default)
L   # bad
L   hash.fetch(key)
L 
L   # good
L   hash[key]
L 
L   # good - fetch with default value is allowed
L   hash.fetch(key, default)
L 
L   # good - fetch with block is allowed
L   hash.fetch(key) { default }
L 
L   # good - `ENV.fetch` is left to `Style/FetchEnvVar` when it is enabled
L   ENV.fetch(key)
S Example: EnforcedStyle: fetch
L   # bad
L   hash[key]
L 
L   # good
L   hash.fetch(key)
S Example: AllowedReceivers: ['Rails.cache']
L   # good
L   Rails.cache.fetch(name, options) { block }
C Style/HashSlice
S Details
L Checks for usages of `Hash#reject`, `Hash#select`, and `Hash#filter` methods
L that can be replaced with `Hash#slice` method.
L 
L This cop should only be enabled on Ruby version 2.5 or higher.
L (`Hash#slice` was added in Ruby 2.5.)
L 
L For safe detection, it is limited to commonly used string and symbol comparisons
L when using `==` or `!=`.
L 
L This cop doesn't check for `Hash#delete_if` and `Hash#keep_if` because they
L modify the receiver.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because it cannot be guaranteed that the receiver
L   is a `Hash` or responds to the replacement method.
L 
L   Additionally, the replacement may change the order of the resulting
L   hash: `Hash#slice` returns entries in the order the keys are given,
L   whereas `select`, `filter`, and `reject` preserve the entry order of
L   the receiver.
L 
L   For example:
L 
L   [source,ruby]
L   ----
L   hash = {foo: 1, bar: 2, baz: 3}
L   keys = %i[baz foo]
L 
L   hash.select { |k, _v| keys.include?(k) } # => {foo: 1, baz: 3}
L   hash.slice(*keys)                        # => {baz: 3, foo: 1}
L   ----
S Example
L   # bad
L   {foo: 1, bar: 2, baz: 3}.select {|k, v| k == :bar }
L   {foo: 1, bar: 2, baz: 3}.reject {|k, v| k != :bar }
L   {foo: 1, bar: 2, baz: 3}.filter {|k, v| k == :bar }
L   {foo: 1, bar: 2, baz: 3}.select {|k, v| k.eql?(:bar) }
L 
L   # bad
L   {foo: 1, bar: 2, baz: 3}.select {|k, v| %i[bar].include?(k) }
L   {foo: 1, bar: 2, baz: 3}.reject {|k, v| !%i[bar].include?(k) }
L   {foo: 1, bar: 2, baz: 3}.filter {|k, v| %i[bar].include?(k) }
L 
L   # good
L   {foo: 1, bar: 2, baz: 3}.slice(:bar)
S Example: AllCops:ActiveSupportExtensionsEnabled: false (default)
L   # good
L   {foo: 1, bar: 2, baz: 3}.select {|k, v| !%i[bar].exclude?(k) }
L   {foo: 1, bar: 2, baz: 3}.reject {|k, v| %i[bar].exclude?(k) }
L 
L   # good
L   {foo: 1, bar: 2, baz: 3}.select {|k, v| k.in?(%i[bar]) }
L   {foo: 1, bar: 2, baz: 3}.reject {|k, v| !k.in?(%i[bar]) }
S Example: AllCops:ActiveSupportExtensionsEnabled: true
L   # bad
L   {foo: 1, bar: 2, baz: 3}.select {|k, v| !%i[bar].exclude?(k) }
L   {foo: 1, bar: 2, baz: 3}.reject {|k, v| %i[bar].exclude?(k) }
L 
L   # bad
L   {foo: 1, bar: 2, baz: 3}.select {|k, v| k.in?(%i[bar]) }
L   {foo: 1, bar: 2, baz: 3}.reject {|k, v| !k.in?(%i[bar]) }
L 
L   # good
L   {foo: 1, bar: 2, baz: 3}.slice(:bar)
C Style/HashSyntax
S Details
L Checks hash literal syntax.
L 
L It can enforce either the use of the class hash rocket syntax or
L the use of the newer Ruby 1.9 syntax (when applicable).
L 
L A separate offense is registered for each problematic pair.
L 
L The supported styles are:
L 
L * ruby19 - forces use of the 1.9 syntax (e.g. `{a: 1}`) when hashes have
L all symbols for keys
L * hash_rockets - forces use of hash rockets for all hashes
L * no_mixed_keys - simply checks for hashes with mixed syntaxes
L * ruby19_no_mixed_keys - forces use of ruby 1.9 syntax and forbids mixed
L syntax hashes
L 
L This cop has `EnforcedShorthandSyntax` option.
L It can enforce either the use of the explicit hash value syntax or
L the use of Ruby 3.1's hash value shorthand syntax.
L 
L The supported styles are:
L 
L * always - forces use of the 3.1 syntax (e.g. {foo:})
L * never - forces use of explicit hash literal value
L * either - accepts both shorthand and explicit use of hash literal value
L * consistent - forces use of the 3.1 syntax only if all values can be omitted in the hash
L * either_consistent - accepts both shorthand and explicit use of hash literal value,
L                       but they must be consistent
S Example: EnforcedStyle: ruby19 (default)
L   # bad
L   {:a => 2}
L   {b: 1, :c => 2}
L 
L   # good
L   {a: 2, b: 1}
L   {:c => 2, 'd' => 2} # acceptable since 'd' isn't a symbol
L   {d: 1, 'e' => 2} # technically not forbidden
S Example: EnforcedStyle: hash_rockets
L   # bad
L   {a: 1, b: 2}
L   {c: 1, 'd' => 5}
L 
L   # good
L   {:a => 1, :b => 2}
S Example: EnforcedStyle: no_mixed_keys
L   # bad
L   {:a => 1, b: 2}
L   {c: 1, 'd' => 2}
L 
L   # good
L   {:a => 1, :b => 2}
L   {c: 1, d: 2}
S Example: EnforcedStyle: ruby19_no_mixed_keys
L   # bad
L   {:a => 1, :b => 2}
L   {c: 2, 'd' => 3} # should just use hash rockets
L 
L   # good
L   {a: 1, b: 2}
L   {:c => 3, 'd' => 4}
S Example: EnforcedShorthandSyntax: always
L   # bad
L   {foo: foo, bar: bar}
L 
L   # good
L   {foo:, bar:}
L 
L   # good - allowed to mix syntaxes
L   {foo:, bar: baz}
S Example: EnforcedShorthandSyntax: never
L   # bad
L   {foo:, bar:}
L 
L   # good
L   {foo: foo, bar: bar}
S Example: EnforcedShorthandSyntax: either (default)
L   # good
L   {foo: foo, bar: bar}
L 
L   # good
L   {foo: foo, bar:}
L 
L   # good
L   {foo:, bar:}
S Example: EnforcedShorthandSyntax: consistent
L   # bad - `foo` and `bar` values can be omitted
L   {foo: foo, bar: bar}
L 
L   # bad - `bar` value can be omitted
L   {foo:, bar: bar}
L 
L   # bad - mixed syntaxes
L   {foo:, bar: baz}
L 
L   # good
L   {foo:, bar:}
L 
L   # good - can't omit `baz`
L   {foo: foo, bar: baz}
S Example: EnforcedShorthandSyntax: either_consistent
L   # good - `foo` and `bar` values can be omitted, but they are consistent, so it's accepted
L   {foo: foo, bar: bar}
L 
L   # bad - `bar` value can be omitted
L   {foo:, bar: bar}
L 
L   # bad - mixed syntaxes
L   {foo:, bar: baz}
L 
L   # good
L   {foo:, bar:}
L 
L   # good - can't omit `baz`
L   {foo: foo, bar: baz}
C Style/HashTransformKeys
S Details
L Looks for uses of `+_.each_with_object({}) {...}+`,
L `+_.map {...}.to_h+`, and `+Hash[_.map {...}]+` that are actually just
L transforming the keys of a hash, and tries to use a simpler & faster
L call to `transform_keys` instead.
L It should only be enabled on Ruby version 2.5 or newer.
L (`transform_keys` was added in Ruby 2.5.)
S Safety
L   This cop identifies the receiver as a hash by checking for literal hash
L   syntax and common methods that are known to return hashes (e.g. `to_h`,
L   `merge`, `invert`, `group_by`, etc.). However, it is unsafe because it
L   is possible for a custom class to define one of these methods and return
L   something other than a hash.
S Example
L   # bad
L   {a: 1, b: 2}.each_with_object({}) { |(k, v), h| h[foo(k)] = v }
L   Hash[{a: 1, b: 2}.collect { |k, v| [foo(k), v] }]
L   {a: 1, b: 2}.map { |k, v| [k.to_s, v] }.to_h
L   {a: 1, b: 2}.to_h { |k, v| [k.to_s, v] }
L   foo.to_h.each_with_object({}) { |(k, v), h| h[k.to_sym] = v }
L   foo.merge(bar).map { |k, v| [k.to_s, v] }.to_h
L 
L   # good
L   {a: 1, b: 2}.transform_keys { |k| foo(k) }
L   {a: 1, b: 2}.transform_keys { |k| k.to_s }
L   foo.to_h.transform_keys { |k| k.to_sym }
L   foo.merge(bar).transform_keys { |k| k.to_s }
L 
L   # Won't register an offense - receiver is not known to be a hash
L   foo.bar.each_with_object({}) { |(k, v), h| h[k.to_s] = v }
L   baz.map { |k, v| [k.to_s, v] }.to_h
C Style/HashTransformValues
S Details
L Looks for uses of `+_.each_with_object({}) {...}+`,
L `+_.map {...}.to_h+`, and `+Hash[_.map {...}]+` that are actually just
L transforming the values of a hash, and tries to use a simpler & faster
L call to `transform_values` instead.
S Safety
L   This cop identifies the receiver as a hash by checking for literal hash
L   syntax and common methods that are known to return hashes (e.g. `to_h`,
L   `merge`, `invert`, `group_by`, etc.). However, it is unsafe because it
L   is possible for a custom class to define one of these methods and return
L   something other than a hash.
S Example
L   # bad
L   {a: 1, b: 2}.each_with_object({}) { |(k, v), h| h[k] = foo(v) }
L   Hash[{a: 1, b: 2}.collect { |k, v| [k, foo(v)] }]
L   {a: 1, b: 2}.map { |k, v| [k, v * v] }.to_h
L   {a: 1, b: 2}.to_h { |k, v| [k, v * v] }
L   foo.to_h.each_with_object({}) { |(k, v), h| h[k] = foo(v) }
L   foo.merge(bar).map { |k, v| [k, v.to_s] }.to_h
L 
L   # good
L   {a: 1, b: 2}.transform_values { |v| foo(v) }
L   {a: 1, b: 2}.transform_values { |v| v * v }
L   foo.to_h.transform_values { |v| foo(v) }
L   foo.merge(bar).transform_values { |v| v.to_s }
L 
L   # Won't register an offense - receiver is not known to be a hash
L   foo.bar.each_with_object({}) { |(k, v), h| h[k] = v.to_s }
L   baz.map { |k, v| [k, v.to_s] }.to_h
C Style/IdenticalConditionalBranches
S Details
L Checks for identical expressions at the beginning or end of
L each branch of a conditional expression. Such expressions should normally
L be placed outside the conditional expression - before or after it.
L 
L NOTE: The cop is poorly named and some people might think that it actually
L checks for duplicated conditional branches. The name will probably be changed
L in a future major RuboCop release.
S Safety
L   Autocorrection is unsafe because changing the order of method invocations
L   may change the behavior of the code. For example:
L 
L   [source,ruby]
L   ----
L   if method_that_modifies_global_state # 1
L     method_that_relies_on_global_state # 2
L     foo                                # 3
L   else
L     method_that_relies_on_global_state # 2
L     bar                                # 3
L   end
L   ----
L 
L   In this example, `method_that_relies_on_global_state` will be moved before
L   `method_that_modifies_global_state`, which changes the behavior of the program.
S Example
L   # bad
L   if condition
L     do_x
L     do_z
L   else
L     do_y
L     do_z
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   if condition
L     do_x
L   else
L     do_y
L   end
L   do_z
L 
L   # bad
L   if condition
L     do_z
L     do_x
L   else
L     do_z
L     do_y
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   do_z
L   if condition
L     do_x
L   else
L     do_y
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   case foo
L   when 1
L     do_x
L   when 2
L     do_x
L   else
L     do_x
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   case foo
L   when 1
L     do_x
L     do_y
L   when 2
L     # nothing
L   else
L     do_x
L     do_z
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   case foo
L   in 1
L     do_x
L   in 2
L     do_x
L   else
L     do_x
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   case foo
L   in 1
L     do_x
L     do_y
L   in 2
L     # nothing
L   else
L     do_x
L     do_z
L   end
C Style/IfInsideElse
S Details
L If the `else` branch of a conditional consists solely of an `if` node,
L it can be combined with the `else` to become an `elsif`.
L This helps to keep the nesting level from getting too deep.
S Example
L   # bad
L   if condition_a
L     action_a
L   else
L     if condition_b
L       action_b
L     else
L       action_c
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   if condition_a
L     action_a
L   elsif condition_b
L     action_b
L   else
L     action_c
L   end
S Example: AllowIfModifier: false (default)
L   # bad
L   if condition_a
L     action_a
L   else
L     action_b if condition_b
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   if condition_a
L     action_a
L   elsif condition_b
L     action_b
L   end
S Example: AllowIfModifier: true
L   # good
L   if condition_a
L     action_a
L   else
L     action_b if condition_b
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   if condition_a
L     action_a
L   elsif condition_b
L     action_b
L   end
C Style/IfUnlessModifier
S Details
L Checks for `if` and `unless` statements that would fit on one line if
L written as modifier `if`/`unless`. The cop also checks for modifier
L `if`/`unless` lines that exceed the maximum line length.
L 
L The maximum line length is configured in the `Layout/LineLength`
L cop. The tab size is configured in the `IndentationWidth` of the
L `Layout/IndentationStyle` cop.
L 
L One-line pattern matching is always allowed. To ensure that there are few cases
L where the match variable is not used, and to prevent oversights. The variable `x`
L becomes undefined and raises `NameError` when the following example is changed to
L the modifier form:
L 
L [source,ruby]
L ----
L if [42] in [x]
L   x # `x` is undefined when using modifier form.
L end
L ----
L 
L The code `def method_name = body if condition` is considered a bad case by
L `Style/AmbiguousEndlessMethodDefinition` cop. So, to respect the user's intention to use
L an endless method definition in the `if` body, the following code is allowed:
L 
L [source,ruby]
L ----
L if condition
L   def method_name = body
L end
L ----
L 
L NOTE: It is allowed when `defined?` argument has an undefined value,
L because using the modifier form causes the following incompatibility:
L 
L [source,ruby]
L ----
L unless defined?(undefined_foo)
L   undefined_foo = 'default_value'
L end
L undefined_foo # => 'default_value'
L 
L undefined_bar = 'default_value' unless defined?(undefined_bar)
L undefined_bar # => nil
L ----
S Example
L   # bad
L   if condition
L     do_stuff(bar)
L   end
L 
L   unless qux.empty?
L     Foo.do_something
L   end
L 
L   do_something_with_a_long_name(arg) if long_condition_that_prevents_code_fit_on_single_line
L 
L   # good
L   do_stuff(bar) if condition
L   Foo.do_something unless qux.empty?
L 
L   if long_condition_that_prevents_code_fit_on_single_line
L     do_something_with_a_long_name(arg)
L   end
L 
L   if short_condition # a long comment that makes it too long if it were just a single line
L     do_something
L   end
C Style/IfUnlessModifierOfIfUnless
S Details
L Checks for if and unless statements used as modifiers of other if or
L unless statements.
S Example
L   # bad
L   tired? ? 'stop' : 'go faster' if running?
L 
L   # bad
L   if tired?
L     "please stop"
L   else
L     "keep going"
L   end if running?
L 
L   # good
L   if running?
L     tired? ? 'stop' : 'go faster'
L   end
C Style/IfWithBooleanLiteralBranches
S Details
L Checks for redundant `if` with boolean literal branches.
L It checks only conditions to return boolean value (`true` or `false`) for safe detection.
L The conditions to be checked are comparison methods, predicate methods, and
L double negation (!!).
L `nonzero?` method is allowed by default.
L These are customizable with `AllowedMethods` option.
L 
L This cop targets only ``if``s with a single `elsif` or `else` branch. The following
L code will be allowed, because it has two `elsif` branches:
L 
L [source,ruby]
L ----
L if foo
L   true
L elsif bar > baz
L   true
L elsif qux > quux # Single `elsif` is warned, but two or more `elsif`s are not.
L   true
L else
L   false
L end
L ----
S Safety
L   Autocorrection is unsafe because there is no guarantee that all predicate methods
L   will return a boolean value. Those methods can be allowed with `AllowedMethods` config.
S Example
L   # bad
L   if foo == bar
L     true
L   else
L     false
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   foo == bar ? true : false
L 
L   # good
L   foo == bar
L 
L   # bad
L   if foo.do_something?
L     true
L   else
L     false
L   end
L 
L   # good (but potentially an unsafe correction)
L   foo.do_something?
S Example: AllowedMethods: ['infinite?', 'nonzero?'] (default)
L   # good
L   num.infinite? ? true : false
L 
L   # good
L   num.nonzero? ? true : false
C Style/IfWithSemicolon
S Details
L Checks for uses of semicolon in if statements.
S Example
L   # bad
L   result = if some_condition; something else another_thing end
L 
L   # good
L   result = some_condition ? something : another_thing
C Style/ImplicitRuntimeError
S Details
L Checks for `raise` or `fail` statements which do not specify an
L explicit exception class. (This raises a `RuntimeError`. Some projects
L might prefer to use exception classes which more precisely identify the
L nature of the error.)
S Example
L   # bad
L   raise 'Error message here'
L 
L   # good
L   raise ArgumentError, 'Error message here'
C Style/InPatternThen
S Details
L Checks for `in;` uses in `case` expressions.
S Example
L   # bad
L   case expression
L   in pattern_a; foo
L   in pattern_b; bar
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   case expression
L   in pattern_a then foo
L   in pattern_b then bar
L   end
C Style/InfiniteLoop
S Details
L Use `Kernel#loop` for infinite loops.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe as the rule should not necessarily apply if the loop
L   body might raise a `StopIteration` exception; contrary to other infinite
L   loops, `Kernel#loop` silently rescues that and returns `nil`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   while true
L     work
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   loop do
L     work
L   end
C Style/InlineComment
S Details
L Checks for trailing inline comments. Inline comments can
L make lines harder to read, especially when they are long.
L Placing comments on their own line above the code they
L describe is often clearer.
S Example
L   # good
L   foo.each do |f|
L     # Standalone comment
L     f.bar
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   foo.each do |f|
L     f.bar # Trailing inline comment
L   end
C Style/InverseMethods
S Details
L Checks for usages of not (`not` or `!`) called on a method
L when an inverse of that method can be used instead.
L 
L Methods that can be inverted by a not (`not` or `!`) should be defined
L in `InverseMethods`.
L 
L Methods that are inverted by inverting the return
L of the block that is passed to the method should be defined in
L `InverseBlocks`.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because it cannot be guaranteed that the method
L   and its inverse method are both defined on receiver, and also are
L   actually inverse of each other.
S Example
L   # bad
L   !foo.none?
L   !foo.any? { |f| f.even? }
L   !foo.blank?
L   !(foo == bar)
L   foo.select { |f| !f.even? }
L   foo.reject { |f| f != 7 }
L 
L   # good
L   foo.none?
L   foo.blank?
L   foo.any? { |f| f.even? }
L   foo != bar
L   foo == bar
L   !!('foo' =~ /^\w+$/)
L   !(foo.class < Numeric) # Checking class hierarchy is allowed
L   # Blocks with guard clauses are ignored:
L   foo.select do |f|
L     next if f.zero?
L     f != 1
L   end
C Style/InvertibleUnlessCondition
S Details
L Checks for usages of `unless` which can be replaced by `if` with inverted condition.
L Code without `unless` is easier to read, but that is subjective, so this cop
L is disabled by default.
L 
L Methods that can be inverted should be defined in `InverseMethods`. Note that
L the relationship of inverse methods needs to be defined in both directions.
L For example,
L 
L [source,yaml]
L ----
L InverseMethods:
L   :!=: :==
L   :even?: :odd?
L   :odd?: :even?
L ----
L 
L will suggest both `even?` and `odd?` to be inverted, but only `!=` (and not `==`).
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because it cannot be guaranteed that the method
L   and its inverse method are both defined on receiver, and also are
L   actually inverse of each other.
S Example
L   # bad (simple condition)
L   foo unless !bar
L   foo unless x != y
L   foo unless x >= 10
L   foo unless x.even?
L   foo unless odd?
L 
L   # good
L   foo if bar
L   foo if x == y
L   foo if x < 10
L   foo if x.odd?
L   foo if even?
L 
L   # bad (complex condition)
L   foo unless x != y || x.even?
L 
L   # good
L   foo if x == y && x.odd?
L 
L   # good (if)
L   foo if !condition
C Style/IpAddresses
S Details
L Checks for hardcoded IP addresses, which can make code
L brittle. IP addresses are likely to need to be changed when code
L is deployed to a different server or environment, which may break
L a deployment if forgotten. Prefer setting IP addresses in ENV or
L other configuration.
S Example
L   # bad
L   ip_address = '127.59.241.29'
L 
L   # good
L   ip_address = ENV['DEPLOYMENT_IP_ADDRESS']
C Style/ItAssignment
S Details
L Checks for local variables and method parameters named `it`,
L where `it` can refer to the first anonymous parameter as of Ruby 3.4.
L Use a meaningful variable name instead.
L 
L NOTE: Although Ruby allows reassigning `it` in these cases, it could
L cause confusion if `it` is used as a block parameter elsewhere.
S Example
L   # bad
L   it = 5
L 
L   # good
L   var = 5
L 
L   # bad
L   def foo(it)
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo(arg)
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   def foo(it = 5)
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo(arg = 5)
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   def foo(*it)
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo(*args)
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   def foo(it:)
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo(arg:)
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   def foo(it: 5)
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo(arg: 5)
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   def foo(**it)
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo(**kwargs)
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   def foo(&it)
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo(&block)
L   end
C Style/ItBlockParameter
S Details
L Checks for blocks with one argument where `it` block parameter can be used.
L 
L It provides four `EnforcedStyle` options:
L 
L 1. `allow_single_line` (default) ... Always uses the `it` block parameter in a single line.
L 2. `only_numbered_parameters` ... Detects only numbered block parameters.
L 3. `always` ... Always uses the `it` block parameter.
L 4. `disallow` ... Disallows the `it` block parameter.
L 
L A single numbered parameter is detected when `allow_single_line`,
L `only_numbered_parameters`, or `always`.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: allow_single_line (default)
L   # bad
L   block do
L     do_something(it)
L   end
L   block { do_something(_1) }
L 
L   # good
L   block { do_something(it) }
L   block { |named_param| do_something(named_param) }
S Example: EnforcedStyle: only_numbered_parameters
L   # bad
L   block { do_something(_1) }
L 
L   # good
L   block { do_something(it) }
L   block { |named_param| do_something(named_param) }
S Example: EnforcedStyle: always
L   # bad
L   block { do_something(_1) }
L   block { |named_param| do_something(named_param) }
L 
L   # good
L   block { do_something(it) }
S Example: EnforcedStyle: disallow
L   # bad
L   block { do_something(it) }
L 
L   # good
L   block { do_something(_1) }
L   block { |named_param| do_something(named_param) }
C Style/KeywordArgumentsMerging
S Details
L When passing an existing hash as keyword arguments, provide additional arguments
L directly rather than using `merge`.
L 
L Providing arguments directly is more performant than using `merge`, and
L also leads to shorter and simpler code.
S Example
L   # bad
L   some_method(**opts.merge(foo: true))
L   some_method(**opts.merge(other_opts))
L 
L   # good
L   some_method(**opts, foo: true)
L   some_method(**opts, **other_opts)
C Style/KeywordParametersOrder
S Details
L Enforces that optional keyword parameters are placed at the
L end of the parameters list.
L 
L This improves readability, because when looking through the source,
L it is expected to find required parameters at the beginning of parameters list
L and optional parameters at the end.
S Example
L   # bad
L   def some_method(first: false, second:, third: 10)
L     # body omitted
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def some_method(second:, first: false, third: 10)
L     # body omitted
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   do_something do |first: false, second:, third: 10|
L     # body omitted
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   do_something do |second:, first: false, third: 10|
L     # body omitted
L   end
C Style/Lambda
S Details
L (by default) checks for uses of the lambda literal syntax for
L single line lambdas, and the method call syntax for multiline lambdas.
L It is configurable to enforce one of the styles for both single line
L and multiline lambdas as well.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: line_count_dependent (default)
L   # bad
L   f = lambda { |x| x }
L   f = ->(x) do
L         x
L       end
L 
L   # good
L   f = ->(x) { x }
L   f = lambda do |x|
L         x
L       end
S Example: EnforcedStyle: lambda
L   # bad
L   f = ->(x) { x }
L   f = ->(x) do
L         x
L       end
L 
L   # good
L   f = lambda { |x| x }
L   f = lambda do |x|
L         x
L       end
S Example: EnforcedStyle: literal
L   # bad
L   f = lambda { |x| x }
L   f = lambda do |x|
L         x
L       end
L 
L   # good
L   f = ->(x) { x }
L   f = ->(x) do
L         x
L       end
C Style/LambdaCall
S Details
L Checks for use of the lambda.(args) syntax.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: call (default)
L   # bad
L   lambda.(x, y)
L 
L   # good
L   lambda.call(x, y)
S Example: EnforcedStyle: braces
L   # bad
L   lambda.call(x, y)
L 
L   # good
L   lambda.(x, y)
C Style/LineEndConcatenation
S Details
L Checks for string literal concatenation at
L the end of a line.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because it cannot be guaranteed that the
L   receiver is a string, in which case replacing `<<` with `\`
L   would result in a syntax error.
L 
L   For example, this would be a false positive:
L   [source,ruby]
L   ----
L   array << 'foo' <<
L            'bar' <<
L            'baz'
L   ----
S Example
L   # bad
L   some_str = 'ala' +
L              'bala'
L 
L   some_str = 'ala' <<
L              'bala'
L 
L   # good
L   some_str = 'ala' \
L              'bala'
C Style/MagicCommentFormat
S Details
L Ensures magic comments are written consistently throughout your code base.
L Looks for discrepancies in separators (`-` vs `_`) and capitalization for
L both magic comment directives and values.
L 
L Required capitalization can be set with the `DirectiveCapitalization` and
L `ValueCapitalization` configuration keys.
L 
L NOTE: If one of these configurations is set to nil, any capitalization is allowed.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: snake_case (default)
L   # The `snake_case` style will enforce that the frozen string literal
L   # comment is written in snake case. (Words separated by underscores)
L   # bad
L   # frozen-string-literal: true
L 
L   module Bar
L     # ...
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   # frozen_string_literal: false
L 
L   module Bar
L     # ...
L   end
S Example: EnforcedStyle: kebab_case
L   # The `kebab_case` style will enforce that the frozen string literal
L   # comment is written in kebab case. (Words separated by hyphens)
L   # bad
L   # frozen_string_literal: true
L 
L   module Baz
L     # ...
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   # frozen-string-literal: true
L 
L   module Baz
L     # ...
L   end
S Example: DirectiveCapitalization: lowercase (default)
L   # bad
L   # FROZEN-STRING-LITERAL: true
L 
L   # good
L   # frozen-string-literal: true
S Example: DirectiveCapitalization: uppercase
L   # bad
L   # frozen-string-literal: true
L 
L   # good
L   # FROZEN-STRING-LITERAL: true
S Example: DirectiveCapitalization: nil
L   # any capitalization is accepted
L 
L   # good
L   # frozen-string-literal: true
L 
L   # good
L   # FROZEN-STRING-LITERAL: true
S Example: ValueCapitalization: nil (default)
L   # any capitalization is accepted
L 
L   # good
L   # frozen-string-literal: true
L 
L   # good
L   # frozen-string-literal: TRUE
S Example: ValueCapitalization: lowercase
L   # when a value is not given, any capitalization is accepted
L 
L   # bad
L   # frozen-string-literal: TRUE
L 
L   # good
L   # frozen-string-literal: TRUE
S Example: ValueCapitalization: uppercase
L   # bad
L   # frozen-string-literal: true
L 
L   # good
L   # frozen-string-literal: TRUE
C Style/MapCompactWithConditionalBlock
S Details
L Prefer `select` or `reject` over `map { ... }.compact`.
L This cop also handles `filter_map { ... }`, similar to `map { ... }.compact`.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because `compact` also removes `nil` elements that
L   were already present in the receiver, whereas `select`/`reject` keep
L   them. The result therefore differs when the collection contains `nil`:
L 
L   [source,ruby]
L   ----
L   [nil, 1].map { |e| e if e }.compact # => [1]
L   [nil, 1].select { |e| e }           # => [nil, 1]
L   ----
S Example
L   # bad
L   array.map { |e| some_condition? ? e : next }.compact
L 
L   # bad
L   array.filter_map { |e| some_condition? ? e : next }
L 
L   # bad
L   array.map do |e|
L     if some_condition?
L       e
L     else
L       next
L     end
L   end.compact
L 
L   # bad
L   array.map do |e|
L     next if some_condition?
L 
L     e
L   end.compact
L 
L   # bad
L   array.map do |e|
L     e if some_condition?
L   end.compact
L 
L   # good
L   array.select { |e| some_condition? }
L 
L   # good
L   array.reject { |e| some_condition? }
C Style/MapIntoArray
S Details
L Checks for usages of `each` with `<<`, `push`, or `append` which
L can be replaced by `map`.
L 
L If `PreferredMethods` is configured for `map` in `Style/CollectionMethods`,
L this cop uses the specified method for replacement.
L 
L NOTE: The return value of `Enumerable#each` is `self`, whereas the
L return value of `Enumerable#map` is an `Array`. They are not autocorrected
L when a return value could be used because these types differ.
L 
L NOTE: It only detects when the mapping destination is either:
L * a local variable initialized as an empty array and referred to only by the
L pushing operation;
L * or, if it is the single block argument to a `[].tap` block.
L This is because, if not, it's challenging to statically guarantee that the
L mapping destination variable remains an empty array:
L
