L [source,ruby]
L ----
L ret = []
L src.each { |e| ret << e * 2 } # `<<` method may mutate `ret`
L 
L dest = []
L src.each { |e| dest << transform(e, dest) } # `transform` method may mutate `dest`
L ----
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because not all objects that have an `each`
L   method also have a `map` method (e.g. `ENV`). Additionally, for calls
L   with a block, not all objects that have a `map` method return an array
L   (e.g. `Enumerator::Lazy`).
S Example
L   # bad
L   dest = []
L   src.each { |e| dest << e * 2 }
L   dest
L 
L   # good
L   dest = src.map { |e| e * 2 }
L 
L   # bad
L   [].tap do |dest|
L     src.each { |e| dest << e * 2 }
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   dest = src.map { |e| e * 2 }
L 
L   # good - contains another operation
L   dest = []
L   src.each { |e| dest << e * 2; puts e }
L   dest
C Style/MapJoin
S Details
L Checks for `map { |x| x.to_s }.join` and similar calls where the
L `map` is redundant because `Array#join` implicitly calls `#to_s` on
L each element.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because it cannot guarantee that the receiver
L   is an `Array` by static analysis. If the receiver does not have
L   an `Array#join`-compatible implementation (i.e. one that calls
L   `#to_s` on elements), the correction may change behavior.
S Example
L   # bad
L   array.map(&:to_s).join(', ')
L 
L   # bad
L   array.map { |x| x.to_s }.join(', ')
L 
L   # bad
L   array.collect(&:to_s).join
L 
L   # good
L   array.join(', ')
L 
L   # good
L   array.join
C Style/MapToHash
S Details
L Looks for uses of `map.to_h` or `collect.to_h` that could be
L written with just `to_h` in Ruby >= 2.6.
L 
L NOTE: `Style/HashTransformKeys` and `Style/HashTransformValues` will
L also change this pattern if only hash keys or hash values are being
L transformed.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe, as it can produce false positives if the receiver
L   is not an `Enumerable`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   something.map { |v| [v, v * 2] }.to_h
L 
L   # good
L   something.to_h { |v| [v, v * 2] }
L 
L   # bad
L   {foo: bar}.collect { |k, v| [k.to_s, v.do_something] }.to_h
L 
L   # good
L   {foo: bar}.to_h { |k, v| [k.to_s, v.do_something] }
C Style/MapToSet
S Details
L Looks for uses of `map.to_set` or `collect.to_set` that could be
L written with just `to_set`.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe, as it can produce false positives if the receiver
L   is not an `Enumerable`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   something.map { |i| i * 2 }.to_set
L 
L   # good
L   something.to_set { |i| i * 2 }
L 
L   # bad
L   [1, 2, 3].collect { |i| i.to_s }.to_set
L 
L   # good
L   [1, 2, 3].to_set { |i| i.to_s }
C Style/MethodCallWithArgsParentheses
S Details
L Enforces the presence (default) or absence of parentheses in
L method calls containing arguments.
L 
L In the default style (require_parentheses), macro methods are allowed.
L Additional methods can be added to the `AllowedMethods` or
L `AllowedPatterns` list. These options are valid only in the default
L style. Macros can be included by either setting `IgnoreMacros` to false,
L adding specific macros to the `IncludedMacros` list, or using
L `IncludedMacroPatterns` for pattern-based matching.
L 
L Precedence of options is as follows:
L 
L 1. `AllowedMethods`
L 2. `AllowedPatterns`
L 3. `IncludedMacros`
L 4. `IncludedMacroPatterns`
L 
L If a method is listed in both `IncludedMacros`/`IncludedMacroPatterns`
L and `AllowedMethods`, then the latter takes precedence (that is, the
L method is allowed).
L 
L In the alternative style (omit_parentheses), there are three additional
L options.
L 
L 1. `AllowParenthesesInChaining` is `false` by default. Setting it to
L    `true` allows the presence of parentheses in the last call during
L    method chaining.
L 
L 2. `AllowParenthesesInMultilineCall` is `false` by default. Setting it
L     to `true` allows the presence of parentheses in multi-line method
L     calls.
L 
L 3. `AllowParenthesesInCamelCaseMethod` is `false` by default. This
L     allows the presence of parentheses when calling a method whose name
L     begins with a capital letter and which has no arguments. Setting it
L     to `true` allows the presence of parentheses in such a method call
L     even with arguments.
L 
L NOTE: The style of `omit_parentheses` allows parentheses in cases where
L omitting them results in ambiguous or syntactically incorrect code.
L 
L Non-exhaustive list of examples:
L 
L - Parentheses are required allowed in method calls with arguments inside
L   literals, logical operators, setting default values in position and
L   keyword arguments, chaining and more.
L - Parentheses are allowed in method calls with arguments inside
L   operators to avoid ambiguity.
L   triple-dot syntax introduced in Ruby 2.7 as omitting them starts an
L   endless range.
L - Parentheses are allowed when forwarding arguments with the
L   triple-dot syntax introduced in Ruby 2.7 as omitting them starts an
L   endless range.
L - Parentheses are required in calls with arguments when inside an
L   endless method definition introduced in Ruby 3.0.
L - Ruby 3.1's hash omission syntax allows parentheses if the method call
L   is in conditionals and requires parentheses if the call
L   is not the value-returning expression. See
L   https://bugs.ruby-lang.org/issues/18396.
L - Parentheses are required in anonymous arguments, keyword arguments
L   and block passing in Ruby 3.2.
L - Parentheses are required when the first argument is a beginless range or
L   the last argument is an endless range.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: require_parentheses (default)
L   # bad
L   array.delete e
L 
L   # good
L   array.delete(e)
L 
L   # good
L   # Operators don't need parens
L   foo == bar
L 
L   # good
L   # Setter methods don't need parens
L   foo.bar = baz
L 
L   # okay with `puts` listed in `AllowedMethods`
L   puts 'test'
L 
L   # okay with `^assert` listed in `AllowedPatterns`
L   assert_equal 'test', x
S Example: EnforcedStyle: omit_parentheses
L   # bad
L   array.delete(e)
L 
L   # good
L   array.delete e
L 
L   # bad
L   action.enforce(strict: true)
L 
L   # good
L   action.enforce strict: true
L 
L   # good
L   # Parentheses are allowed for code that can be ambiguous without
L   # them.
L   action.enforce(condition) || other_condition
L 
L   # good
L   # Parentheses are allowed for calls that won't produce valid Ruby
L   # without them.
L   yield path, File.basename(path)
L 
L   # good
L   # Omitting the parentheses in Ruby 3.1 hash omission syntax can lead
L   # to ambiguous code. We allow them in conditionals and non-last
L   # expressions. See https://bugs.ruby-lang.org/issues/18396
L   if meets(criteria:, action:)
L     safe_action(action) || dangerous_action(action)
L   end
S Example: IgnoreMacros: true (default)
L   # good
L   class Foo
L     bar :baz
L   end
S Example: IgnoreMacros: false
L   # bad
L   class Foo
L     bar :baz
L   end
S Example: AllowedMethods: ["puts", "print"]
L   # good
L   puts "Hello world"
L   print "Hello world"
L   # still enforces parentheses on other methods
L   array.delete(e)
S Example: AllowedPatterns: ["^assert"]
L   # good
L   assert_equal 'test', x
L   assert_match(/foo/, bar)
L   # still enforces parentheses on other methods
L   array.delete(e)
S Example: IncludedMacroPatterns: ["^assert", "^refute"]
L   # bad
L   assert_equal 'test', x
L   refute_nil value
L 
L   # good
L   assert_equal('test', x)
L   refute_nil(value)
S Example: AllowParenthesesInMultilineCall: false (default)
L   # bad
L   foo.enforce(
L     strict: true
L   )
L 
L   # good
L   foo.enforce \
L     strict: true
S Example: AllowParenthesesInMultilineCall: true
L   # good
L   foo.enforce(
L     strict: true
L   )
L 
L   # good
L   foo.enforce \
L     strict: true
S Example: AllowParenthesesInChaining: false (default)
L   # bad
L   foo().bar(1)
L 
L   # good
L   foo().bar 1
S Example: AllowParenthesesInChaining: true
L   # good
L   foo().bar(1)
L 
L   # good
L   foo().bar 1
S Example: AllowParenthesesInCamelCaseMethod: false (default)
L   # bad
L   Array(1)
L 
L   # good
L   Array 1
S Example: AllowParenthesesInCamelCaseMethod: true
L   # good
L   Array(1)
L 
L   # good
L   Array 1
S Example: AllowParenthesesInStringInterpolation: false (default)
L   # bad
L   "#{t('this.is.bad')}"
L 
L   # good
L   "#{t 'this.is.better'}"
S Example: AllowParenthesesInStringInterpolation: true
L   # good
L   "#{t('this.is.good')}"
L 
L   # good
L   "#{t 'this.is.also.good'}"
C Style/MethodCallWithoutArgsParentheses
S Details
L Checks for unwanted parentheses in parameterless method calls.
L 
L This cop's allowed methods can be customized with `AllowedMethods`.
L By default, there are no allowed methods.
L 
L NOTE: This cop allows the use of `it()` without arguments in blocks,
L as in `0.times { it() }`, following `Lint/ItWithoutArgumentsInBlock` cop.
S Example
L   # bad
L   object.some_method()
L 
L   # good
L   object.some_method
S Example: AllowedMethods: [] (default)
L   # bad
L   object.foo()
S Example: AllowedMethods: [foo]
L   # good
L   object.foo()
C Style/MethodCalledOnDoEndBlock
S Details
L Checks for methods called on a do...end block. The point of
L this check is that it's easy to miss the call tacked on to the block
L when reading code.
S Example
L   # bad
L   a do
L     b
L   end.c
L 
L   # good
L   a { b }.c
L 
L   # good
L   foo = a do
L     b
L   end
L   foo.c
C Style/MethodDefParentheses
S Details
L Checks for parentheses around the arguments in method
L definitions. Both instance and class/singleton methods are checked.
L 
L Regardless of style, parentheses are necessary for:
L 
L 1. Endless methods
L 2. Argument lists containing a `forward-arg` (`...`)
L 3. Argument lists containing an anonymous rest arguments forwarding (`*`)
L 4. Argument lists containing an anonymous keyword rest arguments forwarding (`**`)
L 5. Argument lists containing an anonymous block forwarding (`&`)
L 
L Removing the parens would be a syntax error here.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: require_parentheses (default)
L   # The `require_parentheses` style requires method definitions
L   # to always use parentheses
L 
L   # bad
L   def bar num1, num2
L     num1 + num2
L   end
L 
L   def foo descriptive_var_name,
L           another_descriptive_var_name,
L           last_descriptive_var_name
L     do_something
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def bar(num1, num2)
L     num1 + num2
L   end
L 
L   def foo(descriptive_var_name,
L           another_descriptive_var_name,
L           last_descriptive_var_name)
L     do_something
L   end
S Example: EnforcedStyle: require_no_parentheses
L   # The `require_no_parentheses` style requires method definitions
L   # to never use parentheses
L 
L   # bad
L   def bar(num1, num2)
L     num1 + num2
L   end
L 
L   def foo(descriptive_var_name,
L           another_descriptive_var_name,
L           last_descriptive_var_name)
L     do_something
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def bar num1, num2
L     num1 + num2
L   end
L 
L   def foo descriptive_var_name,
L           another_descriptive_var_name,
L           last_descriptive_var_name
L     do_something
L   end
S Example: EnforcedStyle: require_no_parentheses_except_multiline
L   # The `require_no_parentheses_except_multiline` style prefers no
L   # parentheses when method definition arguments fit on single line,
L   # but prefers parentheses when arguments span multiple lines.
L 
L   # bad
L   def bar(num1, num2)
L     num1 + num2
L   end
L 
L   def foo descriptive_var_name,
L           another_descriptive_var_name,
L           last_descriptive_var_name
L     do_something
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def bar num1, num2
L     num1 + num2
L   end
L 
L   def foo(descriptive_var_name,
L           another_descriptive_var_name,
L           last_descriptive_var_name)
L     do_something
L   end
C Style/MinMax
S Details
L Checks for potential uses of `Enumerable#minmax`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   bar = [foo.min, foo.max]
L   return foo.min, foo.max
L 
L   # good
L   bar = foo.minmax
L   return foo.minmax
C Style/MinMaxComparison
S Details
L Enforces the use of `max` or `min` instead of comparison for greater or less.
L 
L NOTE: It can be used if you want to present limit or threshold in Ruby 2.7+.
L It is slow though. So autocorrection will apply generic `max` or `min`:
L 
L [source,ruby]
L ----
L a.clamp(b..) # Same as `[a, b].max`
L a.clamp(..b) # Same as `[a, b].min`
L ----
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because even if a value has `<` or `>` method,
L   it is not necessarily `Comparable`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   a > b ? a : b
L   a >= b ? a : b
L 
L   # good
L   [a, b].max
L 
L   # bad
L   a < b ? a : b
L   a <= b ? a : b
L 
L   # good
L   [a, b].min
C Style/MissingElse
S Details
L Checks for `if` expressions that do not have an `else` branch.
L 
L NOTE: Pattern matching is allowed to have no `else` branch because unlike `if` and `case`,
L it raises `NoMatchingPatternError` if the pattern doesn't match and without having `else`.
L 
L Supported styles are: if, case, both.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: both (default)
L   # warn when an `if` or `case` expression is missing an `else` branch.
L 
L   # bad
L   if condition
L     statement
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   case var
L   when condition
L     statement
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   if condition
L     statement
L   else
L     # the content of `else` branch will be determined by Style/EmptyElse
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   case var
L   when condition
L     statement
L   else
L     # the content of `else` branch will be determined by Style/EmptyElse
L   end
S Example: EnforcedStyle: if
L   # warn when an `if` expression is missing an `else` branch.
L 
L   # bad
L   if condition
L     statement
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   if condition
L     statement
L   else
L     # the content of `else` branch will be determined by Style/EmptyElse
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   case var
L   when condition
L     statement
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   case var
L   when condition
L     statement
L   else
L     # the content of `else` branch will be determined by Style/EmptyElse
L   end
S Example: EnforcedStyle: case
L   # warn when a `case` expression is missing an `else` branch.
L 
L   # bad
L   case var
L   when condition
L     statement
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   case var
L   when condition
L     statement
L   else
L     # the content of `else` branch will be determined by Style/EmptyElse
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   if condition
L     statement
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   if condition
L     statement
L   else
L     # the content of `else` branch will be determined by Style/EmptyElse
L   end
C Style/MissingRespondToMissing
S Details
L Checks for the presence of `method_missing` without also
L defining `respond_to_missing?`.
L 
L Not defining `respond_to_missing?` will cause metaprogramming
L methods like `respond_to?` to behave unexpectedly:
L 
L [source,ruby]
L ----
L class StringDelegator
L   def initialize(string)
L     @string = string
L   end
L 
L   def method_missing(name, *args)
L     @string.send(name, *args)
L   end
L end
L 
L delegator = StringDelegator.new("foo")
L # Claims to not respond to `upcase`.
L delegator.respond_to?(:upcase) # => false
L # But you can call it.
L delegator.upcase # => FOO
L ----
L 
L When `AllCops/UseProjectIndex` is enabled and the `rubydex` gem is
L installed, `respond_to_missing?` defined in another definition of the
L same class or module (e.g. a reopening in another file) also
L satisfies the check.
S Example
L   # bad
L   def method_missing(name, *args)
L     if @delegate.respond_to?(name)
L       @delegate.send(name, *args)
L     else
L       super
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def respond_to_missing?(name, include_private)
L     @delegate.respond_to?(name) || super
L   end
L 
L   def method_missing(name, *args)
L     if @delegate.respond_to?(name)
L       @delegate.send(name, *args)
L     else
L       super
L     end
L   end
C Style/MixinGrouping
S Details
L Checks for grouping of mixins in `class` and `module` bodies.
L By default it enforces mixins to be placed in separate declarations,
L but it can be configured to enforce grouping them in one declaration.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: separated (default)
L   # bad
L   class Foo
L     include Bar, Qox
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class Foo
L     include Qox
L     include Bar
L   end
S Example: EnforcedStyle: grouped
L   # bad
L   class Foo
L     extend Bar
L     extend Qox
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class Foo
L     extend Qox, Bar
L   end
C Style/MixinUsage
S Details
L Checks that `include`, `extend` and `prepend` statements appear
L inside classes and modules, not at the top level, so as to not affect
L the behavior of `Object`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   include M
L 
L   class C
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   extend M
L 
L   class C
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   prepend M
L 
L   class C
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class C
L     include M
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class C
L     extend M
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class C
L     prepend M
L   end
C Style/ModuleFunction
S Details
L Checks for use of `extend self` or `module_function` in a module.
L 
L Supported styles are: `module_function` (default), `extend_self` and `forbidden`.
L 
L A couple of things to keep in mind:
L 
L - `forbidden` style prohibits the usage of both styles
L - in default mode (`module_function`), the cop won't be activated when the module
L   contains any private methods
S Safety
L   Autocorrection is unsafe (and is disabled by default) because `extend self`
L   and `module_function` do not behave exactly the same.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: module_function (default)
L   # bad
L   module Test
L     extend self
L     # ...
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   module Test
L     module_function
L     # ...
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   module Test
L     extend self
L     # ...
L     private
L     # ...
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   module Test
L     class << self
L       # ...
L     end
L   end
S Example: EnforcedStyle: extend_self
L   # bad
L   module Test
L     module_function
L     # ...
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   module Test
L     extend self
L     # ...
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   module Test
L     class << self
L       # ...
L     end
L   end
S Example: EnforcedStyle: forbidden
L   # bad
L   module Test
L     module_function
L     # ...
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   module Test
L     extend self
L     # ...
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   module Test
L     extend self
L     # ...
L     private
L     # ...
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   module Test
L     class << self
L       # ...
L     end
L   end
C Style/ModuleMemberExistenceCheck
S Details
L Checks for usage of `Module` methods returning arrays that can be replaced
L with equivalent predicates.
L 
L Calling a method returning an array then checking if an element is inside
L it is much slower than using an equivalent predicate method. For example,
L `instance_methods.include?` will return an array of all public and protected
L instance methods in the module, then check if a given method is inside that
L array, while `method_defined?` will do direct method lookup, which is much
L faster and consumes less memory.
L 
L NOTE: `constants.include?` is not handled by this cop because
L `Module#const_defined?` has different lookup behavior than
L `Module#constants` - `const_defined?` searches up to `Object`
L (top-level constants like `String`, `Integer`, etc.) while
L `constants` does not, which can cause behavior changes after autocorrection.
S Example
L   # bad
L   Array.instance_methods.include?(:size)
L   Array.instance_methods.member?(:size)
L   Array.instance_methods(true).include?(:size)
L 
L   Array.instance_methods(false).include?(:find)
L 
L   # good
L   Array.method_defined?(:size)
L 
L   Array.method_defined?(:find, false)
L 
L   # bad
L   Array.class_variables.include?(:foo)
L   Array.private_instance_methods.include?(:foo)
L   Array.protected_instance_methods.include?(:foo)
L   Array.public_instance_methods.include?(:foo)
L 
L   # good
L   Array.class_variable_defined?(:foo)
L   Array.private_method_defined?(:foo)
L   Array.protected_method_defined?(:foo)
L   Array.public_method_defined?(:foo)
C Style/MultilineBlockChain
S Details
L Checks for chaining of a block after another block that spans
L multiple lines.
S Example
L   # bad
L   Thread.list.select do |t|
L     t.alive?
L   end.map do |t|
L     t.object_id
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   alive_threads = Thread.list.select do |t|
L     t.alive?
L   end
L   alive_threads.map do |t|
L     t.object_id
L   end
C Style/MultilineIfModifier
S Details
L Checks for uses of if/unless modifiers with multiple-lines bodies.
S Example
L   # bad
L   {
L     result: 'this should not happen'
L   } unless cond
L 
L   # good
L   { result: 'ok' } if cond
C Style/MultilineIfThen
S Details
L Checks for uses of the `then` keyword in multi-line `if` statements.
L In multi-line `if` statements, `then` is redundant because the newline
L already separates the condition from the body.
S Example
L   # bad
L   # This is considered bad practice.
L   if cond then
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   # If statements can contain `then` on the same line.
L   if cond then a
L   elsif cond then b
L   end
C Style/MultilineInPatternThen
S Details
L Checks uses of the `then` keyword in multi-line `in` statement.
S Example
L   # bad
L   case expression
L   in pattern then
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   case expression
L   in pattern
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   case expression
L   in pattern then do_something
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   case expression
L   in pattern then do_something(arg1,
L                                arg2)
L   end
C Style/MultilineMemoization
S Details
L Checks expressions wrapping styles for multiline memoization.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: keyword (default)
L   # bad
L   foo ||= (
L     bar
L     baz
L   )
L 
L   # good
L   foo ||= begin
L     bar
L     baz
L   end
S Example: EnforcedStyle: braces
L   # bad
L   foo ||= begin
L     bar
L     baz
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   foo ||= (
L     bar
L     baz
L   )
C Style/MultilineMethodSignature
S Details
L Checks for method signatures that span multiple lines.
S Example
L   # good
L 
L   def foo(bar, baz)
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L 
L   def foo(bar,
L           baz)
L   end
C Style/MultilineTernaryOperator
S Details
L Checks for multi-line ternary op expressions.
L 
L NOTE: `return if ... else ... end` is syntax error. If `return` is used before
L multiline ternary operator expression, it will be autocorrected to single-line
L ternary operator. The same is true for `break`, `next`, `yield`, `super`,
L `defined?` without parentheses, and method call.
S Example
L   # bad
L   a = cond ?
L     b : c
L   a = cond ? b :
L       c
L   a = cond ?
L       b :
L       c
L 
L   return cond ?
L          b :
L          c
L 
L   # good
L   a = cond ? b : c
L   a = if cond
L     b
L   else
L     c
L   end
L 
L   return cond ? b : c
C Style/MultilineWhenThen
S Details
L Checks uses of the `then` keyword
L in multi-line when statements.
S Example
L   # bad
L   case foo
L   when bar then
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   case foo
L   when bar
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   case foo
L   when bar then do_something
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   case foo
L   when bar then do_something(arg1,
L                              arg2)
L   end
C Style/MultipleComparison
S Details
L Checks against comparing a variable with multiple items, where
L `Array#include?`, `Set#include?` or a `case` could be used instead
L to avoid code repetition.
L It accepts comparisons of multiple method calls to avoid unnecessary method calls
L by default. It can be configured by `AllowMethodComparison` option.
S Example
L   # bad
L   a = 'a'
L   foo if a == 'a' || a == 'b' || a == 'c'
L 
L   # good
L   a = 'a'
L   foo if ['a', 'b', 'c'].include?(a)
L 
L   VALUES = Set['a', 'b', 'c'].freeze
L   # elsewhere...
L   foo if VALUES.include?(a)
L 
L   case foo
L   when 'a', 'b', 'c' then foo
L   # ...
L   end
L 
L   # accepted (but consider `case` as above)
L   foo if a == b.lightweight || a == b.heavyweight
S Example: AllowMethodComparison: true (default)
L   # good
L   foo if a == b.lightweight || a == b.heavyweight
S Example: AllowMethodComparison: false
L   # bad
L   foo if a == b.lightweight || a == b.heavyweight
L 
L   # good
L   foo if [b.lightweight, b.heavyweight].include?(a)
S Example: ComparisonsThreshold: 2 (default)
L   # bad
L   foo if a == 'a' || a == 'b'
S Example: ComparisonsThreshold: 3
L   # good
L   foo if a == 'a' || a == 'b'
C Style/MutableConstant
S Details
L Checks whether some constant value isn't a
L mutable literal (e.g. array or hash).
L 
L When the `Recursive` option is enabled, mutable literals nested inside
L arrays and hashes are also frozen, so an offense on the outermost
L unfrozen literal will autocorrect every nested mutable literal as well.
L When the outer literal already has `.freeze` appended, the cop descends
L into it and reports each outermost unfrozen literal underneath. The
L option is disabled by default to preserve existing behavior; opt in to
L get strict nested freezing.
L 
L Strict mode can be used to freeze all constants, rather than
L just literals.
L Strict mode is considered an experimental feature. It has not been
L updated with an exhaustive list of all methods that will produce
L frozen objects so there is a decent chance of getting some false
L positives. Luckily, there is no harm in freezing an already
L frozen object.
L 
L From Ruby 3.0, this cop honours the magic comment
L 'shareable_constant_value'. When this magic comment is set to any
L acceptable value other than none, it will suppress the offenses
L raised by this cop. It enforces frozen state.
L 
L NOTE: `Regexp` and `Range` literals are frozen objects since Ruby 3.0.
L 
L NOTE: From Ruby 3.0, interpolated strings are not frozen when
L `# frozen-string-literal: true` is used, so this cop enforces explicit
L freezing for such strings.
L 
L NOTE: From Ruby 3.0, this cop allows explicit freezing of constants when
L the `shareable_constant_value` directive is used.
S Safety
L   This cop's autocorrection is unsafe since any mutations on objects that
L   are made frozen will change from being accepted to raising `FrozenError`,
L   and will need to be manually refactored.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: literals (default)
L   # bad
L   CONST = [1, 2, 3]
L 
L   # good
L   CONST = [1, 2, 3].freeze
L 
L   # good
L   CONST = <<~TESTING.freeze
L     This is a heredoc
L   TESTING
L 
L   # good
L   CONST = Something.new
S Example: Recursive: false (default)
L   # good - only the outer container needs to be frozen
L   CONST = [{ a: [], b: 'foo' }].freeze
S Example: Recursive: true
L   # bad - nested mutable literals must be frozen too
L   CONST = [{ a: [], b: 'foo' }].freeze
L 
L   # good
L   CONST = [{ a: [].freeze, b: 'foo'.freeze }.freeze].freeze
S Example: EnforcedStyle: strict
L   # bad
L   CONST = Something.new
L 
L   # bad
L   CONST = Struct.new do
L     def foo
L       puts 1
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   CONST = Something.new.freeze
L 
L   # good
L   CONST = Struct.new do
L     def foo
L       puts 1
L     end
L   end.freeze
L 
L   # good - `Data.define` declares an immutable value type
L   CONST = Data.define(:foo, :bar)
S Example
L   # Magic comment - shareable_constant_value: literal
L 
L   # bad
L   CONST = [1, 2, 3]
L 
L   # good
L   # shareable_constant_value: literal
L   CONST = [1, 2, 3]
C Style/NegatedIf
S Details
L Checks for uses of if with a negated condition. Only ifs
L without else are considered. There are three different styles:
L 
L * both
L * prefix
L * postfix
S Example: EnforcedStyle: both (default)
L   # enforces `unless` for `prefix` and `postfix` conditionals
L 
L   # bad
L 
L   if !foo
L     bar
L   end
L 
L   # good
L 
L   unless foo
L     bar
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L 
L   bar if !foo
L 
L   # good
L 
L   bar unless foo
S Example: EnforcedStyle: prefix
L   # enforces `unless` for just `prefix` conditionals
L 
L   # bad
L 
L   if !foo
L     bar
L   end
L 
L   # good
L 
L   unless foo
L     bar
L   end
L 
L   # good
L 
L   bar if !foo
S Example: EnforcedStyle: postfix
L   # enforces `unless` for just `postfix` conditionals
L 
L   # bad
L 
L   bar if !foo
L 
L   # good
L 
L   bar unless foo
L 
L   # good
L 
L   if !foo
L     bar
L   end
C Style/NegatedIfElseCondition
S Details
L Checks for uses of `if-else` and ternary operators with a negated condition
L which can be simplified by inverting condition and swapping branches.
S Example
L   # bad
L   if !x
L     do_something
L   else
L     do_something_else
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   if x
L     do_something_else
L   else
L     do_something
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   !x ? do_something : do_something_else
L 
L   # good
L   x ? do_something_else : do_something
C Style/NegatedUnless
S Details
L Checks for uses of unless with a negated condition. Only unless
L without else are considered. There are three different styles:
L 
L * both
L * prefix
L * postfix
S Example: EnforcedStyle: both (default)
L   # enforces `if` for `prefix` and `postfix` conditionals
L 
L   # bad
L   unless !foo
L     bar
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   if foo
L     bar
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   bar unless !foo
L 
L   # good
L   bar if foo
S Example: EnforcedStyle: prefix
L   # enforces `if` for just `prefix` conditionals
L 
L   # bad
L   unless !foo
L     bar
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   if foo
L     bar
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   bar unless !foo
S Example: EnforcedStyle: postfix
L   # enforces `if` for just `postfix` conditionals
L 
L   # bad
L   bar unless !foo
L 
L   # good
L   bar if foo
L 
L   # good
L   unless !foo
L     bar
L   end
C Style/NegatedWhile
S Details
L Checks for uses of while with a negated condition.
S Example
L   # bad
L   while !foo
L     bar
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   until foo
L     bar
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   bar until !foo
L 
L   # good
L   bar while foo
L   bar while !foo && baz
C Style/NegativeArrayIndex
S Details
L Identifies usages of `arr[arr.length - n]`, `arr[arr.size - n]`, or
L `arr[arr.count - n]` and suggests to change them to use `arr[-n]` instead.
L Also handles range patterns like `arr[0..(arr.length - n)]`.
L 
L The cop recognizes preserving methods (`sort`, `reverse`, `shuffle`, `rotate`)
L and their combinations, allowing safe replacement when the receiver matches.
L It works with variables, instance variables, class variables, and constants.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because the receiver may not be an Array. For a Hash,
L   `hash[hash.size - 1]` looks up the key `hash.size - 1`, which `hash[-1]`
L   does not. Even for an Array, `arr[arr.length - n]` wraps around to an
L   element from the end when `n` exceeds the length, while `arr[-n]`
L   returns `nil`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   arr[arr.count - 2]
L   arr[0..(arr.length - 2)]
L   arr[0...(arr.length - 4)]
L   arr.sort[arr.reverse.length - 2]
L   arr.sort.reverse[arr.sort.size - 2]
L 
L   # good
L   arr[-2]
L   arr[0..-2]
L   arr[0...-4]
L   arr.sort[-2]
L   arr.sort.reverse[-2]
C Style/NestedFileDirname
S Details
L Checks for nested `File.dirname`.
L It replaces nested `File.dirname` with the level argument introduced in Ruby 3.1.
S Example
L   # bad
L   File.dirname(File.dirname(path))
L 
L   # good
L   File.dirname(path, 2)
C Style/NestedModifier
S Details
L Checks for nested use of if, unless, while and until in their
L modifier form.
S Example
L   # bad
L   something if a if b
L 
L   # good
L   something if b && a
C Style/NestedParenthesizedCalls
S Details
L Checks for unparenthesized method calls in the argument list
L of a parenthesized method call.
L `be`, `be_a`, `be_an`, `be_between`, `be_falsey`, `be_kind_of`, `be_instance_of`,
L `be_truthy`, `be_within`, `eq`, `eql`, `end_with`, `include`, `match`, `raise_error`,
L `respond_to`, and `start_with` methods are allowed by default.
L These are customizable with `AllowedMethods` option.
S Example
L   # good
L   method1(method2(arg))
L 
L   # bad
L   method1(method2 arg)
S Example: AllowedMethods: [foo]
L   # good
L   method1(foo arg)
C Style/NestedTernaryOperator
S Details
L Checks for nested ternary op expressions.
S Example
L   # bad
L   a ? (b ? b1 : b2) : a2
L 
L   # good
L   if a
L     b ? b1 : b2
L   else
L     a2
L   end
C Style/Next
S Details
L Use `next` to skip iteration instead of a condition at the end.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: skip_modifier_ifs (default)
L   # bad
L   [1, 2].each do |a|
L     if a == 1
L       puts a
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   [1, 2].each do |a|
L     next unless a == 1
L     puts a
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   [1, 2].each do |a|
L     puts a if a == 1
L   end
S Example: EnforcedStyle: always
L   # With `always` all conditions at the end of an iteration needs to be
L   # replaced by next - with `skip_modifier_ifs` the modifier if like
L   # this one are ignored: `[1, 2].each { |a| puts a if a == 1 }`
L 
L   # bad
L   [1, 2].each do |a|
L     puts a if a == 1
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   [1, 2].each do |a|
L     if a == 1
L       puts a
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   [1, 2].each do |a|
L     next unless a == 1
L     puts a
L   end
S Example: AllowConsecutiveConditionals: false (default)
L   # bad
L   [1, 2].each do |a|
L     if a == 1
L       puts a
L     end
L     if a == 2
L       puts a
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   [1, 2].each do |a|
L     if a == 1
L       puts a
L     end
L     next unless a == 2
L     puts a
L   end
S Example: AllowConsecutiveConditionals: true
L   # good
L   [1, 2].each do |a|
L     if a == 1
L       puts a
L     end
L     if a == 2
L       puts a
L     end
L   end
C Style/NilComparison
S Details
L Checks for comparison of something with nil using `==` and
L `nil?`. Enforcing a consistent style (either the `nil?`
L predicate or `==` comparison) improves readability.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: predicate (default)
L   # bad
L   if x == nil
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   if x.nil?
L   end
S Example: EnforcedStyle: comparison
L   # bad
L   if x.nil?
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   if x == nil
L   end
C Style/NilLambda
S Details
L Checks for lambdas and procs that always return nil,
L which can be replaced with an empty lambda or proc instead.
L 
L NOTE: A `proc` that returns nil via an explicit `return` is allowed,
L because in a `proc` `return` exits the enclosing method, so removing it
L would change behavior. A lambda is still reported, since there `return`
L only exits the lambda itself.
S Example
L   # bad
L   -> { nil }
L 
L   lambda do
L     next nil
L   end
L 
L   proc { nil }
L 
L   Proc.new do
L     break nil
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   -> {}
L 
L   lambda do
L   end
L 
L   -> (x) { nil if x }
L 
L   proc {}
L 
L   Proc.new { nil if x }
C Style/NonNilCheck
S Details
L Checks for non-nil checks, which are usually redundant.
L 
L With `IncludeSemanticChanges` set to `false` by default, this cop
L does not report offenses for `!x.nil?` and does no changes that might
L change behavior.
L Also `IncludeSemanticChanges` set to `false` with `EnforcedStyle: comparison` of
L `Style/NilComparison` cop, this cop does not report offenses for `x != nil` and
L does no changes to `!x.nil?` style.
L 
L With `IncludeSemanticChanges` set to `true`, this cop reports offenses
L for `!x.nil?` and autocorrects that and `x != nil` to solely `x`, which
L is *usually* OK, but might change behavior.
S Example
L   # bad
L   if x != nil
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   if x
L   end
L 
L   # Non-nil checks are allowed if they are the final nodes of predicate.
L   # good
L   def signed_in?
L     !current_user.nil?
L   end
S Example: IncludeSemanticChanges: false (default)
L   # good
L   if !x.nil?
L   end
S Example: IncludeSemanticChanges: true
L   # bad
L   if !x.nil?
L   end
C Style/Not
S Details
L Checks for uses of the keyword `not` instead of `!`.
L The `not` keyword has lower precedence than `!`, which can
L lead to surprising behavior and often requires parentheses.
S Example
L   # bad - parentheses are required because of op precedence
L   x = (not something)
L 
L   # good
L   x = !something
C Style/NumberedParameters
S Details
L Checks for numbered parameters.
L 
L It can either restrict the use of numbered parameters to
L single-lined blocks, or disallow completely numbered parameters.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: allow_single_line (default)
L   # bad
L   collection.each do
L     puts _1
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   collection.each { puts _1 }
S Example: EnforcedStyle: disallow
L   # bad
L   collection.each { puts _1 }
L 
L   # good
L   collection.each { |item| puts item }
C Style/NumberedParametersLimit
S Details
L Detects use of an excessive amount of numbered parameters in a
L single block. Having too many numbered parameters can make code too
L cryptic and hard to read.
L 
L The cop defaults to registering an offense if there is more than 1 numbered
L parameter but this maximum can be configured by setting `Max`.
S Example: Max: 1 (default)
L   # bad
L   use_multiple_numbered_parameters { _1.call(_2, _3, _4) }
L 
L   # good
L   array.each { use_array_element_as_numbered_parameter(_1) }
L   hash.each { use_only_hash_value_as_numbered_parameter(_2) }
C Style/NumericLiteralPrefix
S Details
L Checks for octal, hex, binary, and decimal literals using
L uppercase prefixes and corrects them to lowercase prefix
L or no prefix (in case of decimals).
S Example: EnforcedOctalStyle: zero_with_o (default)
L   # bad - missing octal prefix
L   num = 01234
L 
L   # bad - uppercase prefix
L   num = 0O1234
L   num = 0X12AB
L   num = 0B10101
L 
L   # bad - redundant decimal prefix
L   num = 0D1234
L   num = 0d1234
L 
L   # good
L   num = 0o1234
L   num = 0x12AB
L   num = 0b10101
L   num = 1234
S Example: EnforcedOctalStyle: zero_only
L   # bad
L   num = 0o1234
L   num = 0O1234
L 
L   # good
L   num = 01234
C Style/NumericLiterals
S Details
L Checks for big numeric literals without `_` between groups
L of digits in them. Underscores make large numbers easier to
L read by visually separating groups of digits.
L 
L Additional allowed patterns can be added by adding regexps to
L the `AllowedPatterns` configuration. All regexps are treated
L as anchored even if the patterns do not contain anchors (so
L `\d{4}_\d{4}` will allow `1234_5678` but not `1234_5678_9012`).
L 
L NOTE: Even if `AllowedPatterns` are given, autocorrection will
L only correct to the standard pattern of an `_` every 3 digits.
S Example
L   # bad
L   1000000
L   1_00_000
L   1_0000
L 
L   # good
L   1_000_000
L   1000
S Example: Strict: false (default)
L   # good
L   10_000_00 # typical representation of $10,000 in cents
S Example: Strict: true
L   # bad
L   10_000_00 # typical representation of $10,000 in cents
S Example: AllowedNumbers: [3000]
L   # good
L   3000 # You can specify allowed numbers. (e.g. port number)
C Style/NumericPredicate
S Details
L Checks for usage of comparison operators (`==`,
L `>`, `<`) to test numbers as zero, positive, or negative.
L These can be replaced by their respective predicate methods.
L This cop can also be configured to do the reverse.
L 
L This cop's allowed methods can be customized with `AllowedMethods`.
L By default, there are no allowed methods.
L 
L This cop disregards `#nonzero?` as its value is truthy or falsey,
L but not `true` and `false`, and thus not always interchangeable with
L `!= 0`.
L 
L This cop allows comparisons to global variables, since they are often
L populated with objects which can be compared with integers, but are
L not themselves `Integer` polymorphic.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because it cannot be guaranteed that the receiver
L   defines the predicates or can be compared to a number, which may lead
L   to a false positive for non-standard classes.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: predicate (default)
L   # bad
L   foo == 0
L   0 > foo
L   bar.baz > 0
L 
L   # good
L   foo.zero?
L   foo.negative?
L   bar.baz.positive?
S Example: EnforcedStyle: comparison
L   # bad
L   foo.zero?
L   foo.negative?
L   bar.baz.positive?
L 
L   # good
L   foo == 0
L   0 > foo
L   bar.baz > 0
S Example: AllowedMethods: [] (default) with EnforcedStyle: predicate
L   # bad
L   foo == 0
L   0 > foo
L   bar.baz > 0
S Example: AllowedMethods: [==] with EnforcedStyle: predicate
L   # good
L   foo == 0
L 
L   # bad
L   0 > foo
L   bar.baz > 0
S Example: AllowedPatterns: [] (default) with EnforcedStyle: comparison
L   # bad
L   foo.zero?
L   foo.negative?
L   bar.baz.positive?
S Example: AllowedPatterns: ['zero'] with EnforcedStyle: predicate
L   # good
L   # bad
L   foo.zero?
L 
L   # bad
L   foo.negative?
L   bar.baz.positive?
C Style/ObjectThen
S Details
L Enforces the use of consistent method names
L `Object#yield_self` or `Object#then`.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: then (default)
L   # bad
L   obj.yield_self { |x| x.do_something }
L 
L   # good
L   obj.then { |x| x.do_something }
S Example: EnforcedStyle: yield_self
L   # bad
L   obj.then { |x| x.do_something }
L 
L   # good
L   obj.yield_self { |x| x.do_something }
C Style/OneClassPerFile
S Details
L Checks that each source file defines at most one top-level class or module.
L 
L Keeping one class or module per file makes it easier to find and navigate
L code, and follows the convention used by most Ruby projects.
L 
L Classes and modules listed in `AllowedClasses` are not counted toward the
L limit. This is useful for small ancillary classes like custom exception
L classes that logically belong with the main class.
S Example
L   # bad - Multiple top-level classes
L   class Foo
L   end
L 
L   class Bar
L   end
L 
L   # bad - Multiple top-level modules
L   module Foo
L   end
L 
L   module Bar
L   end
L 
L   # bad - A top-level class and a top-level module
L   class Foo
L   end
L 
L   module Bar
L   end
L 
L   # good - A single top-level class
L   class Foo
L   end
L 
L   # good - A single top-level module
L   module Foo
L   end
L 
L   # good - Nested classes within a single top-level class
L   class Foo
L     class Bar
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good - Multiple classes within a single top-level module
L   module Foo
L     class Bar
L     end
L 
L     class Baz
L     end
L   end
S Example: AllowedClasses: ['AllowedClass']
L   # good
L   class Foo
L   end
L 
L   class AllowedClass
L   end
C Style/OneLineConditional
S Details
L Checks for uses of `if/then/else/end` constructs on a single line.
L A ternary operator (`?:`) or multi-line `if` is more readable.
L `AlwaysCorrectToMultiline` config option can be set to `true` to autocorrect all offenses to
L multi-line constructs. When `AlwaysCorrectToMultiline` is `false` (default case) the
L autocorrect will first try converting them to ternary operators.
S Example
L   # bad
L   if foo then bar else baz end
L 
L   # bad
L   unless foo then baz else bar end
L 
L   # good
L   foo ? bar : baz
L 
L   # good
L   bar if foo
L 
L   # good
L   if foo then bar end
L 
L   # good
L   if foo
L     bar
L   else
L     baz
L   end
S Example: AlwaysCorrectToMultiline: false (default)
L   # bad
L   if cond then run else dont end
L 
L   # good
L   cond ? run : dont
S Example: AlwaysCorrectToMultiline: true
L   # bad
L   if cond then run else dont end
L 
L   # good
L   if cond
L     run
L   else
L     dont
L   end
C Style/OpenStructUse
S Details
L Flags uses of `OpenStruct`, as it is now officially discouraged
L to be used for performance, version compatibility, and potential security issues.
S Safety
L   Note that this cop may flag false positives; for instance, the following legal
L   use of a hand-rolled `OpenStruct` type would be considered an offense:
L 
L   [source,ruby]
L   -----
L   module MyNamespace
L     class OpenStruct # not the OpenStruct we're looking for
L     end
L 
L     def new_struct
L       OpenStruct.new # resolves to MyNamespace::OpenStruct
L     end
L   end
L   -----
S Example
L   # bad
L   point = OpenStruct.new(x: 0, y: 1)
L 
L   # good
L   Point = Struct.new(:x, :y)
L   point = Point.new(0, 1)
L 
L   # also good
L   point = { x: 0, y: 1 }
L 
L   # bad
L   test_double = OpenStruct.new(a: 'b')
L 
L   # good (assumes test using rspec-mocks)
L   test_double = double
L   allow(test_double).to receive(:a).and_return('b')
C Style/OperatorMethodCall
S Details
L Checks for redundant dot before operator method call.
L The target operator methods are `|`, `^`, `&`, ``<=>``, `==`, `===`, `=~`, `>`, `>=`, `<`,
L ``<=``, `<<`, `>>`, `+`, `-`, `*`, `/`, `%`, `**`, `~`, `!`, `!=`, and `!~`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   foo.+ bar
L   foo.& bar
L 
L   # good
L   foo + bar
L   foo & bar
C Style/OptionHash
S Details
L Checks for options hashes and discourages them if the
L current Ruby version supports keyword arguments.
S Example
L   # bad
L   def fry(options = {})
L     temperature = options.fetch(:temperature, 300)
L     # ...
L   end
L 
L 
L   # good
L   def fry(temperature: 300)
L     # ...
L   end
C Style/OptionalArguments
S Details
L Checks for optional arguments to methods
L that do not come at the end of the argument list.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because changing a method signature will
L   implicitly change behavior.
S Example
L   # bad
L   def foo(a = 1, b, c)
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def baz(a, b, c = 1)
L   end
L 
L   def foobar(a = 1, b = 2, c = 3)
L   end
C Style/OptionalBooleanParameter
S Details
L Checks for places where keyword arguments can be used instead of
L boolean arguments when defining methods. `respond_to_missing?` method is allowed by default.
L These are customizable with `AllowedMethods` option.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because changing a method signature will
L   implicitly change behavior.
S Example
L   # bad
L   def some_method(bar = false)
L     puts bar
L   end
L 
L   # bad - common hack before keyword args were introduced
L   def some_method(options = {})
L     bar = options.fetch(:bar, false)
L     puts bar
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def some_method(bar: false)
L     puts bar
L   end
S Example: AllowedMethods: ['some_method']
L   # good
L   def some_method(bar = false)
L     puts bar
L   end
C Style/OrAssignment
S Details
L Checks for potential usage of the `||=` operator.
S Example
L   # bad
L   name = name ? name : 'Bozhidar'
L 
L   # bad
L   name = if name
L            name
L          else
L            'Bozhidar'
L          end
L 
L   # bad
L   unless name
L     name = 'Bozhidar'
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   name = 'Bozhidar' unless name
L 
L   # good - set name to 'Bozhidar', only if it's nil or false
L   name ||= 'Bozhidar'
C Style/ParallelAssignment
S Details
L Checks for simple usages of parallel assignment.
L Parallel assignment is less readable than individual
L assignments and makes it harder to follow what each
L variable is being set to.
L 
L This will only complain when the number of variables
L being assigned matched the number of assigning variables.
S Example
L   # bad
L   a, b, c = 1, 2, 3
L   a, b, c = [1, 2, 3]
L 
L   # good
L   one, two = *foo
L   a, b = foo
L   a, b = b, a
L 
L   a = 1
L   b = 2
L   c = 3
C Style/ParenthesesAroundCondition
S Details
L Checks for the presence of superfluous parentheses around the
L condition of if/unless/while/until.
L 
L `AllowSafeAssignment` option for safe assignment.
L By safe assignment we mean putting parentheses around
L an assignment to indicate "I know I'm using an assignment
L as a condition. It's not a mistake."
S Example
L   # bad
L   x += 1 while (x < 10)
L   foo unless (bar || baz)
L 
L   if (x > 10)
L   elsif (x < 3)
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   x += 1 while x < 10
L   foo unless bar || baz
L 
L   if x > 10
L   elsif x < 3
L   end
S Example: AllowSafeAssignment: true (default)
L   # good
L   foo unless (bar = baz)
S Example: AllowSafeAssignment: false
L   # bad
L   foo unless (bar = baz)
S Example: AllowInMultilineConditions: false (default)
L   # bad
L   if (x > 10 &&
L      y > 10)
L   end
L 
L   # good
L    if x > 10 &&
L       y > 10
L    end
S Example: AllowInMultilineConditions: true
L   # good
L   if (x > 10 &&
L      y > 10)
L   end
C Style/PartitionInsteadOfDoubleSelect
S Details
L Checks for consecutive calls to `select`/`filter`/`find_all` and `reject`
L on the same receiver with the same block body, where `partition` could be
L used instead. Also detects two `select` or two `reject` calls where one
L block negates the other with `!`. Using `partition` reduces two collection
L traversals to one.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because:
L 
L   * `Hash#select` and `Hash#reject` return hashes, but `Hash#partition`
L     returns nested arrays.
L   * When the receiver has side effects, calling it once (with `partition`)
L     versus twice (with `select` + `reject`) may produce different results.
L   * Custom classes may override `select`/`reject` without providing a
L     compatible `partition` method.
S Example
L   # bad
L   positives = array.select { |x| x > 0 }
L   negatives = array.reject { |x| x > 0 }
L 
L   # bad
L   positives = array.filter { |x| x > 0 }
L   negatives = array.reject { |x| x > 0 }
L 
L   # bad
L   negatives = array.reject { |x| x > 0 }
L   positives = array.select { |x| x > 0 }
L 
L   # bad
L   positives = array.select(&:positive?)
L   negatives = array.reject(&:positive?)
L 
L   # bad
L   positives = array.select(&:positive?)
L   negatives = array.reject { |x| x.positive? }
L 
L   # bad
L   positives = array.select { |x| x.positive? }
L   non_positives = array.select { |x| !x.positive? }
L 
L   # good
L   positives, negatives = array.partition { |x| x > 0 }
L 
L   # good
L   positives, non_positives = array.partition { |x| x.positive? }
L 
L   # good
L   positives, negatives = array.partition(&:positive?)
C Style/PercentLiteralDelimiters
S Details
L Enforces the consistent usage of `%`-literal delimiters.
L Using consistent delimiters across the codebase reduces
L cognitive load when reading `%`-literals.
L 
L Specify the 'default' key to set all preferred delimiters at once. You
L can continue to specify individual preferred delimiters to override the
L default.
S Example
L   # Style/PercentLiteralDelimiters:
L   #   PreferredDelimiters:
L   #     default: '[]'
L   #     '%i':    '()'
L 
L   # good
L   %w[alpha beta] + %i(gamma delta)
L 
L   # bad
L   %W(alpha #{beta})
L 
L   # bad
L   %I(alpha beta)
C Style/PercentQLiterals
S Details
L Checks for usage of the %Q() syntax when %q() would do.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: lower_case_q (default)
L   # The `lower_case_q` style prefers `%q` unless
L   # interpolation is needed.
L   # bad
L   %Q[Mix the foo into the baz.]
L   %Q(They all said: 'Hooray!')
L 
L   # good
L   %q[Mix the foo into the baz]
L   %q(They all said: 'Hooray!')
S Example: EnforcedStyle: upper_case_q
L   # The `upper_case_q` style requires the sole use of `%Q`.
L   # bad
L   %q/Mix the foo into the baz./
L   %q{They all said: 'Hooray!'}
L 
L   # good
L   %Q/Mix the foo into the baz./
L   %Q{They all said: 'Hooray!'}
C Style/PerlBackrefs
S Details
L Looks for uses of Perl-style regexp match
L backreferences and their English versions like
L $1, $2, $&, $MATCH, $PREMATCH, etc.
S Example
L   # bad
L   puts $1
L 
L   # good
L   puts Regexp.last_match(1)
C Style/PredicateWithKind
S Details
L Looks for uses of `any?`, `all?`, `none?`, or `one?` with a block
L containing only an `is_a?`, `kind_of?`, or `instance_of?` check, and
L suggests using the predicate method with the class argument directly.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because `instance_of?` checks for an exact class
L   match, while the pattern argument uses `===` which also matches
L   subclasses. For `is_a?` and `kind_of?`, the behavior is equivalent.
S Example
L   # bad
L   array.any? { |x| x.is_a?(Integer) }
L   array.all? { |x| x.kind_of?(String) }
L   array.none? { |x| x.is_a?(Float) }
L   array.one? { |x| x.instance_of?(Symbol) }
L 
L   # good
L   array.any?(Integer)
L   array.all?(String)
L   array.none?(Float)
L   array.one?(Symbol)
C Style/PreferredHashMethods
S Details
L Checks for uses of methods `Hash#has_key?` and
L `Hash#has_value?`, and suggests using `Hash#key?` and `Hash#value?` instead.
L 
L It is configurable to enforce the verbose method names, by using the
L `EnforcedStyle: verbose` configuration.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because it cannot be guaranteed that the receiver
L   is a `Hash` or responds to the replacement methods.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: short (default)
L   # bad
L   Hash#has_key?
L   Hash#has_value?
L 
L   # good
L   Hash#key?
L   Hash#value?
S Example: EnforcedStyle: verbose
L   # bad
L   Hash#key?
L   Hash#value?
L 
L   # good
L   Hash#has_key?
L   Hash#has_value?
C Style/Proc
S Details
L Checks for uses of `Proc.new` where `Kernel#proc`
L would be more appropriate. `proc` is the shorter and
L more idiomatic way to create procs in Ruby.
S Example
L   # bad
L   p = Proc.new { |n| puts n }
L 
L   # good
L   p = proc { |n| puts n }
C Style/QuotedSymbols
S Details
L Checks if the quotes used for quoted symbols match the configured defaults.
L By default uses the same configuration as `Style/StringLiterals`; if that
L cop is not enabled, the default `EnforcedStyle` is `single_quotes`.
L 
L String interpolation is always kept in double quotes.
L 
L NOTE: `Lint/SymbolConversion` can be used in parallel to ensure that symbols
L are not quoted that don't need to be. This cop is for configuring the quoting
L style to use for symbols that require quotes.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: same_as_string_literals (default) / single_quotes
L   # bad
L   :"abc-def"
L 
L   # good
L   :'abc-def'
L   :"#{str}"
L   :"a\'b"
S Example: EnforcedStyle: double_quotes
L   # bad
L   :'abc-def'
L 
L   # good
L   :"abc-def"
L   :"#{str}"
L   :"a\'b"
C Style/RaiseArgs
S Details
L Checks the args passed to `fail` and `raise`.
L 
L Exploded style (default) enforces passing the exception class and message
L arguments separately, rather than constructing an instance of the error.
L 
L Compact style enforces constructing an error instance.
L 
L Both styles allow passing just a message, or an error instance when there is more
L than one argument.
L 
L The exploded style has an `AllowedCompactTypes` configuration
L option that takes an `Array` of exception name Strings.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because `raise Foo` calls `Foo.exception`, not `Foo.new`.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: exploded (default)
L   # bad
L   raise StandardError.new('message')
L 
L   # good
L   raise StandardError, 'message'
L   fail 'message'
L   raise MyCustomError
L   raise MyCustomError.new(arg1, arg2, arg3)
L   raise MyKwArgError.new(key1: val1, key2: val2)
L 
L   # With `AllowedCompactTypes` set to ['MyWrappedError']
L   raise MyWrappedError.new(obj)
L   raise MyWrappedError.new(obj), 'message'
S Example: EnforcedStyle: compact
L   # bad
L   raise StandardError, 'message'
L   raise RuntimeError, arg1, arg2, arg3
L 
L   # good
L   raise StandardError.new('message')
L   raise MyCustomError
L   raise MyCustomError.new(arg1, arg2, arg3)
L   fail 'message'
C Style/RandomWithOffset
S Details
L Checks for the use of randomly generated numbers,
L added/subtracted with integer literals, as well as those with
L Integer#succ and Integer#pred methods. Prefer using ranges instead,
L as it clearly states the intentions.
S Example
L   # bad
L   rand(6) + 1
L   1 + rand(6)
L   rand(6) - 1
L   1 - rand(6)
L   rand(6).succ
L   rand(6).pred
L   Random.rand(6) + 1
L   Kernel.rand(6) + 1
L   rand(0..5) + 1
L 
L   # good
L   rand(1..6)
L   rand(1...7)
C Style/ReduceToHash
S Details
L Checks for `each_with_object`, `inject`, and `reduce` calls that build
L a hash from an enumerable, where `to_h` with a block could be used instead.
L 
L This cop complements `Style/HashTransformKeys` and `Style/HashTransformValues`,
L which handle hash-to-hash transformations with destructured key-value pairs.
L This cop targets the case where a hash is built from individual elements
L (non-destructured block parameter).
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because it cannot guarantee that the receiver
L   is an `Enumerable` by static analysis, so the correction may
L   not be actually equivalent. Additionally, `each_with_object` returns
L   the hash object while `to_h` returns a new hash, which could matter
L   if the hash object identity is important.
S Example
L   # bad
L   array.each_with_object({}) { |elem, hash| hash[elem.id] = elem.name }
L 
L   # bad
L   array.inject({}) { |hash, elem| hash[elem.id] = elem.name; hash }
L 
L   # bad
L   array.reduce({}) { |hash, elem| hash[elem.id] = elem.name; hash }
L 
L   # bad
L   array.each_with_object({}) { |elem, hash| hash[elem] = elem.to_s }
L 
L   # good
L   array.to_h { |elem| [elem.id, elem.name] }
L 
L   # good
L   array.to_h { |elem| [elem, elem.to_s] }
C Style/RedundantArgument
S Details
L Checks for a redundant argument passed to certain methods.
L 
L NOTE: This cop is limited to methods with single parameter.
L 
L Method names and their redundant arguments can be configured like this:
L 
L [source,yaml]
L ----
L Methods:
L   join: ''
L   sum: 0
L   split: ' '
L   chomp: "\n"
L   chomp!: "\n"
L   foo: 2
L ----
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because of the following limitations:
L 
L   1. This cop matches by method names only and hence cannot tell apart
L      methods with same name in different classes.
L   2. This cop may be unsafe if certain special global variables (e.g. `$;`, `$/`) are set.
L      That depends on the nature of the target methods, of course. For example, the default
L      argument to join is `$OUTPUT_FIELD_SEPARATOR` (or `$,`) rather than `''`, and if that
L      global is changed, `''` is no longer a redundant argument.
S Example
L   # bad
L   array.join('')
L   [1, 2, 3].join("")
L   array.sum(0)
L   exit(true)
L   exit!(false)
L   string.to_i(10)
L   string.split(" ")
L   "first\nsecond".split(" ")
L   string.chomp("\n")
L   string.chomp!("\n")
L   A.foo(2)
L 
L   # good
L   array.join
L   [1, 2, 3].join
L   array.sum
L   exit
L   exit!
L   string.to_i
L   string.split
L   "first second".split
L   string.chomp
L   string.chomp!
L   A.foo
C Style/RedundantArrayConstructor
S Details
L Checks for the instantiation of an array using a redundant `Array` constructor.
L Autocorrect replaces it with an array literal which is the simplest and fastest.
S Example
L   # bad
L   Array.new([])
L   Array[]
L   Array([])
L   Array.new(['foo', 'foo', 'foo'])
L   Array['foo', 'foo', 'foo']
L   Array(['foo', 'foo', 'foo'])
L 
L   # good
L   []
L   ['foo', 'foo', 'foo']
L   Array.new(3, 'foo')
L   Array.new(3) { 'foo' }
C Style/RedundantArrayFlatten
S Details
L Checks for redundant calls of `Array#flatten`.
L 
L `Array#join` joins nested arrays recursively, so flattening an array
L beforehand is redundant.
S Safety
L   Cop is unsafe because the receiver of `flatten` method might not
L   be an `Array`, so it's possible it won't respond to `join` method,
L   or the end result would be different.
L   Also, if the global variable `$,` is set to a value other than the default `nil`,
L   false positives may occur.
S Example
L   # bad
L   x.flatten.join
L   x.flatten(1).join
L 
L   # good
L   x.join
C Style/RedundantAssignment
S Details
L Checks for redundant assignment before returning.
L 
L When there are comments between the assignment and reference,
L the cop will report an offense but it will not autocorrect.
S Example
L   # bad
L   def test
L     x = foo
L     x
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   def test
L     if x
L       z = foo
L       z
L     elsif y
L       z = bar
L       z
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def test
L     foo
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def test
L     if x
L       foo
L     elsif y
L       bar
L     end
L   end
C Style/RedundantBegin
S Details
L Checks for redundant `begin` blocks. A `begin` block is redundant
L when the `rescue`/`ensure` can be handled by the enclosing method
L or block definition directly, avoiding unnecessary indentation.
S Example
L   # bad
L   def redundant
L     begin
L       ala
L       bala
L     rescue StandardError => e
L       something
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def preferred
L     ala
L     bala
L   rescue StandardError => e
L     something
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   begin
L     do_something
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   do_something
L 
L   # bad
L   # When using Ruby 2.5 or later.
L   do_something do
L     begin
L       something
L     rescue => ex
L       anything
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   # In Ruby 2.5 or later, you can omit `begin` in `do-end` block.
L   do_something do
L     something
L   rescue => ex
L     anything
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   # Stabby lambdas don't support implicit `begin` in `do-end` blocks.
L   -> do
L     begin
L       foo
L     rescue Bar
L       baz
L     end
L   end
C Style/RedundantCapitalW
S Details
L Checks for usage of the %W() syntax when %w() would do.
S Example
L   # bad
L   %W(cat dog pig)
L   %W[door wall floor]
L 
L   # good
L   %w/swim run bike/
L   %w[shirt pants shoes]
L   %W(apple #{fruit} grape)
C Style/RedundantCondition
S Details
L Checks for unnecessary conditional expressions.
L 
L NOTE: Since the intention of the comment cannot be automatically determined,
L autocorrection is not applied when a comment is used, as shown below:
L 
L [source,ruby]
L -----
L if b
L   # Important note.
L   b
L else
L   c
L end
L -----
S Example
L   # bad
L   a = b ? b : c
L 
L   # good
L   a = b || c
L 
L   # bad
L   if b
L     b
L   else
L     c
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   b || c
L 
L   # good
L   if b
L     b
L   elsif cond
L     c
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   a.nil? ? true : a
L 
L   # good
L   a.nil? || a
L 
L   # bad
L   if a.nil?
L     true
L   else
L     a
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   a.nil? || a
S Example: AllowedMethods: ['infinite?', 'nonzero?'] (default)
L   # good
L   num.infinite? ? true : false
L 
L   # good
L   num.nonzero? ? true : false
C Style/RedundantConditional
S Details
L Checks for redundant returning of true/false in conditionals.
S Example
L   # bad
L   x == y ? true : false
L 
L   # bad
L   if x == y
L     true
L   else
L     false
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   x == y
L 
L   # bad
L   x == y ? false : true
L 
L   # good
L   x != y
C Style/RedundantConstantBase
S Details
L Avoid redundant `::` prefix on a constant.
L 
L How Ruby searches constants is a bit complicated, and it can often be difficult to
L understand from the code whether the `::` is intended or not. Where `Module.nesting`
L is empty, there is no need to prepend `::`, so it would be nice to consistently
L avoid such meaningless `::` prefix to avoid confusion.
L 
L NOTE: This cop is disabled if `Lint/ConstantResolution` cop is enabled,
L to prevent conflicting rules. This is because it respects user configurations
L that want to enable `Lint/ConstantResolution` cop which is disabled by default.
L 
L When `AllCops/UseProjectIndex` is enabled and the `rubydex` gem is
L installed, a leading `::` inside a namespace is also reported when the
L constant provably resolves to the same declaration with and without
L the base, i.e. nothing in the surrounding nesting shadows it.
S Example
L   # bad
L   ::Const
L 
L   # good
L   Const
L 
L   # bad
L   class << self
L     ::Const
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class << self
L     Const
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class A
L     ::Const
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   module A
L     ::Const
L   end
C Style/RedundantCurrentDirectoryInPath
S Details
L Checks for paths given to `require_relative` that start with
L the current directory (`./`), which can be omitted.
S Example
L   # bad
L   require_relative './path/to/feature'
L 
L   # good
L   require_relative 'path/to/feature'
C Style/RedundantDoubleSplatHashBraces
S Details
L Checks for redundant uses of double splat hash braces.
S Example
L   # bad
L   do_something(**{foo: bar, baz: qux})
L 
L   # good
L   do_something(foo: bar, baz: qux)
L 
L   # bad
L   do_something(**{foo: bar, baz: qux}.merge(options))
L 
L   # good
L   do_something(foo: bar, baz: qux, **options)
C Style/RedundantEach
S Details
L Checks for redundant `each`.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe, as it can produce false positives if the receiver
L   is not an `Enumerator`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   array.each.each { |v| do_something(v) }
L 
L   # good
L   array.each { |v| do_something(v) }
L 
L   # bad
L   array.each.each_with_index { |v, i| do_something(v, i) }
L 
L   # good
L   array.each.with_index { |v, i| do_something(v, i) }
L   array.each_with_index { |v, i| do_something(v, i) }
L 
L   # bad
L   array.each.each_with_object { |v, o| do_something(v, o) }
L 
L   # good
L   array.each.with_object { |v, o| do_something(v, o) }
L   array.each_with_object { |v, o| do_something(v, o) }
C Style/RedundantException
S Details
L Checks for `RuntimeError` as the argument of `raise`/`fail`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   raise RuntimeError, 'message'
L   raise RuntimeError.new('message')
L 
L   # good
L   raise 'message'
L 
L   # bad - message is not a string
L   raise RuntimeError, Object.new
L   raise RuntimeError.new(Object.new)
L 
L   # good
L   raise Object.new.to_s
C Style/RedundantFetchBlock
S Details
L Identifies places where `fetch(key) { value }` can be replaced by `fetch(key, value)`.
L 
L In such cases `fetch(key, value)` method is faster than `fetch(key) { value }`.
L 
L NOTE: The block string `'value'` in `hash.fetch(:key) { 'value' }` is detected
L when frozen string literal magic comment is enabled (i.e. `# frozen_string_literal: true`),
L but not when disabled.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because it cannot be guaranteed that the receiver
L   does not have a different implementation of `fetch`.
S Example: SafeForConstants: false (default)
L   # bad
L   hash.fetch(:key) { 5 }
L   hash.fetch(:key) { true }
L   hash.fetch(:key) { nil }
L   array.fetch(5) { :value }
L   ENV.fetch(:key) { 'value' }
L 
L   # good
L   hash.fetch(:key, 5)
L   hash.fetch(:key, true)
L   hash.fetch(:key, nil)
L   array.fetch(5, :value)
L   ENV.fetch(:key, 'value')
S Example: SafeForConstants: true
L   # bad
L   ENV.fetch(:key) { VALUE }
L 
L   # good
L   ENV.fetch(:key, VALUE)
C Style/RedundantFileExtensionInRequire
S Details
L Checks for the presence of superfluous `.rb` extension in
L the filename provided to `require` and `require_relative`.
L 
L NOTE: If the extension is omitted, Ruby tries adding '.rb', '.so',
L       and so on to the name until found. If the file named cannot be found,
L       a `LoadError` will be raised.
L       There is an edge case where `foo.so` file is loaded instead of a `LoadError`
L       if `foo.so` file exists when `require 'foo.rb'` will be changed to `require 'foo'`,
L       but that seems harmless.
S Example
L   # bad
L   require 'foo.rb'
L   require_relative '../foo.rb'
L 
L   # good
L   require 'foo'
L   require 'foo.so'
L   require_relative '../foo'
L   require_relative '../foo.so'
C Style/RedundantFilterChain
S Details
L Identifies usages of `any?`, `empty?` or `none?` predicate methods
L chained to `select`/`filter`/`find_all` and change them to use predicate method instead.
S Safety
L   This cop's autocorrection is unsafe because `array.select.any?` evaluates all elements
L   through the `select` method, while `array.any?` uses short-circuit evaluation.
L   In other words, `array.select.any?` guarantees the evaluation of every element,
L   but `array.any?` does not necessarily evaluate all of them.
S Example
L   # bad
L   arr.select { |x| x > 1 }.any?
L 
L   # good
L   arr.any? { |x| x > 1 }
L 
L   # bad
L   arr.select { |x| x > 1 }.empty?
L   arr.select { |x| x > 1 }.none?
L 
L   # good
L   arr.none? { |x| x > 1 }
L 
L   # good
L   relation.select(:name).any?
L   arr.select { |x| x > 1 }.any?(&:odd?)
S Example: AllCops:ActiveSupportExtensionsEnabled: false (default)
L   # good
L   arr.select { |x| x > 1 }.many?
L 
L   # good
L   arr.select { |x| x > 1 }.present?
S Example: AllCops:ActiveSupportExtensionsEnabled: true
L   # bad
L   arr.select { |x| x > 1 }.many?
L 
L   # good
L   arr.many? { |x| x > 1 }
L 
L   # bad
L   arr.select { |x| x > 1 }.present?
L 
L   # good
L   arr.any? { |x| x > 1 }
C Style/RedundantFormat
S Details
L Checks for calls to `Kernel#format` or `Kernel#sprintf` that are redundant.
L 
L Calling `format` with only a single string or constant argument is redundant,
L as it can be replaced by the string or constant itself.
L 
L Also looks for `format` calls where the arguments are literals that can be
L inlined into a string easily. This applies to the `%s`, `%d`, `%i`, `%u`, and
L `%f` format specifiers.
S Safety
L   This cop's autocorrection is unsafe because string object returned by
L   `format` and `sprintf` are never frozen. If `format('string')` is autocorrected to
L   `'string'`, `FrozenError` may occur when calling a destructive method like `String#<<`.
L   Consider using `'string'.dup` instead of `format('string')`.
L   Additionally, since the necessity of `dup` cannot be determined automatically,
L   this autocorrection is inherently unsafe.
L 
L   [source,ruby]
L   ----
L   # frozen_string_literal: true
L 
L   format('template').frozen? # => false
L   'template'.frozen?         # => true
L   ----
S Example
L   # bad
L   format('the quick brown fox jumps over the lazy dog.')
L   sprintf('the quick brown fox jumps over the lazy dog.')
L 
L   # good
L   'the quick brown fox jumps over the lazy dog.'
L 
L   # bad
L   format(MESSAGE)
L   sprintf(MESSAGE)
L 
L   # good
L   MESSAGE
L 
L   # bad
L   format('%s %s', 'foo', 'bar')
L   sprintf('%s %s', 'foo', 'bar')
L 
L   # good
L   'foo bar'
C Style/RedundantFreeze
S Details
L Checks for uses of `Object#freeze` on immutable objects.
L 
L NOTE: `Regexp` and `Range` literals are frozen objects since Ruby 3.0.
L 
L NOTE: From Ruby 3.0, this cop allows explicit freezing of interpolated
L string literals when `# frozen-string-literal: true` is used.
S Example
L   # bad
L   CONST = 1.freeze
L 
L   # good
L   CONST = 1
C Style/RedundantHeredocDelimiterQuotes
S Details
L Checks for redundant heredoc delimiter quotes.
S Example
L   # bad
L   do_something(<<~'EOS')
L     no string interpolation style text
L   EOS
L 
L   # good
L   do_something(<<~EOS)
L     no string interpolation style text
L   EOS
L 
L   do_something(<<~'EOS')
L     #{string_interpolation_style_text_not_evaluated}
L   EOS
L 
L   do_something(<<~'EOS')
L     Preserve \
L     newlines
L   EOS
C Style/RedundantInitialize
S Details
L Checks for `initialize` methods that are redundant.
L 
L An initializer is redundant if it does not do anything, or if it only
L calls `super` with the same arguments given to it. If the initializer takes
L an argument that accepts multiple values (`restarg`, `kwrestarg`, etc.) it
L will not register an offense, because it allows the initializer to take a different
L number of arguments as its superclass potentially does.
L 
L NOTE: If an initializer takes any arguments and has an empty body, RuboCop
L assumes it to *not* be redundant. This is to prevent potential `ArgumentError`.
L 
L NOTE: If an initializer argument has a default value, RuboCop assumes it
L to *not* be redundant.
L 
L NOTE: Empty initializers are registered as offenses, but it is possible
L to purposely create an empty `initialize` method to override a superclass's
L initializer.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because removing an empty initializer may alter
L   the behavior of the code, particularly if the superclass initializer
L   raises an exception. In such cases, the empty initializer may act as
L   a safeguard to prevent unintended errors from propagating.
S Example
L   # bad
L   def initialize
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   def initialize
L     super
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   def initialize(a, b)
L     super
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   def initialize(a, b)
L     super(a, b)
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def initialize
L     do_something
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def initialize
L     do_something
L     super
L   end
L 
L   # good (different number of parameters)
L   def initialize(a, b)
L     super(a)
L   end
L 
L   # good (default value)
L   def initialize(a, b = 5)
L     super
L   end
L 
L   # good (default value)
L   def initialize(a, b: 5)
L     super
L   end
L 
L   # good (changes the parameter requirements)
L   def initialize(_)
L   end
L 
L   # good (changes the parameter requirements)
L   def initialize(*)
L   end
L 
L   # good (changes the parameter requirements)
L   def initialize(**)
L   end
L 
L   # good (changes the parameter requirements)
L   def initialize(...)
L   end
S Example: AllowComments: true (default)
L   # good
L   def initialize
L     # Overriding to negate superclass `initialize` method.
L   end
S Example: AllowComments: false
L   # bad
L   def initialize
L     # Overriding to negate superclass `initialize` method.
L   end
C Style/RedundantInterpolation
S Details
L Checks for strings that are just an interpolated expression.
S Safety
L   Autocorrection is unsafe because when calling a destructive method to string,
L   the resulting string may have different behavior or raise `FrozenError`.
L 
L   [source,ruby]
L   ----
L   x = 'a'
L   y = "#{x}"
L   y << 'b'   # return 'ab'
L   x          # return 'a'
L   y = x.to_s
L   y << 'b'   # return 'ab'
L   x          # return 'ab'
L 
L   x = 'a'.freeze
L   y = "#{x}"
L   y << 'b'   # return 'ab'.
L   y = x.to_s
L   y << 'b'   # raise `FrozenError`.
L   ----
S Example
L   # bad
L   "#{@var}"
L 
L   # good
L   @var.to_s
L 
L   # good if @var is already a String
L   @var
C Style/RedundantInterpolationUnfreeze
S Details
L Before Ruby 3.0, interpolated strings followed the frozen string literal
L magic comment which sometimes made it necessary to explicitly unfreeze them.
L Ruby 3.0 changed interpolated strings to always be unfrozen which makes
L unfreezing them redundant.
S Example
L   # bad
L   +"#{foo} bar"
L 
L   # bad
L   "#{foo} bar".dup
L 
L   # bad
L   String.new("#{foo} bar")
L 
L   # good
L   "#{foo} bar"
C Style/RedundantLineContinuation
S Details
L Checks for redundant line continuation.
L 
L A line continuation is redundant when removing the backslash does not
L change how the program parses: the source is reparsed without the
L backslash and the resulting AST is compared to the original. Only
L backslashes that are pure noise are reported; backslashes that are
L significant — inside strings, for string concatenation, before an
L operator or argument that would otherwise start a new statement, and
L so on — are left alone, as are backslashes in comments.
S Example
L   # bad
L   foo. \
L     bar
L   foo \
L     &.bar \
L       .baz
L 
L   # good
L   foo.
L     bar
L   foo
L     &.bar
L       .baz
L 
L   # bad
L   [foo, \
L     bar]
L   {foo: \
L     bar}
L 
L   # good
L   [foo,
L     bar]
L   {foo:
L     bar}
L 
L   # bad
L   foo(bar, \
L     baz)
L 
L   # good
L   foo(bar,
L     baz)
L 
L   # also good - backslash in string concatenation is not redundant
L   foo('bar' \
L     'baz')
L 
L   # also good - backslash at the end of a comment is not redundant
L   foo(bar, # \
L     baz)
L 
L   # also good - backslash at the line following the newline begins with a + or -,
L   # it is not redundant
L   1 \
L     + 2 \
L       - 3
L 
L   # also good - backslash with newline between the method name and its arguments,
L   # it is not redundant.
L   some_method \
L     (argument)
C Style/RedundantMinMaxBy
S Details
L Identifies places where `max_by { ... }`, `min_by { ... }`, or
L `minmax_by { ... }` can be replaced by `max`, `min`, or `minmax`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   array.max_by { |x| x }
L   array.min_by { |x| x }
L   array.minmax_by { |x| x }
L 
L   # good
L   array.max
L   array.min
L   array.minmax
C Style/RedundantParentheses
S Details
L Checks for redundant parentheses.
S Example
L   # bad
L   (x) if ((y.z).nil?)
L 
L   # good
L   x if y.z.nil?
C Style/RedundantPercentQ
S Details
L Checks for usage of the %q/%Q syntax when '' or "" would do.
S Example
L   # bad
L   name = %q(Bruce Wayne)
L   time = %q(8 o'clock)
L   question = %q("What did you say?")
L 
L   # good
L   name = 'Bruce Wayne'
L   time = "8 o'clock"
L   question = '"What did you say?"'
C Style/RedundantRegexpArgument
S Details
L Identifies places where argument can be replaced from
L a deterministic regexp to a string.
S Example
L   # bad
L   'foo'.byteindex(/f/)
L   'foo'.byterindex(/f/)
L   'foo'.gsub(/f/, 'x')
L   'foo'.gsub!(/f/, 'x')
L   'foo'.partition(/f/)
L   'foo'.rpartition(/f/)
L   'foo'.scan(/f/)
L   'foo'.split(/f/)
L   'foo'.start_with?(/f/)
L   'foo'.sub(/f/, 'x')
L   'foo'.sub!(/f/, 'x')
L 
L   # good
L   'foo'.byteindex('f')
L   'foo'.byterindex('f')
L   'foo'.gsub('f', 'x')
L   'foo'.gsub!('f', 'x')
L   'foo'.partition('f')
L   'foo'.rpartition('f')
L   'foo'.scan('f')
L   'foo'.split('f')
L   'foo'.start_with?('f')
L   'foo'.sub('f', 'x')
L   'foo'.sub!('f', 'x')
C Style/RedundantRegexpCharacterClass
S Details
L Checks for unnecessary single-element `Regexp` character classes.
S Example
L   # bad
L   r = /[x]/
L 
L   # good
L   r = /x/
L 
L   # bad
L   r = /[\s]/
L 
L   # good
L   r = /\s/
L 
L   # bad
L   r = %r{/[b]}
L 
L   # good
L   r = %r{/b}
L 
L   # good
L   r = /[ab]/
C Style/RedundantRegexpConstructor
S Details
L Checks for the instantiation of a regexp using a redundant `Regexp.new` or `Regexp.compile`.
L Autocorrect replaces it with a regexp literal which is the simplest and fastest.
S Example
L   # bad
L   Regexp.new(/regexp/)
L   Regexp.compile(/regexp/)
L 
L   # good
L   /regexp/
L   Regexp.new('regexp')
L   Regexp.compile('regexp')
C Style/RedundantRegexpEscape
S Details
L Checks for redundant escapes inside `Regexp` literals.
S Example
L   # bad
L   %r{foo\/bar}
L 
L   # good
L   %r{foo/bar}
L 
L   # good
L   /foo\/bar/
L 
L   # good
L   %r/foo\/bar/
L 
L   # good
L   %r!foo\!bar!
L 
L   # bad
L   /a\-b/
L 
L   # good
L   /a-b/
L 
L   # bad
L   /[\+\-]\d/
L 
L   # good
L   /[+\-]\d/
C Style/RedundantReturn
S Details
L Checks for redundant `return` expressions. Ruby methods
L implicitly return the value of the last evaluated expression,
L so an explicit `return` at the end of a method body is unnecessary.
S Example
L   # These bad cases should be extended to handle methods whose body is
L   # if/else or a case expression with a default branch.
L 
L   # bad
L   def test
L     return something
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   def test
L     one
L     two
L     three
L     return something
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   def test
L     return something if something_else
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def test
L     something if something_else
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def test
L     if x
L     elsif y
L     else
L     end
L   end
S Example: AllowMultipleReturnValues: false (default)
L   # bad
L   def test
L     return x, y
L   end
S Example: AllowMultipleReturnValues: true
L   # good
L   def test
L     return x, y
L   end
C Style/RedundantSelf
S Details
L Checks for redundant uses of `self`.
L 
L The usage of `self` is only needed when:
L 
L * Sending a message to same object with zero arguments in
L   presence of a method name clash with an argument or a local
L   variable.
L 
L * Calling an attribute writer to prevent a local variable assignment.
L 
L Note, with using explicit self you can only send messages with public or
L protected scope, you cannot send private messages this way.
L 
L Note we allow uses of `self` with operators because it would be awkward
L otherwise. Also allows the use of `self.it` without arguments in blocks,
L as in `0.times { self.it }`, following `Lint/ItWithoutArgumentsInBlock` cop.
S Example
L   # bad
L   def foo(bar)
L     self.baz
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo(bar)
L     self.bar  # Resolves name clash with the argument.
L   end
L 
L   def foo
L     bar = 1
L     self.bar  # Resolves name clash with the local variable.
L   end
L 
L   def foo
L     %w[x y z].select do |bar|
L       self.bar == bar  # Resolves name clash with argument of the block.
L     end
L   end
C Style/RedundantSelfAssignment
S Details
L Checks for places where redundant assignments are made for in place
L modification methods.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe, because it can produce false positives for
L   user defined methods having one of the expected names, but not modifying
L   its receiver in place.
S Example
L   # bad
L   args = args.concat(ary)
L   hash = hash.merge!(other)
L 
L   # good
L   args.concat(foo)
L   args += foo
L   hash.merge!(other)
L 
L   # good
L   foo.concat(ary)
C Style/RedundantSelfAssignmentBranch
S Details
