L Checks for places where conditional branch makes redundant self-assignment.
L 
L It only detects local variable because it may replace state of instance variable,
L class variable, and global variable that have state across methods with `nil`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   foo = condition ? bar : foo
L 
L   # good
L   foo = bar if condition
L 
L   # bad
L   foo = condition ? foo : bar
L 
L   # good
L   foo = bar unless condition
C Style/RedundantSort
S Details
L Identifies instances of sorting and then
L taking only the first or last element. The same behavior can
L be accomplished without a relatively expensive sort by using
L `Enumerable#min` instead of sorting and taking the first
L element and `Enumerable#max` instead of sorting and taking the
L last element. Similarly, `Enumerable#min_by` and
L `Enumerable#max_by` can replace `Enumerable#sort_by` calls
L after which only the first or last element is used.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe, because `sort...last` and `max` may not return the
L   same element in all cases.
L 
L   In an enumerable where there are multiple elements where ``a <=> b == 0``,
L   or where the transformation done by the `sort_by` block has the
L   same result, `sort.last` and `max` (or `sort_by.last` and `max_by`)
L   will return different elements. `sort.last` will return the last
L   element but `max` will return the first element.
L 
L   For example:
L 
L   [source,ruby]
L   ----
L   class MyString < String; end
L   strings = [MyString.new('test'), 'test']
L   strings.sort.last.class #=> String
L   strings.max.class       #=> MyString
L   ----
L 
L   [source,ruby]
L   ----
L   words = %w(dog horse mouse)
L   words.sort_by { |word| word.length }.last #=> 'mouse'
L   words.max_by { |word| word.length }       #=> 'horse'
L   ----
S Example
L   # bad
L   [2, 1, 3].sort.first
L   [2, 1, 3].sort[0]
L   [2, 1, 3].sort.at(0)
L   [2, 1, 3].sort.slice(0)
L 
L   # good
L   [2, 1, 3].min
L 
L   # bad
L   [2, 1, 3].sort.last
L   [2, 1, 3].sort[-1]
L   [2, 1, 3].sort.at(-1)
L   [2, 1, 3].sort.slice(-1)
L 
L   # good
L   [2, 1, 3].max
L 
L   # bad
L   arr.sort_by(&:foo).first
L   arr.sort_by(&:foo)[0]
L   arr.sort_by(&:foo).at(0)
L   arr.sort_by(&:foo).slice(0)
L 
L   # good
L   arr.min_by(&:foo)
L 
L   # bad
L   arr.sort_by(&:foo).last
L   arr.sort_by(&:foo)[-1]
L   arr.sort_by(&:foo).at(-1)
L   arr.sort_by(&:foo).slice(-1)
L 
L   # good
L   arr.max_by(&:foo)
C Style/RedundantSortBy
S Details
L Identifies places where `sort_by { ... }` can be replaced by
L `sort`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   array.sort_by { |x| x }
L   array.sort_by do |var|
L     var
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   array.sort
C Style/RedundantStringEscape
S Details
L Checks for redundant escapes in string literals.
S Example
L   # bad - no need to escape # without following {/$/@
L   "\#foo"
L 
L   # bad - no need to escape single quotes inside double quoted string
L   "\'foo\'"
L 
L   # bad - heredocs are also checked for unnecessary escapes
L   <<~STR
L     \#foo \"foo\"
L   STR
L 
L   # good
L   "#foo"
L 
L   # good
L   "\#{no_interpolation}"
L 
L   # good
L   "'foo'"
L 
L   # good
L   "foo\
L   bar"
L 
L   # good
L   <<~STR
L     #foo "foo"
L   STR
C Style/RedundantStructKeywordInit
S Details
L Checks for redundant `keyword_init` option for `Struct.new`.
L 
L Since Ruby 3.2, `keyword_init` in `Struct.new` defaults to `nil` behavior.
L Therefore, this cop detects and autocorrects redundant `keyword_init: nil`
L and `keyword_init: true` in `Struct.new`.
L 
L This cop is disabled by default because `keyword_init: true` is not purely
L redundant. It changes behavior in the following ways:
L 
L - `Struct#keyword_init?` returns `true` instead of `nil`.
L - A `Struct` with `keyword_init: true` accepts a `Hash` argument and
L   expands it as keyword arguments, whereas without it the `Hash` is
L   treated as a positional argument.
L - `keyword_init: true` raises an `ArgumentError` for positional arguments,
L   enforcing keyword-only initialization.
S Safety
L   This autocorrect is unsafe because when the value of `keyword_init` changes
L   from `true` to `nil`, the return value of `Struct#keyword_init?` changes.
S Example
L   # bad
L   Struct.new(:foo, keyword_init: nil)
L   Struct.new(:foo, keyword_init: true)
L 
L   # good
L   Struct.new(:foo)
C Style/RegexpLiteral
S Details
L Enforces using `//` or `%r` around regular expressions.
L 
L NOTE: The following `%r` cases using a regexp that starts with a blank or `=`
L as a method argument are allowed to prevent syntax errors.
L 
L [source,ruby]
L ----
L do_something %r{ regexp} # `do_something / regexp/` is an invalid syntax.
L do_something %r{=regexp} # `do_something /=regexp/` is an invalid syntax.
L ----
S Example: EnforcedStyle: slashes (default)
L   # bad
L   snake_case = %r{^[\dA-Z_]+$}
L 
L   # bad
L   regex = %r{
L     foo
L     (bar)
L     (baz)
L   }x
L 
L   # good
L   snake_case = /^[\dA-Z_]+$/
L 
L   # good
L   regex = /
L     foo
L     (bar)
L     (baz)
L   /x
S Example: EnforcedStyle: percent_r
L   # bad
L   snake_case = /^[\dA-Z_]+$/
L 
L   # bad
L   regex = /
L     foo
L     (bar)
L     (baz)
L   /x
L 
L   # good
L   snake_case = %r{^[\dA-Z_]+$}
L 
L   # good
L   regex = %r{
L     foo
L     (bar)
L     (baz)
L   }x
S Example: EnforcedStyle: mixed
L   # bad
L   snake_case = %r{^[\dA-Z_]+$}
L 
L   # bad
L   regex = /
L     foo
L     (bar)
L     (baz)
L   /x
L 
L   # good
L   snake_case = /^[\dA-Z_]+$/
L 
L   # good
L   regex = %r{
L     foo
L     (bar)
L     (baz)
L   }x
S Example: AllowInnerSlashes: false (default)
L   # If `false`, the cop will always recommend using `%r` if one or more
L   # slashes are found in the regexp string.
L 
L   # bad
L   x =~ /home\//
L 
L   # good
L   x =~ %r{home/}
S Example: AllowInnerSlashes: true
L   # good
L   x =~ /home\//
C Style/RequireOrder
S Details
L Sort `require` and `require_relative` in alphabetical order.
S Safety
L   This cop's autocorrection is unsafe because it will obviously change the execution order.
S Example
L   # bad
L   require 'b'
L   require 'a'
L 
L   # good
L   require 'a'
L   require 'b'
L 
L   # bad
L   require_relative 'b'
L   require_relative 'a'
L 
L   # good
L   require_relative 'a'
L   require_relative 'b'
L 
L   # good (sorted within each section separated by a blank line)
L   require 'a'
L   require 'd'
L 
L   require 'b'
L   require 'c'
L 
L   # good
L   require 'b'
L   require_relative 'c'
L   require 'a'
L 
L   # bad
L   require 'a'
L   require 'c' if foo
L   require 'b'
L 
L   # good
L   require 'a'
L   require 'b'
L   require 'c' if foo
L 
L   # bad
L   require 'c'
L   if foo
L     require 'd'
L     require 'b'
L   end
L   require 'a'
L 
L   # good
L   require 'c'
L   if foo
L     require 'b'
L     require 'd'
L   end
L   require 'a'
C Style/RescueModifier
S Details
L Checks for uses of `rescue` in its modifier form. It is added for the following
L reasons:
L 
L * The syntax of modifier form `rescue` can be misleading because it
L   might lead us to believe that `rescue` handles the given exception
L   but it actually rescues all exceptions to return the given rescue
L   block. In this case, value returned by handle_error or
L   SomeException.
L 
L * Modifier form `rescue` would rescue all the exceptions. It would
L   silently skip all exceptions or errors and handle the error.
L   Example: If `NoMethodError` is raised, modifier form rescue would
L   handle the exception.
S Example
L   # bad
L   some_method rescue handle_error
L 
L   # bad
L   some_method rescue SomeException
L 
L   # good
L   begin
L     some_method
L   rescue
L     handle_error
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   begin
L     some_method
L   rescue SomeException
L     handle_error
L   end
C Style/RescueStandardError
S Details
L Checks for rescuing `StandardError`. There are two supported
L styles `implicit` and `explicit`. This cop will not register an offense
L if any error other than `StandardError` is specified.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: explicit (default)
L   # `explicit` will enforce using `rescue StandardError`
L   # instead of `rescue`.
L 
L   # bad
L   begin
L     foo
L   rescue
L     bar
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   begin
L     foo
L   rescue StandardError
L     bar
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   begin
L     foo
L   rescue OtherError
L     bar
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   begin
L     foo
L   rescue StandardError, SecurityError
L     bar
L   end
S Example: EnforcedStyle: implicit
L   # `implicit` will enforce using `rescue` instead of
L   # `rescue StandardError`.
L 
L   # bad
L   begin
L     foo
L   rescue StandardError
L     bar
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   begin
L     foo
L   rescue
L     bar
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   begin
L     foo
L   rescue OtherError
L     bar
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   begin
L     foo
L   rescue StandardError, SecurityError
L     bar
L   end
C Style/ReturnNil
S Details
L Enforces consistency between `return nil` and `return`.
L 
L This cop is disabled by default. Because there seems to be a perceived semantic difference
L between `return` and `return nil`. The former can be seen as just halting evaluation,
L while the latter might be used when the return value is of specific concern.
L 
L Supported styles are `return` and `return_nil`.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: return (default)
L   # bad
L   def foo(arg)
L     return nil if arg
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo(arg)
L     return if arg
L   end
S Example: EnforcedStyle: return_nil
L   # bad
L   def foo(arg)
L     return if arg
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo(arg)
L     return nil if arg
L   end
C Style/ReturnNilInPredicateMethodDefinition
S Details
L Checks for predicate method definitions that return `nil`.
L A predicate method should only return a boolean value.
S Safety
L   Autocorrection is marked as unsafe because the change of the return value
L   from `nil` to `false` could potentially lead to incompatibility issues.
S Example
L   # bad
L   def foo?
L     return if condition
L 
L     do_something?
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   def foo?
L     return nil if condition
L 
L     do_something?
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo?
L     return false if condition
L 
L     do_something?
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   def foo?
L     if condition
L       nil
L     else
L       true
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def foo?
L     if condition
L       false
L     else
L       true
L     end
L   end
S Example: AllowedMethods: ['foo?']
L   # good
L   def foo?
L     return if condition
L 
L     do_something?
L   end
S Example: AllowedPatterns: [/foo/]
L   # good
L   def foo?
L     return if condition
L 
L     do_something?
L   end
C Style/ReverseFind
S Details
L Identifies places where `array.reverse.find` can be replaced by `array.rfind`.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because it cannot be guaranteed that the receiver
L   is an `Array` or responds to the replacement method.
S Example
L   # bad
L   array.reverse.find { |item| item.even? }
L   array.reverse.detect { |item| item.even? }
L   array.reverse_each.find { |item| item.even? }
L   array.reverse_each.detect { |item| item.even? }
L 
L   # good
L   array.rfind { |item| item.even? }
C Style/SafeNavigation
S Details
L Transforms usages of a method call safeguarded by a non `nil`
L check for the variable whose method is being called to
L safe navigation (`&.`). If there is a method chain, all of the methods
L in the chain need to be checked for safety, and all of the methods will
L need to be changed to use safe navigation.
L 
L The default for `ConvertCodeThatCanStartToReturnNil` is `false`.
L When configured to `true`, this will
L check for code in the format `!foo.nil? && foo.bar`. As it is written,
L the return of this code is limited to `false` and whatever the return
L of the method is. If this is converted to safe navigation,
L `foo&.bar` can start returning `nil` as well as what the method
L returns.
L 
L The default for `MaxChainLength` is `2`.
L We have limited the cop to not register an offense for method chains
L that exceed this option's value.
L 
L NOTE: This cop will recognize offenses but not autocorrect code when the
L right hand side (RHS) of the `&&` statement is an `||` statement
L (eg. `foo && (foo.bar? || foo.baz?)`). It can be corrected
L manually by removing the `foo &&` and adding `&.` to each `foo` on the RHS.
S Safety
L   Autocorrection is unsafe because if a value is `false`, the resulting
L   code will have different behavior or raise an error.
L 
L   [source,ruby]
L   ----
L   x = false
L   x && x.foo  # return false
L   x&.foo      # raises NoMethodError
L   ----
L 
L   Additionally, when a method chain is converted, a `NoMethodError` that
L   the original code raised for an intermediate `nil` value is suppressed:
L 
L   [source,ruby]
L   ----
L   x = Struct.new(:foo).new(nil)
L   x && x.foo.bar  # raises NoMethodError
L   x&.foo&.bar     # returns nil
L   ----
S Example
L   # bad
L   foo.bar if foo
L   foo.bar.baz if foo
L   foo.bar(param1, param2) if foo
L   foo.bar { |e| e.something } if foo
L   foo.bar(param) { |e| e.something } if foo
L 
L   foo.bar if !foo.nil?
L   foo.bar unless !foo
L   foo.bar unless foo.nil?
L 
L   foo && foo.bar
L   foo && foo.bar.baz
L   foo && foo.bar(param1, param2)
L   foo && foo.bar { |e| e.something }
L   foo && foo.bar(param) { |e| e.something }
L 
L   foo ? foo.bar : nil
L   foo.nil? ? nil : foo.bar
L   !foo.nil? ? foo.bar : nil
L   !foo ? nil : foo.bar
L 
L   # good
L   foo&.bar
L   foo&.bar&.baz
L   foo&.bar(param1, param2)
L   foo&.bar { |e| e.something }
L   foo&.bar(param) { |e| e.something }
L   foo && foo.bar.baz.qux # method chain with more than 2 methods
L   foo && foo.nil? # method that `nil` responds to
L 
L   # Method calls that do not use `.`
L   foo && foo < bar
L   foo < bar if foo
L 
L   # When checking `foo&.empty?` in a conditional, `foo` being `nil` will actually
L   # do the opposite of what the author intends.
L   foo && foo.empty?
L 
L   # This could start returning `nil` as well as the return of the method
L   foo.nil? || foo.bar
L   !foo || foo.bar
L 
L   # Methods that are used on assignment, arithmetic operation or
L   # comparison should not be converted to use safe navigation
L   foo.baz = bar if foo
L   foo.baz + bar if foo
L   foo.bar > 2 if foo
L 
L   foo ? foo[index] : nil    # Ignored `foo&.[](index)` due to unclear readability benefit.
L   foo ? foo[idx] = v : nil  # Ignored `foo&.[]=(idx, v)` due to unclear readability benefit.
L   foo ? foo * 42 : nil      # Ignored `foo&.*(42)` due to unclear readability benefit.
C Style/SafeNavigationChainLength
S Details
L Enforces safe navigation chains length to not exceed the configured maximum.
L The longer the chain is, the harder it becomes to track what on it could be
L returning `nil`.
L 
L There is a potential interplay with `Style/SafeNavigation` - if both are enabled
L and their settings are "incompatible", one of the cops will complain about what
L the other proposes.
L 
L E.g. if `Style/SafeNavigation` is configured with `MaxChainLength: 2` (default)
L and this cop is configured with `Max: 1`, then for `foo.bar.baz if foo` the former
L will suggest `foo&.bar&.baz`, which is an offense for the latter.
S Example: Max: 2 (default)
L   # bad
L   user&.address&.zip&.upcase
L 
L   # good
L   user&.address&.zip
L   user.address.zip if user
C Style/Sample
S Details
L Identifies usages of `shuffle.first`,
L `shuffle.last`, and `shuffle[]` and change them to use
L `sample` instead.
L 
L NOTE: An offense involving a `random:` argument is registered but not autocorrected:
L `shuffle` and `sample` consume the given generator differently, so for a seeded generator
L the correction would select different elements.
S Example
L   # bad
L   [1, 2, 3].shuffle.first
L   [1, 2, 3].shuffle.first(2)
L   [1, 2, 3].shuffle.last
L   [2, 1, 3].shuffle.at(0)
L   [2, 1, 3].shuffle.slice(0)
L   [1, 2, 3].shuffle[2]
L   [1, 2, 3].shuffle[0, 2]    # sample(2) will do the same
L   [1, 2, 3].shuffle[0..2]    # sample(3) will do the same
L   [1, 2, 3].shuffle(random: Random.new).first
L 
L   # good
L   [1, 2, 3].shuffle
L   [1, 2, 3].sample
L   [1, 2, 3].sample(3)
L   [1, 2, 3].shuffle[1, 3]    # sample(3) might return a longer Array
L   [1, 2, 3].shuffle[1..3]    # sample(3) might return a longer Array
L   [1, 2, 3].shuffle[foo, bar]
L   [1, 2, 3].shuffle(random: Random.new)
C Style/SelectByKind
S Details
L Looks for places where a subset of an Enumerable (array,
L range, set, etc.; see note below) is calculated based on a class type
L check, and suggests `grep` or `grep_v` instead.
L 
L NOTE: Hashes do not behave as you may expect with `grep`, which
L means that `hash.grep` is not equivalent to `hash.select`. Although
L RuboCop is limited by static analysis, this cop attempts to avoid
L registering an offense when the receiver is a hash (hash literal,
L `Hash.new`, `Hash#[]`, or `to_h`/`to_hash`).
S Safety
L   Autocorrection is marked as unsafe because the cop cannot guarantee
L   that the receiver is actually an array by static analysis, so the
L   correction may not be actually equivalent.
S Example
L   # bad (select or find_all)
L   array.select { |x| x.is_a?(Foo) }
L   array.select { |x| x.kind_of?(Foo) }
L 
L   # bad (reject)
L   array.reject { |x| x.is_a?(Foo) }
L 
L   # bad (negative form)
L   array.reject { |x| !x.is_a?(Foo) }
L 
L   # good
L   array.grep(Foo)
L   array.grep_v(Foo)
C Style/SelectByRange
S Details
L Looks for places where a subset of an Enumerable (array,
L range, set, etc.; see note below) is calculated based on a range
L check, and suggests `grep` or `grep_v` instead.
L 
L NOTE: Hashes do not behave as you may expect with `grep`, which
L means that `hash.grep` is not equivalent to `hash.select`. Although
L RuboCop is limited by static analysis, this cop attempts to avoid
L registering an offense when the receiver is a hash (hash literal,
L `Hash.new`, `Hash#[]`, or `to_h`/`to_hash`).
S Safety
L   Autocorrection is marked as unsafe because the cop cannot guarantee
L   that the receiver is actually an array by static analysis, so the
L   correction may not be actually equivalent.
S Example
L   # bad (select or find_all)
L   array.select { |x| x.between?(1, 10) }
L   array.select { |x| (1..10).cover?(x) }
L   array.select { |x| (1..10).include?(x) }
L 
L   # bad (reject)
L   array.reject { |x| x.between?(1, 10) }
L 
L   # bad (find or detect)
L   array.find { |x| x.between?(1, 10) }
L   array.detect { |x| (1..10).cover?(x) }
L 
L   # bad (negative form)
L   array.reject { |x| !x.between?(1, 10) }
L   array.find { |x| !(1..10).cover?(x) }
L 
L   # good
L   array.grep(1..10)
L   array.grep_v(1..10)
L   array.grep(1..10).first
L   array.grep_v(1..10).first
C Style/SelectByRegexp
S Details
L Looks for places where a subset of an Enumerable (array,
L range, set, etc.; see note below) is calculated based on a `Regexp`
L match, and suggests `grep` or `grep_v` instead.
L 
L NOTE: Hashes do not behave as you may expect with `grep`, which
L means that `hash.grep` is not equivalent to `hash.select`. Although
L RuboCop is limited by static analysis, this cop attempts to avoid
L registering an offense when the receiver is a hash (hash literal,
L `Hash.new`, `Hash#[]`, or `to_h`/`to_hash`).
L 
L NOTE: `grep` and `grep_v` were optimized when used without a block
L in Ruby 3.0, but may be slower in previous versions.
L See https://bugs.ruby-lang.org/issues/17030
S Safety
L   Autocorrection is marked as unsafe because `MatchData` will
L   not be created by `grep`, but may have previously been relied
L   upon after the `match?` or `=~` call.
L 
L   Additionally, the cop cannot guarantee that the receiver of
L   `select` or `reject` is actually an array by static analysis,
L   so the correction may not be actually equivalent.
S Example
L   # bad (select, filter, or find_all)
L   array.select { |x| x.match? /regexp/ }
L   array.select { |x| /regexp/.match?(x) }
L   array.select { |x| x =~ /regexp/ }
L   array.select { |x| /regexp/ =~ x }
L 
L   # bad (reject)
L   array.reject { |x| x.match? /regexp/ }
L   array.reject { |x| /regexp/.match?(x) }
L   array.reject { |x| x =~ /regexp/ }
L   array.reject { |x| /regexp/ =~ x }
L 
L   # bad (negative form)
L   array.reject { |x| !x.match? /regexp/ }
L 
L   # good
L   array.grep(regexp)
L   array.grep_v(regexp)
C Style/SelfAssignment
S Details
L Enforces the use of the shorthand for self-assignment.
S Example
L   # bad
L   x = x + 1
L 
L   # good
L   x += 1
C Style/Semicolon
S Details
L Checks for multiple expressions placed on the same line.
L It also checks for lines terminated with a semicolon.
L In idiomatic Ruby, each expression should be on its own line
L for readability.
L 
L This cop has `AllowAsExpressionSeparator` configuration option.
L It allows `;` to separate several expressions on the same line.
S Example
L   # bad
L   foo = 1; bar = 2;
L   baz = 3;
L 
L   # good
L   foo = 1
L   bar = 2
L   baz = 3
S Example: AllowAsExpressionSeparator: false (default)
L   # bad
L   foo = 1; bar = 2
S Example: AllowAsExpressionSeparator: true
L   # good
L   foo = 1; bar = 2
C Style/Send
S Details
L Checks for the use of the send method.
S Example
L   # bad
L   Foo.send(bar)
L   quuz.send(fred)
L 
L   # good
L   Foo.__send__(bar)
L   quuz.public_send(fred)
C Style/SendWithLiteralMethodName
S Details
L Detects the use of the `public_send` method with a literal method name argument.
L Since the `send` method can be used to call private methods, by default,
L only the `public_send` method is detected.
L 
L NOTE: Writer methods with names ending in `=` are always permitted because their
L behavior differs as follows:
L 
L [source,ruby]
L ----
L def foo=(foo)
L   @foo = foo
L   42
L end
L 
L self.foo = 1   # => 1
L send(:foo=, 1) # => 42
L ----
S Safety
L   This cop is not safe because it can incorrectly detect based on the receiver.
L   Additionally, when `AllowSend` is set to `true`, it cannot determine whether
L   the `send` method being detected is calling a private method.
S Example
L   # bad
L   obj.public_send(:method_name)
L   obj.public_send('method_name')
L 
L   # good
L   obj.method_name
S Example: AllowSend: true (default)
L   # good
L   obj.send(:method_name)
L   obj.send('method_name')
L   obj.__send__(:method_name)
L   obj.__send__('method_name')
S Example: AllowSend: false
L   # bad
L   obj.send(:method_name)
L   obj.send('method_name')
L   obj.__send__(:method_name)
L   obj.__send__('method_name')
L 
L   # good
L   obj.method_name
C Style/SignalException
S Details
L Checks for uses of `fail` and `raise`.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: only_raise (default)
L   # The `only_raise` style enforces the sole use of `raise`.
L   # bad
L   begin
L     fail
L   rescue Exception
L     # handle it
L   end
L 
L   def watch_out
L     fail
L   rescue Exception
L     # handle it
L   end
L 
L   Kernel.fail
L 
L   # good
L   begin
L     raise
L   rescue Exception
L     # handle it
L   end
L 
L   def watch_out
L     raise
L   rescue Exception
L     # handle it
L   end
L 
L   Kernel.raise
S Example: EnforcedStyle: only_fail
L   # The `only_fail` style enforces the sole use of `fail`.
L   # bad
L   begin
L     raise
L   rescue Exception
L     # handle it
L   end
L 
L   def watch_out
L     raise
L   rescue Exception
L     # handle it
L   end
L 
L   Kernel.raise
L 
L   # good
L   begin
L     fail
L   rescue Exception
L     # handle it
L   end
L 
L   def watch_out
L     fail
L   rescue Exception
L     # handle it
L   end
L 
L   Kernel.fail
S Example: EnforcedStyle: semantic
L   # The `semantic` style enforces the use of `fail` to signal an
L   # exception, then will use `raise` to trigger an offense after
L   # it has been rescued.
L   # bad
L   begin
L     raise
L   rescue Exception
L     # handle it
L   end
L 
L   def watch_out
L     # Error thrown
L   rescue Exception
L     fail
L   end
L 
L   Kernel.fail
L   Kernel.raise
L 
L   # good
L   begin
L     fail
L   rescue Exception
L     # handle it
L   end
L 
L   def watch_out
L     fail
L   rescue Exception
L     raise 'Preferably with descriptive message'
L   end
L 
L   explicit_receiver.fail
L   explicit_receiver.raise
C Style/SingleArgumentDig
S Details
L Sometimes using `dig` method ends up with just a single
L argument. In such cases, dig should be replaced with `[]`.
L 
L Since replacing `hash&.dig(:key)` with `hash[:key]` could potentially lead to error,
L calls to the `dig` method using safe navigation will be ignored.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because it cannot be guaranteed that the receiver
L   is an `Enumerable` or does not have a nonstandard implementation
L   of `dig`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   { key: 'value' }.dig(:key)
L   [1, 2, 3].dig(0)
L 
L   # good
L   { key: 'value' }[:key]
L   [1, 2, 3][0]
L 
L   # good
L   { key1: { key2: 'value' } }.dig(:key1, :key2)
L   [1, [2, [3]]].dig(1, 1)
L 
L   # good
L   keys = %i[key1 key2]
L   { key1: { key2: 'value' } }.dig(*keys)
C Style/SingleLineBlockParams
S Details
L Checks whether the block parameters of a single-line
L method accepting a block match the names specified via configuration.
L 
L For instance one can configure `reduce`(`inject`) to use |a, e| as
L parameters.
L 
L Configuration option: Methods
L Should be set to use this cop. `Array` of hashes, where each key is the
L method name and value - array of argument names.
S Example: Methods: [{reduce: %w[a b]}]
L   # bad
L   foo.reduce { |c, d| c + d }
L   foo.reduce { |_, _d| 1 }
L 
L   # good
L   foo.reduce { |a, b| a + b }
L   foo.reduce { |a, _b| a }
L   foo.reduce { |a, (id, _)| a + id }
L   foo.reduce { true }
L 
L   # good
L   foo.reduce do |c, d|
L     c + d
L   end
C Style/SingleLineDoEndBlock
S Details
L Checks for single-line `do`...`end` block.
L 
L In practice a single line `do`...`end` is autocorrected when `EnforcedStyle: semantic`
L is configured for `Style/BlockDelimiters`. The autocorrection maintains the
L `do` ... `end` syntax to preserve semantics and does not change it to `{`...`}` block.
L 
L NOTE: If `InspectBlocks` is set to `true` for `Layout/RedundantLineBreak`, blocks will
L be autocorrected to be on a single line if possible. This cop respects that configuration
L by not registering an offense if it would subsequently cause a
L `Layout/RedundantLineBreak` offense.
S Example
L   # bad
L   foo do |arg| bar(arg) end
L 
L   # good
L   foo do |arg|
L     bar(arg)
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   ->(arg) do bar(arg) end
L 
L   # good
L   ->(arg) { bar(arg) }
C Style/SingleLineMethods
S Details
L Checks for single-line method definitions that contain a body.
L Single-line methods with a body are harder to read and debug
L than their multi-line equivalents. It will accept single-line
L methods with no body.
L 
L Endless methods added in Ruby 3.0 are also accepted by this cop.
L 
L If `Style/EndlessMethod` is enabled with `EnforcedStyle: allow_single_line`, `allow_always`,
L `require_single_line`, or `require_always`, single-line methods will be autocorrected
L to endless methods if there is only one statement in the body.
S Example
L   # bad
L   def some_method; body end
L   def link_to(url); {:name => url}; end
L   def @table.columns; super; end
L 
L   # good
L   def self.resource_class=(klass); end
L   def @table.columns; end
L   def some_method() = body
S Example: AllowIfMethodIsEmpty: true (default)
L   # good
L   def no_op; end
S Example: AllowIfMethodIsEmpty: false
L   # bad
L   def no_op; end
C Style/SlicingWithRange
S Details
L Checks that arrays are not sliced with the redundant `ary[0..-1]`, replacing it with `ary`,
L and ensures arrays are sliced with endless ranges instead of `ary[start..-1]` on Ruby 2.6+,
L and with beginless ranges instead of `ary[nil..end]` on Ruby 2.7+.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because `x..-1` and `x..` are only guaranteed to
L   be equivalent for `Array#[]`, `String#[]`, and the cop cannot determine what class
L   the receiver is.
L 
L   For example:
L   [source,ruby]
L   ----
L   sum = proc { |ary| ary.sum }
L   sum[-3..-1] # => -6
L   sum[-3..] # Hangs forever
L   ----
S Example
L   # bad
L   items[0..-1]
L   items[0..nil]
L   items[0...nil]
L 
L   # good
L   items
L 
L   # bad
L   items[1..-1]   # Ruby 2.6+
L   items[1..nil]  # Ruby 2.6+
L 
L   # good
L   items[1..]     # Ruby 2.6+
L 
L   # bad
L   items[nil..42] # Ruby 2.7+
L 
L   # good
L   items[..42]    # Ruby 2.7+
L   items[0..42]   # Ruby 2.7+
C Style/SoleNestedConditional
S Details
L If the branch of a conditional consists solely of a conditional node,
L its conditions can be combined with the conditions of the outer branch.
L This helps to keep the nesting level from getting too deep.
S Example
L   # bad
L   if condition_a
L     if condition_b
L       do_something
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   if condition_b
L     do_something
L   end if condition_a
L 
L   # good
L   if condition_a && condition_b
L     do_something
L   end
S Example: AllowModifier: false (default)
L   # bad
L   if condition_a
L     do_something if condition_b
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   if condition_b
L     do_something
L   end if condition_a
S Example: AllowModifier: true
L   # good
L   if condition_a
L     do_something if condition_b
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   if condition_b
L     do_something
L   end if condition_a
C Style/SpecialGlobalVars
S Details
L Looks for uses of Perl-style global variables.
L Perl-style global variables like `$;` or `$/` are cryptic
L and hard to understand without consulting documentation.
L The `English` library provides descriptive aliases like
L `$FIELD_SEPARATOR` and `$INPUT_RECORD_SEPARATOR`.
L 
L Correcting to global variables in the `English` library
L will add a require statement to the top of the file if
L enabled by RequireEnglish config.
S Safety
L   Autocorrection is marked as unsafe because if `RequireEnglish` is not
L   true, replacing perl-style variables with english variables will break.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: use_english_names (default)
L   # good
L   require 'English' # or this could be in another file.
L 
L   puts $LOAD_PATH
L   puts $LOADED_FEATURES
L   puts $PROGRAM_NAME
L   puts $ERROR_INFO
L   puts $ERROR_POSITION
L   puts $FIELD_SEPARATOR # or $FS
L   puts $OUTPUT_FIELD_SEPARATOR # or $OFS
L   puts $INPUT_RECORD_SEPARATOR # or $RS
L   puts $OUTPUT_RECORD_SEPARATOR # or $ORS
L   puts $INPUT_LINE_NUMBER # or $NR
L   puts $LAST_READ_LINE
L   puts $DEFAULT_OUTPUT
L   puts $DEFAULT_INPUT
L   puts $PROCESS_ID # or $PID
L   puts $CHILD_STATUS
L   puts $LAST_MATCH_INFO
L   puts $IGNORECASE
L   puts $ARGV # or ARGV
S Example: EnforcedStyle: use_perl_names
L   # good
L   puts $:
L   puts $"
L   puts $0
L   puts $!
L   puts $@
L   puts $;
L   puts $,
L   puts $/
L   puts $\
L   puts $.
L   puts $_
L   puts $>
L   puts $<
L   puts $$
L   puts $?
L   puts $~
L   puts $=
L   puts $*
S Example: EnforcedStyle: use_builtin_english_names
L   # good
L   # Like `use_perl_names` but allows builtin global vars.
L   puts $LOAD_PATH
L   puts $LOADED_FEATURES
L   puts $PROGRAM_NAME
L   puts ARGV
L   puts $:
L   puts $"
L   puts $0
L   puts $!
L   puts $@
L   puts $;
L   puts $,
L   puts $/
L   puts $\
L   puts $.
L   puts $_
L   puts $>
L   puts $<
L   puts $$
L   puts $?
L   puts $~
L   puts $=
L   puts $*
C Style/StabbyLambdaParentheses
S Details
L Checks for parentheses around stabby lambda arguments.
L There are two different styles. Defaults to `require_parentheses`.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: require_parentheses (default)
L   # bad
L   ->a,b,c { a + b + c }
L 
L   # good
L   ->(a,b,c) { a + b + c}
S Example: EnforcedStyle: require_no_parentheses
L   # bad
L   ->(a,b,c) { a + b + c }
L 
L   # good
L   ->a,b,c { a + b + c}
C Style/StaticClass
S Details
L Checks for places where classes with only class methods can be
L replaced with a module. Classes should be used only when it makes sense to create
L instances out of them.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe, because it is possible that this class is a parent
L   for some other subclass, monkey-patched with instance methods or
L   a dummy instance is instantiated from it somewhere.
L 
L When `AllCops/UseProjectIndex` is enabled and the `rubydex` gem is
L installed, classes that are subclassed anywhere in the project are
L not reported, since converting them to modules would break their
L subclasses.
S Example
L   # bad
L   class SomeClass
L     def self.some_method
L       # body omitted
L     end
L 
L     def self.some_other_method
L       # body omitted
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   module SomeModule
L     module_function
L 
L     def some_method
L       # body omitted
L     end
L 
L     def some_other_method
L       # body omitted
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good - has instance method
L   class SomeClass
L     def instance_method; end
L     def self.class_method; end
L   end
C Style/StderrPuts
S Details
L Identifies places where `$stderr.puts` can be replaced by
L `warn`. The latter has the advantage of easily being disabled by,
L the `-W0` interpreter flag or setting `$VERBOSE` to `nil`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   $stderr.puts('hello')
L 
L   # good
L   warn('hello')
C Style/StringChars
S Details
L Checks for uses of `String#split` with empty string or regexp literal argument.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because it cannot be guaranteed that the receiver
L   is actually a string. If another class has a `split` method with
L   different behavior, it would be registered as a false positive.
S Example
L   # bad
L   string.split(//)
L   string.split('')
L 
L   # good
L   string.chars
C Style/StringConcatenation
S Details
L Checks for places where string concatenation
L can be replaced with string interpolation.
L 
L The cop can autocorrect simple cases but will skip autocorrecting
L more complex cases where the resulting code would be harder to read.
L In those cases, it might be useful to extract statements to local
L variables or methods which you can then interpolate in a string.
L 
L NOTE: When concatenation between two strings is broken over multiple
L lines, this cop does not register an offense; instead,
L `Style/LineEndConcatenation` will pick up the offense if enabled.
L 
L Two modes are supported:
L 1. `aggressive` style checks and corrects all occurrences of `+` where
L either the left or right side of `+` is a string literal.
L 2. `conservative` style on the other hand, checks and corrects only if
L left side (receiver of `+` method call) is a string literal.
L This is useful when the receiver is some expression that returns string like `Pathname`
L instead of a string literal.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe in `aggressive` mode, as it cannot be guaranteed that
L   the receiver is actually a string, which can result in a false positive.
S Example: Mode: aggressive (default)
L   # bad
L   email_with_name = user.name + ' <' + user.email + '>'
L   Pathname.new('/') + 'test'
L 
L   # good
L   email_with_name = "#{user.name} <#{user.email}>"
L   email_with_name = format('%s <%s>', user.name, user.email)
L   "#{Pathname.new('/')}test"
L 
L   # accepted, line-end concatenation
L   name = 'First' +
L     'Last'
S Example: Mode: conservative
L   # bad
L   'Hello' + user.name
L 
L   # good
L   "Hello #{user.name}"
L   user.name + '!!'
L   Pathname.new('/') + 'test'
C Style/StringHashKeys
S Details
L Checks for the use of strings as keys in hashes. The use of
L symbols is preferred instead.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because while symbols are preferred for hash keys,
L   there are instances when string keys are required.
S Example
L   # bad
L   { 'one' => 1, 'two' => 2, 'three' => 3 }
L 
L   # good
L   { one: 1, two: 2, three: 3 }
C Style/StringLiterals
S Details
L Checks if uses of quotes match the configured preference.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: single_quotes (default)
L   # bad
L   "No special symbols"
L   "No string interpolation"
L   "Just text"
L 
L   # good
L   'No special symbols'
L   'No string interpolation'
L   'Just text'
L   "Wait! What's #{this}!"
S Example: EnforcedStyle: double_quotes
L   # bad
L   'Just some text'
L   'No special chars or interpolation'
L 
L   # good
L   "Just some text"
L   "No special chars or interpolation"
L   "Every string in #{project} uses double_quotes"
C Style/StringLiteralsInInterpolation
S Details
L Checks that quotes inside string, symbol, and regexp interpolations
L match the configured preference.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: single_quotes (default)
L   # bad
L   string = "Tests #{success ? "PASS" : "FAIL"}"
L   symbol = :"Tests #{success ? "PASS" : "FAIL"}"
L   heredoc = <<~TEXT
L     Tests #{success ? "PASS" : "FAIL"}
L   TEXT
L   regexp = /Tests #{success ? "PASS" : "FAIL"}/
L 
L   # good
L   string = "Tests #{success ? 'PASS' : 'FAIL'}"
L   symbol = :"Tests #{success ? 'PASS' : 'FAIL'}"
L   heredoc = <<~TEXT
L     Tests #{success ? 'PASS' : 'FAIL'}
L   TEXT
L   regexp = /Tests #{success ? 'PASS' : 'FAIL'}/
S Example: EnforcedStyle: double_quotes
L   # bad
L   string = "Tests #{success ? 'PASS' : 'FAIL'}"
L   symbol = :"Tests #{success ? 'PASS' : 'FAIL'}"
L   heredoc = <<~TEXT
L     Tests #{success ? 'PASS' : 'FAIL'}
L   TEXT
L   regexp = /Tests #{success ? 'PASS' : 'FAIL'}/
L 
L   # good
L   string = "Tests #{success ? "PASS" : "FAIL"}"
L   symbol = :"Tests #{success ? "PASS" : "FAIL"}"
L   heredoc = <<~TEXT
L     Tests #{success ? "PASS" : "FAIL"}
L   TEXT
L   regexp = /Tests #{success ? "PASS" : "FAIL"}/
C Style/StringMethods
S Details
L Enforces the use of consistent method names
L from the `String` class.
S Example
L   # bad
L   'name'.intern
L   'var'.unfavored_method
L 
L   # good
L   'name'.to_sym
L   'var'.preferred_method
C Style/Strip
S Details
L Identifies places where `lstrip.rstrip` can be replaced by
L `strip`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   'abc'.lstrip.rstrip
L   'abc'.rstrip.lstrip
L 
L   # good
L   'abc'.strip
C Style/StructInheritance
S Details
L Checks for inheritance from `Struct.new`. Inheriting from `Struct.new`
L adds a superfluous level in inheritance tree.
S Safety
L   Autocorrection is unsafe because it will change the inheritance
L   tree (e.g. return value of `Module#ancestors`) of the constant.
L 
L   It is also unsafe because constants that the class body resolves through its
L   ancestors (e.g. one provided by an included module) fall out of scope inside
L   the block.
S Example
L   # bad
L   class Person < Struct.new(:first_name, :last_name)
L     def age
L       42
L     end
L   end
L 
L   Person.ancestors
L   # => [Person, #<Class:0x000000010b4e14a0>, Struct, (...)]
L 
L   # good
L   Person = Struct.new(:first_name, :last_name) do
L     def age
L       42
L     end
L   end
L 
L   Person.ancestors
L   # => [Person, Struct, (...)]
C Style/SuperArguments
S Details
L Checks for redundant argument forwarding when calling super with arguments identical to
L the method definition.
L 
L Using zero arity `super` within a `define_method` block results in `RuntimeError`:
L 
L [source,ruby]
L ----
L def m
L   define_method(:foo) { super() } # => OK
L end
L 
L def m
L   define_method(:foo) { super }   # => RuntimeError
L end
L ----
L 
L Furthermore, any arguments accompanied by a block may potentially be delegating to
L `define_method`, therefore, `super` used within these blocks will be allowed.
L This approach might result in false negatives, yet ensuring safe detection takes precedence.
L 
L NOTE: When forwarding the same arguments but replacing the block argument with a new inline
L block, it is not necessary to explicitly list the non-block arguments. As such, an offense
L will be registered in this case.
S Example
L   # bad
L   def method(*args, **kwargs)
L     super(*args, **kwargs)
L   end
L 
L   # good - implicitly passing all arguments
L   def method(*args, **kwargs)
L     super
L   end
L 
L   # good - forwarding a subset of the arguments
L   def method(*args, **kwargs)
L     super(*args)
L   end
L 
L   # good - forwarding no arguments
L   def method(*args, **kwargs)
L     super()
L   end
L 
L   # bad - forwarding with overridden block
L   def method(*args, **kwargs, &block)
L     super(*args, **kwargs) { do_something }
L   end
L 
L   # good - implicitly passing all non-block arguments
L   def method(*args, **kwargs, &block)
L     super { do_something }
L   end
L 
L   # good - assigning to the block variable before calling super
L   def method(&block)
L     # Assigning to the block variable would pass the old value to super,
L     # under this circumstance the block must be referenced explicitly.
L     block ||= proc { 'fallback behavior' }
L     super(&block)
L   end
C Style/SuperWithArgsParentheses
S Details
L Enforces the presence of parentheses in `super` containing arguments.
L 
L `super` is a keyword and is provided as a distinct cop from those designed for method call.
S Example
L   # bad
L   super name, age
L 
L   # good
L   super(name, age)
C Style/SwapValues
S Details
L Enforces the use of shorthand-style swapping of 2 variables.
S Safety
L   Autocorrection is unsafe, because the temporary variable used to
L   swap variables will be removed, but may be referred to elsewhere.
S Example
L   # bad
L   tmp = x
L   x = y
L   y = tmp
L 
L   # good
L   x, y = y, x
C Style/SymbolArray
S Details
L Checks for array literals made up of symbols that are not
L using the %i() syntax.
L 
L Alternatively, it checks for symbol arrays using the %i() syntax on
L projects which do not want to use that syntax, perhaps because they
L support a version of Ruby lower than 2.0.
L 
L Configuration option: MinSize
L If set, arrays with fewer elements than this value will not trigger the
L cop. For example, a `MinSize` of `3` will not enforce a style on an
L array of 2 or fewer elements.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: percent (default)
L   # good
L   %i[foo bar baz]
L 
L   # bad
L   [:foo, :bar, :baz]
L 
L   # bad (contains spaces)
L   %i[foo\ bar baz\ quux]
L 
L   # bad (contains [] with spaces)
L   %i[foo \[ \]]
L 
L   # bad (contains () with spaces)
L   %i(foo \( \))
S Example: EnforcedStyle: brackets
L   # good
L   [:foo, :bar, :baz]
L 
L   # bad
L   %i[foo bar baz]
C Style/SymbolLiteral
S Details
L Checks symbol literal syntax.
S Example
L   # bad
L   :"symbol"
L 
L   # good
L   :symbol
C Style/SymbolProc
S Details
L Use symbols as procs when possible.
L 
L If you prefer a style that allows block for method with arguments,
L please set `true` to `AllowMethodsWithArguments`.
L `define_method?` methods are allowed by default.
L These are customizable with `AllowedMethods` option.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because there is a difference that a `Proc`
L   generated from `Symbol#to_proc` behaves as a lambda, while
L   a `Proc` generated from a block does not.
L   For example, a lambda will raise an `ArgumentError` if the
L   number of arguments is wrong, but a non-lambda `Proc` will not.
L 
L   For example:
L 
L   [source,ruby]
L   ----
L   class Foo
L     def bar
L       :bar
L     end
L   end
L 
L   def call(options = {}, &block)
L     block.call(Foo.new, options)
L   end
L 
L   call { |x| x.bar }
L   #=> :bar
L   call(&:bar)
L   # ArgumentError: wrong number of arguments (given 1, expected 0)
L   ----
L 
L   It is also unsafe because `Symbol#to_proc` does not work with
L   `protected` methods which would otherwise be accessible.
L 
L   For example:
L 
L   [source,ruby]
L   ----
L   class Box
L     def initialize
L       @secret = rand
L     end
L 
L     def normal_matches?(*others)
L       others.map { |other| other.secret }.any?(secret)
L     end
L 
L     def symbol_to_proc_matches?(*others)
L       others.map(&:secret).any?(secret)
L     end
L 
L     protected
L 
L     attr_reader :secret
L   end
L 
L   boxes = [Box.new, Box.new]
L   Box.new.normal_matches?(*boxes)
L   # => false
L   boxes.first.normal_matches?(*boxes)
L   # => true
L   Box.new.symbol_to_proc_matches?(*boxes)
L   # => NoMethodError: protected method `secret' called for #<Box...>
L   boxes.first.symbol_to_proc_matches?(*boxes)
L   # => NoMethodError: protected method `secret' called for #<Box...>
L   ----
S Example
L   # bad
L   something.map { |s| s.upcase }
L   something.map { _1.upcase }
L 
L   # good
L   something.map(&:upcase)
S Example: AllowMethodsWithArguments: false (default)
L   # bad
L   something.do_something(foo) { |o| o.bar }
L 
L   # good
L   something.do_something(foo, &:bar)
S Example: AllowMethodsWithArguments: true
L   # good
L   something.do_something(foo) { |o| o.bar }
S Example: AllowComments: false (default)
L   # bad
L   something.do_something do |s| # some comment
L     # some comment
L     s.upcase # some comment
L     # some comment
L   end
S Example: AllowComments: true
L   # good  - if there are comment in either position
L   something.do_something do |s| # some comment
L     # some comment
L     s.upcase # some comment
L     # some comment
L   end
S Example: AllowedMethods: [define_method] (default)
L   # good
L   define_method(:foo) { |foo| foo.bar }
S Example: AllowedPatterns: [] (default)
L   # bad
L   something.map { |s| s.upcase }
S Example: AllowedPatterns: ['map'] (default)
L   # good
L   something.map { |s| s.upcase }
S Example: AllCops:ActiveSupportExtensionsEnabled: false (default)
L   # bad
L   ->(x) { x.foo }
L   proc { |x| x.foo }
L   Proc.new { |x| x.foo }
L 
L   # good
L   lambda(&:foo)
L   proc(&:foo)
L   Proc.new(&:foo)
S Example: AllCops:ActiveSupportExtensionsEnabled: true
L   # good
L   ->(x) { x.foo }
L   proc { |x| x.foo }
L   Proc.new { |x| x.foo }
C Style/TallyMethod
S Details
L Checks for manual counting patterns that can be replaced by `Enumerable#tally`.
L 
L The cop detects the following patterns:
L 
L - `each_with_object(Hash.new(0)) { |item, counts| counts[item] += 1 }`
L - `group_by(&:itself).transform_values(&:count)`
L - `group_by { |x| x }.transform_values(&:size)`
L - `group_by { |x| x }.transform_values { |v| v.length }`
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because it cannot guarantee that the receiver
L   is an `Enumerable` by static analysis, so the correction may
L   not be actually equivalent.
S Example
L   # bad
L   array.each_with_object(Hash.new(0)) { |item, counts| counts[item] += 1 }
L 
L   # bad
L   array.group_by(&:itself).transform_values(&:count)
L 
L   # bad
L   array.group_by { |item| item }.transform_values(&:size)
L 
L   # bad
L   array.group_by { |item| item }.transform_values { |v| v.length }
L 
L   # good
L   array.tally
C Style/TernaryParentheses
S Details
L Checks for the presence of parentheses around ternary
L conditions. It is configurable to enforce inclusion or omission of
L parentheses using `EnforcedStyle`. Omission is only enforced when
L removing the parentheses won't cause a different behavior.
L 
L `AllowSafeAssignment` option for safe assignment.
L By safe assignment we mean putting parentheses around
L an assignment to indicate "I know I'm using an assignment
L as a condition. It's not a mistake."
S Example: EnforcedStyle: require_no_parentheses (default)
L   # bad
L   foo = (bar?) ? a : b
L   foo = (bar.baz?) ? a : b
L   foo = (bar && baz) ? a : b
L 
L   # good
L   foo = bar? ? a : b
L   foo = bar.baz? ? a : b
L   foo = bar && baz ? a : b
S Example: EnforcedStyle: require_parentheses
L   # bad
L   foo = bar? ? a : b
L   foo = bar.baz? ? a : b
L   foo = bar && baz ? a : b
L 
L   # good
L   foo = (bar?) ? a : b
L   foo = (bar.baz?) ? a : b
L   foo = (bar && baz) ? a : b
S Example: EnforcedStyle: require_parentheses_when_complex
L   # bad
L   foo = (bar?) ? a : b
L   foo = (bar.baz?) ? a : b
L   foo = bar && baz ? a : b
L 
L   # good
L   foo = bar? ? a : b
L   foo = bar.baz? ? a : b
L   foo = (bar && baz) ? a : b
S Example: AllowSafeAssignment: true (default)
L   # good
L   foo = (bar = baz) ? a : b
S Example: AllowSafeAssignment: false
L   # bad
L   foo = (bar = baz) ? a : b
C Style/TimeNow
S Details
L Checks for `Time.new` without arguments, which is an implicit way
L to retrieve the current system time. Prefer the more explicit `Time.now`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   Time.new
L 
L   # good
L   Time.now
L 
L   # good - `Time.new` with arguments constructs a specific time
L   Time.new(2026, 8, 19)
C Style/TopLevelMethodDefinition
S Details
L Newcomers to Ruby applications may write top-level methods,
L when ideally they should be organized in appropriate classes or modules.
L This cop looks for definitions of top-level methods and warns about them.
L 
L However, for Ruby scripts it is perfectly fine to use top-level methods.
L Hence this cop is disabled by default.
S Example
L   # bad
L   def some_method
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   def self.some_method
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   define_method(:foo) { puts 1 }
L 
L   # good
L   module Foo
L     def some_method
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class Foo
L     def self.some_method
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   Struct.new do
L     def some_method
L     end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class Foo
L     define_method(:foo) { puts 1 }
L   end
C Style/TrailingBodyOnClass
S Details
L Checks for trailing code after the class definition.
S Example
L   # bad
L   class Foo; def foo; end
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class Foo
L     def foo; end
L   end
C Style/TrailingBodyOnMethodDefinition
S Details
L Checks for trailing code after the method definition.
L 
L NOTE: It always accepts endless method definitions that are basically on the same line.
S Example
L   # bad
L   def some_method; do_stuff
L   end
L 
L   def f(x); b = foo
L     b[c: x]
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   def some_method
L     do_stuff
L   end
L 
L   def f(x)
L     b = foo
L     b[c: x]
L   end
L 
L   def endless_method = do_stuff
C Style/TrailingBodyOnModule
S Details
L Checks for trailing code after the module definition.
S Example
L   # bad
L   module Foo extend self
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   module Foo
L     extend self
L   end
C Style/TrailingCommaInArguments
S Details
L Checks for trailing comma in argument lists.
L The supported styles are:
L 
L * `consistent_comma`: Requires a comma after the last argument,
L for all parenthesized multi-line method calls with arguments.
L * `comma`: Requires a comma after the last argument, but only for
L parenthesized method calls where each argument is on its own line.
L * `diff_comma`: Requires a comma after the last argument, but only
L when that argument is followed by an immediate newline, even if
L there is an inline comment on the same line.
L * `no_comma`: Requires that there is no comma after the last
L argument.
L 
L Regardless of style, trailing commas are not allowed in
L single-line method calls.
S Example: EnforcedStyleForMultiline: consistent_comma
L   # bad
L   method(1, 2,)
L 
L   # good
L   method(1, 2)
L 
L   # good
L   method(
L     1, 2,
L     3,
L   )
L 
L   # good
L   method(
L     1, 2, 3,
L   )
L 
L   # good
L   method(
L     1,
L     2,
L   )
S Example: EnforcedStyleForMultiline: comma
L   # bad
L   method(1, 2,)
L 
L   # good
L   method(1, 2)
L 
L   # bad
L   method(
L     1, 2,
L     3,
L   )
L 
L   # good
L   method(
L     1, 2,
L     3
L   )
L 
L   # bad
L   method(
L     1, 2, 3,
L   )
L 
L   # good
L   method(
L     1, 2, 3
L   )
L 
L   # good
L   method(
L     1,
L     2,
L   )
S Example: EnforcedStyleForMultiline: diff_comma
L   # bad
L   method(1, 2,)
L 
L   # good
L   method(1, 2)
L 
L   # good
L   method(
L     1, 2,
L     3,
L   )
L 
L   # good
L   method(
L     1, 2, 3,
L   )
L 
L   # good
L   method(
L     1,
L     2,
L   )
L 
L   # bad
L   method(1, [
L     2,
L   ],)
L 
L   # good
L   method(1, [
L     2,
L   ])
L 
L   # bad
L   object[1, 2,
L          3, 4,]
L 
L   # good
L   object[1, 2,
L          3, 4]
S Example: EnforcedStyleForMultiline: no_comma (default)
L   # bad
L   method(1, 2,)
L 
L   # bad
L   object[1, 2,]
L 
L   # good
L   method(1, 2)
L 
L   # good
L   object[1, 2]
L 
L   # good
L   method(
L     1,
L     2
L   )
C Style/TrailingCommaInArrayLiteral
S Details
L Checks for trailing comma in array literals.
L The configuration options are:
L 
L * `consistent_comma`: Requires a comma after the last item of all non-empty, multiline array
L literals.
L * `comma`: Requires a comma after the last item in an array, but only when each item is on
L its own line.
L * `diff_comma`: Requires a comma after the last item in an array, but only when that item is
L followed by an immediate newline, even if there is an inline comment on the same line.
L * `no_comma`: Does not require a comma after the last item in an array
S Example: EnforcedStyleForMultiline: consistent_comma
L   # bad
L   a = [1, 2,]
L 
L   # good
L   a = [1, 2]
L 
L   # good
L   a = [
L     1, 2,
L     3,
L   ]
L 
L   # good
L   a = [
L     1, 2, 3,
L   ]
L 
L   # good
L   a = [
L     1,
L     2,
L   ]
L 
L   # bad
L   a = [1, 2,
L        3, 4]
L 
L   # good
L   a = [1, 2,
L        3, 4,]
S Example: EnforcedStyleForMultiline: comma
L   # bad
L   a = [1, 2,]
L 
L   # good
L   a = [1, 2]
L 
L   # bad
L   a = [
L     1, 2,
L     3,
L   ]
L 
L   # good
L   a = [
L     1, 2,
L     3
L   ]
L 
L   # bad
L   a = [
L     1, 2, 3,
L   ]
L 
L   # good
L   a = [
L     1, 2, 3
L   ]
L 
L   # good
L   a = [
L     1,
L     2,
L   ]
S Example: EnforcedStyleForMultiline: diff_comma
L   # bad
L   a = [1, 2,]
L 
L   # good
L   a = [1, 2]
L 
L   # good
L   a = [
L     1, 2,
L     3,
L   ]
L 
L   # good
L   a = [
L     1, 2, 3,
L   ]
L 
L   # good
L   a = [
L     1,
L     2,
L   ]
L 
L   # bad
L   a = [1, 2,
L        3, 4,]
L 
L   # good
L   a = [1, 2,
L        3, 4]
S Example: EnforcedStyleForMultiline: no_comma (default)
L   # bad
L   a = [1, 2,]
L 
L   # good
L   a = [
L     1,
L     2
L   ]
C Style/TrailingCommaInBlockArgs
S Details
L Checks whether trailing commas in block arguments are
L required. Blocks with only one argument and a trailing comma require
L that comma to be present. Blocks with more than one argument never
L require a trailing comma.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because a trailing comma can indicate there are
L   more parameters that are not used.
L 
L   For example:
L   [source,ruby]
L   ----
L   # with a trailing comma
L   {foo: 1, bar: 2, baz: 3}.map {|key,| key }
L   #=> [:foo, :bar, :baz]
L 
L   # without a trailing comma
L   {foo: 1, bar: 2, baz: 3}.map {|key| key }
L   #=> [[:foo, 1], [:bar, 2], [:baz, 3]]
L   ----
L 
L   This can be fixed by replacing the trailing comma with a placeholder
L   argument (such as `|key, _value|`).
S Example
L   # bad
L   add { |foo, bar,| foo + bar }
L 
L   # good
L   add { |foo, bar| foo + bar }
L 
L   # good
L   add { |foo,| foo }
L 
L   # good
L   add { foo }
L 
L   # bad
L   add do |foo, bar,|
L     foo + bar
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   add do |foo, bar|
L     foo + bar
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   add do |foo,|
L     foo
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   add do
L     foo + bar
L   end
C Style/TrailingCommaInHashLiteral
S Details
L Checks for trailing comma in hash literals.
L The configuration options are:
L 
L * `consistent_comma`: Requires a comma after the last item of all non-empty, multiline hash
L literals.
L * `comma`: Requires a comma after the last item in a hash, but only when each item is on its
L own line.
L * `diff_comma`: Requires a comma after the last item in a hash, but only when that item is
L followed by an immediate newline, even if there is an inline comment on the same line.
L * `no_comma`: Does not require a comma after the last item in a hash
S Example: EnforcedStyleForMultiline: consistent_comma
L   # bad
L   a = { foo: 1, bar: 2, }
L 
L   # good
L   a = { foo: 1, bar: 2 }
L 
L   # good
L   a = {
L     foo: 1, bar: 2,
L     qux: 3,
L   }
L 
L   # good
L   a = {
L     foo: 1, bar: 2, qux: 3,
L   }
L 
L   # good
L   a = {
L     foo: 1,
L     bar: 2,
L   }
L 
L   # bad
L   a = { foo: 1, bar: 2,
L         baz: 3, qux: 4 }
L 
L   # good
L   a = { foo: 1, bar: 2,
L         baz: 3, qux: 4, }
S Example: EnforcedStyleForMultiline: comma
L   # bad
L   a = { foo: 1, bar: 2, }
L 
L   # good
L   a = { foo: 1, bar: 2 }
L 
L   # bad
L   a = {
L     foo: 1, bar: 2,
L     qux: 3,
L   }
L 
L   # good
L   a = {
L     foo: 1, bar: 2,
L     qux: 3
L   }
L 
L   # bad
L   a = {
L     foo: 1, bar: 2, qux: 3,
L   }
L 
L   # good
L   a = {
L     foo: 1, bar: 2, qux: 3
L   }
L 
L   # good
L   a = {
L     foo: 1,
L     bar: 2,
L   }
S Example: EnforcedStyleForMultiline: diff_comma
L   # bad
L   a = { foo: 1, bar: 2, }
L 
L   # good
L   a = { foo: 1, bar: 2 }
L 
L   # good
L   a = {
L     foo: 1, bar: 2,
L     qux: 3,
L   }
L 
L   # good
L   a = {
L     foo: 1, bar: 2, qux: 3,
L   }
L 
L   # good
L   a = {
L     foo: 1,
L     bar: 2,
L   }
L 
L   # bad
L   a = { foo: 1, bar: 2,
L         baz: 3, qux: 4, }
L 
L   # good
L   a = { foo: 1, bar: 2,
L         baz: 3, qux: 4 }
S Example: EnforcedStyleForMultiline: no_comma (default)
L   # bad
L   a = { foo: 1, bar: 2, }
L 
L   # good
L   a = {
L     foo: 1,
L     bar: 2
L   }
C Style/TrailingMethodEndStatement
S Details
L Checks for trailing code after the method definition.
S Example
L   # bad
L   def some_method
L   do_stuff; end
L 
L   def do_this(x)
L     baz.map { |b| b.this(x) } end
L 
L   def foo
L     block do
L       bar
L     end end
L 
L   # good
L   def some_method
L     do_stuff
L   end
L 
L   def do_this(x)
L     baz.map { |b| b.this(x) }
L   end
L 
L   def foo
L     block do
L       bar
L     end
L   end
C Style/TrailingUnderscoreVariable
S Details
L Checks for extra underscores in variable assignment.
S Example
L   # bad
L   a, b, _ = foo
L   a, b, _, = foo
L   a, _, _ = foo
L   a, _, _, = foo
L 
L   # good
L   a, b, = foo
L   a, = foo
L   *a, b, _ = foo
L   # => We need to know to not include 2 variables in a
L   a, *b, _ = foo
L   # => The correction `a, *b, = foo` is a syntax error
S Example: AllowNamedUnderscoreVariables: true (default)
L   # good
L   a, b, _something = foo
S Example: AllowNamedUnderscoreVariables: false
L   # bad
L   a, b, _something = foo
C Style/TrivialAccessors
S Details
L Looks for trivial reader/writer methods, that could
L have been created with the attr_* family of functions automatically.
L `to_ary`, `to_a`, `to_c`, `to_enum`, `to_h`, `to_hash`, `to_i`, `to_int`, `to_io`,
L `to_open`, `to_path`, `to_proc`, `to_r`, `to_regexp`, `to_str`, `to_s`, and `to_sym` methods
L are allowed by default. These are customizable with `AllowedMethods` option.
S Example
L   # bad
L   def foo
L     @foo
L   end
L 
L   def bar=(val)
L     @bar = val
L   end
L 
L   def self.baz
L     @baz
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   attr_reader :foo
L   attr_writer :bar
L 
L   class << self
L     attr_reader :baz
L   end
S Example: ExactNameMatch: true (default)
L   # good
L   def name
L     @other_name
L   end
S Example: ExactNameMatch: false
L   # bad
L   def name
L     @other_name
L   end
S Example: AllowPredicates: true (default)
L   # good
L   def foo?
L     @foo
L   end
S Example: AllowPredicates: false
L   # bad
L   def foo?
L     @foo
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   attr_reader :foo
S Example: AllowDSLWriters: true (default)
L   # good
L   def on_exception(action)
L     @on_exception=action
L   end
S Example: AllowDSLWriters: false
L   # bad
L   def on_exception(action)
L     @on_exception=action
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   attr_writer :on_exception
S Example: IgnoreClassMethods: false (default)
L   # bad
L   def self.foo
L     @foo
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   class << self
L     attr_reader :foo
L   end
S Example: IgnoreClassMethods: true
L   # good
L   def self.foo
L     @foo
L   end
S Example: AllowedMethods: ['allowed_method']
L   # good
L   def allowed_method
L     @foo
L   end
C Style/UnlessElse
S Details
L Looks for `unless` expressions with `else` clauses.
S Example
L   # bad
L   unless foo_bar.nil?
L     # do something...
L   else
L     # do a different thing...
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   if foo_bar.present?
L     # do something...
L   else
L     # do a different thing...
L   end
C Style/UnlessLogicalOperators
S Details
L Checks for the use of logical operators in an `unless` condition.
L It discourages such code, as the condition becomes more difficult
L to read and understand.
L 
L This cop supports two styles:
L 
L - `forbid_mixed_logical_operators` (default)
L - `forbid_logical_operators`
L 
L `forbid_mixed_logical_operators` style forbids the use of more than one type
L of logical operators. This makes the `unless` condition easier to read
L because either all conditions need to be met or any condition needs to be met
L in order for the expression to be truthy or falsey.
L 
L `forbid_logical_operators` style forbids any use of logical operators.
L This makes it even easier to read the `unless` condition as
L there is only one condition in the expression.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: forbid_mixed_logical_operators (default)
L   # bad
L   return unless a || b && c
L   return unless a && b || c
L   return unless a && b and c
L   return unless a || b or c
L   return unless a && b or c
L   return unless a || b and c
L 
L   # good
L   return unless a && b && c
L   return unless a || b || c
L   return unless a and b and c
L   return unless a or b or c
L   return unless a?
S Example: EnforcedStyle: forbid_logical_operators
L   # bad
L   return unless a || b
L   return unless a && b
L   return unless a or b
L   return unless a and b
L 
L   # good
L   return unless a
L   return unless a?
C Style/UnpackFirst
S Details
L Checks for accessing the first element of `String#unpack`
L which can be replaced with the shorter method `unpack1`.
S Example
L   # bad
L   'foo'.unpack('h*').first
L   'foo'.unpack('h*')[0]
L   'foo'.unpack('h*').slice(0)
L   'foo'.unpack('h*').at(0)
L 
L   # good
L   'foo'.unpack1('h*')
C Style/VariableInterpolation
S Details
L Checks for variable interpolation (like "#@ivar").
S Example
L   # bad
L   "His name is #$name"
L   /check #$pattern/
L   "Let's go to the #@store"
L 
L   # good
L   "His name is #{$name}"
L   /check #{$pattern}/
L   "Let's go to the #{@store}"
C Style/WhenThen
S Details
L Checks for `when;` uses in `case` expressions.
S Example
L   # bad
L   case foo
L   when 1; 'baz'
L   when 2; 'bar'
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   case foo
L   when 1 then 'baz'
L   when 2 then 'bar'
L   end
C Style/WhileUntilDo
S Details
L Checks for uses of `do` in multi-line `while/until` statements.
S Example
L   # bad
L   while x.any? do
L     do_something(x.pop)
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   while x.any?
L     do_something(x.pop)
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   until x.empty? do
L     do_something(x.pop)
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   until x.empty?
L     do_something(x.pop)
L   end
C Style/WhileUntilModifier
S Details
L Checks for while and until statements that would fit on one line
L if written as a modifier while/until. The maximum line length is
L configured in the `Layout/LineLength` cop.
S Example
L   # bad
L   while x < 10
L     x += 1
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   x += 1 while x < 10
L 
L   # good
L   while x < 10
L     y += 1 if x.odd?
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   until x > 10
L     x += 1
L   end
L 
L   # good
L   x += 1 until x > 10
L 
L   # good
L   until x > 10
L     y += 1 unless x.even?
L   end
L 
L   # bad
L   x += 100 while x < 500 # a long comment that makes code too long if it were a single line
L 
L   # good
L   while x < 500 # a long comment that makes code too long if it were a single line
L     x += 100
L   end
C Style/WordArray
S Details
L Checks for array literals made up of word-like
L strings, that are not using the %w() syntax.
L 
L Alternatively, it can check for uses of the %w() syntax, in projects
L which do not want to include that syntax.
L 
L NOTE: When using the `percent` style, %w() arrays containing a space
L will be registered as offenses.
L 
L Configuration option: MinSize
L If set, arrays with fewer elements than this value will not trigger the
L cop. For example, a `MinSize` of `3` will not enforce a style on an
L array of 2 or fewer elements.
S Example: EnforcedStyle: percent (default)
L   # good
L   %w[foo bar baz]
L 
L   # bad
L   ['foo', 'bar', 'baz']
L 
L   # bad (contains spaces)
L   %w[foo\ bar baz\ quux]
L 
L   # bad
L   [
L     ['one', 'One'],
L     ['two', 'Two']
L   ]
L 
L   # good
L   [
L     %w[one One],
L     %w[two Two]
L   ]
L 
L   # good (2d array containing spaces)
L   [
L     ['one', 'One'],
L     ['two', 'Two'],
L     ['forty two', 'Forty Two']
L   ]
S Example: EnforcedStyle: brackets
L   # good
L   ['foo', 'bar', 'baz']
L 
L   # bad
L   %w[foo bar baz]
L 
L   # good (contains spaces)
L   ['foo bar', 'baz quux']
L 
L   # good
L   [
L     ['one', 'One'],
L     ['two', 'Two']
L   ]
L 
L   # bad
L   [
L     %w[one One],
L     %w[two Two]
L   ]
C Style/YAMLFileRead
S Details
L Checks for the use of `YAML.load`, `YAML.safe_load`, and `YAML.parse` with
L `File.read` argument.
L 
L NOTE: `YAML.safe_load_file` was introduced in Ruby 3.0.
S Example
L   # bad
L   YAML.load(File.read(path))
L   YAML.parse(File.read(path))
L 
L   # good
L   YAML.load_file(path)
L   YAML.parse_file(path)
L 
L   # bad
L   YAML.safe_load(File.read(path)) # Ruby 3.0 and newer
L 
L   # good
L   YAML.safe_load_file(path)       # Ruby 3.0 and newer
C Style/YodaCondition
S Details
L Enforces or forbids Yoda conditions,
L i.e. comparison operations where the order of expression is reversed.
L eg. `5 == x`
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because comparison operators can be defined
L   differently on different classes, and are not guaranteed to
L   have the same result if reversed.
L 
L   For example:
L 
L   [source,ruby]
L   ----
L   class MyKlass
L     def ==(other)
L       true
L     end
L   end
L 
L   obj = MyKlass.new
L   obj == 'string'   #=> true
L   'string' == obj   #=> false
L   ----
S Example: EnforcedStyle: forbid_for_all_comparison_operators (default)
L   # bad
L   99 == foo
L   "bar" != foo
L   42 >= foo
L   10 < bar
L   99 == CONST
L 
L   # good
L   foo == 99
L   foo == "bar"
L   foo <= 42
L   bar > 10
L   CONST == 99
L   "#{interpolation}" == foo
L   /#{interpolation}/ == foo
S Example: EnforcedStyle: forbid_for_equality_operators_only
L   # bad
L   99 == foo
L   "bar" != foo
L 
L   # good
L   99 >= foo
L   3 < a && a < 5
S Example: EnforcedStyle: require_for_all_comparison_operators
L   # bad
L   foo == 99
L   foo == "bar"
L   foo <= 42
L   bar > 10
L 
L   # good
L   99 == foo
L   "bar" != foo
L   42 >= foo
L   10 < bar
S Example: EnforcedStyle: require_for_equality_operators_only
L   # bad
L   99 >= foo
L   3 < a && a < 5
L 
L   # good
L   99 == foo
L   "bar" != foo
C Style/YodaExpression
S Details
L Forbids Yoda expressions, i.e. binary operations (using `*`, `+`, `&`, `|`,
L and `^` operators) where the order of expression is reversed, eg. `1 + x`.
L This cop complements `Style/YodaCondition` cop, which has a similar purpose.
L 
L This cop is disabled by default to respect user intentions such as:
L 
L [source,ruby]
L ----
L config.server_port = 9000 + ENV["TEST_ENV_NUMBER"].to_i
L ----
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because binary operators can be defined
L   differently on different classes, and are not guaranteed to
L   have the same result if reversed.
S Example: SupportedOperators: ['*', '+', '&', '|', '^'] (default)
L   # bad
L   10 * y
L   1 + x
L   1 & z
L   1 | x
L   1 ^ x
L   1 + CONST
L 
L   # good
L   y * 10
L   x + 1
L   z & 1
L   x | 1
L   x ^ 1
L   CONST + 1
L   60 * 24
C Style/ZeroLengthPredicate
S Details
L Checks for numeric comparisons that can be replaced
L by a predicate method, such as `receiver.length == 0`,
L `receiver.length > 0`, and `receiver.length != 0`,
L `receiver.length < 1` and `receiver.size == 0` that can be
L replaced by `receiver.empty?` and `!receiver.empty?`.
L 
L NOTE: `File`, `Tempfile`, `StringIO`, and `File::Stat` do not have `empty?`
L so allow `size == 0` and `size.zero?`. Note that when a `File::Stat` object
L is stored in a variable (e.g. `stat = File.stat(path); stat.size.zero?`),
L the cop cannot detect the type and may still register a false positive.
S Safety
L   This cop is unsafe because it cannot be guaranteed that the receiver
L   has an `empty?` method that is defined in terms of `length`. If there
L   is a non-standard class that redefines `length` or `empty?`, the cop
L   may register a false positive.
S Example
L   # bad
L   [1, 2, 3].length == 0
L   0 == "foobar".length
L   array.length < 1
L   {a: 1, b: 2}.length != 0
L   string.length > 0
L   hash.size > 0
L 
L   # good
L   [1, 2, 3].empty?
L   "foobar".empty?
L   array.empty?
L   !{a: 1, b: 2}.empty?
L   !string.empty?
L   !hash.empty?
